Alexandrov geometry: foundations
Stephanie Alexander, Vitali Kapovitch

Abstract




Preface

Alexandrov spaces are defined via axioms similar to those given by Euclid.
The Alexandrov axioms replace certain equalities with inequalities.
Depending on the signs of the inequalities, we obtain Alexandrov spaces with
curvature bounded above
(CBA) and
curvature bounded below
(CBB).
The definitions of the two classes of spaces are similar, but their properties and known applications are quite different.

The goal of this book is to give a comprehensive exposition of the structure theory of Alexandrov spaces
with curvature bounded above and below.
It includes all the basic material as well as selected topics inspired by considering the two contexts simultaneously.
We only consider the intrinsic theory, leaving applications aside.
Our presentation is linear,
with a few exceptions where topics are deferred to later chapters to streamline the exposition.
This book includes material
up to the definition of dimension
.
Another volume still in preparation will cover further topics.

Brief history

The first synthetic description of curvature is due to Abraham Wald
[
156
]
;
it was given in a lone publication on a “coordinateless description of Gauss surfaces” published in 1936.
In 1941, similar definitions were rediscovered by Alexandr Alexandrov
[
16
]
.

In Alexandrov’s work the first fruitful applications of this approach were given.
Mainly:
Alexandrov’s embedding theorem

[
12
,
13
]
, which describes closed convex surfaces in Euclidean 3-space,
and the
gluing theorem

[
14
]
, which gave a flexible tool to modify non-negatively curved metrics on a sphere.
These two results together gave an intuitive geometric tool to study embeddings and bending of surfaces in Euclidean space and changed the subject dramatically.
They formed the foundation of the branch of geometry now called
Alexandrov geometry
.

Curvature bounded below.

The theory grew out of studying intrinsic and extrinsic geometry of convex surfaces without the smoothness condition.
It was developed by Alexandr Alexandrov
and his school.
Here is a very incomplete list of contributors to the subject:
Yuriy Borisov,
Yuriy Burago,
Boris Dekster,
Iosif Liberman,
Sergey Olovyanishnikov,
Aleksey Pogorelov,
Yuriy Reshetnyak,
Yuriy Volkov,
Viktor Zalgaller.

The first result in higher dimensional Alexandrov spaces was the splitting theorem.
It was proved by Anatoliy Milka
[
116
]
and appeared in 1967.
Milka used a global definition similar to the one used in this book.

In the 80’s the interest in convergence of Riemannian manifolds spurred by
Gromov’s compactness theorem

[
70
]
turned attention toward the singular spaces that can occur as limits of Riemannian manifolds.
Immediately it was recognized that if the manifolds have a uniform lower sectional curvature bound, then the limit spaces have a lower curvature bound in the sense of Alexandrov.
There followed during the 90’s an explosion of work on intrinsic theory of Alexandrov spaces starting with papers of Yuriy Burago, Grigory Perelman, and Michael Gromov
[
43
,
124
]
.
Similar ideas were developed independently by Karsten Grove
and Peter Petersen, whose work was not converted into a publication, and also by
Conrad Plaut
[
134
]
.

Around the same time an implicit application of higher-dimensional Alexandrov geometry was given by Michael Gromov in his bound on Betti numbers
[
74
]
.
Another implicit application, which essentially used Alexandrov geometry before it was was actually introduced, given later by Wu-Yi Hsiang and Bruce Kleiner in their paper on non-negatively curved 4-manifolds with infinite symmetry groups
[
82
]
.
The work of Hsiang and Kleiner and its extension by Karsten Grove and Burkhard Wilking
[
77
]
are some of the most beautiful applications of this branch of Alexandrov geometry.

The above activity was very much related to so-called
comparison geometry
,
a branch of differential geometry that compares Riemannian manifolds to spaces of constant curvature.
In addition to the already-mentioned
Gromov’s compactness theorem
,
the following results had a big influence on the development of Alexandrov geometry:

Toponogov comparison theorem

[
153
]
, which is a generalization of the theorem of Alexandrov
[
15
]
;

Toponogov splitting theorem

[
153
]
, which is a generalization of Cohn-Vossen’s theorem
[
54
]
;

Finiteness theorems
of
Cheeger
and
Grove–Petersen
[
51
,
76
]
;
Gromov’s bound on the number of generators of the fundamental group

[
72
, 1.5]
;
and

Yamaguchi fibration theorem

[
159
]
.

Let us give a list of available introductory texts on Alexandrov spaces with curvature bounded below:

\diamond

The first introduction to Alexandrov geometry is given in the original paper of Yuriy Burago, Michael Gromov, and Grigory Perelman
[
43
]

and its extension
[
124
]
written by Perelman.

\diamond

A brief and reader-friendly introduction was written by Katsuhiro Shiohama
[
147
, Sections 1–8]
.

\diamond

[
36
, Chapter 10]
gives another reader-friendly introduction, written by Dmiti Burago, Yuriy Burago, and Sergei Ivanov.

In addition, let us mention two surveys, one by Conrad Plaut
[
136
]
and the other by the third author
[
129
]
.

Curvature bounded above.

The study of spaces with curvature bounded above started later,
inspired by analogy with the theory of curvature bounded below.
The first paper on the subject was written by Alexandrov
[
18
]
, appearing in 1951.
An analogous weaker definition was considered earlier by Herbert Busemann
[
44
]
.

Contributions to the subject were made by
Valerii Berestovskii,
Arne Beurling,
Igor Nikolaev,
Dmitry Sokolov,
Yuriy Reshetnyak,
Samuel Shefel; this list is not complete as well.
The most fundamental results were obtained by Yuriy Reshetnyak.
They include his
majorization theorem
and
gluing theorem
.
The gluing theorem states that if two non-positively curved spaces have isometric convex sets, then the space obtained by gluing these sets along an isometry is also non-positively curved.

The development of Alexandrov geometry was greatly influenced by the
Hadamard–Cartan theorem
.
Its original formulation states that the exponential map at any point of a complete Riemannian manifold with nonpositive sectional curvature is a covering.
In particular, it implies that the universal cover is diffeomorphic to Euclidean space of the same dimension.
See further discussion below (
9M
).

An influential implicit application of Alexandrov spaces with curvature bounded above can be seen in
Euclidean buildings
, introduced by Jacques Tits as a means to study algebraic groups.

Here is a list of available texts covering the basics of Alexandrov spaces with curvature bounded above:

\diamond

The book of Martin Bridson and André Haefliger
[
34
]
gives the most comprehensive introduction available today.

\diamond

The lecture notes of Werner Ballmann
[
21
,
22
]
include a brief
and clear
introduction.

\diamond

[
36
, Chapter 9]
gives another reader-friendly introduction, by Yuriy Burago, Dmitry Burago, and Sergei Ivanov.

\diamond

A book by the three authors of the present volume
[
11
]
gives an introduction aiming at reaching interesting applications and theorems with a minimum of preparation.

\diamond

The book of Jürgen Jost
[
88
]
gives a more analytic viewpoint to the subject.

One of the most striking applications of
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
spaces was given by Dmitry Burago, Sergei Ferleger, and Alexey Kononenko
[
37
]
,
who used them to study
billiards
; this idea was developed further in
[
38
,
39
,
40
,
41
,
42
]
.
Another beautiful application is the construction of
exotic aspherical manifolds
by Michael Davis
[
58
]
; related results are surveyed in
[
59
,
49
]
.
Both of these topics are discussed in
[
11
]
.
The study of group actions on
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
spaces and
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
cube complexes played a key role in the proof of the
virtually fibered conjecture
that a finite cover of every closed hyperbolic 3-manifold fibers over the circle.

Satellites and successors.

Surfaces with
bounded integral curvature
were studied by Alexandrov’s school.
An excellent book on the subject was written by Alexandr Alexandrov and Viktor Zalgaller
[
1
]
; see also a more up-to-date survey by Yuriy Reshetnyak
[
138
]
.

Spaces with
two-sided bounded curvature
is another subject already studied by Alexandrov’s school;
a good survey is written by Valerij Berestovskij and Igor Nikolaev
[
24
]
.

A spin-off of the idea of synthetically defining upper curvature bounds
was given by Michael Gromov
[
75
]
.
He defined so-called
\updelta
-hyperbolic spaces
, which satisfy a coarse version of the negative curvature condition, applying in particular to discrete metric spaces.
This notion and its various generalizations such as semi-hyperbolicity (a coarse version of non-positive curvature) and relative hyperbolicity have led to the emergence of the subject of
geometric group theory
, which relates geometric properties of groups to their algebraic ones.
This is a well-developed subject with a large number of subfields and applications, such as the theory of small cancellation groups, automatic groups, mapping class groups, automorphisms of free groups, isoperimetric inequalities on groups, actions on
\mathbb{R}
-trees, Gromov’s boundaries of groups.

The so-called
curvature dimension condition
introduced by John Lott, Cédric Villani, and Karl-Theodor Sturm gives a synthetic description of Ricci curvature bounded below; see the book of Villani
[
155
]
and references therein.

Alexandrov geometry influenced the development of
analysis on metric spaces
.
An excellent book on the subject was written by Juha Heinonen, Pekka Koskela, Nageswari Shanmugalingam, and Jeremy Tyson
[
81
]
.

Part I
Preliminaries

Chapter 1
Model plane

A
Trigonometry

Given a real number
\upkappa
, the
model
\upkappa
-plane
will be a complete simply connected 2-dimensional Riemannian manifold of constant curvature
\upkappa
.

The model
\upkappa
-plane will be denoted by
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
.

\diamond

If
\upkappa>0
,
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
is isometric to a sphere of radius
\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\upkappa}}
; the unit sphere
\mathbb{M}^{2}({1})
will be also denoted by
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.

\diamond

If
\upkappa=0
,
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
is the Euclidean plane, which is also denoted by
\mathbb{E}^{2}
.

\diamond

If
\upkappa<0
,
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
is the Lobachevsky plane with curvature
\upkappa
.

Let
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}=\mathop{\rm diam}\nolimits\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
, so

\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}=\infty
if
\upkappa\leqslant\penalty 0
and
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}=\uppi/\sqrt{\upkappa}
if
\upkappa>0
;

\varpi
is just a cursive form of
\uppi
.

The distance between points
x,y\in\penalty\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
will be denoted by
|{x}-\penalty{y}|
, and
[xy]

will denote the geodesic segment connecting
x
and
y
.
The segment
[xy]
is uniquely defined for
\upkappa\leqslant\penalty 0
and for
\upkappa>0
it is defined uniquely if
|{x}-\penalty{y}|<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}=\uppi/\sqrt{\upkappa}
.

A triangle in
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
with vertices
x,y,z
will be denoted by
[{x}{y}{z}]
.
Formally, a triangle is an ordered set of its sides, so
[{x}{y}{z}]
is just a short notation for the triple
([yz],[zx],[xy])
.

The angle of
[{x}{y}{z}]
at
x
will be denoted by
\measuredangle[x\,{}^{y}_{z}]
.

By
{\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}\{a,b,c\}
we denote a triangle in

\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
with side lengths
a,b,c
, so

[{x}{y}{z}]={\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}\{a,b,c\}
means that
x,y,z\in\penalty\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
are such that

|{x}-\penalty{y}|=c,\quad|{y}-\penalty{z}|=a,\quad|{z}-\penalty{x}|=b.

For
{\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}\{a,b,c\}
to be defined, the sides
a,b,c
must satisfy the triangle inequality. If
\upkappa>0
, we
require
in addition that
a+b+c<2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
;
otherwise
{\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}\{a,b,c\}
is considered to be undefined.

Trigonometric functions.

We will need three
trigonometric functions
in
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
:
cosine
,
sine
, and
modified distance
, denoted by
\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}
,
\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}
, and
\mathrm{md}^{\upkappa}
respectively.

They are defined as the solutions of the following initial value problems respectively:

\begin{cases}x^{\prime\prime}+\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x=0,\\
x(0)=1,\\
x^{\prime}(0)=0.\end{cases}\quad\begin{cases}y^{\prime\prime}+\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y=0,\\
y(0)=0,\\
y^{\prime}(0)=1.\end{cases}\quad\begin{cases}z^{\prime\prime}+\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}z=1,\\
z(0)=0,\\
z^{\prime}(0)=0.\end{cases}

Namely, we set
\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}(t)=x(t)
,
\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}(t)=y(t)
, and

\mathrm{md}^{\upkappa}(t)=\begin{cases}z(t)&\textrm{ if }0\leqslant\penalty t\leqslant\penalty\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}},\\
\tfrac{2}{\upkappa}&\textrm{ if }t>\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}.\end{cases}

Here are the tables which relate our trigonometric functions to the standard ones, where
we take
\upkappa>0
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{sn}^{\pm\upkappa}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\upkappa}}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{sn}^{\pm 1}({x}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}{\sqrt{\upkappa}});

\displaystyle\mathrm{cs}^{\pm\upkappa}=\mathrm{cs}^{\pm 1}({x}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}{\sqrt{\upkappa}});

\displaystyle\mathrm{md}^{\pm\upkappa}=\tfrac{1}{\upkappa}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{md}^{\pm 1}({x}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}{\sqrt{\upkappa}});

\displaystyle\mathrm{sn}^{-1}x=\sinh x;

\displaystyle\mathrm{cs}^{-1}x=\cosh x;

\displaystyle\mathrm{md}^{-1}x=\cosh x-1;

\displaystyle\mathrm{sn}^{0}x=x;

\displaystyle\mathrm{cs}^{0}x=1;

\displaystyle\mathrm{md}^{0}x=\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x^{2};

\displaystyle\mathrm{sn}^{1}x=\sin x;

\displaystyle\mathrm{cs}^{1}x=\cos x;

\displaystyle\mathrm{md}^{1}x=\left\{\begin{smallmatrix}1-\cos x&\text{for}\ x\leqslant\penalty\uppi,\\
2&\text{for}\ x>\uppi.\end{smallmatrix}\right.

Note that

\mathrm{md}^{\upkappa}(x)=\int\limits_{0}^{x}\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}(t){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\bm{d}t\quad\text{ for }\quad x\leqslant\penalty\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}.

Let
\upvarphi
be the angle of
{\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}\{a,b,c\}

opposite to
a
.
In this case, we will write

a=\mathop{\tilde{\curlyvee}}\nolimits^{\upkappa}\{\upvarphi;b,c\}\quad\text{or}\quad\upvarphi=\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}\{a;b,c\}.

The functions
\mathop{\tilde{\curlyvee}}\nolimits^{\upkappa}
and
\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}
will be called respectively the
model side
and the
model angle
.
Let

\mathop{\tilde{\curlyvee}}\nolimits^{\upkappa}\{\upvarphi;b,-c\}=\mathop{\tilde{\curlyvee}}\nolimits^{\upkappa}\{\upvarphi;-b,c\}:=\mathop{\tilde{\curlyvee}}\nolimits^{\upkappa}\{\uppi-\upvarphi;b,c\};

in this way we define
\mathop{\tilde{\curlyvee}}\nolimits^{\upkappa}\{\upvarphi;b,c\}
when one of the numbers
b
and
c
is negative.

1.1. Properties of standard functions.

(a)

For fixed
a
and
\upvarphi
, the function
y(t)=\mathrm{md}^{\upkappa}\left(\mathop{\tilde{\curlyvee}}\nolimits^{\upkappa}\{\upvarphi;a,t\}\right)

satisfies the following differential equation:

y^{\prime\prime}+\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y=1.

(b)

Let
\upalpha\colon[a,b]\to\penalty\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
be a unit-speed geodesic, and
A
be the image of a complete geodesic. If
f(t)
is the distance from
\upalpha(t)
to
A
, the function

y(t)=\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}(f(t))

satisfies the following differential equation:

y^{\prime\prime}+\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y=0

for
y\neq 0
.

(c)

For fixed
\upkappa
,
b
, and
c
, the function

a\mapsto\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}\{a;b,c\}

is increasing and defined on a real interval.
Equivalently, the function

\upvarphi\mapsto\penalty\mathop{\tilde{\curlyvee}}\nolimits^{\upkappa}\{\upvarphi;b,c\}

is increasing and defined if
b,c<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
, and
\upvarphi\in\penalty[0,\uppi]
.
(Formally speaking, if
\upkappa>0
and
b+c\geqslant\penalty\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
, it is defined only for
\upvarphi\in\penalty[0,\uppi)
, but
\mathop{\tilde{\curlyvee}}\nolimits^{\upkappa}\{\upvarphi;b,c\}
can be extended to
[0,\uppi]
as a continuous function.)

(d)

For fixed
\upvarphi,a,b,c
, the function

\upkappa\mapsto\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}\{a;b,c\}\quad\text{and}\quad\upkappa\mapsto\penalty\mathop{\tilde{\curlyvee}}\nolimits^{\upkappa}\{\upvarphi;b,c\}

are respectively nondereasing (in fact, increasing, if
|b-\penalty c|<\penalty a<\penalty b+c
)
and nonincreasing (in fact, increasing, if
0<\penalty\upvarphi<\penalty\uppi
).

(e)

(Alexandrov’s lemma)
Assume that for real numbers
a
,
b
,
a^{\prime}
,
b^{\prime}
,
x
, and
\upkappa
, the following two expressions are defined:

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}\{a;b,x\}+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}\{a^{\prime};b^{\prime},x\}-\uppi,\quad\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}\{a^{\prime};b+b^{\prime},a\}-\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}\{x;a,b\},

Then they have the same sign.

All the properties except Alexandrov’s lemma
1A
can be shown by direct calculation. Alexandrov’s lemma is reformulated in
6B
and is proved there.

Cosine law.

The formulas
a=\mathop{\tilde{\curlyvee}}\nolimits^{\upkappa}\{\upvarphi;b,c\}
and

\upvarphi=\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}\{a;b,c\}

can be rewritten using the cosine law in
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
:

\cos\upvarphi=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{b^{2}+c^{2}-a^{2}}{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}c}&\text{if}&\quad\upkappa=0,\\
&\frac{\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}a-\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}c}{\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}c}&\text{if}&\quad\upkappa\neq 0.\end{aligned}\right.

However, rather than using these explicit formulas, we mainly will use
the properties of
\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}
and
\mathop{\tilde{\curlyvee}}\nolimits^{\upkappa}
listed in
1A
.

B
Hemisphere lemma

1.2. Hemisphere lemma.

For
\upkappa>0
, any closed path of length less than
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
(respectively, at most
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
) in
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
lies in an open (respectively, closed) hemisphere.

Proof.

Applying rescaling, we may assume that
\upkappa=1
, and thus
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}=\uppi
and
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})=\mathbb{S}^{2}
.
Let
\upalpha
be a closed curve in
\mathbb{S}^{2}
of length
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell
.

Assume
\ell<\uppi
.
Let
\check{\upalpha}
be a subarc of
\upalpha
of length
\ell
, with endpoints
p
and
q
.
Since
|{p}-\penalty{q}|\leqslant\penalty\ell<\uppi
, there is a unique geodesic
[pq]
in
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.
Let
z
be the midpoint of
[pq]
.
We claim that
\upalpha
lies in the open hemisphere centered at
z
.
If not,
\upalpha
intersects the boundary great circle of this hemisphere; let
r
be a point in the intersection.
Without loss of generality, we may assume that
r\in\penalty\check{\upalpha}
.
The arc
\check{\upalpha}
together with its reflection in
z
form a closed curve of length
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell
that contains
r
and its antipodal point
r^{\prime}
.
Thus

\ell=\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\check{\upalpha}\geqslant\penalty|{r}-\penalty{r^{\prime}}|=\uppi,

a contradiction.

If
\ell=\uppi
, then either
\upalpha
is a local geodesic, and hence a great circle,
or
\upalpha
may be strictly shortened by substituting a geodesic arc for a subarc of
\upalpha

whose endpoints
p^{1},p^{2}
are arbitrarily close to a point
p
on
\upalpha
.
In both cases
\upalpha
lies in a closed hemisphere;
the former case is trivial, and in the latter case,
\upalpha
lies in a closed hemisphere obtained as a limit of closures of open hemispheres containing the shortened curves as
p^{1},p^{2}
approach
p
.
∎

1.3. Exercise.

Give a proof of the hemisphere lemma (
1B
) based on Crofton’s formula.

Chapter 2
Metric spaces

In this chapter we fix conventions and notations. We are assuming that the reader is familiar with basic notions in metric geometry.

A
Metrics and their relatives

Definitions.

Let
\mathbb{I}
be a subinterval of
[0,\infty]
.
A function
\uprho
defined on
\mathcal{X}\times\penalty\mathcal{X}
is called an
\mathbb{I}
-valued metric if the following conditions hold:

\diamond

\uprho(x,x)=0
for any
x
;

\diamond

\uprho(x,y)\in\penalty\mathbb{I}
for any pair
x\neq y
;

\diamond

\uprho(x,y)+\uprho(x,z)\geqslant\penalty\uprho(y,z)
for any triple of points
x,y,z
.

The value
\uprho(x,y)
is also called the
distance
between
x
and
y
.

The above definition will be used for four choices of interval
\mathbb{I}
:
(0,\infty)
,
(0,\infty]
,
[0,\infty)
, and
[0,\infty]
.
Any
\mathbb{I}
-valued metric can be referred to briefly as a metric;
the interval should be apparent from context but by default, a metric is
(0,\infty)
-valued.
If we need to be more specific we may also use the following names:

\diamond

a
(0,\infty)
-valued metric may be called a
genuine metric
.

\diamond

a
(0,\infty]
-valued metric may be called an
\infty
-metric
.

\diamond

a
[0,\infty)
-valued metric may be called a
genuine pseudometric
.

\diamond

A
[0,\infty]
-valued metric may be called a
pseudometric
or
\infty
-pseudometric
.

A metric space is a set equipped with a metric.
The distance between points
x
and
y
in a metric space
\mathcal{X}
will usually be denoted by

|{x}-\penalty{y}|\qquad\text{or}\quad|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\mathcal{X}};

the latter will be used if we need to emphasize that we are working in the space
\mathcal{X}
.

The function
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{x}{}{}\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
defined as

\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{x}{}{}\colon y\mapsto\penalty|{x}-\penalty{y}|

will be called the
distance function
from
x
.

Any subset
A
in a metric space
\mathcal{X}
will be also considered as a
subspace
;
that is, a metric space with the metric defined by restricting the metric of
\mathcal{X}
to
A\times\penalty A\subset\penalty\mathcal{X}\times\penalty\mathcal{X}
.

The
direct product

\mathcal{X}\times\penalty\mathcal{Y}
of two metric spaces
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
is defined as the metric space carrying the metric

|{(p,\upvarphi)}-\penalty{(q,\uppsi)}|=\sqrt{|{p}-\penalty{q}|^{2}+|{\upvarphi}-\penalty{\uppsi}|^{2}}

for
p,q\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
and
\upvarphi,\uppsi\in\penalty\mathcal{Y}
.

A map between two metric spaces is called an
isometry
if it is a bijection and preserves distances between points.

Zero and infinity.

Genuine metric spaces are the main objects of study in this book.
However, the generalizations above are useful
in various definitions and constructions.
For example, the construction of length metric (see Section
2C
) uses infinite distances.
The following definition gives another example.

2.1. Definition.

Assume
\{\mathcal{X}_{\upalpha}\}_{\upalpha\in\penalty\mathcal{A}}
is a collection of
\infty
-metric spaces.
The disjoint union

\bm{X}=\bigsqcup_{\upalpha\in\penalty\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{X}_{\upalpha}

has a natural
\infty
-metric on it defined as follows:
given two points
x\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\upalpha}
and
y\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\upbeta}
,
let

\begin{matrix}|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\bm{X}}=\infty&\text{if}\quad\upalpha\neq\upbeta,\\
|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\bm{X}}=|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\mathcal{X}_{\upalpha}}&\text{if}\quad\upalpha=\upbeta.\end{matrix}

The resulting
\infty
-metric space
\bm{X}
will be called the
disjoint union
of
\{\mathcal{X}_{\upalpha}\}_{\upalpha\in\penalty\mathcal{A}}
, denoted by

\bigsqcup_{\upalpha\in\penalty\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{X}_{\upalpha}.

Now let us give examples showing that vanishing and infinite distance between distinct points is useful.

Suppose a set
{\mathcal{X}}
comes with a set of metrics
|{*}-\penalty{*}|_{\upalpha}
for
\upalpha\in\penalty\mathcal{A}
.
Then

|{x}-\penalty{y}|=\sup\left\{\,{|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\upalpha}}\ {:}\ {\upalpha\in\penalty\mathcal{A}}\,\right\}

is in general only an
\infty
-metric;
that is, even if the metrics
|{*}-\penalty{*}|_{\upalpha}
are genuine, then
|{*}-\penalty{*}|
might be
(0,\infty]
-valued.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a set,

\mathcal{Y}
be a metric space, and

\Phi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathcal{Y}
be a map.
If
\Phi
is not injective,
then the
pullback

|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\mathcal{X}}=|{\Phi(x)}-\penalty{\Phi(y)}|_{\mathcal{Y}}

defines only a pseudometric on
\mathcal{X}
.

Corresponding metric space and metric component.

The following two observations show that
nearly any question about metric spaces can be reduced to a question about genuine metric spaces.

Assume
\mathcal{X}
is a pseudometric space.
Set

x\sim y
if
|{x}-\penalty{y}|=0
.
Note that if
x\sim x^{\prime}
, then
|{y}-\penalty{x}|=|{y}-\penalty{x^{\prime}}|
for any
y\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
.
Thus,
|{*}-\penalty{*}|
defines a metric on the
quotient set
\mathcal{X}/{\sim}
.
This way we obtain a metric space
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
.
The space
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
is called the

corresponding metric space
for the pseudometric space
\mathcal{X}
.
Often we do not distinguish between
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{X}
.

Set
x\approx\penalty y
if and only if
|{x}-\penalty{y}|<\infty
;
this is another equivalence relation on
\mathcal{X}
.
The equivalence class of a point
x\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
will be called the
metric component

of
x
; it will be denoted by
\mathcal{X}_{x}
.
One could think of
\mathcal{X}_{x}
as
\mathrm{B}{}(x,\infty)_{\mathcal{X}}
, the open ball centered at
x
and radius
\infty
in
\mathcal{X}
; see definition below.

It follows that any
\infty
-metric space is a
disjoint union
of genuine metric spaces, the metric components of the original
\infty
-metric space; see Definition
2A

To summarize this discussion: Given a
[0,\infty]
-valued metric space
\mathcal{X}
, we may pass to the corresponding
(0,\infty]
-valued metric space
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
and break the latter into a disjoint union of metric components, each of which is a genuine metric space.

B
Notations

Balls.

Given
R\in\penalty[0,\infty]
and a point
x
in a metric space
\mathcal{X}
, the sets

\displaystyle\mathrm{B}{}(x,R)

\displaystyle=\left\{\,{y\in\penalty\mathcal{X}}\ {:}\ {|{x}-\penalty{y}|<R}\,\right\},

\displaystyle\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[x,R]

\displaystyle=\left\{\,{y\in\penalty\mathcal{X}}\ {:}\ {|{x}-\penalty{y}|\leqslant\penalty R}\,\right\}

are called respectively the
open
and the
closed balls
of radius
R
with center
x
.

If we need to emphasize that these balls are taken in the space
\mathcal{X}
,
we write

\mathrm{B}{}(x,R)_{\mathcal{X}}\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[x,R]_{\mathcal{X}}

respectively.

Since in the model space
\mathbb{M}^{m}({\upkappa})
all balls of the same radius are isometric,
often we will not need to specify the center of the ball,
and may write

\mathrm{B}{}(R)_{\mathbb{M}^{m}({\upkappa})}\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[R]_{\mathbb{M}^{m}({\upkappa})}

respectively.

A set
A\subset\penalty\mathcal{X}
is called
bounded
if
A\subset\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(x,R)
for some
x\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
and
R<\infty
.

Distances to sets.

For subset
A\subset\penalty\mathcal{X}
,
let us denote the distance from
A
to a point
x
in
\mathcal{X}
by
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{A}{x}
;
that is,

\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{A}{x}:=\inf\left\{\,{|{a}-\penalty{x}|}\ {:}\ {a\in\penalty A}\,\right\}.

For any subset
A\subset\penalty\mathcal{X}
, the sets

\displaystyle\mathrm{B}{}(A,R)

\displaystyle=\left\{\,{y\in\penalty\mathcal{X}}\ {:}\ {\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{A}{y}{}<R}\,\right\},

\displaystyle\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[A,R]

\displaystyle=\left\{\,{y\in\penalty\mathcal{X}}\ {:}\ {\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{A}{y}{}\leqslant\penalty R}\,\right\}

are called respectively the
open
and
closed
R
-neighborhoods
of
A
.

Diameter, radius, and packing.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a metric space.
Then the
diameter
of
\mathcal{X}
is defined as

\mathop{\rm diam}\nolimits\mathcal{X}=\sup\left\{\,{|{x}-\penalty{y}|}\ {:}\ {x,y\in\penalty\mathcal{X}}\,\right\}.

The
radius
of
\mathcal{X}
is defined as

\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\mathcal{X}=\inf\left\{\,{R>0}\ {:}\ {\mathrm{B}{}(x,R)=\mathcal{X}\ \text{for some}\ x\in\penalty\mathcal{X}}\,\right\}.

The
\upvarepsilon
-pack
of
\mathcal{X}
(or
packing number
) is the maximal number (possibly infinite) of points in
\mathcal{X}
at distance
>\upvarepsilon
from each other; it is denoted by
\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\upvarepsilon}\mathcal{X}
.
If
m=\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\upvarepsilon}\mathcal{X}<\infty
, then a set
\{x^{1},x^{2},\dots\penalty,x^{m}\}
in
\mathcal{X}

such that
|{x^{i}}-\penalty{x^{j}}|>\upvarepsilon
is called a
maximal
\upvarepsilon
-packing
in
\mathcal{X}
.

G-delta sets.

Recall that an arbitrary union of open balls in a metric space is called an
open set
.
A subset of a metric space is called a
G-delta set
if it can be presented as an intersection of a countable number of open subsets.

2.2. Baire’s theorem.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a complete metric space
and
\{\Omega_{n}\}
,
n\in\penalty\mathbb{N}
, be a collection of open dense subsets of
\mathcal{X}
.
Then
\bigcap_{n\in\penalty\mathbb{N}}\Omega_{n}
is dense in
\mathcal{X}
.

Proper spaces.

A metric space
\mathcal{X}
is called
proper
if all closed bounded sets in
\mathcal{X}
are compact.
This condition is equivalent to each of the following statements:

1.

For some (and therefore any) point
p\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
and any
R<\infty
,
the closed ball
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[p,R]\subset\penalty\mathcal{X}
is compact.

2.

The function
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}{}{}\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
is proper for some (and therefore any) point
p\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
.

We will also often use the following two classical statements:

2.3. Proposition.

Proper metric spaces are separable and second countable.

2.4. Proposition.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a metric space.
Then the following are equivalent

(a)

\mathcal{X}
is compact;

(b)

\mathcal{X}
is
sequentially compact
; that is, any sequence of points in
\mathcal{X}
contains a convergent subsequence;

(c)

\mathcal{X}
is complete and for any
\upvarepsilon>0
there is a
finite
\upvarepsilon
-net
in
\mathcal{X}
; that is, there is a finite collection of points
p_{1},\ldots,p_{N}
such that
\bigcup_{i}\mathrm{B}{}(p_{i},\upvarepsilon)=\mathcal{X}
.

(d)

\mathcal{X}
is complete and for any
\upvarepsilon>0
there is a
compact
\upvarepsilon
-net
in
\mathcal{X}
; that is,
\mathrm{B}{}(K,\upvarepsilon)=\mathcal{X}
for a compact set
K\subset\penalty\mathcal{X}
.

C
Length spaces

A
curve
in a metric space
\mathcal{X}
is a continuous map
\upalpha\colon\mathbb{I}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
, where
\mathbb{I}
is a
real interval
(that is, an arbitrary convex subset of
\mathbb{R}
).

2.5. Definition.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a metric space.
Given a curve
\upalpha\colon\mathbb{I}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
, we define its
length
as

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upalpha:=\sup\left\{\,{\sum_{i\geqslant\penalty 1}|{\upalpha(t_{i})}-\penalty{\upalpha(t_{i-1})}|}\ {:}\ {t_{0},\dots\penalty,t_{n}\in\penalty\mathbb{I},t_{0}\leqslant\penalty\ldots\leqslant\penalty t_{n}}\,\right\}.

The following lemma is an easy exercise.

2.6. Lower semicontinuity of length.

Assume
\upalpha_{n}\colon\mathbb{I}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
is a sequence of curves that converges pointwise to a curve
\upalpha_{\infty}\colon\mathbb{I}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
.
Then

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upalpha_{\infty}\leqslant\penalty\varliminf_{n\to\penalty\infty}\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upalpha_{n}.

Given two points
x
and
y
in a metric space
\mathcal{X}
,
consider the value

\Arrowvert{x}-\penalty{y}\Arrowvert=\inf_{\upalpha}\{\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upalpha\},

where infimum is taken for all paths
\upalpha
from
x
to
y
.

It is easy to see that
\Arrowvert{*}-\penalty{*}\Arrowvert
defines a
(0,\infty]
-valued metric on
\mathcal{X}
;
it will be called the
induced length metric
on
\mathcal{X}
.
Clearly

\Arrowvert{x}-\penalty{y}\Arrowvert\geqslant\penalty|{x}-\penalty{y}|

for any
x,y\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
.

It easily follows from the definition that the length of a curve
\upalpha
with respect to
\Arrowvert{*}-\penalty{*}\Arrowvert
is equal to the length of
\upalpha
with respect to
|{*}-\penalty{*}|
.
In particular, iterating the construction produces the same metric
\Arrowvert{*}-\penalty{*}\Arrowvert
.

2.7. Definition.

If
\Arrowvert{x}-\penalty{y}\Arrowvert=|{x}-\penalty{y}|
for any pair of points
x,y
in a metric space
\mathcal{X}
, then
|{*}-\penalty{*}|
is called
length metric
, and
\mathcal{X}
is called a
length space
.

In other words, a metric space
\mathcal{X}
is a

length space

if for any
\upvarepsilon>0
and any two points
x,y\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
with
|{x}-\penalty{y}|<\infty
there is a path
\upalpha\colon[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
connecting
x
to
y
such that

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upalpha<|{x}-\penalty{y}|+\upvarepsilon.

In this book, most of the time we consider length spaces.
If
\mathcal{X}
is a length space,
and
A\subset\penalty\mathcal{X}
, then the set
A
comes with the inherited metric from
\mathcal{X}
,
which might be not a length metric.
The corresponding length metric on
A
will be denoted by
|{*}-\penalty{*}|_{A}
.

Variations of the definition.

We will need the following variations of Definition
2C
:

\diamond

Assume
R>0
.
If
\Arrowvert{x}-\penalty{y}\Arrowvert=|{x}-\penalty{y}|
for any pair
|{x}-\penalty{y}|<R
, then
\mathcal{X}
is called an
R
-length space
.

\diamond

If any point in
\mathcal{X}
admits a neighborhood
\Omega
such that
\Arrowvert{x}-\penalty{y}\Arrowvert=|{x}-\penalty{y}|
for any pair of points
x,y\in\penalty\Omega

then
\mathcal{X}
is called a
locally length space
.

\diamond

A metric space is called

geodesic

if for any two points
x,y
with
|{x}-\penalty{y}|<\infty
there is a geodesic
[xy]
in
\mathcal{X}

\diamond

Assume
R>0
. A metric space is called

R
-geodesic

if for any two points
x,y
such that
|{x}-\penalty{y}|<R
there is a geodesic
[xy]
in
\mathcal{X}
.

Note that the notions of
\infty
-length spaces and length spaces are the same.
Clearly, any geodesic space is a length space
and any
R
-geodesic space is
R
-length.

2.8. Example.

Consider a metric space
\mathcal{X}
obtained by gluing a countable collection of disjoint intervals
\mathbb{I}_{n}
of length
1+\tfrac{1}{n}
where for each
\mathbb{I}_{n}
one end is glued to
p
and the other to
q
.
Then
\mathcal{X}
carries a natural complete length metric such that
|{p}-\penalty{q}|=1
, but there is no geodesic connecting
p
to
q
.

2.9. Exercise.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a metric space
and
\Arrowvert{*}-\penalty{*}\Arrowvert
be the length metric on it.
Show the following:

(a)

If
\mathcal{X}
is complete, then
(\mathcal{X},\Arrowvert{*}-\penalty{*}\Arrowvert)
is complete.

(b)

If
\mathcal{X}
is compact, then
(\mathcal{X},\Arrowvert{*}-\penalty{*}\Arrowvert)
is geodesic.

2.10. Exercise.

Give an example of a complete length space such that no pair of distinct points can be joined by a geodesic.

2.11. Exercise.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a complete length space.
Show that for any compact subset
K
in
\mathcal{X}

there is a compact path-connected subset
K^{\prime}
that contains
K
.

2.12. Definition.

Consider two points
x
and
y
in a metric space
\mathcal{X}
.

(i)

A point
z\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
is called a
midpoint
between
x
and
y
if

|{x}-\penalty{z}|=|{y}-\penalty{z}|=\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{x}-\penalty{y}|.

(ii)

Assume
\upvarepsilon\geqslant\penalty 0
.
A point
z\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
is called an
\upvarepsilon
-midpoint
between
x
and
y

if

|{x}-\penalty{z}|\leqslant\penalty\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{x}-\penalty{y}|+\upvarepsilon,\quad\text{and}\quad|{y}-\penalty{z}|\leqslant\penalty\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{x}-\penalty{y}|+\upvarepsilon.

Note that a
0
-midpoint is the same as a midpoint.

The following lemma was essentially proved by Karl Menger
[
114
, Section 6]
.

2.13. Lemma.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a complete metric space.

(a)

Assume that for any pair of points
x,y\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
, and any
\upvarepsilon>0

there is an
\upvarepsilon
-midpoint
z
.
Then
\mathcal{X}
is a length space.

(b)

Assume that for any pair of points
x,y\in\penalty\mathcal{X}

there is a midpoint
z
.
Then
\mathcal{X}
is a geodesic space.

(c)

If for some
R>0
, the assumptions
2C
or
2C
hold only for pairs of points
x,y\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
such that
|{x}-\penalty{y}|<R
,
then
\mathcal{X}
is an
R
-length or an
R
-geodesic space respectively.

Proof.

Fix a pair of points
x,y\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
.
Let
\upvarepsilon_{n}=\frac{\upvarepsilon}{2^{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}n}}
.

Set
\upalpha(0)=x
,
and
\upalpha(1)=y
.
Let
\upalpha(\tfrac{1}{2})
be an
\upvarepsilon_{1}
-midpoint between
\upalpha(0)
and
\upalpha(1)
.
Further, let
\upalpha(\frac{1}{4})

and
\upalpha(\frac{3}{4})
be
\upvarepsilon_{2}
-midpoints
between the pairs
(\upalpha(0),\upalpha(\tfrac{1}{2})

and
(\upalpha(\tfrac{1}{2}),\upalpha(1)
respectively.
Applying the above procedure recursively,
on the
n
-th step we define
\upalpha(\tfrac{k}{2^{n}})
,
for every odd integer
k
such that
0<\tfrac{k}{2^{n}}<1
, to be an
\upvarepsilon_{n}
-midpoint between the already defined

\upalpha(\tfrac{k-1}{2^{n}})
and
\upalpha(\tfrac{k+1}{2^{n}})
.

This way we define
\upalpha(t)
for all dyadic rationals
t
in
[0,1]
.
If
t\in\penalty[0,1]
is not a dyadic rational, consider a sequence of dyadic rationals
t_{n}\to\penalty t
as
n\to\penalty\infty
.
By completeness of
\mathcal{X}
, the sequence
\upalpha(t_{n})
converges;
let
\upalpha(t)
be its limit.
It is easy to see that
\upalpha(t)

does not depend on the choice of the sequence
t_{n}
,
and
\upalpha\colon[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
is a path from
x
to
y
.
Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upalpha

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty|{x}-\penalty{y}|+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}2^{n-1}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon_{n}\leqslant\penalty

➊

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty|{x}-\penalty{y}|+\tfrac{\upvarepsilon}{2}.

Since
\upvarepsilon>0
is arbitrary, we have
2C
.

To prove
2C
,
one should repeat the same argument
taking midpoints instead of
\upvarepsilon_{n}
-midpoints.
In this case,
➊
holds for
\upvarepsilon_{n}=\upvarepsilon=0
.

The proof of
2C
is obtained by a straightforward modification of the proofs above.
∎

Since in a compact set a sequence of
\tfrac{1}{n}
-midpoints
(z_{n})
contains a convergent subsequence,
Lemma
2C
implies the following.

2.14. Corollary.

A proper length space is geodesic.

2.15. Hopf–Rinow theorem.

Any complete, locally compact length space is proper.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a locally compact length space.
Given
x\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
, denote by
\uprho(x)
the supremum of all
R>0
such that
the closed ball
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[x,R]
is compact.
Since
\mathcal{X}
is locally compact,

\uprho(x)>0\quad\text{for any}\quad x\in\penalty\mathcal{X}.

➋

It is sufficient to show that
\uprho(x)=\infty
for some (and therefore any) point
x\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
.

Assume the contrary; that is,
\uprho(x)<\infty
.

➌

B=\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[x,\uprho(x)]
is compact for any
x
.

Indeed,
\mathcal{X}
is a length space;
therefore for any
\upvarepsilon>0
,
the set
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[x,\uprho(x)-\penalty\upvarepsilon]
is a compact
\upvarepsilon
-net in
B
.
Since
B
is closed and hence complete, it is compact by Proposition
2B
.

\vartriangle

➍

|\uprho(x)-\uprho(y)|\leqslant\penalty|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\mathcal{X}}
for any
x,y\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
;
in particular
\uprho\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
is a continuous function.

Indeed, assume the contrary; that is,
\uprho(x)+|{x}-\penalty{y}|<\uprho(y)
for
x,y\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
.
Then

\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[x,\uprho(x)+\upvarepsilon]
is a closed subset of
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[y,\uprho(y)]
for
\upvarepsilon>0
.
Then compactness of
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[y,\uprho(y)]
implies compactness of
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[x,\uprho(x)+\upvarepsilon]
, a contradiction.

\vartriangle

Set
\upvarepsilon=\min_{y\in\penalty B}\{\uprho(y)\}
;
the minimum is defined since
B
is compact.
From
➋
, we have
\upvarepsilon>0
.

Choose a finite
\tfrac{\upvarepsilon}{10}
-net
\{a_{1},a_{2},\dots\penalty,a_{n}\}
in
B
.
The union
W
of the closed balls
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[a_{i},\upvarepsilon]
is compact.
Clearly

\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[x,\uprho(x)+\frac{\upvarepsilon}{10}]\subset\penalty W
.
Therefore
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[x,\uprho(x)+\frac{\upvarepsilon}{10}]
is compact,
a contradiction.
∎

2.16. Exercise.

Construct a geodesic space that is locally compact,
but whose completion is neither geodesic nor locally compact.

D
Convex sets

2.17. Definition.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a geodesic space and
A\subset\penalty\mathcal{X}
.

A
is

convex

if for every two points
p,q\in\penalty A
any geodesic
[pq]
lies in
A
.

A
is

weakly convex

if for every two points
p,q\in\penalty A

there is a geodesic
[pq]

that lies in
A
.

We say that
A
is

totally convex

if for every two points
p,q\in\penalty A
, every local geodesic from
p
to
q
lies in
A
.

If for some
R\in\penalty(0,\infty]
these definitions are applied only for pairs of points such that
|{p}-\penalty{q}|<R
and only for the geodesics of length
<R
,
then
A
is called respectively

R
-convex
,

weakly
R
-convex
,
or
totally
R
-convex
.

A set
A\subset\penalty\mathcal{X}
is called

locally convex

if every point
a\in\penalty A
admits an open neighborhood
\Omega\ni a

such that for every two points
p,q\in\penalty A\cap\penalty\Omega
every geodesic
[pq]\subset\penalty\Omega
lies in
A
.
Similarly one defines locally weakly convex and locally totally convex sets.

Remarks.
Let us state a few observations that easily follow from the definition.

\diamond

The notion of (
weakly
)
convex set
is the same as (
weakly
)
\infty
-convex set
.

\diamond

The inherited metric on a weakly convex set coincides with its length metric.

\diamond

Any open set is locally convex by definition.

The following proposition states that weak convexity survives under ultralimits.
An analogous statement about convexity does not hold;
for example, there is a sequence of convex discs in
\mathbb{S}^{2}
that converges to a hemisphere, which is not convex.

2.18. Proposition.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{n},
be a sequence of geodesic spaces.
Let
be an ultrafilter on
\mathbb{N}
(see Definition
4A
).
Assume that
A_{n}\subset\penalty\mathcal{X}_{n}
is a sequence of weakly convex sets,

\mathcal{X}_{n}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}
,
and
A_{n}\to\penalty A_{\text{\rm}}\subset\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.
Then
A_{\text{\rm}}
is a weakly convex set of
\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}
.

Proof.

Fix
x_{\text{\rm}},y_{\text{\rm}}\in\penalty A_{\text{\rm}}
.
Consider sequences
x_{n},y_{n}\in\penalty A_{n}
such that
x_{n}\to\penalty x_{\text{\rm}}
and
y_{n}\to\penalty y_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.

Denote by
\upalpha_{n}
a geodesic path from
x_{n}
to
y_{n}
that lies in
A_{n}
.
Let

\upalpha_{\text{\rm}}(t)=\lim_{n\to\penalty\text{\rm}}\upalpha_{n}(t).

It remains to observe that
\upalpha_{\text{\rm}}
is a geodesic path that lies in
A_{\text{\rm}}
.
∎

E
Quotient spaces

Quotient spaces.
Assume
\mathcal{X}
is a metric space
with an equivalence relation
\sim
.
Note that given a family of pseudometrics
\uprho_{\upalpha}
on
\mathcal{X}/{\sim}
,
their least upper bound

\uprho(x,y)=\sup_{\upalpha}\{\,\uprho_{\upalpha}(x,y)\,\}

is also a pseudometric.
If for the projections
\mathcal{X}\to\penalty(\mathcal{X}/{\sim},\uprho_{\upalpha})
are
short
(that is,
distance non-increasing
), then so is
\mathcal{X}\to\penalty(\mathcal{X}/{\sim},\uprho)
.

It follows that
the quotient space
\mathcal{X}/{\sim}
admits a natural quotient pseudometric;
this is the maximal pseudometric on
\mathcal{X}/{\sim}

that makes the quotient map

\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}/{\sim}
short.
The corresponding metric space will be also denoted as
\mathcal{X}/{\sim}

and will be called the
quotient space
of
\mathcal{X}
by the equivalence relation
\sim
.

In general, the points of the metric space
\mathcal{X}/{\sim}

are formed by equivalence classes in
\mathcal{X}

for a wider equivalence relation.
However, in most of the cases we will consider,
the set of equivalence classes will coincide with the set of points in the metric space
\mathcal{X}/{\sim}
.

2.19. Proposition.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a length space and

\sim
be an equivalence relation on
\mathcal{X}
. Then
\mathcal{X}/{\sim}

is a length space.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{Y}
be an arbitrary metric space.
Since
\mathcal{X}
is a length space,
a map
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathcal{Y}
is short if and only if

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits(f\circ\penalty\upalpha)\leqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upalpha

for any curve

\upalpha\colon\mathbb{I}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
.
Denote by
\Arrowvert{*}-\penalty{*}\Arrowvert
the length metric on
\mathcal{Y}
.
It follows that if
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathcal{Y}
is short
then so is

f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty(\mathcal{Y},\Arrowvert{*}-\penalty{*}\Arrowvert)
.

Consider the quotient map

f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}/{\sim}
.
Recall that the space
\mathcal{X}/{\sim}
is defined by the maximal pseudometric that makes
f
short.

Denoting by
\Arrowvert{*}-\penalty{*}\Arrowvert
the length metric on
\mathcal{X}/{\sim}
,
it follows that

f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty(\mathcal{X}/{\sim},\Arrowvert{*}-\penalty{*}\Arrowvert)

is also short.

Note that

\Arrowvert{x}-\penalty{y}\Arrowvert\geqslant\penalty|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\mathcal{X}/{\sim}}

for any
x,y\in\penalty\mathcal{X}/{\sim}
.
From maximality of
|{*}-\penalty{*}|_{\mathcal{X}/{\sim}}
, we get

\Arrowvert{x}-\penalty{y}\Arrowvert=|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\mathcal{X}/{\sim}}

for any
x,y\in\penalty\mathcal{X}/{\sim}
;
that is,
\mathcal{X}/{\sim}
is a length space.
∎

Group actions.

Assume a group
G
acts on a metric space
\mathcal{X}
.
Consider a relation
\sim
on
\mathcal{X}

defined by
x\sim y
if there is
g\in\penalty G
such that
x=g{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y
.
Note that
\sim
is an equivalence relation.

In this case, the quotient space
\mathcal{X}/{\sim}
will also be denoted by
\mathcal{X}/G
, and can be regarded as the space of
G
-orbits in
\mathcal{X}
.

Assume that a group
G
acts on
\mathcal{X}
by isometries.
Then the distance between orbits
G{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x
and
G{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y
in
\mathcal{X}/G

can be defined directly:

|{G{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x}-\penalty{G{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y}|_{\mathcal{X}/G}=\inf\left\{\,{|{x}-\penalty{g{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y}|_{\mathcal{X}}=|{g^{-1}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x}-\penalty{y}|_{\mathcal{X}}}\ {:}\ {g\in\penalty G}\,\right\}.

If the
G
-orbits are closed, then
|{G{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x}-\penalty{G{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y}|_{\mathcal{X}/G}=0
if and only if
G{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x=G{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y
.
In this case, the quotient space
\mathcal{X}/G
is a genuine metric space.

The following proposition follows from the definition of a quotient space:

2.20. Proposition.

Assume
\mathcal{X}
is a metric space and a group
G
acts on
\mathcal{X}
by isometries.
Then the projection
\uppi\colon\thinspace\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}/G
is a
submetry
;
that is,
\uppi(\mathrm{B}{}(p,r))=\mathrm{B}{}(\uppi(p),r)
for any
p\in\penalty\mathcal{X},r>0
(see Definition
3C
).

F
Gluing and doubling

Gluing.

Recall that the disjoint union of metric spaces can be also considered as a metric space; see Definition
2A
.
Therefore the quotient space construction works as well for an equivalence relation on the disjoint union of metric spaces.

Consider two metric spaces
\mathcal{X}_{1}
and
\mathcal{X}_{2}

with subsets
A_{1}\subset\penalty\mathcal{X}_{1}
and
A_{2}\subset\penalty\mathcal{X}_{2}
,
and a bijection
\upvarphi\colon A_{1}\to\penalty A_{2}
.
Consider the minimal equivalence relation on
\mathcal{X}_{1}\sqcup\mathcal{X}_{2}

such that
a\sim\upvarphi(a)
for any
a\in\penalty A_{1}
.
In this case, the corresponding quotient space

(\mathcal{X}_{1}\sqcup\mathcal{X}_{2})/{\sim}
will be called the
gluing of
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
along
\upvarphi
and denoted by

\mathcal{X}_{1}\sqcup_{\upvarphi}\mathcal{X}_{2}.

Note that if the map
\upvarphi\colon A_{1}\to\penalty A_{2}
is distance-preserving,
then the inclusions
\upiota_{i}\colon\mathcal{X}_{i}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}_{1}\sqcup_{\upvarphi}\mathcal{X}_{2}
are also distance-preserving, and

|{\upiota_{1}(x_{1})}-\penalty{\upiota_{2}(x_{2})}|_{\mathcal{X}_{1}\sqcup_{\upvarphi}\mathcal{X}_{2}}=\inf_{a_{2}=\upvarphi(a_{1})}\{\,|{x_{1}}-\penalty{a_{1}}|_{\mathcal{X}_{1}}+|{x_{2}}-\penalty{a_{2}}|_{\mathcal{X}_{2}}\,\}

for any
x_{1}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{1}
and
x_{2}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{2}
.

Doubling.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a metric space
and
A\subset\penalty\mathcal{V}
be a closed subset.
A metric space
\mathcal{W}
glued from two copies of
\mathcal{V}
along
A
is called the
doubling of
\mathcal{V}
in
A
.

The space
\mathcal{W}
is completely described by the following properties:

\diamond

The space
\mathcal{W}
contains
\mathcal{V}
as a subspace;
in particular the set
A
can be treated as a subset of
\mathcal{W}
.

\diamond

There is an isometric involution of
\mathcal{W}
which is called
reflection in
A
;
it will be denoted by
x\mapsto\penalty x^{\prime}
.

\diamond

For any
x\in\penalty\mathcal{W}
we have
x\in\penalty\mathcal{V}
or
x^{\prime}\in\penalty\mathcal{V}
and

|{x^{\prime}}-\penalty{y}|_{\mathcal{W}}=|{x}-\penalty{y^{\prime}}|_{\mathcal{W}}=\inf_{a\in\penalty A}\{|{x}-\penalty{a}|_{\mathcal{V}}+|{a}-\penalty{y}|_{\mathcal{V}}\}

for any
x,y\in\penalty\mathcal{V}
.

The image of
\mathcal{V}
under reflection in
A
will be denoted by
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
.
The subspace
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
is an isometric copy of
\mathcal{V}
.
Clearly
\mathcal{V}\cup\penalty\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=\mathcal{W}
and
\mathcal{V}\cap\penalty\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=A
.
Moreover,
a=a^{\prime}

\iff

a\in\penalty A
.

The following proposition follows directly from the definitions.

2.21. Proposition.

Assume
\mathcal{W}
is the doubling of the metric space
\mathcal{V}
in its closed subset
A
.
Then:

(a)

If
\mathcal{V}
is a complete length space, then so is
\mathcal{W}
.

(b)

If
\mathcal{V}
is proper, then so is
\mathcal{W}
.
In this case, for any
x,y\in\penalty\mathcal{V}
there is
a\in\penalty A
such that

|{x}-\penalty{a}|_{\mathcal{V}}+|{a}-\penalty{y}|_{\mathcal{V}}=|{x}-\penalty{y^{\prime}}|_{\mathcal{W}}.

(c)

Given
x\in\penalty\mathcal{W}
, let
\bar{x}=x
if
x\in\penalty\mathcal{V}
,
and
\bar{x}=x^{\prime}
otherwise. The map
\mathcal{W}\to\penalty\mathcal{V}
defined by
x\mapsto\penalty\bar{x}
is short and length-preserving.
In particular, if
\upgamma
is a geodesic in
\mathcal{W}
with ends in
\mathcal{V}
, then
\bar{\upgamma}
is a geodesic in
\mathcal{V}
with the same ends.

G
Kuratowsky embedding

Given a metric space
\mathcal{X}
,
let us denote by
\mathrm{Bnd}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R})
the space of all bounded functions on
\mathcal{X}
equipped with the sup-norm

\Arrowvert f\Arrowvert=\sup_{x\in\penalty\mathcal{X}}\{|f(x)|\}.

Kuratowski embedding.

Given a point
p\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
, consider the
map
\mathop{\rm kur}\nolimits_{p}\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathrm{Bnd}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R})

defined by
\mathop{\rm kur}\nolimits_{p}x=\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{x}{}{}-\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}{}{}
.
The map
\mathop{\rm kur}\nolimits_{p}
will be called the
Kuratowski map at
p
.

From the triangle inequality, we have

\Arrowvert\mathop{\rm kur}\nolimits_{p}x-\mathop{\rm kur}\nolimits_{p}y\Arrowvert=\sup_{z\in\penalty\mathcal{X}}\{||{x}-\penalty{z}|-|{y}-\penalty{z}||\}=|{x}-\penalty{y}|.

Therefore, for any
p\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
, the Kuratowski map gives a distance-preserving map
\mathop{\rm kur}\nolimits_{p}\colon\mathcal{X}\hookrightarrow\penalty\mathrm{Bnd}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R})
.
Thus we can (and often will) consider the space
\mathcal{X}
as a subset of
\mathrm{Bnd}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R})
.

2.22. Exercise.

Show that any compact metric space is isometric to a subspace in a compact length space.

Chapter 3
Maps and functions

Here we introduce several classes of maps between metric spaces and develop a language to describe various notions of convexity/concavity of real-valued functions on general metric spaces.

A
Submaps

We will often need maps and functions defined on subsets of a metric space.
We call them
submaps
and
subfunctions
.
Thus, given metric spaces
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
,
a submap
\Phi\colon\mathcal{X}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathcal{Y}
is a map defined on a subset
\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits\Phi\subset\penalty\mathcal{X}
.

A submap
\Phi\colon\mathcal{X}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathcal{Y}
is
continuous
if the inverse image of any open set is open.
Since
\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits\Phi=\Phi^{-1}(\mathcal{Y})
, the domain
\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits\Phi
of a continuous submap is open.
The same holds for upper and lower semicontinuous functions
f\colon\mathcal{X}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
since they are continuous functions for a special topology on
\mathbb{R}
.

(Continuous partially defined maps could be defined via closed sets; namely, one could require that inverse images of closed sets are closed.
While this condition is equivalent to continuity for functions defined on the whole space,
it is different for partially defined functions.
In particular, with this definition the domain of a continuous submap would have to be closed.)

B
Lipschitz conditions

3.1. Lipschitz maps.

Suppose
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
are metric spaces,

\Phi\colon\mathcal{X}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathcal{Y}
is a continuous submap,
and
{\ell}\in\penalty\mathbb{R}
.

(a)

The submap
\Phi
is called
{\ell}
-Lipschitz
if

|{\Phi(x)}-\penalty{\Phi(y)}|_{\mathcal{Y}}\leqslant\penalty{\ell}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\mathcal{X}}

for any two points
x,y\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits\Phi
.

\circ\penalty

1
-Lipschitz maps will be also called
short
.

(b)

We say that
\Phi
is
Lipschitz
if it is
{\ell}
-Lipschitz for a constant
{\ell}
.
The minimal such constant is denoted by
\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits\Phi
.

(c)

We say that
\Phi
is
locally Lipschitz

if any point
x\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits\Phi
admits a neighborhood

\Omega\subset\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits\Phi
such that the restriction
\Phi|_{\Omega}
is Lipschitz.

(d)

Given
p\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits\Phi
, we denote by
\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits_{p}\Phi
the infimum of the real values
{\ell}
such that

p
admits a neighborhood

\Omega\subset\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits\Phi
such that the restriction
\Phi|_{\Omega}
is
{\ell}
-Lipschitz.

Note that
\Phi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathcal{Y}
is
{\ell}
-Lipschitz if and only if

\Phi(\mathrm{B}{}(x,R)_{\mathcal{X}})\subset\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(\Phi(x),{\ell}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}R)_{\mathcal{Y}}

for any
R\geqslant\penalty 0
and
x\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
.
A dual version of this property is considered in the following definition.

3.2. Definitions.

Let

\Phi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathcal{Y}
be a map between metric spaces,
and
{\ell}\in\penalty\mathbb{R}
.

(a)

The map
\Phi
is called
{\ell}
-co-Lipshitz
if

\Phi(\mathrm{B}{}(x,{\ell}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}R)_{\mathcal{X}})\supset\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(\Phi(x),R)_{\mathcal{Y}}

for any
x\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
and
R>0
.

(b)

The map
\Phi
is called
co-Lipschitz
if it is
{\ell}
-co-Lipschitz
for some constant
{\ell}
.
The minimal such constant is denoted by
\mathop{\rm colip}\nolimits\Phi
.

From the definition of co-Lipschitz maps we get the following:

3.3. Proposition.

Any co-Lipschitz map is open and surjective.

In other words,
{\ell}
-co-Lipschitz maps
can be considered as a quantitative version of open maps.
For that reason they are also called
{\ell}
-open
[
43
]
.
Also, be aware that some authors
refer to our
{\ell}
-co-Lipschitz maps
as
\tfrac{1}{{\ell}}
-co-Lipschitz.

3.4. Proposition.

Let
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
be metric spaces such that
\mathcal{X}
is complete, and let

\Phi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathcal{Y}
be a continuous co-Lipschitz map.
Then
\mathcal{Y}
is complete.

Proof.

Choose a Cauchy sequence
y_{n}
in
\mathcal{Y}
.
Passing to a subsequence if necessary, we may assume that
|{y_{n}}-\penalty{y_{n+1}}|_{\mathcal{Y}}<\tfrac{1}{2^{n}}
for each
n
.
It is sufficient to show that
y_{n}
converges in
\mathcal{Y}
.

Denote by
{\ell}
a co-Lipschitz constant for
\Phi
.
Note that there is a sequence
x_{n}
in
\mathcal{X}

such that

\Phi(x_{n})=y_{n}\quad\text{and}\quad|{x_{n}}-\penalty{x_{n+1}}|_{\mathcal{X}}<\tfrac{{\ell}}{2^{n}}

➊

for each
n
.
Indeed, such a sequence can be constructed recursively.
Assuming that the points
x_{1},\dots\penalty,x_{n-1}
are already constructed,
the existence of a sequence
x_{n}
satisfying
➊

follows since
\Phi
is
{\ell}
-co-Lipschitz.

Notice that the sequence
x_{n}
is Cauchy.
Since
\mathcal{X}
is complete,
x_{n}
converges in
\mathcal{X}
; denote its limit by
x_{\infty}

and set
y_{\infty}=\Phi(x_{\infty})
.
Since
\Phi
is continuous,

y_{n}\to\penalty y_{\infty}
as
n\to\penalty\infty
.
Hence the result.
∎

3.5. Lemma.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a metric space and
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a continuous function.
Then for any
\upvarepsilon>0
there is a locally Lipschitz function
f_{\upvarepsilon}\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}

such that
|f(x)-f_{\upvarepsilon}(x)|<\upvarepsilon
for any
x\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
.

Proof.

Assume that
f\geqslant\penalty 1
.
Construct a continuous positive function
\uprho\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that

|{x}-\penalty{y}|<\uprho(x)\quad\Rightarrow\quad|f(x)-f(y)|<\upvarepsilon.

Consider the function

f_{\upvarepsilon}(y)=\sup\left\{\,{f(x){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\left(1-\tfrac{|{x}-\penalty{y}|}{\uprho(x)}\right)}\ {:}\ {x\in\penalty\mathcal{X}}\,\right\}.

It is straightforward to check that each
f_{\upvarepsilon}
is locally Lipschitz and
0\leqslant\penalty f_{\upvarepsilon}-f<\upvarepsilon
.

Since any continuous function can be presented as the difference of two continuous functions bounded below by
1
, the result follows.
∎

C
Isometries and submetries

3.6. Isometry.

Let
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
be metric spaces
and
\Phi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathcal{Y}
be a map

(a)

The map
\Phi
is
distance-preserving
if

|{\Phi(x)}-\penalty{\Phi(x^{\prime})}|_{\mathcal{Y}}=|{x}-\penalty{x^{\prime}}|_{\mathcal{X}}

for any
x,x^{\prime}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
.

(b)

A distance-preserving bijection
\Phi
is called an
isometry
.

(c)

The spaces
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
are called
isometric
(briefly
\mathcal{X}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it iso}}}\penalty\mathcal{Y}
)
if there is an isometry
\Phi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathcal{Y}
.

3.7. Submetry.

A map
\upsigma\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathcal{Y}
between the metric spaces
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}

is called a
submetry
if

\upsigma(\mathrm{B}{}(p,r)_{\mathcal{X}})=\mathrm{B}{}(\upsigma(p),r)_{\mathcal{Y}}

for any
p\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
and
r\geqslant\penalty 0
.

Note
\upsigma\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathcal{Y}
is a submetry if it is 1-Lipschitz and 1-co-Lipschitz.

Note also that any submetry is an onto map.

The main source of examples of submetries comes from isometric group actions.
Namely, assume
\mathcal{X}
is a metric space and
G
is a subgroup of isometries of
\mathcal{X}
.
Denote by
[x]=G{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x
the
G
-orbit of
x\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{X}/G
the set of all
G
-orbits;
let us equip it with the pseudometric defined by

|{[x]}-\penalty{[y]}|_{\mathcal{X}/G}=\inf\left\{\,{|{g{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x}-\penalty{h{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y}|_{\mathcal{X}}}\ {:}\ {g,h\in\penalty G}\,\right\}.

Note that if all the
G
-orbits form closed sets in
\mathcal{X}
,
then
\mathcal{X}/G
is a genuine metric space.

3.8. Proposition.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a metric space.
Assume that a group
G
acts on
\mathcal{X}
by isometries
and in such a way that every
G
-orbit is closed.
Then the projection map
\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}/G
is a submetry.

Proof.

We need to show that the map
x\mapsto\penalty[x]=G{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x
is
1
-Lipschitz and
1
-co-Lipschitz.
The co-Lipschitz part follows directly from the definitions of Hausdorff distance and co-Lipschitz maps.

Assume
|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\mathcal{X}}<r
; equivalently
\mathrm{B}{}(x,r)_{\mathcal{X}}\ni y
.
Since the action
G\curvearrowright\mathcal{X}
is isometric,

\mathrm{B}{}(g{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x,r)_{\mathcal{X}}\ni g{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y
for any
g\in\penalty G
.

In particular, the orbit
G{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y
lies in the open
r
-neighborhood of the orbit
G{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x
.
In the same way we can prove that the orbit
G{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x
lies in the open
r
-neighborhood of the orbit
G{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y
.
That is, the Hausdorff distance between the orbits
G{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x
and
G{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y
is less than
r

or, equivalently,
|{[x]}-\penalty{[y]}|_{\mathcal{X}/G}<r
.
Since
x
and
y
are arbitrary, the map
x\mapsto\penalty[x]
is
1
-Lipschitz.
∎

3.9. Proposition.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a length space
and
\upsigma\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathcal{Y}
be a submetry.
Then
\mathcal{Y}
is a length space.

Proof.

Fix
\upvarepsilon>0
and a pair of points
x,y\in\penalty\mathcal{Y}
.

Since
\upsigma
is
1
-co-Lipschitz, there are points
\hat{x},\hat{y}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}

such that

\upsigma(\hat{x})=\penalty x
,

\upsigma(\hat{y})=\penalty y
,
and
|{\hat{x}}-\penalty{\hat{y}}|_{\mathcal{X}}<|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\mathcal{Y}}+\upvarepsilon
.

Since
\mathcal{X}
is a length space,
there is a curve
\upgamma

joining
\hat{x}
to
\hat{y}
in
{\mathcal{X}}

such that

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upgamma\leqslant\penalty|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\mathcal{Y}}+\upvarepsilon.

The curve
\upsigma\circ\penalty\upgamma
joins
x
to
y
.
Since
\upsigma
is
1
-Lipschitz,
and by the above,

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upsigma\circ\penalty\upgamma

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upgamma\leqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\mathcal{Y}}+\upvarepsilon.

Since
\upvarepsilon>0
is arbitrary,

\mathcal{Y}
is a length space.
∎

D
Speed of curves

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a metric space.
Recall that a
curve

in
\mathcal{X}
is a continuous map
\upalpha\colon\mathbb{I}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
, where
\mathbb{I}
is a real interval.
A curve is called
Lipschitz
or
locally Lipschitz
if
\upalpha
is Lipschitz or locally Lipschitz respectively.
Length of curves is defined in
2C
.

The following theorem follows from
[
36
, 2.7]
.

3.10. Theorem.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a metric space
and
\upalpha\colon\mathbb{I}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
be a locally Lipschitz
curve.
Then the speed function

\mathop{\rm speed}\nolimits_{t_{0}}\upalpha=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\to\penalty t_{0}+\\
s\to\penalty t_{0}-\end{subarray}}\frac{|{\upalpha(t)}-\penalty{\upalpha(s)}|}{|t-s|}

is defined for almost all
t_{0}\in\penalty\mathbb{I}
, and

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upalpha=\int\limits_{\mathbb{I}}\mathop{\rm speed}\nolimits_{t}\upalpha{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\bm{d}t,

where
\int
denotes the Lebesgue integral.

A curve
\upalpha\colon\mathbb{I}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
is
unit-speed

if

b-a=\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits(\upalpha|_{[a,b]})

for any subinterval
[a,b]\subset\penalty\mathbb{I}
.
If
\upalpha
is Lipschitz, then, according to the above theorem, this is equivalent to

\mathop{\rm speed}\nolimits\upalpha\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it a.e.}}}1.

The following generalization of the standard Rademacher theorem
on differentiability almost everywhere of Lipschitz maps between smooth manifolds
[
36
, 5.5.2]
was proved by Bernd Kirchheim
[
93
]
.

The conclusion of the standard Rademacher theorem does not make sense for maps to a metric space since the target might have no linear structure.
But the theorem does not hold even if we assume that the target is a Banach space.
For example, consider the map
[0,1]\to\penalty L^{1}[0,1]
, defined by
x\mapsto\penalty\upchi_{[0,x]}
, where
\upchi_{A}
denotes the characteristic function of
A
.
This map is distance-preserving and in particular Lipschitz,
but its differential is undefined at any point.

3.11. Theorem.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a metric space
and
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathcal{X}
be
1
-Lipschitz.
Then for almost all
x\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
there is a pseudonorm

\lVert*\rVert_{x}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

|{f(y)}-\penalty{f(z)}|_{\mathcal{X}}=\lVert z-y\rVert_{x}+o(|y-x|+|z-x|).

Given
f
, the (pseudo)norm
\lVert*\rVert_{x}
in the above theorem
will be called its
differential of the induced metric
at
x
, or
metric differential
at
x
.

E
Convex real-to-real functions

In this section we will discuss generalized solutions
of the following differential inequalities

y^{\prime\prime}+\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y\geqslant\penalty\uplambda\quad\text{and respectively}\quad y^{\prime\prime}+\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y\leqslant\penalty\uplambda

➊

for fixed
\upkappa,\uplambda\in\penalty\mathbb{R}
.
The solutions
y\colon\mathbb{R}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
are only assumed to be upper (respectively lower) semicontinuous subfunctions.

The inequalities
➊
are understood in the sense of distributions.
That is, for any smooth function
\upvarphi
with compact support
\mathop{\rm Supp}\nolimits\upvarphi\subset\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits y
the following inequality should be satisfied:

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits y}\left[y(t){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarphi^{\prime\prime}(t)+\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y(t){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarphi(t)-\uplambda\right]{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\bm{d}t

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty 0,

➋

\displaystyle\text{respectively}

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty 0.

The integral is understood in the sense of Lebesgue;
in particular the inequality
➋

makes sense for any Borel-measurable subfunction
y
.
The proofs of the following propositions are straightforward.

3.12. Proposition.

Let
\mathbb{I}\subset\penalty\mathbb{R}
be an open interval and
y_{n}\colon\mathbb{I}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a sequence of solutions of one of the inequalities in
➊
.
Assume
y_{n}(t)\to\penalty y_{\infty}(t)
as
n\to\penalty\infty
for any
t\in\penalty\mathbb{I}
.
Then
y_{\infty}
is a solution of the same inequality in
➊
.

Assume
y
is a solution of one of the inequalities in
➊
.
For
t_{0}\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits y
, let us define the
right (left) derivative

y^{+}(t_{0})
(
y^{-}(t_{0})
) at
t_{0}
by

y^{\pm}(t_{0})=\lim_{t\to\penalty t_{0}\pm}\frac{y(t)-y(t_{0})}{|t-t_{0}|}.

Note that our sign convention for
y^{-}
is not standard — for
y(t)=t
we have

y^{+}(t)=1
and
y^{-}(t)=-1
.

3.13. Proposition.

Let
\mathbb{I}\subset\penalty\mathbb{R}
be an open interval and
y\colon\mathbb{I}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a solution of an inequality in
➊
.
Then
y
is locally Lipschitz; its right and left derivatives
y^{+}(t_{0})
and
y^{-}(t_{0})
are defined
for any
t_{0}\in\penalty\mathbb{I}
.
Moreover

y^{+}(t_{0})+y^{-}(t_{0})\geqslant\penalty 0\quad\text{or respectively}\quad y^{+}(t_{0})+y^{-}(t_{0})\leqslant\penalty 0.

The next theorem gives a number of equivalent ways to define such generalized solutions.

3.14. Theorem.

Let
\mathbb{I}
be an open real interval and
y\colon\mathbb{I}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a locally Lipschitz function.
Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(a)

y^{\prime\prime}\geqslant\penalty\uplambda-\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y
(respectively
y^{\prime\prime}\leqslant\penalty\uplambda-\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y).

(b)

(barrier inequality) For any
t_{0}\in\penalty\mathbb{I}
,
there is a solution
\bar{y}

of the ordinary differential equation
\bar{y}^{\prime\prime}=\uplambda-\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\bar{y}

with
\bar{y}(t_{0})=\penalty y(t_{0})
such that
\bar{y}\geqslant\penalty y
(respectively
\bar{y}\leqslant\penalty y
) for all
t\in\penalty[t_{0}-\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}},t_{0}+\penalty\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}]\cap\penalty\mathbb{I}
.

The function
\bar{y}
is called a
lower
(respectively
upper
)
barrier
of
y
at
t_{0}
.

(c)

(Jensen’s inequality)

For any pair of values
t_{1}<t_{2}
in
\mathbb{I}
such that
|t_{2}-t_{1}|<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
, the unique solution
z(t)
of

z^{\prime\prime}=\penalty\uplambda-\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}z

such that

z(t_{1})=y(t_{1}),\quad z(t_{2})=y(t_{2})

satisfies
y(t)\leqslant\penalty z(t)
(respectively
y(t)\geqslant\penalty z(t)
) for all
t\in\penalty[t_{1},t_{2}]
.

Further, the following property holds:

(d)

Suppose
y^{\prime\prime}\leqslant\penalty\uplambda-\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y
. Let
t_{0}\in\penalty\mathbb{I}
, and
\bar{y}
be a solution of the
ordinary differential equation
\bar{y}^{\prime\prime}=\uplambda-\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\bar{y}

such that
\bar{y}(t_{0})=\penalty y(t_{0})
and

y^{+}(t_{0})\leqslant\penalty\bar{y}^{+}(t_{0})\leqslant\penalty-y^{-}(t_{0})
.
(Note that such a
\bar{y}
is unique if
y
is differentiable at
t_{0}
.)

Then
\bar{y}\geqslant\penalty y
for all
t\in\penalty[t_{0}-\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}},t_{0}+\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}]\cap\penalty\mathbb{I}
; that is,
\bar{y}
is a barrier of
y
at
t_{0}
. (Similarly, by reversing inequalities, for
y^{\prime\prime}\geqslant\penalty\uplambda-\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y
.)

Note that Theorem
3E
implies that
y
satisfies
y^{\prime\prime}\geqslant\penalty\uplambda
(
y^{\prime\prime}\leqslant\penalty\uplambda
) on an interval
\mathbb{I}\subset\penalty\mathbb{R}
if and only if
y(t)-\frac{\uplambda}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}t^{2}
is convex (concave) on
\mathbb{I}
.

We will often need the following fact about convergence of derivatives of convex functions:

3.15. Two-shoulder lemma.

Let
\mathbb{I}
be an open interval
and
f_{n}\colon\mathbb{I}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a sequence of
convex functions.
Assume the functions
f_{n}
pointwise converge to a function
f_{\infty}\colon\mathbb{I}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
.
Then for any
t_{0}\in\penalty\mathbb{I}
,

f_{\infty}^{\pm}(t_{0})\leqslant\penalty\varliminf_{n\to\penalty\infty}f^{\pm}_{n}(t_{0}).

Proof.

Since the
f_{n}
are convex, we have
f^{+}_{n}(t_{0})+f^{-}_{n}(t_{0})\geqslant\penalty 0
, and for any
t
,

f_{n}(t)\geqslant\penalty f_{n}(t_{0})\pm f^{\pm}(t_{0}){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(t-t_{0}).

Passing to the limit, we get

f_{\infty}(t)\geqslant\penalty f_{\infty}(t_{0})+\left[\varlimsup_{n\to\penalty\infty}f^{+}_{n}(t_{0})\right]{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(t-t_{0})

for
t\geqslant\penalty t_{0}
, and

f_{\infty}(t)\geqslant\penalty f_{\infty}(t_{0})-\left[\varlimsup_{n\to\penalty\infty}f^{-}_{n}(t_{0})\right]{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(t-t_{0})

for
t\leqslant\penalty t_{0}
.
Hence the result.
∎

3.16. Corollary.

Let
\mathbb{I}
be an open interval
and
f_{n}\colon\mathbb{I}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a sequence of functions such that
f_{n}^{\prime\prime}\leqslant\penalty\uplambda
that converge pointwise to a function
f_{\infty}\colon\mathbb{I}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
.
Then:

(a)

If
f_{\infty}
is differentiable at
t_{0}\in\penalty\mathbb{I}
, then

f_{\infty}^{\prime}(t_{0})=\pm\lim_{n\to\penalty\infty}f^{\pm}_{n}(t_{0}).

(b)

If all
f_{n}
and
f_{\infty}
are differentiable at
t_{0}\in\penalty\mathbb{I}
, then

f_{\infty}^{\prime}(t_{0})=\lim_{n\to\penalty\infty}f^{\prime}_{n}(t_{0}).

Proof.
Set
\hat{f}_{n}(t)=f_{n}(t)-\tfrac{\uplambda}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}t^{2}
and
\hat{f}_{\infty}(t)=f_{\infty}(t)-\tfrac{\uplambda}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}t^{2}
.
Note that the
\hat{f}_{n}
are concave and
\hat{f}_{n}\to\penalty\hat{f}_{\infty}
pointwise.
Thus the theorem follows from the two-shoulder lemma (
3E
).∎

F
Convex functions on a metric space

In this section we define different types of convexity/concavity
in the context of metric spaces; it will be mostly used for geodesic spaces.
The notation refers to the corresponding second-order ordinary differential inequality.

3.17. Definition.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a metric space.
We say that an upper semicontinuous subfunction
f\colon\mathcal{X}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty(-\infty,\infty]

satisfies the inequality

f^{\prime\prime}+\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f\geqslant\penalty\uplambda

if for
any
unit-speed geodesic
\upgamma
in
\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
,
the real-to-real function
y(t)=\penalty f\circ\penalty\upgamma(t)

satisfies

y^{\prime\prime}+\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y\geqslant\penalty\uplambda

in the domain
\left\{\,{t}\ {:}\ {y(t)<\infty}\,\right\}
;
see the definition in Section
3E
.

We say that a lower semicontinuous subfunction
f\colon\mathcal{X}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty[-\infty,\infty)

satisfies the inequality

f^{\prime\prime}+\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f\leqslant\penalty\uplambda

if the subfunction
h=-f

satisfies

h^{\prime\prime}-\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}h\geqslant\penalty-\uplambda.

Functions satisfying the inequalities

f^{\prime\prime}\geqslant\penalty\uplambda\quad\text{and}\quad f^{\prime\prime}\leqslant\penalty\uplambda

are called

\uplambda
-convex
and
\uplambda
-concave
respectively.

0
-convex and
0
-concave subfunctions will also be called
convex
and
concave
respectively.

If
f
is
\uplambda
-convex for
\uplambda>0
, then
f
will be called
strongly convex
;
correspondingly, if
f
is
\uplambda
-concave for
\uplambda<0
, then
f
will be called
strongly concave
.

If for any point
p\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f

there is a neighborhood
\Omega\ni p
and a real number
\uplambda

such that the restriction
f|_{\Omega}
is
\uplambda
-convex (or
\uplambda
-concave),
then
f
is called
semiconvex
(respectively
semiconcave
).

Various authors define the class of
\uplambda
-convex (
\uplambda
-concave) functions differently.
Their definitions may correspond to
\pm\uplambda
-convex (
\pm\uplambda
-concave) or
\pm\tfrac{\uplambda}{2}
-convex (
\pm\tfrac{\uplambda}{2}
-concave) functions in our definitions.

3.18. Proposition.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a metric space.
Assume that
f\colon\mathcal{X}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
is a semiconvex subfunction
and
\upvarphi\colon\mathbb{R}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
is a nondecreasing semiconvex function.
Then the composition
\upvarphi\circ\penalty f
is a semiconvex subfunction.

The proof is straightforward.

Chapter 4
Ultralimits

Here we introduce ultralimits of sequences of points, metric spaces, and functions.
Our presentation is based on
[
95
]
.

Ultralimits are closely related to Gromov–Hausdorff limits.
We use them only as a canonical way to pass to convergent subsequences.
We could avoid using them at the cost of saying “pass to a convergent subsequence” too many times.
Doing this might be cumbersome and it obscure ideas of the proof;
see for example the proof of the globalization theorem for general
CBB
spaces.
Also the use of ultralimits is convenient when dealing with
CAT
spaces due to the lack of compactness results.

A
Ultrafilters

We will need the existence of a selective ultrafilter
that will be fixed once and for all.
The existence follows from the axiom of choice and the continuum hypothesis.

Measure-theoretic definition.

Recall that
\mathbb{N}
denotes the set of natural numbers,
\mathbb{N}=\{1,2,\dots\penalty\}
.

4.1. Definition.

A finitely additive measure

on
\mathbb{N}

is called an
ultrafilter
if it satisfies

(a)

\text{\rm}(S)=0
or
1
for any subset
S\subset\penalty\mathbb{N}
.

An ultrafilter
is called

nonprincipal
if in addition

(b)

\text{\rm}(F)=0
for any finite subset
F\subset\penalty\mathbb{N}
.

A nonprincipal ultrafilter
is called

selective
if in addition

(c)

for any partition of
\mathbb{N}
into sets
\{C_{\upalpha}\}_{\upalpha\in\penalty\mathcal{A}}
such that
\text{\rm}(C_{\upalpha})=\penalty 0
for each
\upalpha
,
there is a set
S\subset\penalty\mathbb{N}
such that
\text{\rm}(S)=1
and
S\cap\penalty C_{\upalpha}
is a one-point set for each
\upalpha\in\penalty\mathcal{A}
.

If
\text{\rm}(S)=0
for some subset
S\subset\penalty\mathbb{N}
,
we say that
S
is
-small
.
If
\text{\rm}(S)=1
, we say that
S
contains
-almost all
elements of
\mathbb{N}
.

4.2. Advanced exercise.

Let
be an ultrafilter and
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to\penalty\penalty\mathbb{N}
.
Suppose that
\text{\rm}(S)\leqslant\penalty\text{\rm}(f^{-1}(S))
for any set
S\subset\penalty\mathbb{N}
.
Show that
f(n)=n
for
-almost all
n\in\penalty\mathbb{N}
.

Classical definition.

More commonly, a nonprincipal ultrafilter is defined as a collection, say
\mathfrak{F}
, of sets in
\mathbb{N}
such that

1.

if
P\in\penalty\mathfrak{F}
and
Q\supset\penalty P
, then
Q\in\penalty\mathfrak{F}
,

2.

if
P,Q\in\penalty\mathfrak{F}
, then
P\cap\penalty Q\in\penalty\mathfrak{F}
,

3.

for any subset
P\subset\penalty\mathbb{N}
, either
P
or its complement is an element of
\mathfrak{F}
,

4.

if
F\subset\penalty\mathbb{N}
is finite, then
F\notin\mathfrak{F}
.

Setting

P\in\penalty\mathfrak{F}\quad\iff\quad\text{\rm}(P)=1

makes these two definitions equivalent.

A nonempty collection of sets
\mathfrak{F}
that does not include the empty set and satisfies only conditions
1
and
2
is called a
filter
;
if in addition
\mathfrak{F}
satisfies Condition
3
it is called an
ultrafilter
.
From Zorn’s lemma, it follows that every filter is contained in an ultrafilter.
Thus there is an ultrafilter
\mathfrak{F}
contained in the filter of all complements of finite sets; clearly this
\mathfrak{F}
is nonprincipal.

The existence of a selective ultrafilter follows from the continuum hypothesis;
this was proved by Walter Rudin
[
141
]
.

Stone–Čech compactification.

Given a set
S\subset\penalty\mathbb{N}
, consider the subset
\Omega_{S}
of all ultrafilters
such that
\text{\rm}(S)=1
.
It is straightforward to check that the sets
\Omega_{S}
for all
S\subset\penalty\mathbb{N}
