form a topology on the set of ultrafilters on
\mathbb{N}
.
The resulting space is called the
Stone–Čech compactification
of
\mathbb{N}
; it is usually denoted by
\upbeta\mathbb{N}
.

There is a natural embedding
\mathbb{N}\hookrightarrow\penalty\upbeta\mathbb{N}
defined by

n\mapsto\penalty\text{\rm}_{n}
, where
\text{\rm}_{n}
is the principal ultrafilter such that
\text{\rm}_{n}(S)=1
if and only if
n\in\penalty S
.
Using this embedding, we can (and will) consider
\mathbb{N}
as a subset of
\upbeta\mathbb{N}
.

The space
\upbeta\mathbb{N}
is the maximal compact Hausdorff space that contains
\mathbb{N}
as an everywhere dense subset.
More precisely, for any compact Hausdorff space
\mathcal{X}

and a map
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
, there is a unique continuous map
\bar{f}\colon\upbeta\mathbb{N}\to\penalty X
such that the restriction
\bar{f}|_{\mathbb{N}}
coincides with
f
.

B
Ultralimits of points

Fix an ultrafilter
.
Assume
x_{n}
is a sequence of points in a metric space
\mathcal{X}
.
Define an
-limit
of
x_{n}
to be a point
x_{\text{\rm}}

such that for any
\upvarepsilon>0
,
-almost all elements of
x_{n}
lie in
\mathrm{B}{}(x_{\text{\rm}},\upvarepsilon)
;
that is,

\text{\rm}\left\{\,{n\in\penalty\mathbb{N}}\ {:}\ {|{x_{\text{\rm}}}-\penalty{x_{n}}|<\upvarepsilon}\,\right\}=1.

In this case, we write

x_{\text{\rm}}=\lim_{n\to\penalty\text{\rm}}x_{n}\quad\text{or}\quad x_{n}\to\penalty x_{\text{\rm}}\quad\text{as}\quad n\to\penalty\text{\rm}.

Also, if
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}
we write
\lim_{n\to\penalty\text{\rm}}x_{n}=\pm\infty
if

\text{\rm}\left\{\,{n\in\penalty\mathbb{N}}\ {:}\ {\pm x_{n}>L}\,\right\}=1

for any
L\in\penalty\mathbb{R}
.

It easily follows from the definition that
-limits are unique if they exist.
For example, if
is the principal ultrafilter such that
\text{\rm}(\{n\})=1
for some
n\in\penalty\mathbb{N}
, then

x_{\text{\rm}}=x_{n}
.

Note that
-limits of a sequence and its subsequences may differ.
For example, in general

\lim_{n\to\penalty\text{\rm}}x_{n}\neq\lim_{n\to\penalty\text{\rm}}x_{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}n}.

The sequence
x_{n}
can be regarded as a map
\mathbb{N}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
.
If
\mathcal{X}
is compact, then this map can be uniquely extended to a continuous map to the Stone–Čech compactification
\upbeta\mathbb{N}
of
\mathbb{N}
.
Then
x_{\text{\rm}}
is the image of
.

4.3. Proposition.

Let
be a nonprincipal ultrafilter.
Assume
x_{n}
is a sequence of points in a metric space
\mathcal{X}

and
x_{n}\to\penalty x_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.
Then there is a subsequence of
x_{n}
that converges to
x_{\text{\rm}}
in the usual sense.

Moreover, if
is selective,
then the subsequence
(x_{n})_{n\in\penalty S}
can be chosen so that
\text{\rm}(S)=1
.

Proof.

Given
\upvarepsilon>0
,
let
S_{\upvarepsilon}=\left\{\,{n\in\penalty\mathbb{N}}\ {:}\ {|{x_{n}}-\penalty{x_{\text{\rm}}}|<\upvarepsilon}\,\right\}
.

Note that
\text{\rm}(S_{\upvarepsilon})=1
for any
\upvarepsilon>0
.
Since
is nonprincipal, the set
S_{\upvarepsilon}
is infinite.
Therefore we can choose an increasing sequence
n_{k}

such that
n_{k}\in\penalty S_{\frac{1}{k}}
for each
k\in\penalty\mathbb{N}
.
Clearly
x_{n_{k}}\to\penalty x_{\text{\rm}}
as
k\to\penalty\infty
.

Now assume that
is selective.
Consider the sets

\displaystyle C_{k}

\displaystyle=\left\{\,{n\in\penalty\mathbb{N}}\ {:}\ {\tfrac{1}{k}<|{x_{n}}-\penalty{x_{\text{\rm}}}|\leqslant\penalty\tfrac{1}{k-1}}\,\right\},

where we assume
\tfrac{1}{0}=\infty
, and the set

\displaystyle C_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\left\{\,{n\in\penalty\mathbb{N}}\ {:}\ {x_{n}=x_{\text{\rm}}}\,\right\}.

Note that
\text{\rm}(C_{k})=0
for any
k\in\penalty\mathbb{N}
.

If
\text{\rm}(C_{\infty})=1
, we can take the subsequence consisting of the
x_{n}
,
n\in\penalty C_{\infty}
.

Otherwise, discarding all empty sets among
C_{k}
and
C_{\infty}
gives a partition of
\mathbb{N}
into a countable collection of
-small sets.
Since
is selective, we can choose a set
S\subset\penalty\mathbb{N}
such that

S
meets each set of the partition at one point and
\text{\rm}(S)=1
.
Clearly the subsequence consisting of the
x_{n}
,
n\in\penalty S

converges to
x_{\text{\rm}}
in the usual sense.
∎

The following proposition
is analogous to the statement that any sequence in a compact metric space
has a convergent subsequence;
it can be proved in the same way.

4.4. Proposition.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a compact metric space.
Then
any sequence of points
x_{n}
in
\mathcal{X}
has a unique
-limit
x_{\text{\rm}}
.

In particular, a bounded sequence of real numbers has a unique
-limit.

The following lemma is an ultralimit analog of the Cauchy convergence test.

4.5. Lemma.

Let
x_{n}
be a sequence of points in a complete metric space
\mathcal{X}
.
If for each subsequence
y_{n}
of
x_{n}
,
the
-limit

y_{\text{\rm}}=\lim_{n\to\penalty\text{\rm}}y_{n}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}

is defined and does not depend on the choice of a subsequence,
then the sequence
x_{n}
converges in the usual sense.

Proof.
Assume the contrary.
Then for some
\upvarepsilon>0
, there is a subsequence
y_{n}
of
x_{n}
such that
|{x_{n}}-\penalty{y_{n}}|\geqslant\penalty\upvarepsilon
for all
n
.

It follows that
|{x_{\text{\rm}}}-\penalty{y_{\text{\rm}}}|\geqslant\penalty\upvarepsilon
, a contradiction.
∎

\ell^{\infty}
denotes the space of bounded sequences of real numbers.

L\colon\ell^{\infty}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}

s_{1},s_{2},\dots\penalty

Suppose that
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to\penalty\mathbb{N}

\lim_{n\to\penalty\text{\rm}}x_{n}=\lim_{n\to\penalty\text{\rm}}x_{f(n)}

for any bounded sequence
x_{n}
of real numbers.
Show that

for
-almost all

.

C
Ultralimits of spaces

Fix a selective ultrafilter
on the set of natural numbers.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{n}
be a sequence of metric spaces.
Consider all sequences

x_{n}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{n}
.
On the set of all such sequences,
define a pseudometric by the formula

|{(x_{n})}-\penalty{(y_{n})}|=\lim_{n\to\penalty\text{\rm}}|{x_{n}}-\penalty{y_{n}}|.

➊

Note that the
-limit on the right-hand side is always defined
and takes value in
[0,\infty]
.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}
be the corresponding metric space;
that is, the underlying set of
\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}
is formed by equivalence classes of sequences of points
x_{n}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{n}

defined by the relation

(x_{n})\sim(y_{n})\quad\iff\quad\lim_{n\to\penalty\text{\rm}}|{x_{n}}-\penalty{y_{n}}|=0,

and the distance is defined as in
➊
.

The space
\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}
is called the
-limit
of
\mathcal{X}_{n}
.
Typically
\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}
will denote the

-limit of a sequence
\mathcal{X}_{n}
;
we may also write

\mathcal{X}_{n}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}\quad\text{as}\quad n\to\penalty\text{\rm}\quad\text{or}\quad\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}=\lim_{n\to\penalty\text{\rm}}\mathcal{X}_{n}.

Given a sequence
x_{n}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{n}
,
we will denote by
x_{\text{\rm}}
its equivalence class, which is a point in
\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}
;
in this case, we may write

x_{n}\to\penalty x_{\text{\rm}}\quad\text{as}\quad n\to\penalty\text{\rm}\quad\text{or}\quad x_{\text{\rm}}=\lim_{n\to\penalty\text{\rm}}x_{n}.

4.8. Observation.

The
-limit of any sequence of metric spaces is complete.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{n}
be a sequence of metric spaces and
\mathcal{X}_{n}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.
Choose a Cauchy sequence
x_{n}
in
\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}
.
Passing to a subsequence, we can assume that
|{x_{k}}-\penalty{x_{m}}|_{\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}}<\tfrac{1}{k}
for any
k<m
.

Let us choose points
x_{n,m}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{n}
such that for any fixed
m
we have
x_{n,m}\to\penalty x_{m}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.
Note that for any
k<m
the inequality
|{x_{n,k}}-\penalty{x_{n,m}}|<\tfrac{1}{k}
holds for
-almost all
n
.
It follows that we can choose a nested sequence of sets

\mathbb{N}=S_{1}\supset\penalty S_{2}\supset\penalty\dots\penalty

such that

\diamond

\text{\rm}(S_{m})=1
for each
m
,

\diamond

\bigcap_{m}S_{m}=\varnothing
, and

\diamond

|{x_{n,k}}-\penalty{x_{n,l}}|<\tfrac{1}{k}
for
k<l\leqslant\penalty m
and
n\in\penalty S_{m}
.

Consider the sequence
y_{n}=x_{n,m(n)}
, where
m(n)
is the largest value such that
n\in\penalty S_{m(n)}
.
Denote by
y_{\text{\rm}}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}
the
-limit of
y_{n}
.

Observe that
|y_{m}-x_{n,m}|<\tfrac{1}{m}
for
-almost all
n
.
It follows that
|x_{m}-y_{\text{\rm}}|\leqslant\penalty\tfrac{1}{m}
for any
m
.
Therefore,
x_{n}\to\penalty y_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\infty
.
That is, any Cauchy sequence in
\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}
converges.
∎

4.9. Observation.

The
-limit of any sequence of length spaces is geodesic.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{X}_{n}
is a sequence of length spaces, then for any sequence of pairs
(x_{n},y_{n})
in
\mathcal{X}_{n}
there is a sequence of
\tfrac{1}{n}
-midpoints
z_{n}
.

Let
x_{n}\to\penalty x_{\text{\rm}}
,
y_{n}\to\penalty y_{\text{\rm}}
, and
z_{n}\to\penalty z_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.
Note that
z_{\text{\rm}}
is a midpoint between
x_{\text{\rm}}
and
y_{\text{\rm}}
in
\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}
.

By Observation
4C
,
\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}
is complete.
Applying Lemma
2C
we obtain the statement.
∎

A geodesic space
\mathcal{T}
is called a
metric tree
if any pair of points in
\mathcal{T}
are connected by a unique geodesic,
and the union of any two geodesics
[xy]_{\mathcal{T}}
, and
[yz]_{\mathcal{T}}
contain the geodesic
[xz]_{\mathcal{T}}
.
The latter means that any triangle in
\mathcal{T}
is a tripod;
that is, for any three points
x
,
y
, and
z
there is a point
p
such that

[xy]\cup\penalty[yz]\cup\penalty[zx]=[px]\cup\penalty[py]\cup\penalty[pz].

4.10. Exercise.

Let
\mathcal{T}

.

(a)

Show that

is a complete metric tree.

(b)

Show that

s,t\in\penalty\mathcal{T}
there is an isometry of
\mathcal{T}
that maps
s
to

.

(c)

Show that
\mathcal{T}
has
continuum degree
at any point;
that is, for any point
t\in\penalty\mathcal{T}
the set of connected components of the complement
\mathcal{T}\setminus\{t\}
has cardinality continuum.

Ultrapower.
If all the metric spaces in a sequence are identical,
\mathcal{X}_{n}=\penalty\mathcal{X}
,
the
-limit

\lim_{n\to\penalty\text{\rm}}\mathcal{X}_{n}

is denoted by
\mathcal{X}^{\text{\rm}}

and called the
-power of
\mathcal{X}
.

By Theorem
5E
,
there is a distance-preserving map

\upiota\colon\mathcal{X}\hookrightarrow\penalty\mathcal{X}^{\text{\rm}}
, where
\upiota(y)
is the equivalence class of the constant sequence
y_{n}=y
.

The image
\upiota(\mathcal{X})
might be a proper subset of
\mathcal{X}^{\text{\rm}}
.
For example,
\mathbb{R}^{\text{\rm}}
has pairs of points at distance
\infty
from each other, while each metric component of
\mathbb{R}^{\text{\rm}}
is isometric to
\mathbb{R}
.

According to Theorem
5E
,
if
\mathcal{X}
is compact then
\upiota(\mathcal{X})=\mathcal{X}^{\text{\rm}}
;
in particular,
\mathcal{X}^{\text{\rm}}
is isometric to
\mathcal{X}
.
If
\mathcal{X}
is proper, then
\upiota(\mathcal{X})
forms a metric component of
\mathcal{X}^{\text{\rm}}
.

The embedding
\upiota
allows us to treat
\mathcal{X}
as a subset of its ultrapower
\mathcal{X}^{\text{\rm}}
.

4.11. Observation.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a complete metric space.
Then
\mathcal{X}^{\text{\rm}}
is a geodesic space if and only if
\mathcal{X}
is a length space.

Proof.

Assume
\mathcal{X}^{\text{\rm}}
is a geodesic space.
Then any pair of points
x,y\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
has a midpoint
z_{\text{\rm}}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}^{\text{\rm}}
.
Fix a sequence of points
z_{n}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
such that
z_{n}\to\penalty z_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.

Note that

|{x}-\penalty{z_{n}}|_{\mathcal{X}}\to\penalty\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\mathcal{X}}

and

|{y}-\penalty{z_{n}}|_{\mathcal{X}}\to\penalty\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\mathcal{X}}

as

n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.
In particular, for any
\upvarepsilon>0
, the point
z_{n}
is an
\upvarepsilon
-midpoint between
x
and
y
for
-almost all
n
.
It remains to apply Lemma
2C
.

The if part follows from Observation
4C
.
∎

Note that the proof above together with Lemma
4B
imply the following:

4.12. Corollary.

Assume
\mathcal{X}
is a complete length space
and
p,q\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
cannot be joined by a geodesic in
\mathcal{X}
.
Then there are at least continuum distinct geodesics between
p
and
q

in the ultrapower
\mathcal{X}^{\text{\rm}}
.

and

D
Ultralimits of sets

Let
\mathcal{X}_{n}
be a sequence of metric spaces and
\mathcal{X}_{n}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}

as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.

For a sequence of sets
\Omega_{n}\subset\penalty\mathcal{X}_{n}
,
consider the maximal set
\Omega_{\text{\rm}}\subset\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}
such that
for any
x_{\text{\rm}}\in\penalty\Omega_{\text{\rm}}
and any sequence
x_{n}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{n}
such that
x_{n}\to\penalty x_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
, we have
x_{n}\in\penalty\Omega_{n}
for
-almost all
n
.

The set
\Omega_{\text{\rm}}
is called the
open
-limit
of
\Omega_{n}
;
we could also write
\Omega_{n}\to\penalty\Omega_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
or
\Omega_{\text{\rm}}=\lim_{n\to\penalty\text{\rm}}\Omega_{n}
.

Applying Observation
4C
to the sequence of complements
\mathcal{X}_{n}\setminus\Omega_{n}
, we see that
\Omega_{\text{\rm}}
is open for any sequence
\Omega_{n}
.

This definition can be applied to arbitrary sequences of sets,
but we will apply it only for sequences of open sets.

E
Ultralimits of functions

Recall that a family of submaps (see section
3A
) between metric spaces
\{f_{\upalpha}\colon\thinspace\mathcal{X}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathcal{Y}\}_{\upalpha\in\penalty\mathcal{A}}
is called
equicontinuous
if for any
\upvarepsilon>0
there is
\updelta>0
such that for any
p,q\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
with
|{p}-\penalty{q}|<\updelta
and any
\upalpha\in\penalty\mathcal{A}
we have
|{f_{\upalpha}(p)}-\penalty{f_{\upalpha}(q)}|<\upvarepsilon
.

Let
f_{n}\colon\mathcal{X}_{n}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a sequence of subfunctions.

Set
\Omega_{n}=\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f_{n}
.
Consider the open
-limit set
\Omega_{\text{\rm}}\subset\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}
of
\Omega_{n}
.

Assume there is a subfunction
f_{\text{\rm}}\colon\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}

that satisfies the following conditions:
(1)
\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f_{\text{\rm}}=\Omega_{\text{\rm}}
, (2) if
x_{n}\to\penalty x_{\text{\rm}}\in\penalty\Omega_{\text{\rm}}
for a sequence of points
x_{n}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{n}
, then
f_{n}(x_{n})\to\penalty f_{\text{\rm}}(x_{\text{\rm}})
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.
In this case, the subfunction
f_{\text{\rm}}\colon\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
is said to be the
-limit of
f_{n}\colon\mathcal{X}_{n}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
.

The following lemma gives a mild condition on a sequence of functions
f_{n}

guaranteeing the existence of its
-limit.

4.16. Lemma.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{n}
be a sequence of metric spaces
and
f_{n}\colon\mathcal{X}_{n}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a sequence of subfunctions.

Assume that for any positive integer
k
, there is an open set
\Omega_{n}(k)\subset\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f_{n}

such that the restrictions
f_{n}|_{\Omega_{n}(k)}
are uniformly bounded and equicontinuous
and the open
-limit of
\Omega_{n}(n)
coincides with the open
-limit of
\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f_{n}
.
Then the
-limit
f_{\text{\rm}}
of
f_{n}
is defined;
moreover
f_{\text{\rm}}
is a continuous subfunction.

In particular, if the functions
f_{n}
are uniformly bounded and equicontinuous, then its
-limit
f_{\text{\rm}}
is defined, bounded and uniformly continuous.

The proof is straightforward.

4.17. Exercise.

Construct a sequence of compact length spaces

\mathcal{X}_{n}

with a converging sequence of

-Lipschitz concave (see Definition
3F
) functions
f_{n}\colon\mathcal{X}_{n}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
such that
the
-limit
\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}
of
\mathcal{X}_{n}
is compact
and the
-limit
f_{\text{\rm}}\colon\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
of
f_{n}
is not concave.

If
f\colon\mathcal{X}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
is a subfunction,
the
-limit of the constant sequence
f_{n}=f
is called the
-power of
f
and is denoted by
f^{\text{\rm}}
.
So

f^{\text{\rm}}\colon\mathcal{X}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R},\quad f^{\text{\rm}}(x_{\text{\rm}})=\lim_{n\to\penalty\text{\rm}}f(x_{n}).

Evidently, if
f^{\text{\rm}}
is defined, then
f
is continuous.

Recall that we treat
\mathcal{X}
as a subset of its
-power
\mathcal{X}^{\text{\rm}}
.
Note that
\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f=\mathcal{X}\cap\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f^{\text{\rm}}
.
Moreover,
if
\mathrm{B}{}(x,\upvarepsilon)_{\mathcal{X}}\subset\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f

then
\mathrm{B}{}(x,\upvarepsilon)_{\mathcal{X}^{\text{\rm}}}\subset\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f^{\text{\rm}}
.

Chapter 5
Space of spaces

In this chapter we discuss the
Gromov–Hausdorff convergence of metric spaces.

To the best of our knowledge, Hausdorff convergence of subsets of a fixed metric space was first introduced by Felix Hausdorff
[
80
]
,
and a couple of years later an equivalent definition was given by Wilhelm Blaschke
[
29
]
.
A further refinement of this definition was introduced by Zdeněk Frolík
[
65
]

and then rediscovered by Robert Wijsman
[
157
]
.
However this refinement was a step in the direction of the so-called
closed convergence
introduced by Hausdorff in the original book.
For that reason we call it Hausdorff convergence
instead of

Hausdorff–Blashcke–Frolík–Wijsman convergence
.

Gromov–Hausdorff convergence was first introduced by David Edwards
[
61
]

and rediscovered later by Michael Gromov
[
71
]
.
It was an essential tool in Gromov’s proof that any group of polynomial growth has a nilpotent subgroup of finite index.
Other versions of convergence of metric spaces
were considered earlier, but each time
the definition was limited to very specific types of problems.

The definition of Gromov–Hausdorff convergence of metric spaces uses
the notion of Hausdorff convergence.
Gromov–Hausdorff convergence means that a sequence of metric spaces admits a sequence of distance-preserving embeddings into a common ambient metric space so that their images converge in the Hausdorff sense.
Our definition of Gromov–Hausdorff convergence and Gromov–Hausdorff distance differ somewhat from the standard definition.

A
Convergence of subsets

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a metric space and
A\subset\penalty\mathcal{X}
.
Recall that the distance from
A
to a point
x
in
\mathcal{X}

is given by

\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{A}x:=\inf\left\{\,{|{a}-\penalty{x}|}\ {:}\ {a\in\penalty A}\,\right\}.

By this definition, we have
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\varnothing}x=\infty
for any
x
.

5.1. Definition of Hausdorff convergence.

Given a sequence of closed sets
A_{n}
in a metric space
\mathcal{X}
,
a closed set
A_{\infty}\subset\penalty\mathcal{X}
is called the Hausdorff limit of
A_{n}
,
briefly
A_{n}\mathbin{\begin{picture}\put(-1.0,1.0){$\longrightarrow$}\put(9.0,-1.5){\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\text{\sf\tiny H}}}\end{picture}}\penalty A_{\infty}
, if

\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{A_{n}}x\to\penalty\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{A_{\infty}}x\quad\text{as}\quad n\to\penalty\infty

for any fixed
x\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
.

In this case the, sequence of closed sets
A_{n}
is said to be
converging
or
converging in the sense of Hausdorff
.

5.2. Selection theorem.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a proper metric space
and
A_{n}
be a sequence of closed sets in
\mathcal{X}
.
Then
A_{n}
has a converging subsequence in the sense of Hausdorff.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{X}
is proper,
we can choose a countable dense set
\{x_{1},x_{2},\dots\penalty\}
in
\mathcal{X}
.

If the sequence
a_{n}=\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{A_{n}}x_{k}
is unbounded for some
k
,
then we can pass to a subsequence of
A_{n}
such that

\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{A_{n}}x_{k}\to\penalty\infty
as
n\to\penalty\infty
for
any

k
.
The obtained sequence converges to the empty set.

Now suppose that
a_{n}=\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{A_{n}}x_{k}
is bounded for each
k
.
In this case, passing to a subsequence of
A_{n}
,
we can assume that
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{A_{n}}x_{k}
converges as
n\to\penalty\infty
for any fixed
k
.

Note that for each
n
, the function
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{A_{n}}\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
is 1-Lipschitz and nonnegative.
Therefore the sequence
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{A_{n}}
converges pointwise to a 1-Lipschitz nonnegative function
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
.

Set
A_{\infty}=f^{-1}(0)
.
Since
f
is 1-Lipschitz,

\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{A_{\infty}}y\geqslant\penalty f(y)
for any
y\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
.
It remains to show that
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{A_{\infty}}y\leqslant\penalty f(y)
for any
y
.

Assume the contrary,
that is,
f(z)<R<\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{A_{\infty}}z
for
z\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
and
R>0
.
Then for any sufficiently large
n
there is a point
z_{n}\in\penalty A_{n}
such that

|{x}-\penalty{z_{n}}|\leqslant\penalty R
.
Since
\mathcal{X}
is proper, we can pass to a partial limit
z_{\infty}
of
z_{n}
as
n\to\penalty\infty
.

Clearly
f(z_{\infty})=0
, that is,
z_{\infty}\in\penalty A_{\infty}
.
On the other hand,

\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{A_{\infty}}y\leqslant\penalty|{z_{\infty}}-\penalty{y}|\leqslant\penalty R<\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{A_{\infty}}y,

a contradiction.
∎

B
Convergence of spaces

5.3. Definition.

Let
\left\{\,{\mathcal{X}_{\upalpha}}\ {:}\ {\upalpha\in\penalty\mathcal{A}}\,\right\}
be a set of metric spaces.
A metric space
\bm{X}

is called a
common space
of
\left\{\,{\mathcal{X}_{\upalpha}}\ {:}\ {\upalpha\in\penalty\mathcal{A}}\,\right\}
if its underlying set is formed by the disjoint union

\bigsqcup_{\upalpha\in\penalty\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{X}_{\upalpha}

and each inclusion
\upiota_{\upalpha}\colon\mathcal{X}_{\upalpha}\hookrightarrow\penalty\bm{X}

is distance-preserving.

5.4. Definition.

Let
\bm{X}
be a common space for proper metric spaces

\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{X}_{2},\dots\penalty
, and
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
.
Assume that
\mathcal{X}_{n}
forms an open set in
\bm{X}
for each
n<\infty
and

\mathcal{X}_{n}\mathbin{\begin{picture}\put(-1.0,1.0){$\longrightarrow$}\put(9.0,-1.5){\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\text{\sf\tiny H}}}\end{picture}}\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
in
\bm{X}
as
n\to\penalty\infty
.

Then the topology
\uptau
of
\bm{X}
is called a
Gromov–Hausdorff convergence

and we write
\mathcal{X}_{n}\mathbin{\begin{picture}\put(-1.0,1.0){$\longrightarrow$}\put(11.0,-1.5){\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\text{\tiny\rm GH}}}\end{picture}}\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
or
\mathcal{X}_{n}\xrightarrow{\uptau}\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
;
the latter notation is used if we need to consider the specific Gromov–Hausdorff convergence
\uptau
.
The space
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
is called the
limit space
of the sequence
\mathcal{X}_{n}
along
\uptau
.

When we write
\mathcal{X}_{n}\mathbin{\begin{picture}\put(-1.0,1.0){$\longrightarrow$}\put(11.0,-1.5){\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\text{\tiny\rm GH}}}\end{picture}}\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
we mean that we made a choice of a Gromov–Hausdorff convergence.

Note that for a fixed sequence
\mathcal{X}_{n}
of metric spaces, one may construct different Gromov–Hausdorff convergences, say
\mathcal{X}_{n}\xrightarrow{\uptau}\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
and
\mathcal{X}_{n}\xrightarrow{\uptau^{\prime}}\mathcal{X}_{\infty}^{\prime}
, and their limit spaces
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
and
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}^{\prime}
need not be isometric to each other.
For example, for the constant sequence
\mathcal{X}_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it iso}}}\penalty\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant\penalty 0}
,
one may take
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it iso}}}\penalty\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant\penalty 0}
.
In this case, a point in the disjoint space
\bm{X}
can be regarded as a pair
(x,n)\in\penalty\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant\penalty 0}\times\penalty(\mathbb{Z}_{>}\cup\penalty\{\infty\})

and the metric on
\bm{X}
can be defined by

|{(x,n)}-\penalty{(y,m)}|_{\bm{X}}:=|\tfrac{1}{n}+\tfrac{1}{m}|+|x-y|,

where we assume that
0=\tfrac{1}{\infty}
.
On the other hand, one can take
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it iso}}}\penalty\mathbb{R}
,
and consider the metric

\displaystyle|{(x,n)}-\penalty{(y,m)}|_{\bm{X}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=|\tfrac{1}{n}-\tfrac{1}{m}|+|(x-n)-(y-m)|,

\displaystyle|{(x,n)}-\penalty{(y,\infty)}|_{\bm{X}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{n}+|(x-n)-y|,

\displaystyle|{(x,\infty)}-\penalty{(y,\infty)}|_{\bm{X}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=|x-y|,

where
n,m<\infty
.

5.5. Induced convergences.

Suppose
\mathcal{X}_{n}\xrightarrow{\uptau}\mathcal{X}_{\infty}

as in Definition
5B
,
and
\upiota_{n}\colon\mathcal{X}_{n}\hookrightarrow\penalty\bm{X}
,
\upiota_{\infty}\colon\mathcal{X}_{\infty}\hookrightarrow\penalty\bm{X}
are the corresponding inclusions.

(a)

A sequence of points
x_{n}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{n}
converges to
x_{\infty}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
(briefly,
x_{n}\to\penalty x_{\infty}
or
x_{n}\xrightarrow{\uptau}x_{\infty}
)
if
|{x_{n}}-\penalty{x_{\infty}}|_{\bm{X}}\to\penalty 0
.

(b)

A sequence of closed sets

\mathfrak{C}_{n}\subset\penalty\mathcal{X}_{n}

converges to a closed set

\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}\subset\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
(briefly,
\mathfrak{C}_{n}\to\penalty\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}
or
\mathfrak{C}_{n}\xrightarrow{\uptau}\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}
)
if
\mathfrak{C}_{n}\mathbin{\begin{picture}\put(-1.0,1.0){$\longrightarrow$}\put(9.0,-1.5){\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\text{\sf\tiny H}}}\end{picture}}\penalty\mathfrak{C}_{\infty}
as subsets of
\bm{X}
.

(c)

A sequence of open sets
\Omega_{n}\subset\penalty\mathcal{X}_{n}

converges to an open set
\Omega_{\infty}\subset\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\infty}

(briefly,
\Omega_{n}\to\penalty\Omega_{\infty}

or
\Omega_{n}\xrightarrow{\uptau}\Omega_{\infty}
)
if the complements
\mathcal{X}_{n}\setminus\Omega_{n}
converge to the complement
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}\setminus\Omega_{\infty}
as closed sets.

(d)

Let
\mathcal{X}_{n}\xrightarrow{\uptau}\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{n}\xrightarrow{\upvartheta}\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}
.
A sequence of submaps (where a submap is a map defined on a subset; see Section
3A
)
\Phi_{n}\colon\mathcal{X}_{n}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathcal{Y}_{n}
converges to a submap
\Phi_{\infty}\colon\mathcal{X}_{\infty}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}
if the following conditions holds

\circ\penalty

\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits\Phi_{n}\to\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits\Phi_{\infty}
as a sequence of open sets.

\circ\penalty

for any
x_{\infty}\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits\Phi_{\infty}
and any sequence
x_{n}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{n}
such that
x_{n}\to\penalty x_{\infty}
, we have

\mathcal{Y}_{n}\ni\Phi_{n}(x_{n})\xrightarrow{\upvartheta}\Phi_{\infty}(x_{\infty})\in\penalty\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}

as
n\to\penalty\infty
.

(e)

Given a sequence of measures
\upmu_{n}
on
\mathcal{X}_{n}
,
we say that
\upmu_{n}
weakly converges to a measure
\upmu_{\infty}
on
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}

(briefly,
\upmu_{n}\xrightarrow{}\upmu_{\infty}
or
\upmu_{n}\xrightarrow{\uptau}\upmu_{\infty}
)
if the pushforward measures of
\upmu_{n}

weakly converge to the pushforward measure of
\upmu_{\infty}
.

In other words,
if for any continuous function
\upvarphi\colon\bm{X}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
with a compact support, we have

\int\limits_{\mathcal{X}_{n}}\upvarphi\circ\penalty\upiota_{n}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upmu_{n}\to\penalty\int\limits_{\mathcal{X}_{\infty}}\upvarphi\circ\penalty\upiota_{\infty}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upmu_{\infty}

as
n\to\penalty\infty
.

Liftings.

Given a Gromov–Hausdorff convergence

\mathcal{X}_{n}\mathbin{\begin{picture}\put(-1.0,1.0){$\longrightarrow$}\put(11.0,-1.5){\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\text{\tiny\rm GH}}}\end{picture}}\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\infty}

and a point
p_{\infty}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
, any sequence of points
p_{n}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{n}
such that
p_{n}\mathbin{\begin{picture}\put(-1.0,1.0){$\longrightarrow$}\put(11.0,-1.5){\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\text{\tiny\rm GH}}}\end{picture}}\penalty p_{\infty}
will be called a
lifting
of
p_{\infty}
.
The point
p_{n}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{n}
will be called a
lifting
of
p_{\infty}
to
\mathcal{X}_{n}
.
We will also say that
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p_{n}}{}{}\colon\mathcal{X}_{n}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}

is a
lifting

of the distance function
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p_{\infty}}{}{}\colon\mathcal{X_{\infty}}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
.
Clearly
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p_{n}}{}{}\mathbin{\begin{picture}\put(-1.0,1.0){$\longrightarrow$}\put(11.0,-1.5){\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\text{\tiny\rm GH}}}\end{picture}}\penalty\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p_{\infty}}{}{}
.

Note that liftings are not uniquely defined.

Similarly, we may refer to liftings of a point array

\bm{p}_{\infty}=\penalty(p_{\infty}^{1},p_{\infty}^{2},\dots\penalty,p_{\infty}^{k})

and of the corresponding distance map

\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{p}_{\infty}}{}{}\colon\mathcal{X}_{\infty}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}^{k}
,

\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{p}_{\infty}}{}{}\colon x\mapsto\penalty(|{p_{\infty}^{1}}-\penalty{x}|,|{p_{\infty}^{2}}-\penalty{x}|,\dots\penalty,|{p_{\infty}^{k}}-\penalty{x}|).

C
Gromov’s selection theorem

5.6. Gromov’s selection theorem.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{n}
be a sequence of proper metric spaces
with marked points
x_{n}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{n}
.
Assume that for any
R>0
,
\upvarepsilon>0
, there is
N=N(R,\upvarepsilon)\in\penalty\mathbb{Z}_{>0}

such that for each
n

the ball
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[x_{n},R]\subset\penalty\mathcal{X}_{n}
admits a finite
\upvarepsilon
-net with at most
N
points.
Then a subsequence of
\mathcal{X}_{n}
admits a Gromov–Hausdorff convergence
such that the sequence of marked points
x_{n}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{n}

converges.

Proof.

By the main assumption, there is a sequence of integers
M_{1}<\penalty M_{2}<\dots\penalty

such that in each space
\mathcal{X}_{n}

there is a sequence of points
z_{i,n}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{n}
for which

|{z_{i,n}}-\penalty{x_{n}}|_{\mathcal{X}_{n}}\leqslant\penalty k+1\quad\text{if}\quad i\leqslant\penalty M_{k}

and
\{z_{1,n},\dots\penalty,z_{M_{k},n}\}
is a
\tfrac{1}{k}
-net in
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[x_{n},k]_{\mathcal{X}_{n}}
.

Passing to a subsequence, we may assume that the sequence

\ell_{n}=|{z_{i,n}}-\penalty{z_{j,n}}|_{\mathcal{X}_{n}}

converges for any
i
and
j
.

Let us consider a countable set of points
\mathcal{W}=\{w_{1},w_{2},\dots\penalty\}

equipped with the pseudometric defined by

|{w_{i}}-\penalty{w_{j}}|_{\mathcal{W}}=\lim_{n\to\penalty\infty}|{z_{i,n}}-\penalty{z_{j,n}}|_{\mathcal{X}_{n}}.

Let
\hat{\mathcal{W}}
be the metric space corresponding to
\mathcal{W}
.
Denote by

\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
the completion of
\hat{\mathcal{W}}
.

It remains to construct a metric on the disjoint union of

\bm{X}=\mathcal{X}_{\infty}\sqcup\mathcal{X}_{1}\sqcup\mathcal{X}_{2}\sqcup\dots\penalty

satisfying definitions
5B
and
5B
.

Such a metric can be defined as follows.
Fix a sequence
\upvarepsilon_{k}\to\penalty 0+

and let
N_{k}
be the minimal integer such that

|{w_{i}}-\penalty{w_{j}}|_{\mathcal{W}}\lessgtr|{z_{i,n}}-\penalty{z_{j,n}}|_{\mathcal{X}_{n}}\pm\upvarepsilon_{k}

if
i,j\leqslant\penalty N_{k}
and
n\geqslant\penalty N_{k}
.
Let us equip
\bm{X}
with the maximal metric such that all the inclusions
\upiota_{n}\colon\mathcal{X}_{n}\to\penalty\bm{X}
and
\upiota_{\infty}\colon\mathcal{X}_{\infty}\to\penalty\bm{X}
are isometric and

|{z_{i,n}}-\penalty{w_{i}}|\leqslant\penalty\upvarepsilon_{k}

for
i\leqslant\penalty N_{k}
and
n\geqslant\penalty N_{k}
.
It is easy to verify that such a metric on
\bm{X}
satisfies
5B
and
5B
.
∎

D
Convergence of compact spaces.

5.7. Definition.

Let
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
be metric spaces. A map
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathcal{Y}

is called an
\upvarepsilon
-isometry

if the following two conditions hold:

(a)

\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits f
is an
\upvarepsilon
-net in
\mathcal{Y}
.

(b)

\bigl||{f(x)}-\penalty{f(x^{\prime})}|_{\mathcal{Y}}-|{x}-\penalty{x^{\prime}}|_{\mathcal{X}}\bigr|\leqslant\penalty\upvarepsilon
for any
x,x^{\prime}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
.

5.8. Lemma.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{X}_{2},\dots\penalty
, and
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
be metric spaces and
\upvarepsilon_{n}\to\penalty 0+
as
n\to\penalty\infty
.
Suppose that either

(a)

for each
n
there is an
\upvarepsilon_{n}
-isometry
f_{n}\colon\mathcal{X}_{n}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
, or

(b)

for each
n
there is an
\upvarepsilon_{n}
-isometry
h_{n}\colon\mathcal{X}_{\infty}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}_{n}
.

Then there is a Gromov–Hausdorff convergence
\mathcal{X}_{n}\mathbin{\begin{picture}\put(-1.0,1.0){$\longrightarrow$}\put(11.0,-1.5){\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\text{\tiny\rm GH}}}\end{picture}}\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
.

Proof.

To prove part (
(a)
) let us construct a common space
\bm{X}
for the spaces
\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{X}_{2},\dots\penalty
, and
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}

by taking the metric
\uprho
on the disjoint union
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}\sqcup\mathcal{X}_{1}\sqcup\mathcal{X}_{2}\sqcup\dots\penalty
that is defined the following way:

\displaystyle\uprho(x_{n},y_{n})

\displaystyle=|{x_{n}}-\penalty{y_{n}}|_{\mathcal{X}_{n}},\quad\uprho(x_{\infty},y_{\infty})=|{x_{\infty}}-\penalty{y_{\infty}}|_{\mathcal{X}_{\infty}},

\displaystyle\uprho(x_{n},x_{\infty})

\displaystyle=\inf\left\{\,{|{x_{n}}-\penalty{y_{n}}|_{\mathcal{X}_{n}}+\upvarepsilon_{n}+|{x_{\infty}}-\penalty{f(y_{n})}|_{\mathcal{X}_{\infty}}}\ {:}\ {{y_{n}}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{n}}\,\right\},

\displaystyle\uprho(x_{n},x_{m})

\displaystyle=\inf\left\{\,{\uprho(x_{n},y_{\infty})+\uprho(x_{m},y_{\infty})}\ {:}\ {y_{\infty}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\infty}}\,\right\},

where we assume that
x_{m}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{m}
,
x_{n}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{n}
, and
x_{\infty}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
.

It remains to observe that
\uprho
is indeed a metric and

\mathcal{X}_{n}\mathbin{\begin{picture}\put(-1.0,1.0){$\longrightarrow$}\put(9.0,-1.5){\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\text{\sf\tiny H}}}\end{picture}}\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
in
\bm{X}
.

The proof of the second part is analogous; one only needs to change one line in the definition of
\uprho
to the following:

\uprho(x_{n},x_{\infty})=\inf\left\{\,{|{x_{n}}-\penalty{h(y_{\infty})}|_{\mathcal{X}_{n}}+\upvarepsilon_{n}+|{x_{\infty}}-\penalty{y_{\infty}}|_{\mathcal{X}_{\infty}}}\ {:}\ {{y_{\infty}}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\infty}}\,\right\}.

∎

5.9. Definition.

Given two compact spaces
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
, we will write

\diamond

\mathcal{X}\leqslant\penalty\mathcal{Y}
if there is a noncontracting map
\Phi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathcal{Y}
.

\diamond

\mathcal{X}\leqslant\penalty\mathcal{Y}+\upvarepsilon
if there is a map
\Phi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathcal{Y}
such that for any
x,x^{\prime}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
we have

|{x}-\penalty{x^{\prime}}|\leqslant\penalty|{\Phi(x)}-\penalty{\Phi(x^{\prime})}|+\upvarepsilon.

5.10. Lemma.

Let
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
be two metric spaces and
\mathcal{X}
be compact. Then

\mathcal{X}\geqslant\penalty\mathcal{Y}\geqslant\penalty\mathcal{X}\quad\iff\quad\mathcal{X}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it iso}}}\penalty\mathcal{Y}.

The following proof was suggested by Travis Morrison.

Proof.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathcal{Y}

and
g\colon\mathcal{Y}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
be noncontracting mappings.
It is sufficient to prove that
h=g\circ\penalty f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
is an isometry.

Given any pair of points
x,y\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
,
let
x_{n}=\penalty h^{\circ\penalty n}(x)
and
y_{n}=\penalty h^{\circ\penalty n}(y)
.
Since
\mathcal{X}
is compact, one can choose an increasing sequence of integers
n_{k}

such that both sequences
x_{n_{i}}
and
y_{n_{i}}

converge.
In particular, both of these sequences are
Cauchy,
that is,

|{x_{n_{i}}}-\penalty{x_{n_{j}}}|,|{y_{n_{i}}}-\penalty{y_{n_{j}}}|\to\penalty 0

as
\min\{i,j\}\to\penalty\infty
.
Since
h
is noncontracting, we have

|{x}-\penalty{x_{|n_{i}-n_{j}|}}|\leqslant\penalty|{x_{n_{i}}}-\penalty{x_{n_{j}}}|.

It follows that
there is a sequence
m_{i}\to\penalty\infty
such that

x_{m_{i}}\to\penalty x\quad\text{and}\quad y_{m_{i}}\to\penalty y\quad\text{as}\quad i\to\penalty\infty.

➊

Let
\ell_{n}=|{x_{n}}-\penalty{y_{n}}|
.
Since
h
is noncontracting, the sequence
\ell_{n}
is nondecreasing.
On the other hand,
from
➊
it follows that
\ell_{m_{i}}\to\penalty|{x}-\penalty{y}|=\ell_{0}
as
m_{i}\to\penalty\infty
;
that is,
\ell_{n}
is a constant sequence.
In particular,
\ell_{0}=\ell_{1}
for any
x
and
y
in
\mathcal{X}
,
so
h
is a distance-preserving map.

Thus
h(\mathcal{X})
is isometric to
\mathcal{X}
.
From
➊
,
h(\mathcal{X})
is everywhere dense.
Since
\mathcal{X}
is compact,
h(\mathcal{X})=\mathcal{X}
.
∎

The
Gromov–Hausdorff distance
between isometry classes of compact metric spaces
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
, is defined by

\text{\sc d}_{\text{\sf\tiny GH}}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}):=\inf\left\{\,{\upvarepsilon>0}\ {:}\ {\mathcal{X}\leqslant\penalty\mathcal{Y}+\upvarepsilon\ \text{and}\ \mathcal{Y}\leqslant\penalty\mathcal{X}+\upvarepsilon}\,\right\}.

The Gromov–Hausdorff distance turns the set of all isometry classes of compact metric spaces into a metric space.
The following theorem shows that convergence in this space coincides with the Gromov–Hausdorff convergence defined above.

5.11. Theorem.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{X}_{2},\dots\penalty
, and
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
be compact metric spaces.
Then there is a convergence
\mathcal{X}_{n}\mathbin{\begin{picture}\put(-1.0,1.0){$\longrightarrow$}\put(11.0,-1.5){\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\text{\tiny\rm GH}}}\end{picture}}\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
if and only if

\text{\sc d}_{\text{\sf\tiny GH}}(\mathcal{X}_{n},\mathcal{X}_{\infty})\to\penalty 0
as
n\to\penalty\infty
.

Proof; if part.

Suppose
a_{n}\colon\mathcal{X}_{\infty}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}_{n}

and
b_{n}\colon\mathcal{X}_{n}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
are sequences of maps such that

|{a_{n}(x)}-\penalty{a_{n}(y)}|_{\mathcal{X}_{\infty}}\geqslant\penalty|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\mathcal{X}_{n}}-\updelta_{n},

|{b_{n}(v)}-\penalty{b_{n}(w)}|_{\mathcal{X}_{n}}\geqslant\penalty|{v}-\penalty{w}|_{\mathcal{X}_{\infty}}-\updelta_{n}

for any
x,y\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{n}
,
v,w\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
, and a sequence
\updelta_{n}\to\penalty 0+
.

Fix
\upvarepsilon>0
and choose a maximal
\upvarepsilon
-packing
\{x^{1},x^{2},\dots\penalty,x^{k}\}
in
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
such that

\sum_{i<j}|{x^{i}}-\penalty{x^{j}}|
is maximal.
Note that

|{a_{n}\circ\penalty b_{n}(x^{i})}-\penalty{a_{n}\circ\penalty b_{n}(x^{j})}|\geqslant\penalty|{x^{i}}-\penalty{x^{j}}|-2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\updelta_{n}.

Since
\sum_{i<j}|{x^{i}}-\penalty{x^{j}}|
is maximal,

|{a_{n}\circ\penalty b_{n}(x^{i})}-\penalty{a_{n}\circ\penalty b_{n}(x^{j})}|\to\penalty|{x^{i}}-\penalty{x^{j}}|

for all
i
and
j
as
n\to\penalty\infty
.
For all large
n
,
we have
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\updelta_{n}<|{x^{i}}-\penalty{x^{j}}|-\upvarepsilon
,
and so

|{b_{n}(x^{i})}-\penalty{b_{n}(x^{j})}|_{\mathcal{X}_{n}}>\upvarepsilon\quad\text{and}\quad|{a_{n}\circ\penalty b_{n}(x^{i})}-\penalty{a_{n}\circ\penalty b_{n}(x^{j})}|_{\mathcal{X}_{\infty}}>\upvarepsilon

for all
i\neq j
.
Therefore for each large
n
,
the set
\{a_{n}\circ\penalty b_{n}(x^{i})\}
is a maximal
\upvarepsilon
-packing and hence an
\upvarepsilon
-net in
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
.

Since
\{a_{n}\circ\penalty b_{n}(x^{i})\}
is an
\upvarepsilon
-net in
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
, we have that
for any
y_{n}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{n}
there is
x^{i}
such that
|{a_{n}\circ\penalty b_{n}(x^{i})}-\penalty{a_{n}(y_{n})}|<\upvarepsilon
.
Thus
|{b_{n}(x^{i})}-\penalty{y_{n}}|<\upvarepsilon+\updelta_{n}
,
that is,
\{b_{n}(x^{i})\}
is a
(\upvarepsilon+\updelta_{n})
-net in
\mathcal{X}_{n}
.

Given
y\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{n}
, choose
x^{i}
so that
|{b_{n}(x^{i})}-\penalty{y_{n}}|<\upvarepsilon+\updelta_{n}
and define
h_{n}(y)=a_{n}\circ\penalty b_{n}(x^{i})
.
Observe that
h_{n}
is a
3{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon
-isometry for all large
n
.
Since
\upvarepsilon>0
is arbitrary, there is a sequence of
\upvarepsilon_{n}
-isometries
\mathcal{X}_{n}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
such that
\upvarepsilon_{n}\to\penalty 0+
as
n\to\penalty\infty
.
It remains to apply
5D
.

Only-if part.

Assume
\mathcal{X}_{n}\xrightarrow{\uptau}\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
.
Fix
\upvarepsilon>0
, and choose a maximal
\upvarepsilon
-packing
\{x^{1},x^{2},\dots\penalty,x^{k}\}
in
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
.
For each
x^{i}
,
choose a sequence
x^{i}_{n}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{n}

such that
x^{i}_{n}\to\penalty x^{i}
.
Define a map
a_{n}\colon\mathcal{X}_{n}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\infty}

such that
a_{n}(x^{i}_{n})=x_{n}
.
Note that for all large
n
, we have
|{x^{i}_{n}}-\penalty{x^{j}_{n}}|>\upvarepsilon
.
For each point
z\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
, choose
x^{i}
so that
|{z}-\penalty{x^{i}}|<\upvarepsilon
.
Define a map
b_{n}\colon\mathcal{X}_{\infty}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}_{n}
by setting

b_{n}(z)=x^{i}_{n}
.
Observe that

|{b_{n}(y)}-\penalty{b_{n}(z)}|_{\mathcal{X}_{n}}+3{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon>|{y}-\penalty{z}|_{\mathcal{X}_{\infty}}

for all large
n
.

In the same way we can construct a map
a_{n}\colon\mathcal{X}_{n}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
such that

|{a_{n}(y)}-\penalty{a_{n}(z)}|_{\mathcal{X}_{\infty}}+3{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon>|{y}-\penalty{z}|_{\mathcal{X}_{n}}.

Hence
\text{\sc d}_{\text{\sf\tiny GH}}(\mathcal{X}_{n},\mathcal{X}_{\infty})\to\penalty 0
as
n\to\penalty\infty
.
∎

The following theorem states that the isometry class of a Gromov–Hausdorff limit is uniquely defined if it is compact.

5.12. Theorem.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{X}_{2},\dots\penalty
, and
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
and
\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty}
be metric spaces
such that
\mathcal{X}_{n}\xrightarrow{\uptau}\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
,

\mathcal{X}_{n}\xrightarrow{\bar{\uptau}}\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty}
.

Assume that
\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty}
is compact.
Then
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it iso}}}\penalty\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty}
.

Proof.

For each point
x_{\infty}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
,
choose liftings
x_{n}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{n}
.

Choose a nonprincipal ultrafilter
on
\mathbb{N}
.
Define
\bar{x}_{\infty}\in\penalty\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty}
as the
-limit of
x_{n}
with respect to
\bar{\uptau}
.
We claim that the map
x_{\infty}\to\penalty\bar{x}_{\infty}
is an isometry.

Indeed, by the definition of Gromov–Hausdorff convergence,

|{\bar{x}_{\infty}}-\penalty{\bar{y}_{\infty}}|_{\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty}}=\lim_{n\to\penalty\text{\rm}}|{x_{n}}-\penalty{y_{n}}|_{\mathcal{X}_{n}}=|{x_{\infty}}-\penalty{y_{\infty}}|_{\mathcal{X}_{\infty}}.

Thus the map
x_{\infty}\to\penalty\bar{x}_{\infty}
gives a distance-preserving map

\Phi\colon\mathcal{X}_{\infty}\hookrightarrow\penalty\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty}
.
In particular,

\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
is compact.
Switching
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
and
\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty}
and applying the same argument,
we get an isometric embedding

\bar{\mathcal{X}}_{\infty}\hookrightarrow\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
.
Now the result follows from Lemma
5D
.
∎

5.13. Exercise.

(a)

Show that a sequence of compact simply connected length spaces cannot converge to a circle.

(b)

Construct a sequence of compact simply connected length spaces that converges to a compact non-simply connected space.

5.14. Exercise.

(a)

Show that a sequence of length metrics on the 2-sphere cannot converge to the unit disk.

(b)

Construct a sequence of length metrics on the 3-sphere that converges to a unit 3-ball.

5.15. Exercise.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{n}
be a sequence of metric spaces that admits
two Gromov–Hausdorff convergences

\uptau
and
\uptau^{\prime}
.
Assume

\mathcal{X}_{n}\xrightarrow{\uptau}\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
and
\mathcal{X}_{n}\xrightarrow{\uptau^{\prime}}\mathcal{X}_{\infty}^{\prime}
.
Show that if
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
is proper and there is a sequence of points
x_{n}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{n}

that converges in both

\uptau
and
\uptau^{\prime}
, then
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it iso}}}\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\infty}^{\prime}
.

E
Ultralimits revisited

Recall that
denotes an ultrafilter of the set of natural numbers.

5.16. Theorem.

Assume
\mathcal{X}_{n}
is a sequence of complete metric spaces.
Let
\mathcal{X}_{n}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
,
and let
\mathcal{Y}_{n}

be a sequence of subspaces of the
\mathcal{X}_{n}
such that
\mathcal{Y}_{n}\mathbin{\begin{picture}\put(-1.0,1.0){$\longrightarrow$}\put(11.0,-1.5){\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\text{\tiny\rm GH}}}\end{picture}}\penalty\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}
.
Then there is a distance-preserving map

\upiota:\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}
.

Moreover:

(a)

If
\mathcal{X}_{n}\mathbin{\begin{picture}\put(-1.0,1.0){$\longrightarrow$}\put(11.0,-1.5){\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\text{\tiny\rm GH}}}\end{picture}}\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\infty}

and
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
is compact, then

\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
is isometric to
\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}
.

(b)

If
\mathcal{X}_{n}\mathbin{\begin{picture}\put(-1.0,1.0){$\longrightarrow$}\put(11.0,-1.5){\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\text{\tiny\rm GH}}}\end{picture}}\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\infty}

and
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
is proper, then

\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
is isometric to a metric component of
\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}
.

Proof.

For each point
y_{\infty}\in\penalty\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}

choose a lifting
y_{n}\in\penalty\mathcal{Y}_{n}
.
Pass to the
-limit
y_{\text{\rm}}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}
of
y_{n}
.
Clearly for any
y_{\infty},z_{\infty}\in\penalty\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}
,
we have

|{y_{\infty}}-\penalty{z_{\infty}}|_{\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}}=|{y_{\text{\rm}}}-\penalty{z_{\text{\rm}}}|_{\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}};

that is, the map
y_{\infty}\mapsto\penalty y_{\text{\rm}}
gives a distance-preserving map
\upiota:\mathcal{Y}_{\infty}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}
.

5E
+
5E
.

Fix
x_{\text{\rm}}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}
.
Choose a sequence
x_{n}
of points in
\mathcal{X}_{n}
,
such that
x_{n}\to\penalty x_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.

Denote by
\bm{X}=\mathcal{X}_{\infty}\sqcup\mathcal{X}_{1}\sqcup\mathcal{X}_{2}\sqcup\dots\penalty
the common space for the convergence
\mathcal{X}_{n}\mathbin{\begin{picture}\put(-1.0,1.0){$\longrightarrow$}\put(11.0,-1.5){\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\text{\tiny\rm GH}}}\end{picture}}\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
,
as in the definition of Gromov–Hausdorff convergence.
Note that
x_{n}
is a sequence of points in
\bm{X}
.

If the
-limit
x_{\infty}
of
x_{n}
in
\bm{X}
exists,
it must lie in
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
.

The point
x_{\infty}
, if defined, does not depend on the choice of
x_{n}
.
Indeed, if
y_{n}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{n}
is another sequence such that
y_{n}\to\penalty x_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
, then

|{y_{\infty}}-\penalty{x_{\infty}}|=\lim_{n\to\penalty\text{\rm}}|{y_{n}}-\penalty{x_{n}}|=0;

therefore,
x_{\infty}=y_{\infty}
.

This way we obtain a map
\upnu\colon x_{\text{\rm}}\to\penalty x_{\infty}
, defined on
\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits\upnu\subset\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}
.
By construction of
\upiota
,
we have
\upiota\circ\penalty\upnu(x_{\text{\rm}})=x_{\text{\rm}}
for any
x_{\text{\rm}}\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits\upnu
.

Finally note that if
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
is compact, then
\upnu
is defined on all of
\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}
;
this proves
5E
.

If
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
is proper, choose any point
z_{\infty}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\infty}

and set
z_{\text{\rm}}=\upiota(z_{\infty})
.
For any point
x_{\text{\rm}}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}_{\text{\rm}}
at finite distance from
z_{\text{\rm}}
,
for the sequence
x_{n}

as above we have that
|{z_{n}}-\penalty{x_{n}}|
is bounded for
-almost all
n
.
Since
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
is proper,
\upnu(x_{\text{\rm}})
is defined;
in other words,
\upnu
is defined on the metric component of
z_{\text{\rm}}
.
Hence
5E
follows.
∎

Chapter 6
The ghost of Euclid

A
Geodesics, triangles and hinges

Geodesics and their relatives.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a metric space and
\mathbb{I}\subset\penalty\mathbb{R}
be an interval.
A globally distance-preserving map
\upgamma\colon\mathbb{I}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
is called a
unit-speed geodesic
.
(Various authors call it differently:
shortest path
,
minimizing geodesic
.)
In other words,
\upgamma\colon\mathbb{I}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
is a unit-speed geodesic if the equality

|{\upgamma(s)}-\penalty{\upgamma(t)}|_{\mathcal{X}}=|s-t|

holds for any pair
s,t\in\penalty\mathbb{I}
.

A unit-speed geodesic between
p
and
q
in
\mathcal{X}
will be denoted by
\mathrm{geod}_{[pq]}
.
We will always assume
\mathrm{geod}_{[pq]}
is parametrized starting at
p
;
that is,
\mathrm{geod}_{[pq]}(0)=p
and
\mathrm{geod}_{[pq]}(|{p}-\penalty{q}|)=q
.
The image of
\mathrm{geod}_{[pq]}
will be denoted by
[pq]
and called a
geodesic
.
The term
geodesic
will also be used for a linear reparametrization of a unit-speed geodesic.
With a slight abuse of notation, we will use the notation
[pq]
also for the class of all linear reparametrizations of
\mathrm{geod}_{[pq]}
.

A unit-speed geodesic
\upgamma\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant\penalty 0}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
is called a
half-line
.

A unit-speed geodesic
\upgamma\colon\mathbb{R}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
is called a
line
.

A piecewise geodesic curve is called
polygonal line
;
we may say
polygonal line
p_{1},\dots\penalty,p_{n}
meaning polygonal line with edges
[p_{1}p_{2}],\dots\penalty,[p_{n-1}p_{n}]
.
A closed polygonal line will be also called a polygon.

We may write
[pq]_{\mathcal{X}}

to emphasize that the geodesic
[pq]
is in the space
{\mathcal{X}}
.
Also, we use the following short-cut notation:

\displaystyle\mathopen{]}pq\mathclose{[}

\displaystyle=[pq]\setminus\{p,q\},

\displaystyle\mathopen{]}pq]

\displaystyle=[pq]\setminus\{p\},

\displaystyle[pq\mathclose{[}

\displaystyle=[pq]\setminus\{q\}.

In general, a geodesic between
p
and
q
need not exist and if it exists, it need not to be unique.
However, once we write
\mathrm{geod}_{[pq]}
or
[pq]
we mean that we have fixed a choice of a geodesic.

A constant-speed geodesic
\upgamma\colon[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
is called a
geodesic path
.
Given a geodesic
[pq]
,
we denote by
\mathrm{path}_{[pq]}
the corresponding geodesic path;
that is,

\mathrm{path}_{[pq]}(t)\equiv\penalty\mathrm{geod}_{[pq]}(t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{p}-\penalty{q}|).

A curve
\upgamma\colon\mathbb{I}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
is called a
local geodesic
if for any
t\in\penalty\mathbb{I}
there is a neighborhood
U\ni t
in
\mathbb{I}
such that the restriction
\upgamma|_{U}
is a constant-speed geodesic.
If
\mathbb{I}=[0,1]
, then
\upgamma
is called a
local geodesic path
.

6.1. Proposition.

Suppose
\mathcal{X}
is a metric space and
\upgamma\colon[0,\infty)\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
is a half-line.
Then the
Busemann function

\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{\upgamma}\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}

\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{\upgamma}(x)=\lim_{t\to\penalty\infty}|{\upgamma(t)}-\penalty{x}|-t

➊

is defined
and
1
-Lipschitz.

Proof.

By the triangle inequality, the function
t\mapsto\penalty|{\upgamma(t)}-\penalty{x}|-t
is nonincreasing.
Clearly
|{\upgamma(t)}-\penalty{x}|-t\geqslant\penalty-|{\upgamma(0)}-\penalty{x}|
.
Thus the limit in
➊
is defined,
and it is 1-Lipschitz as a limit of 1-Lipschitz functions.
∎

6.2. Example.

If
\mathcal{X}
is a Euclidean space and
\upgamma(t)=p+t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}v
where
v
is a unit vector,
then

\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{\upgamma}(x)=\langle x-p,v\rangle.

Triangles.

For a triple of points
p,q,r\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
, a choice of a triple of geodesics
([qr],[rp],[pq])
will be called a
triangle
, and we will use the short notation

[{p}{q}{r}]=([qr],[rp],[pq])
.
Again, given a triple
p,q,r\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
, there may be no triangle

[{p}{q}{r}]
, simply because one of the pairs of these points cannot be joined by a geodesic. Or there may be many different triangles, any of which can be denoted by
[{p}{q}{r}]
.
Once we write
[{p}{q}{r}]
, it means we have chosen such a triangle;
that is, made a choice of each
[qr]
,
[rp]
, and
[pq]
.

The value

|{p}-\penalty{q}|+|{q}-\penalty{r}|+|{r}-\penalty{p}|

will be called the
perimeter of triangle

[{p}{q}{r}]
;
it obviously coincides with perimeter of the triple
p
,
q
,
r
as defined below.

Hinges.

Let
p,x,y\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
be a triple of points such that
p
is distinct from
x
and
y
.
A pair of geodesics
([px],[py])
will be called a
hinge
, and will be denoted by

[p\,{}^{x}_{y}]=([px],[py])
.

B
Model angles and triangles

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a metric space,

p,q,r\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
,
and
\upkappa\in\penalty\mathbb{R}
.
Let us define the
model triangle

[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{q}}{\tilde{r}}]

(briefly,

[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{q}}{\tilde{r}}]={\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}(pqr)
) to be a triangle in the model plane
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
such that

|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{q}}|=|{p}-\penalty{q}|,\quad|{\tilde{q}}-\penalty{\tilde{r}}|=|{q}-\penalty{r}|,\quad|{\tilde{r}}-\penalty{\tilde{p}}|=|{r}-\penalty{p}|.

In the notation of Section
1A
,

{\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}(pqr)={\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}\{|{q}-\penalty{r}|,|{r}-\penalty{p}|,|{p}-\penalty{q}|\}
.

If
\upkappa\leqslant\penalty 0
, the model triangle is always defined, that is, it exists and is unique up to an isometry of
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
.
If
\upkappa>0
, the model triangle is said to be defined if in addition

|{p}-\penalty{q}|+|{q}-\penalty{r}|+|{r}-\penalty{p}|<2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}};

here
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
denotes the diameter of the model space
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
.
In this case, the model triangle also exists and is unique up to an isometry of
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
.
The value
|{p}-\penalty{q}|+|{q}-\penalty{r}|+|{r}-\penalty{p}|
will be called the
perimeter of the triple

p
,
q
,
r
.

If for
p,q,r\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
,

[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{q}}{\tilde{r}}]={\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}(pqr)
is defined
and
|{p}-\penalty{q}|,|{p}-\penalty{r}|>0
, the angle measure of

[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{q}}{\tilde{r}}]
at
\tilde{p}
will be called the
model angle
of the triple
p
,
q
,
r
, and will be denoted by

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{q}_{r}\hskip-0.86108pt)
.

In the notation of Section
1A
,

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{q}_{r}\hskip-0.86108pt)=\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}\{|{q}-\penalty{r}|;|{p}-\penalty{q}|,|{p}-\penalty{r}|\}.

6.3. Alexandrov’s lemma.

Let
p,q,r,z
be distinct points in a metric space such that
z\in\penalty\mathopen{]}pr\mathclose{[}
and

|{p}-\penalty{q}|+|{q}-\penalty{r}|+|{r}-\penalty{p}|<2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}.

Then
the following expressions have the same sign:

(a)

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{q}_{r}\hskip-0.86108pt)-\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{q}_{z}\hskip-0.86108pt)
,

(b)

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(z\,{}^{q}_{p}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(z\,{}^{q}_{r}\hskip-0.86108pt)-\uppi
.

Moreover,

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(q\,{}^{p}_{r}\hskip-0.86108pt)\geqslant\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(q\,{}^{p}_{z}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(q\,{}^{z}_{r}\hskip-0.86108pt),

with equality if and only if the expressions in
6B
and
6B
vanish.

Proof.

By the triangle inequality,

|{p}-\penalty{q}|+|{q}-\penalty{z}|+|{z}-\penalty{p}|\leqslant\penalty|{p}-\penalty{q}|+|{q}-\penalty{r}|+|{r}-\penalty{p}|<2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}.

Therefore the model triangle
[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{q}}{\tilde{z}}]={\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}pqz
is defined.
Take
a point
\tilde{r}
on the extension of

[\tilde{p}\tilde{z}]
beyond
\tilde{z}
so that
|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{r}}|=|{p}-\penalty{r}|
(and therefore
|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{z}}|=|{p}-\penalty{z}|
).

From monotonicity of the function
a\mapsto\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}\{a;b,c\}
(
1A
),
the following expressions have the same sign:

(i)

\measuredangle[\tilde{p}\,{}^{\tilde{q}}_{\tilde{r}}]-\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{q}_{r}\hskip-0.86108pt)
;

(ii)

|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{r}}|-|{p}-\penalty{r}|
;

(iii)

\measuredangle[\tilde{z}\,{}^{\tilde{q}}_{\tilde{r}}]-\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(z\,{}^{q}_{r}\hskip-0.86108pt)
.

Since

\measuredangle[\tilde{p}\,{}^{\tilde{q}}_{\tilde{r}}]=\measuredangle[\tilde{p}\,{}^{\tilde{q}}_{\tilde{z}}]=\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{q}_{z}\hskip-0.86108pt)

and

\measuredangle[\tilde{z}\,{}^{\tilde{q}}_{\tilde{r}}]=\uppi-\measuredangle[\tilde{z}\,{}^{\tilde{p}}_{\tilde{q}}]=\uppi-\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(z\,{}^{p}_{q}\hskip-0.86108pt),

the first statement follows.

For the second statement, let us redifine
\tilde{r}
;
construct
[{\tilde{q}}{\tilde{z}}{\tilde{r}}]={\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}qzr
on the opposite side of
[\tilde{q}\tilde{z}]
from
[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{q}}{\tilde{z}}]
.
Since

\displaystyle|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{r}}|

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{z}}|+|{\tilde{z}}-\penalty{\tilde{r}}|=

\displaystyle=|{p}-\penalty{z}|+|{z}-\penalty{r}|=

\displaystyle=|{p}-\penalty{r}|,

we have

\displaystyle\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(q\,{}^{p}_{z}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(q\,{}^{z}_{r}\hskip-0.86108pt)

\displaystyle=\measuredangle[\tilde{q}\,{}^{\tilde{p}}_{\tilde{z}}]+\measuredangle[\tilde{q}\,{}^{\tilde{z}}_{\tilde{r}}]=

\displaystyle=\measuredangle[\tilde{q}\,{}^{\tilde{p}}_{\tilde{r}}]\leqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(q\,{}^{p}_{r}\hskip-0.86108pt).

Equality holds if and only if
|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{r}}|=|{p}-\penalty{r}|
,
as required.∎

C
Angles and the first variation

Given a hinge
[p\,{}^{x}_{y}]
, we define its
angle
to be

\measuredangle[p\,{}^{x}_{y}]:=\lim_{\bar{x},\bar{y}\to\penalty p}\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{\bar{x}}_{\bar{y}}\hskip-0.86108pt),

➊

for
\bar{x}\in\penalty\mathopen{]}px]
and
\bar{y}\in\penalty\mathopen{]}py]
, if this limit exists.

Similarly to
\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{x}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt)
,
we will use the short notation

\mathop{\tilde{\curlyvee}}\nolimits^{\upkappa}[p\,{}^{x}_{y}]=\mathop{\tilde{\curlyvee}}\nolimits^{\upkappa}\left\{\measuredangle[p\,{}^{x}_{y}];|{p}-\penalty{x}|,|{p}-\penalty{y}|\right\},

where the right-hand side is defined in Section
1A
. The value
\mathop{\tilde{\curlyvee}}\nolimits^{\upkappa}[p\,{}^{x}_{y}]
will be called the
model side

of the hinge
[p\,{}^{x}_{y}]
.

6.4. Lemma.

Let
p,x,y
be a triple of points in a metric space with perimeter
\ell
.
Then for any
\upkappa,\mathrm{K}\in\penalty\mathbb{R}
,

|\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\mathrm{K}}(p\,{}^{x}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt)-\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{x}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt)|\leqslant\penalty 100(|\mathrm{K}|+|\upkappa|){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell^{2},

[formula]

whenever the left-hand side is defined.

Lemma
6C
implies that
the definition of angle is independent of
\upkappa
.
In particular, one can take
\upkappa=0
in
➊
;
thus the angle can be calculated from the cosine law:

\cos\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{0}(p\,{}^{x}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt)=\frac{|{p}-\penalty{x}|^{2}+|{p}-\penalty{y}|^{2}-|{x}-\penalty{y}|^{2}}{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{p}-\penalty{x}|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{p}-\penalty{y}|}.

Proof.

The function
\upkappa\mapsto\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{x}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt)
is nondecreasing (
1A
).
Thus, for
\mathrm{K}>\upkappa
, we have

\displaystyle 0\leqslant\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\mathrm{K}}(p\,{}^{x}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt)-\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{x}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt)

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\mathrm{K}}(p\,{}^{x}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\mathrm{K}}(x\,{}^{p}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\mathrm{K}}(y\,{}^{p}_{x}\hskip-0.86108pt)-

\displaystyle\quad-\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{x}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt)-\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{p}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt)-\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(y\,{}^{p}_{x}\hskip-0.86108pt)=

\displaystyle=\mathrm{K}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits{\tilde{\triangle}}^{\mathrm{K}}(pxy)-\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits{\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}(pxy).

Note that for
\upkappa\geqslant\penalty 0
a triangle of perimeter
\ell
in
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
lies in a ball of radius
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell
, which easily implies that
\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits{\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}(pxy)\leqslant\penalty 100{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell^{2}
.
For
\upkappa<0
one gets the same estimate by a direct computation in the hyperbolic plane.

Therefore

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits{\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}(pxy)

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty 100{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell^{2},

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits{\tilde{\triangle}}^{\mathrm{K}}(pxy)

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty 100{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell^{2}.

Thus

➋
,

follows.
∎

6.5. Triangle inequality for angles.

Let
[px^{1}]
,
[px^{2}]
, and
[px^{3}]
be three geodesics in a metric space.
If all of the angles
\upalpha^{ij}=\measuredangle[p\,{}^{x^{i}}_{x^{j}}]
are defined then they satisfy the triangle inequality:

\upalpha^{13}\leqslant\penalty\upalpha^{12}+\upalpha^{23}.

Proof.

Since
\upalpha^{13}\leqslant\penalty\uppi
, we can assume that
\upalpha^{12}+\upalpha^{23}<\uppi
.
Set
\upgamma^{i}=\penalty\mathrm{geod}_{[px^{i}]}
.
Given any
\upvarepsilon>0
, for all sufficiently small
t,\uptau,s\in\penalty\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant\penalty 0}
we have

\displaystyle|{\upgamma^{1}(t)}-\penalty{\upgamma^{3}(\uptau)}|

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty|{\upgamma^{1}(t)}-\penalty{\upgamma^{2}(s)}|+|{\upgamma^{2}(s)}-\penalty{\upgamma^{3}(\uptau)}|<

\displaystyle<\sqrt{t^{2}+s^{2}-2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos(\upalpha^{12}+\upvarepsilon)}+

\displaystyle\quad+\sqrt{s^{2}+\uptau^{2}-2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uptau{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos(\upalpha^{23}+\upvarepsilon)}\leqslant\penalty

Below we define

s(t,\uptau)
so that for

s=s(t,\uptau)
, this chain of inequalities can be continued as follows:

\leqslant\penalty\sqrt{t^{2}+\uptau^{2}-2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uptau{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos(\upalpha^{12}+\upalpha^{23}+2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon)}.

Thus for any
\upvarepsilon>0
,

\upalpha^{13}\leqslant\penalty\upalpha^{12}+\upalpha^{23}+2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon.

Hence the result follows.

To define
s(t,\uptau)
, consider three half-lines
\tilde{\upgamma}^{1}
,
\tilde{\upgamma}^{2}
,
\tilde{\upgamma}^{3}
on a Euclidean plane starting at one point, such that

\measuredangle(\tilde{\upgamma}^{1},\tilde{\upgamma}^{2})=\penalty\upalpha^{12}+\upvarepsilon
,

\measuredangle(\tilde{\upgamma}^{2},\tilde{\upgamma}^{3})=\penalty\upalpha^{23}+\upvarepsilon
,
and
\measuredangle(\tilde{\upgamma}^{1},\tilde{\upgamma}^{3})=\penalty\upalpha^{12}+\penalty\upalpha^{23}+\penalty 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon
.
We parametrize each half-line by the distance from the starting point.
Given two positive numbers
t,\uptau\in\penalty\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant\penalty 0}
, let
s=s(t,\uptau)
be
the number such that

\tilde{\upgamma}^{2}(s)\in\penalty[\tilde{\upgamma}^{1}(t)\ \tilde{\upgamma}^{3}(\uptau)]
.
Clearly
s\leqslant\penalty\max\{t,\uptau\}
, so
t,\uptau,s
may be taken sufficiently small.
∎

6.6. Exercise.

Prove that the sum of adjacent angles is at least
\uppi
.

More precisely,
suppose that the hinges
[p\,{}^{x}_{z}]
and
[p\,{}^{y}_{z}]
are
adjacent
;
that is, the side
[pz]
is shared and the union of two sides
[px]
and
[py]
is a geodesic
[xy]
.
Then

\measuredangle[p\,{}^{x}_{z}]+\measuredangle[p\,{}^{y}_{z}]\geqslant\penalty\uppi

whenever each angle on the left-hand side is defined.

The above inequality can be strict.
For example in a metric tree angles between any two different edges coming out of the same vertex are all equal to
\uppi
.

6.7. First variation inequality.

Assume that for a hinge
[q\,{}^{p}_{x}]
,
the angle
\upalpha=\measuredangle[q\,{}^{p}_{x}]
is defined. Then

|{p}-\penalty{\mathrm{geod}_{[qx]}(t)}|\leqslant\penalty|{q}-\penalty{p}|-t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\upalpha+o(t).

Proof.

Take a sufficiently small
\upvarepsilon>0
.
For all sufficiently small
t>0
, we have

\displaystyle|{\mathrm{geod}_{[qp]}(t/\upvarepsilon)}-\penalty{\mathrm{geod}_{[qx]}(t)}|

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty\tfrac{t}{\upvarepsilon}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sqrt{1+\upvarepsilon^{2}-2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\upalpha}+o(t)\leqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty\tfrac{t}{\upvarepsilon}-t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\upalpha+t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon.

Applying the triangle inequality, we get

\displaystyle|{p}-\penalty{\mathrm{geod}_{[qx]}(t)}|

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty|{p}-\penalty{\mathrm{geod}_{[qp]}(t/\upvarepsilon)}|+|{\mathrm{geod}_{[qp]}(t/\upvarepsilon)}-\penalty{\mathrm{geod}_{[qx]}(t)}|\leqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty|{p}-\penalty{q}|-t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\upalpha+t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon

for any
\upvarepsilon>0
and all sufficiently small
t
.
Hence the result.
∎

D
Space of directions

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a metric space.
If the angle
\measuredangle[p\,{}^{x}_{y}]
is defined for any hinge
[p\,{}^{x}_{y}]
in
\mathcal{X}
,
then we will say that the space
\mathcal{X}
has
defined angles
.

Let us note that this is a strong condition. For example a Banach space which has defined angles must be Hilbert.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a space with defined angles. For
p\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
,
consider the set
\mathfrak{S}_{p}

of all nontrivial unit-speed geodesics starting at
p
.
By
6C
, the triangle inequality holds for
\measuredangle
on
\mathfrak{S}_{p}
,
that is,
(\mathfrak{S}_{p},\measuredangle)

forms a pseudometric space.

The metric space corresponding to
(\mathfrak{S}_{p},\measuredangle)
is called the
space of geodesic directions
at
p
, denoted by
\Sigma^{\prime}_{p}
or
\Sigma^{\prime}_{p}\mathcal{X}
.
The elements of
\Sigma^{\prime}_{p}
are called
geodesic directions
at
p
.
Each geodesic direction is formed by an equivalence class of geodesics starting from
p

for the equivalence relation

[px]\sim[py]\quad\iff\quad\measuredangle[p\,{}^{x}_{y}]=0;

the direction of
[px]
is denoted by
{\uparrow}_{[{p}{x}]}
.

The completion of
\Sigma^{\prime}_{p}
is called the
space of directions
at
p
and is denoted by
\Sigma_{p}
or
\Sigma_{p}\mathcal{X}
.
The elements of
\Sigma_{p}
are called
directions
at
p
.

E
Tangent space

The
Euclidean cone

\mathcal{Y}=\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits\mathcal{X}

over a metric space
\mathcal{X}

is defined as the metric space whose underlying set consists of
equivalence classes in

[0,\infty)\times\penalty\mathcal{X}
with the equivalence relation “
\sim
” given by
(0,p)\sim(0,q)
for any points
p,q\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
,
and whose metric is given by the cosine rule

|{(s,p)}-\penalty{(t,q)}|_{\mathcal{Y}}=\sqrt{s^{2}+t^{2}-2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\upvartheta},

where
\upvartheta=\min\{\uppi,|{p}-\penalty{q}|_{\mathcal{X}}\}
.
The point in
\mathcal{Y}
that corresponds
(t,x)\in\penalty[0,\infty)\times\penalty\mathcal{X}
will be denoted by
t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x
.

The point in
\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits{\mathcal{X}}
formed by the equivalence class of
\{0\}\times\penalty\mathcal{X}
is called the
tip of the cone
and is denoted by
0
or
0_{\mathcal{Y}}
.
For
v\in\penalty\mathcal{Y}
the distance
|{0}-\penalty{v}|_{\mathcal{Y}}
is called the norm of
v
and is denoted by
|v|
or
|v|_{\mathcal{Y}}
.

The
scalar product

\langle v,w\rangle

of two vectors
v=s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}p
and
w=t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}q

is defined by

\langle v,w\rangle:=|v|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|w|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\upvartheta;

we set
\langle v,w\rangle:=0
if
v=0
or
w=0
.

6.8. Example.

\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits\mathbb{S}^{n}
is isometric to
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.
If
G<\mathrm{O}(n+1)
is a closed subgroup, then
\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits(\mathbb{S}^{n}/G)
is isometric to
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}/G
.

The Euclidean cone
\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits\Sigma_{p}
over the space of directions
\Sigma_{p}
is called the
tangent space
at
p
and denoted by
\text{\rm T}_{p}
or
\text{\rm T}_{p}\mathcal{X}
.
The elements of
\text{\rm T}_{p}\mathcal{X}
will be called
tangent vectors
at
p

(despite the fact that
\text{\rm T}_{p}
is only a cone — not a vector space).

The tangent space
\text{\rm T}_{p}
could be also defined directly, without introducing the space of directions.
To do so, consider the set
\mathfrak{T}_{p}
of all geodesics starting at
p
, with arbitrary speed.
Given
\upalpha,\upbeta\in\penalty\mathfrak{T}_{p}
,
set

|{\upalpha}-\penalty{\upbeta}|_{\mathfrak{T}_{p}}=\lim_{\upvarepsilon\to\penalty 0}\frac{|{\upalpha(\upvarepsilon)}-\penalty{\upbeta(\upvarepsilon)}|_{\mathcal{X}}}{\upvarepsilon}.

[formula]

If the angles in
\mathcal{X}
are defined, then so is
the limit in

➊
.

, and we obtain a pseudometric on
\mathfrak{T}_{p}
.

The corresponding metric space admits a natural isometric identification with the cone
\text{\rm T}^{\prime}_{p}=\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits\Sigma^{\prime}_{p}
.
The vectors of
\text{\rm T}^{\prime}_{p}
are the equivalence classes for the relation

\upalpha\sim\upbeta\quad\iff\quad|{\upalpha(t)}-\penalty{\upbeta(t)}|_{\mathcal{X}}=o(t).

The completion of
\text{\rm T}^{\prime}_{p}
is therefore naturally isometric to
\text{\rm T}_{p}
.
A vector in
\text{\rm T}^{\prime}_{p}
that corresponds to the geodesic path
\mathrm{geod}_{[pq]}
is called
logarithm of
[pq]
and denoted by
\mathrm{log}[{p}{q}]
.

F
Velocity of curves

6.9. Definition.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a metric space,

a>0
,
and
\upalpha\colon[0,a)\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
be a function, not necessarily continuous, such that
\upalpha(0)=p
.
We say that
v\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}
is the
right derivative
of
\upalpha
at
0
,
briefly
\upalpha^{+}(0)=\penalty v
, if for some (and therefore any) sequence of vectors
v_{n}\in\penalty\text{\rm T}^{\prime}_{p}
such that
v_{n}\to\penalty v
as
n\to\penalty\infty
,
and corresponding geodesics
\upgamma_{n}
,
we have

\varlimsup_{\upvarepsilon\to\penalty 0+}\frac{|{\upalpha(\upvarepsilon)}-\penalty{\upgamma_{n}(\upvarepsilon)}|_{\mathcal{X}}}{\upvarepsilon}\to\penalty 0\quad\text{as}\quad n\to\penalty\infty.

We define right and left derivatives
\upalpha^{+}(t_{0})
and
\upalpha^{-}(t_{0})

of
\upalpha
at
t_{0}\in\penalty\mathbb{I}
by

\upalpha^{\pm}(t_{0})=\check{\upalpha}^{+}(0),

where
\check{\upalpha}(t)=\upalpha(t_{0}\pm t)
.

The sign convention is not quite standard; if
\upalpha
is a smooth curve in a Riemannian manifold then we have

\upalpha^{+}(t)=-\upalpha^{-}(t)
.

Note that if
\upgamma
is a geodesic starting at
p

and the tangent vector
v\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}^{\prime}
corresponds to
\upgamma
,
then
\upgamma^{+}(0)=v
.

6.10. Exercise.

Assume
\mathcal{X}
is a metric space with defined angles,
and let
\upalpha,\upbeta\colon[0,a)\to\penalty\mathcal{X}

be two maps such that the right derivatives
\upalpha^{+}(0)
,
\upbeta^{+}(0)
are defined and
\upalpha^{+}(0)=\upbeta^{+}(0)
.
Show that

|{\upalpha(t)}-\penalty{\upbeta(t)}|_{\mathcal{X}}=o(t).

6.11. Proposition.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a metric space with defined angles and
p\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
.
Then for any tangent vector
v\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}\mathcal{X}
there is a map
\upalpha\colon[0,\upvarepsilon)\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
such that
\upalpha^{+}(0)=v
.

Proof.

If
v\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}^{\prime}
, then for the corresponding geodesic
\upalpha
we have
\upalpha^{+}(0)=\penalty v
.

Given
v\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}
, construct a sequence
v_{n}\in\penalty\text{\rm T}^{\prime}_{p}

such that
v_{n}\to\penalty v
, and let
\upgamma_{n}
be a sequence of corresponding geodesics.

The needed map
\upalpha
can be found among the maps such that
\upalpha(0)=\penalty p
and

\upalpha(t)=\upgamma_{n}(t)\quad\text{if}\quad\upvarepsilon_{n+1}\leqslant\penalty t<\upvarepsilon_{n},

where
\upvarepsilon_{n}

is a decreasing sequence converging to
0
as
n\to\penalty\infty
.
In order to satisfy the conclusion of the proposition, one has to choose the sequence
\upvarepsilon_{n}
converging to
0
very fast.
Note that in this construction
\upalpha
is not continuous.
∎

6.12. Definition.

Let

\mathcal{X}
be a metric space
and
\upalpha\colon\mathbb{I}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
be a curve.

For
t_{0}\in\penalty\mathbb{I}
,
if
\upalpha^{+}(t_{0})
or
\upalpha^{-}(t_{0})
or both are defined,
we say respectively that
\upalpha
is

right
or
left
or
both-sided differentiable
at
t_{0}
.
In the exceptional cases where
t_{0}
is the left (respectively right) end of
\mathbb{I}
,
\upalpha
is by definition left (respectively right) differentiable at
t_{0}
.

If
\upalpha
is both-sided differentiable at
t
, and

|\upalpha^{+}(t)|=|\upalpha^{-}(t)|=\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{\upalpha^{+}(t)}-\penalty{\upalpha^{-}(t)}|_{\text{\rm T}_{\upalpha(t)}},

then we say that
\upalpha
is
differentiable
at
t
.

6.13. Exercise.

Assume
\mathcal{X}
is a metric space with defined angles.
Show that any geodesic
\upgamma\colon\mathbb{I}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
is differentiable everywhere.

Recall that the speed of a curve is defined in
3D
.

6.14. Exercise.

Let
\upalpha
be a curve in a metric space with defined angles.
Suppose that
\mathop{\rm speed}\nolimits_{t}\upalpha
,
\upalpha^{+}(t)
, and
\upalpha^{-}(t)
are defined.

Show that
\upalpha
is differentiable at
t
.

G
Differential

6.15. Definition.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a metric space with defined angles, and

f\colon\mathcal{X}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a subfunction.
For
p\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
, a function
\upvarphi\colon\text{\rm T}_{p}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
is called the
differential
of
f
at
p

(briefly
\upvarphi=\bm{d}_{p}f
) if for any map
\upalpha\colon\mathbb{I}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
such that
\mathbb{I}
is a real interval,
\upalpha(0)=p
, and
\upalpha^{+}(0)
is defined, we have

(f\circ\penalty\upalpha)^{+}(0)=\upvarphi(\upalpha^{+}(0)).

6.16. Proposition.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{X}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a locally Lipschitz semiconcave subfunction
on a metric space
\mathcal{X}
with defined angles.
Then the differential
\bm{d}_{p}f
is uniquely defined for any
p\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
. Moreover,

(a)

The differential
\bm{d}_{p}f\colon\text{\rm T}_{p}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
is Lipschitz and

\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits\bm{d}_{p}f\leqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm lip}\nolimits_{p}f;

that is, the Lipschitz constant of
\bm{d}_{p}f
does not exceed the Lipschitz constant of
f
in any neighborhood of
p
.

(b)

\bm{d}_{p}f\colon\text{\rm T}_{p}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
is a positive homogeneous function;
that is, for any
r\geqslant\penalty 0
and
v\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}
we have

r{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\bm{d}_{p}f(v)=\bm{d}_{p}f(r{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}v).

(c)

The differential
\bm{d}_{p}f\colon\text{\rm T}_{p}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
is the restriction of ultradifferential defined in Section
6I
;
that is,

\bm{d}_{p}f=\bm{d}^{\text{\rm}}_{p}f|_{\text{\rm T}_{p}}.

Proof.

Passing to a subdomain of
f
if necessary,
we can assume that
f
is
{\ell}
-Lipschitz and
\uplambda
-concave for some
{\ell},\uplambda\in\penalty\mathbb{R}
.

Take a geodesic
\upgamma
in
\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
starting at
p
.
Since
f\circ\penalty\upgamma
is
\uplambda
-concave,
the right derivative
(f\circ\penalty\upgamma)^{+}(0)
is defined.
Since
f
is
{\ell}
-Lipschitz, we have

|(f\circ\penalty\upgamma)^{+}(0)-(f\circ\penalty\upgamma_{1})^{+}(0)|\leqslant\penalty{\ell}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{\upgamma^{+}(0)}-\penalty{\upgamma_{1}^{+}(0)}|

➊

for any other geodesic
\upgamma_{1}
starting at
p
.

Define
\upvarphi\colon\text{\rm T}^{\prime}_{p}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}\colon\upgamma^{+}(0)\mapsto\penalty(f\circ\penalty\upgamma)^{+}(0)
.
From
➊
,
\upvarphi
is an
{\ell}
-Lipschtz function defined on
\text{\rm T}_{p}^{\prime}
.
Thus we can extend
\upvarphi
to all of
\text{\rm T}_{p}
as an
{\ell}
-Lipschitz function.

It remains to check that
\upvarphi
is the differential of
f
at
p
.
Assume
\upalpha\colon[0,a)\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
is a map such that
\upalpha(0)=p
and
\upalpha^{+}(0)=v\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}
.
Let
\upgamma_{n}\in\penalty\Gamma_{p}
be a sequence of geodesics as in the definition
6F
;
that is, if
