\measuredangle[p_{n}\,{}^{x_{n}}_{y_{n}}]\geqslant\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p_{n}\,{}^{\bar{x}_{n}}_{\bar{y}_{n}}\hskip-0.86108pt)
.
Hence the result.
∎

8.41. Angle continuity.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{n}
be a sequence of complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
spaces,
and
\mathcal{L}_{n}\to\penalty\mathcal{L}_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.
Assume that sequences of points
p_{n},x_{n},y_{n}
in
\mathcal{L}_{n}

converge to points
p_{\text{\rm}},x_{\text{\rm}},y_{\text{\rm}}
in
\mathcal{L}_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
,
and the following two conditions hold:

(a)

p_{\text{\rm}}\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Str}\nolimits(x_{\text{\rm}})
,

(b)

p_{\text{\rm}}\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Str}\nolimits(y_{\text{\rm}})
or
y_{\text{\rm}}\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Str}\nolimits(p_{\text{\rm}})
.

Then

\measuredangle[p_{\text{\rm}}\,{}^{x_{\text{\rm}}}_{y_{\text{\rm}}}]=\lim_{n\to\penalty\text{\rm}}\measuredangle[p_{n}\,{}^{x_{n}}_{y_{n}}].

Proof.

By Corollary
8F
,
we may assume that
\upkappa\leqslant\penalty 0
.

By Plaut’s theorem (
8B
),
the hinge

[p_{\text{\rm}}\,{}^{x_{\text{\rm}}}_{y_{\text{\rm}}}]

is uniquely defined.
Therefore the hinges

[p_{n}\,{}^{x_{n}}_{y_{n}}]

converge to

[p_{\text{\rm}}\,{}^{x_{\text{\rm}}}_{y_{\text{\rm}}}]

as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.
Hence by the angle semicontinuity (
8G
),
we have

\measuredangle[p_{\text{\rm}}\,{}^{x_{\text{\rm}}}_{y_{\text{\rm}}}]\leqslant\penalty\lim_{n\to\penalty\text{\rm}}\measuredangle[p_{n}\,{}^{x_{n}}_{y_{n}}].

It remains to show that

\measuredangle[p_{\text{\rm}}\,{}^{x_{\text{\rm}}}_{y_{\text{\rm}}}]\geqslant\penalty\lim_{n\to\penalty\text{\rm}}\measuredangle[p_{n}\,{}^{x_{n}}_{y_{n}}].

➊

Fix
\upvarepsilon>0
.
Since
p_{\text{\rm}}\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Str}\nolimits(x_{\text{\rm}})
,
there is a point
q_{\text{\rm}}\in\penalty\mathcal{L}_{\text{\rm}}

such that

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p_{\text{\rm}}\,{}^{x_{\text{\rm}}}_{q_{\text{\rm}}}\hskip-0.86108pt)>\uppi-\upvarepsilon.

The hinge comparison (
8C
) implies that

\measuredangle[p_{\text{\rm}}\,{}^{x_{\text{\rm}}}_{q_{\text{\rm}}}]>\uppi-\upvarepsilon.

➋

By the triangle inequality for angles (
6C
),

\displaystyle\measuredangle[p_{\text{\rm}}\,{}^{x_{\text{\rm}}}_{y_{\text{\rm}}}]

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty\measuredangle[p_{\text{\rm}}\,{}^{x_{\text{\rm}}}_{q_{\text{\rm}}}]-\measuredangle[p_{\text{\rm}}\,{}^{y_{\text{\rm}}}_{q_{\text{\rm}}}]>

➌

\displaystyle>\uppi-\upvarepsilon-\measuredangle[p_{\text{\rm}}\,{}^{y_{\text{\rm}}}_{q_{\text{\rm}}}].

Note that we can assume in addition that
q_{\text{\rm}}\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Str}\nolimits(p_{\text{\rm}})
.
Choose
q_{n}\in\penalty\mathcal{L}_{n}

such that
q_{n}\to\penalty q_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.
Note that by angle semicontinuity
we again have

\displaystyle\measuredangle[p_{\text{\rm}}\,{}^{x_{\text{\rm}}}_{q_{\text{\rm}}}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty\lim_{n\to\penalty\text{\rm}}\measuredangle[p_{n}\,{}^{x_{n}}_{q_{n}}],

➍

\displaystyle\measuredangle[p_{\text{\rm}}\,{}^{y_{\text{\rm}}}_{q_{\text{\rm}}}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty\lim_{n\to\penalty\text{\rm}}\measuredangle[p_{n}\,{}^{y_{n}}_{q_{n}}].

By the
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
comparison (
8A
) and
8C
,

\measuredangle[p_{n}\,{}^{x_{n}}_{y_{n}}]+\measuredangle[p_{n}\,{}^{y_{n}}_{q_{n}}]+\measuredangle[p_{n}\,{}^{x_{n}}_{q_{n}}]\leqslant\penalty 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi

for all
n
.
Together with
➍
,
➋
and
➌
,
this implies

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\penalty\text{\rm}}\measuredangle[p_{n}\,{}^{x_{n}}_{y_{n}}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi-\lim_{n\to\penalty\text{\rm}}\measuredangle[p_{n}\,{}^{x_{n}}_{q_{n}}]-\lim_{n\to\penalty\text{\rm}}\measuredangle[p_{n}\,{}^{y_{n}}_{q_{n}}]\leqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi-\measuredangle[p_{\text{\rm}}\,{}^{x_{\text{\rm}}}_{q_{\text{\rm}}}]-\measuredangle[p_{\text{\rm}}\,{}^{y_{\text{\rm}}}_{q_{\text{\rm}}}]<

\displaystyle<\measuredangle[p_{\text{\rm}}\,{}^{x_{\text{\rm}}}_{y_{\text{\rm}}}]+2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon.

Since
\upvarepsilon>0
is arbitrary,
➊
follows.
∎

8.42. First variation formula.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
CBB
space.
For any point
q
and any geodesic
[px]
in
\mathcal{L}
with
p\neq q
, we have

|{q}-\penalty{\mathrm{geod}_{[px]}(t)}|=|{q}-\penalty{p}|-t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\upvarphi+o(t),

➎

where
\upvarphi
is the infimum of angles between
[px]
and all geodesics from
p
to
q
in the ultrapower
\mathcal{L}^{\text{\rm}}
.

Remark.

If
\mathcal{L}
is a proper space, then
\mathcal{L}^{\text{\rm}}=\mathcal{L}
; see Section
4B
.
Therefore the infimum
\upvarphi
is achieved on a particular geodesic from
p
to
q
.

As a corollary we obtain the following classical result:

8.43. Strong angle lemma.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
CBB
space and
p\neq q\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
be such that there is unique geodesic from
p
to
q
in the ultrapower
\mathcal{L}^{\text{\rm}}
.
Then for any hinge
[p\,{}^{q}_{x}]
we have

\measuredangle[p\,{}^{q}_{x}]=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bar{x}\to\penalty p\\
\bar{x}\in\penalty\,\mathopen{]}px]\end{subarray}}\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{q}_{\bar{x}}\hskip-0.86108pt)

➏

for any
\upkappa\in\penalty\mathbb{R}
such that
|{p}-\penalty{q}|<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
.

In particular,
➏
holds if
p\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Str}\nolimits(q)
as well as if
q\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Str}\nolimits(p)
.

Remark.

\diamond

The above lemma is essentially due to Alexandrov.
The right-hand side in
➏
is called the
strong angle
of the hinge
[p\,{}^{q}_{x}]
.
Note that in a general metric space the angle and the strong angle of the same hinge might differ.

\diamond

As follows from Corollary
4C
,
if there is a unique geodesic
[pq]
in the ultrapower
\mathcal{L}^{\text{\rm}}
, then
[pq]
lies in
\mathcal{L}
.

Proof of
8G
.

The first statement follows directly from the first variation formula (
8G
)
and the definition of model angle (see Section
6C
).

The second statement follows from Plaut’s theorem (
8B
) applied to
\mathcal{L}^{\text{\rm}}
.
(Note that according to Proposition
8A
,
\mathcal{L}^{\text{\rm}}
is a complete length
CBB
space.)
∎

Proof of
8G
.

By Corollary
8F
, we can assume that
\upkappa\leqslant\penalty 0
.
The inequality

|{q}-\penalty{\mathrm{geod}_{[px]}(t)}|\leqslant\penalty|{q}-\penalty{p}|-t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\upvarphi+o(t)

follows from the first variation inequality (
6C
).
Thus, it is sufficient to show that

|{q}-\penalty{\mathrm{geod}_{[px]}(t)}|\geqslant\penalty|{q}-\penalty{p}|-t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\upvarphi+o(t).

Assume the contrary. Then there is
\upvarepsilon>0
such that
\upvarphi+\upvarepsilon<\uppi
,
and for a sequence
t_{n}\to\penalty 0+
we have

|{q}-\penalty{\mathrm{geod}_{[px]}(t_{n})}|<|{q}-\penalty{p}|-t_{n}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos(\upvarphi-\upvarepsilon).

➐

Let
x_{n}=\mathrm{geod}_{[px]}(t_{n})
.
Clearly

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x_{n}\,{}^{p}_{q}\hskip-0.86108pt)>\uppi-\upvarphi+\tfrac{\upvarepsilon}{2}

for all large
n
.

Assume
\mathcal{L}
is geodesic.
Choose a sequence of geodesics
[x_{n}q]
.
Let
[x_{n}q]\to\penalty[pq]_{\mathcal{L}^{\text{\rm}}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
(in general
[pq]
might lie in
\mathcal{L}^{\text{\rm}}
).
Applying both parts of hinge comparison (
8C
),
we have
\measuredangle[x_{n}\,{}^{q}_{x}]<\penalty\upvarphi-\tfrac{\upvarepsilon}{2}
for all large
n
.
According to
8G
, the angle between
[pq]
and
[px]
is at most
\upvarphi-\tfrac{\upvarepsilon}{2}
, a contradiction.

Finally, if
\mathcal{L}
is not geodesic, choose a sequence
q_{n}\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Str}\nolimits(x_{n})
, such that
q_{n}\to\penalty q
and the inequality

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x_{n}\,{}^{p}_{q_{n}}\hskip-0.86108pt)>\penalty\uppi-\upvarphi+\tfrac{\upvarepsilon}{2}

still holds.
Then the same argument as above shows that
[x_{n}q_{n}]

-converges to a geodesic
[pq]_{\mathcal{L}^{\text{\rm}}}
from
p
to
q
having angle at most
\upvarphi-\tfrac{\upvarepsilon}{2}
with
[px]
.
∎

H
On positive lower bound

In this section we consider
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
spaces for
\upkappa>0
.
Applying rescaling we can assume that
\upkappa=1
.

The following theorem states that if one ignores a few exceptional spaces, then the diameter of a space with positive lower curvature bound is bounded.
Many authors (but not us) exclude these spaces in the definition of Alexandrov space with positive lower curvature bound.

8.44. On diameter of a space.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({1})
space.
Then either

(a)

\mathop{\rm diam}\nolimits\mathcal{L}\leqslant\penalty\uppi
;

(b)

or
\mathcal{L}
is isometric to one of the following exceptional spaces:

1.

real line
\mathbb{R}
,

2.

a half-line
\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant\penalty 0}
,

3.

a closed interval
[0,a]\in\penalty\mathbb{R}
,
a>\uppi
,

4.

a circle
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{a}
of length
a>2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi
.

Proof.

Assume that
\mathcal{L}
is a geodesic space and
\mathop{\rm diam}\nolimits\mathcal{L}>\uppi
.
Choose
x,y\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
so that
|{x}-\penalty{y}|=\uppi+\upvarepsilon
,
0<\upvarepsilon<\tfrac{\uppi}{4}
.
By moving
y
slightly, we can also assume that the geodesic
[xy]
is unique;
to prove this, use either Plaut’s theorem (
8B
)
or the fact that geodesics do not split (
8G
).
Let
z
be the midpoint of the geodesic
[xy]
.

Consider the function
f=\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{x}{}{}+\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{y}{}{}
.
As follows from Lemma
8H
,

f
is concave in
\mathrm{B}{}(z,\tfrac{\upvarepsilon}{4})
.
Let
p\in\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(z,\tfrac{\upvarepsilon}{4})
.
Choose a geodesic
[zp]
,
and let
h(t)=f\circ\penalty\mathrm{geod}_{[zp]}(t)
and
\ell=|{z}-\penalty{p}|
.
Clearly
h
is concave.
From the adjacent angle lemma (
8G
), we have
h^{+}(0)=0
.
Therefore
h
is nonincreasing which means that

|{x}-\penalty{p}|+|{y}-\penalty{p}|=h(\ell)\leqslant\penalty h(0)=|{x}-\penalty{y}|.

Since the geodesic
[xy]
is unique this means that
p\in\penalty[xy]
, and hence
\mathrm{B}{}(z,\tfrac{\upvarepsilon}{4})
only contains points of
[xy]
.

Since in
CBB
spaces, geodesics do not bifurcate (
8G
),
it follows that all of
\mathcal{L}
coincides with the maximal extension of
[xy]
as a local geodesic
\upgamma

(which might not be minimizing).
In other words,
\mathcal{L}
is isometric to a 1-dimensional Riemannian manifold with possibly nonempty boundary.
From this, it is easy to see that
\mathcal{L}
falls into one of the exceptional spaces described in the theorem.

Lastly, if
\mathcal{L}
is not geodesic and
\mathop{\rm diam}\nolimits\mathcal{L}>\uppi
, then the above argument applied to
\mathcal{L}^{\text{\rm}}
yields that each metric component of
\mathcal{L}^{\text{\rm}}
is isometric to one of the exceptional spaces.
As all of those spaces are proper,
\mathcal{L}
is a metric component in
\mathcal{L}^{\text{\rm}}
.
∎

8.45. Lemma.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({1})
space and
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
.
Then
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}{}{}\colon\mathcal{L}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
is concave in
\mathrm{B}{}(p,\uppi)\setminus\mathrm{B}{}(p,\tfrac{\uppi}{2})
.

In particular, if
\mathop{\rm diam}\nolimits\mathcal{L}\leqslant\penalty\uppi
,
then the complements
\mathcal{L}\setminus\mathrm{B}{}(p,r)
and
\mathcal{L}\setminus\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[p,r]
are convex for any
r>\tfrac{\uppi}{2}
.

Proof.

This is a consequence of
8D
.
∎

8.46. Exercise.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({1})
space that is not exceptional (that is,
\mathop{\rm diam}\nolimits\mathcal{L}\leqslant\penalty\uppi
).
Assume that a group
G
acts on
\mathcal{L}
by isometries, has closed orbits, and

\mathop{\rm diam}\nolimits(\mathcal{L}/G)>\tfrac{\uppi}{2}.

Show that the action of
G
has a fixed point in
\mathcal{L}
.

8.47. Advanced exercise.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({1})
space
Show that
\mathcal{L}
contains at most 3 points with space of directions
\leqslant\penalty\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathbb{S}^{n}

(see Definition
5D
).

8.48. On perimeter of a triple.

Suppose

\mathcal{L}
is a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({1})
space
and
\mathop{\rm diam}\nolimits\mathcal{L}\leqslant\penalty\uppi
.
Then the perimeter of any triple of points
p,q,r\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
is at most
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi
.

Proof.

Arguing by contradiction, suppose

|{p}-\penalty{q}|+|{q}-\penalty{r}|+|{r}-\penalty{p}|>2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi

➊

for
p,q,r\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
.
Rescaling the space slightly, we can assume that
\mathop{\rm diam}\nolimits\mathcal{L}<\uppi
,
but the inequality
➊
still holds.
By Corollary
8F
,
after rescaling
\mathcal{L}
is still
\text{\rm CBB}({1})
.

Since
\mathcal{L}
is G-delta geodesic (
8B
), it is sufficient to consider the case when there is a geodesic
[qr]
.

First note that since
\mathop{\rm diam}\nolimits\mathcal{L}<\uppi
, by
8D
the function

y(t)=\mathrm{md}^{1}|{p}-\penalty{\mathrm{geod}_{[qr]}(t)}|

satisfies the differential inequality
y^{\prime\prime}\leqslant\penalty 1-y
.

Take
z_{0}\in\penalty[qr]
so that the restriction
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}{}{}|_{[qr]}
attains its maximum at
z_{0}
,
and set
t_{0}=|{q}-\penalty{z_{0}}|
so
z_{0}=\mathrm{geod}_{[qr]}(t_{0})
.
Consider the following model configuration: two geodesics
[\tilde{p}\tilde{z}_{0}]
,
[\tilde{q}\tilde{r}]
in
\mathbb{S}^{2}
such that

\displaystyle|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{z}_{0}}|

\displaystyle=|{p}-\penalty{z_{0}}|,

\displaystyle|{\tilde{q}}-\penalty{\tilde{r}}|

\displaystyle=|{q}-\penalty{r}|,

\displaystyle|{\tilde{z}_{0}}-\penalty{\tilde{q}}|

\displaystyle=|{z_{0}}-\penalty{q}|,

\displaystyle|{\tilde{z}_{0}}-\penalty{\tilde{r}}|

\displaystyle=|{z_{0}}-\penalty{q}|

and

\measuredangle[\tilde{z}_{0}\,{}^{\tilde{q}}_{\tilde{p}}]=\measuredangle[\tilde{z}_{0}\,{}^{\tilde{r}}_{\tilde{p}}]=\tfrac{\uppi}{2}.

Clearly,

\bar{y}(t)=\mathrm{md}^{1}|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\mathrm{geod}_{[\tilde{q}\tilde{r}]}(t)}|

satisfies
\bar{y}^{\prime\prime}=1-\bar{y}
and
\bar{y}^{\prime}(t_{0})=0
,

\bar{y}(t_{0})=y(t_{0})
.
Since
z_{0}
is a maximum point,

y(t)\leqslant\penalty y(t_{0})+o(t-t_{0})
;
thus,
\bar{y}(t)
is a barrier for

y(t)=\mathrm{md}^{1}|{p}-\penalty{\mathrm{geod}_{[qr]}(t)}|
at

t_{0}
by
3E
.
From the barrier inequality
3E
, we get

|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\mathrm{geod}_{[\tilde{q}\tilde{r}]}(t)}|\geqslant\penalty|{p}-\penalty{\mathrm{geod}_{[qr]}(t)}|,

and hence
|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{q}}|\geqslant\penalty|{p}-\penalty{q}|
and
|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{r}}|\geqslant\penalty|{p}-\penalty{r}|
.

Therefore

|{p}-\penalty{q}|+|{q}-\penalty{r}|+|{r}-\penalty{p}|
cannot exceed the perimeter of the spherical triangle
[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{q}}{\tilde{r}}]
.
In particular,

|{p}-\penalty{q}|+|{q}-\penalty{r}|+|{r}-\penalty{p}|\leqslant\penalty 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi

— a contradiction.
∎

Let
\upkappa>0
.
Consider the following extension
\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa+}({*}\,{}^{{*}}_{{*}}\hskip-0.86108pt)

of the model angle function
\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}({*}\,{}^{{*}}_{{*}}\hskip-0.86108pt)
.
This definition works well for
CBB
spaces; for
CAT
spaces there is a similar but definition.
Some authors define the comparison angle to be
\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa+}({*}\,{}^{{*}}_{{*}}\hskip-0.86108pt)
.

8.49. Definition of extended angle.

Suppose
p,q,r
are points in a metric space, and
p\neq q
,
p\neq r
.
Let

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa+}(p\,{}^{q}_{r}\hskip-0.86108pt)=\sup\left\{\,{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\mathrm{K}}(p\,{}^{q}_{r}\hskip-0.86108pt)}\ {:}\ {\mathrm{K}\leqslant\penalty\upkappa}\,\right\}.

The value
\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa+}(p\,{}^{q}_{r}\hskip-0.86108pt)
is called the
extended model angle
of the triple
p
,
q
,
r
.

8.50. Extended angle comparison.

Let
\upkappa>0

and
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length

\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space.
Then for any hinge

[p\,{}^{q}_{r}]
we have

\measuredangle[p\,{}^{q}_{r}]\geqslant\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa+}(p\,{}^{q}_{r}\hskip-0.86108pt)
.

Moreover, the extended model angle
\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa+}(p\,{}^{q}_{r}\hskip-0.86108pt)
can be calculated using the following rule:

(a)

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa+}(p\,{}^{q}_{r}\hskip-0.86108pt)=\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{q}_{r}\hskip-0.86108pt)
if
\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{q}_{r}\hskip-0.86108pt)
is defined;

(b)

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa+}(p\,{}^{q}_{r}\hskip-0.86108pt)=\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa+}(p\,{}^{r}_{q}\hskip-0.86108pt)=0
if
|{p}-\penalty{q}|+|{q}-\penalty{r}|=|{p}-\penalty{r}|
;

(c)

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa+}(p\,{}^{q}_{r}\hskip-0.86108pt)=\uppi
if none of the above is applicable.

Proof.

From Corollary
8F
,
\mathrm{K}<\upkappa
implies that any complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\mathrm{K}})
space is
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
;
thus the extended angle comparison follows from the definition.

The rule for calculating extended angle is an easy consequence of its definition.
∎

I
Remarks

The question whether the first part of
8C
suffices to conclude that
\mathcal{L}
is
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
is a long-standing open problem (possibly dating back to Alexandrov);
it was first stated in
[
36
, footnote in 4.1.5]
.

8.51. Open question.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete geodesic space (you can also assume that
\mathcal{L}
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{S}^{2}
or
\mathbb{R}^{2}
)
such that for any hinge
[x\,{}^{p}_{y}]
in
\mathcal{L}
,
the angle
\measuredangle[x\,{}^{p}_{y}]
is defined and

\measuredangle[x\,{}^{p}_{y}]\geqslant\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{0}(x\,{}^{p}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt).

Is it true that
\mathcal{L}
is
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
?

Examples and constructions.

Let us list important sources of examples of
CBB
spaces.
We do not provide all the proofs and some proofs are deferred to later chapters.

Complete Riemannian manifolds
with sectional curvature at least
\upkappa
, their
Gromov–Hausdorff limits
, as well as their
ultralimits
, are
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
.
This statement follows from
8A
,
5E
and the Toponogov comparison which is a partial case of the globalization theorem (
8F
).
For example,
if
M
is a Riemannian manifold of nonnegative sectional curvature,
then the limit of its rescalings
\tfrac{1}{n}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}M
as
n\to\penalty\infty
is
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
; this is the so-called
asymptotic cone
of
M
.

Most of applications to Riemannian geometry are based on the described sources and the following corollary of Gromov’s selection theorem (
5C
) and the Bishop–Gromov inequality.

8.52. Gromov compactness theorem.

Let
M_{n}
be a sequence of Riemannian manifolds with sectional curvature at least
\upkappa
.
Then, for any choice of marked points
p_{n}\in\penalty M_{n}
,
a subsequence of
M_{n}
admits a Gromov–Hausdorff convergence
such that the corresponding subsequence of
p_{n}
converges.

By Corollary
8F
, the
target of submetry
from
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
is
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
.
In particular, if
M
is a Riemannian manifold with sectional curvature at least
\upkappa
and

G
is a closed subgroup of isometries on
M
,
then the
quotient space

M/G
is
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
.

Yet another source is given by
convex hypersurfaces
.
Namely, suppose
M
is a complete Riemannian manifold of sectional curvature at least
\upkappa
.
Let
N
be a closed (as a subset) hypersurface in
M
.
Then
N
with the induced inner metric is
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
.
In particular, any closed convex hypersurface in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
.

The smooth case of this statement follows from the Gauss formula.
This has been generalized by Sergei Buyalo
[
45
]
to the nonsmooth case and sharpened by the authors
[
10
]
.

Let us mention that an analogous statement about convex hypersurfaces in
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space is completely open.
Namely, it is unknown if
the boundary of a complete finite-dimensional
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
length space has to be
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
.

Further,
CBB
spaces behave nicely with respect to several natural constructions.
For example, the product of
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
spaces is a
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
.
Also, the Euclidean cone over
\text{\rm CBB}({1})
space is a
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
.
These are the first examples of the so-called
warped products
that are discussed in Chapter
11
;
a general statement is given in
11E
.
Perelman’s doubling theorem can be considered as a partial case;
it states that if
\mathcal{L}
is a finite-dimensional
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
length space with nonempty boundary, then its
doubling across the boundary
is
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
as well.

More conceptually,
Wasserstein space
of order
2
over
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
space is
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
.
Also, there is a natural metric on the
space of metric-measure spaces
that makes it
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
space;
it was constructed by Karl-Theodor Sturm
[
151
]
.

Among less important examples, let us mention
polyhedral spaces
,
an if-and-only-if condition is given in
12B
.
Also, in addition to Perelman’s doubling theorem,
there are several versions of gluing theorems
[
130
,
66
,
96
]
;
they give conditions that guarantee that gluing of two (or more) spaces is
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
.

Chapter 9
Fundamentals of curvature bounded above

A
Four-point comparison.

9.1. Four-point comparison.

A quadruple of points
p^{1},p^{2},x^{1},x^{2}
in a metric space
satisfies

\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
comparison

if

(a)

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p^{1}\,{}^{x^{1}}_{x^{2}}\hskip-0.86108pt)\leqslant\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p^{1}\,{}^{p^{2}}_{x^{1}}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p^{1}\,{}^{p^{2}}_{x^{2}}\hskip-0.86108pt)
, or

(b)

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p^{2}\,{}^{x^{1}}_{x^{2}}\hskip-0.86108pt)\leqslant\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p^{2}\,{}^{p^{1}}_{x^{1}}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p^{2}\,{}^{p^{1}}_{x^{2}}\hskip-0.86108pt)
, or

(c)

one of the six model angles

\displaystyle\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p^{1}\,{}^{x^{1}}_{x^{2}}\hskip-0.86108pt),\quad

\displaystyle\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p^{1}\,{}^{p^{2}}_{x^{1}}\hskip-0.86108pt),\quad\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p^{1}\,{}^{p^{2}}_{x^{2}}\hskip-0.86108pt),

\displaystyle\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p^{2}\,{}^{x^{1}}_{x^{2}}\hskip-0.86108pt),\quad

\displaystyle\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p^{2}\,{}^{p^{1}}_{x^{1}}\hskip-0.86108pt),\quad\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p^{2}\,{}^{p^{1}}_{x^{2}}\hskip-0.86108pt)

is undefined.

Here is a more intuitive formulation.

9.2. Reformulation.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a metric space.
A quadruple
p^{1},p^{2},x^{1},x^{2}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
satisfies

\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
comparison if one of the following holds:

(a)

One of the triples

(p^{1},p^{2},x^{1})

or

(p^{1},p^{2},x^{2})

has perimeter
>2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
.

(b)

If
[{\tilde{p}^{1}}{\tilde{p}^{2}}{\tilde{x}^{1}}]={\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}(p^{1}p^{2}x^{1})

and

[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{p}^{2}}{\tilde{x}^{2}}]=\penalty{\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}p^{1}p^{2}x^{2}
, then

|{\tilde{x}^{1}}-\penalty{\tilde{z}}|+|{\tilde{z}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{2}}|\geqslant\penalty|{x^{1}}-\penalty{x^{2}}|,

for any
\tilde{z}\in\penalty[\tilde{p}^{1}\tilde{p}^{2}]
.

9.3. Definition.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a metric space.

(a)

\mathcal{U}
is

\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})

if any quadruple
p^{1},p^{2},x^{1},x^{2}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
satisfies
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
comparison.

(b)

\mathcal{U}
is

locally
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})

if any point
q\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
admits a neighborhood
\Omega\ni q
such that any quadruple
p^{1},p^{2},x^{1},x^{2}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
satisfies
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
comparison.

(c)

\mathcal{U}
is a

CAT
space if
\mathcal{U}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
for some
\upkappa\in\penalty\mathbb{R}
.

The condition
\mathcal{U}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
should be understood as “
\mathcal{U}
has global curvature
\leqslant\penalty\upkappa
”.
In Proposition
9C
, it will be shown that this formulation makes sense;
in particular, if
\upkappa\leqslant\penalty\mathrm{K}
, then any
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space is
\text{\rm CAT}({\mathrm{K}})
.

This terminology was introduced by Michael Gromov;

CAT
stands for Élie Cartan, Alexandr Alexandrov, and Victor Toponogov.
Originally these spaces were called
\mathfrak{R}_{\upkappa}
domains
;
this is Alexandrov’s terminology and is still in use.

9.4. Exercise.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a metric space.
Show that
\mathcal{U}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})

if and only if every quadruple of points in
\mathcal{U}
admits a labeling by
(p,x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})
such that the three angles

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{x^{1}}_{x^{2}}\hskip-0.86108pt)
,

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{x^{2}}_{x^{3}}\hskip-0.86108pt)
and

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{x^{1}}_{x^{3}}\hskip-0.86108pt)

satisfy all three triangle inequalities or one of these angles is undefined.

9.5. Exercise.

Show that
\mathcal{U}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})

if and only if for any quadruple of points

p^{1},p^{2},x^{1},x^{2}
in
\mathcal{U}
such that

|{p^{1}}-\penalty{p^{2}}|,|{x^{1}}-\penalty{x^{2}}|\leqslant\penalty\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
,
there is a quadruple
q^{1},q^{2},y^{1},y^{2}
in
\mathbb{M}^{m}({\upkappa})

such that

\displaystyle|{q^{1}}-\penalty{q^{2}}|

\displaystyle=|{p^{1}}-\penalty{p^{2}}|,

\displaystyle|{y^{1}}-\penalty{y^{2}}|

\displaystyle=|{x^{1}}-\penalty{x^{2}}|,

\displaystyle|{q^{i}}-\penalty{y^{j}}|

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty|{p^{i}}-\penalty{x^{j}}|

for any
i
and
j
.

9.6. Advanced exercise.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a complete length space such that for any quadruple
p,q,x,y\in\penalty\mathcal{L}

the following inequality holds

|{p}-\penalty{q}|^{2}+|{x}-\penalty{y}|^{2}\leqslant\penalty|{p}-\penalty{x}|^{2}+|{p}-\penalty{y}|^{2}+|{q}-\penalty{x}|^{2}+|{q}-\penalty{y}|^{2}.

➊

Prove that
\mathcal{U}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
.

Construct a 4-point metric space
\mathcal{X}
that satisfies inequality
➊
for any relabeling of its points by
p,q,x,y
, and such that
\mathcal{X}
is not
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
.

The next proposition follows directly from Definition
9A
and the definitions of ultralimit and ultrapower;
see Section
4B
for the related definitions.
Recall that
denotes a fixed selective ultrafilter on
\mathbb{N}
.

9.7. Proposition.

Let
\mathcal{U}_{n}
be a
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa_{n}})
space for each
n\in\penalty\mathbb{N}
.
Assume
\mathcal{U}_{n}\to\penalty\mathcal{U}_{\text{\rm}}
and
\upkappa_{n}\to\penalty\upkappa_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.
Then
\mathcal{U}_{\text{\rm}}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa_{\text{\rm}}})
.

Moreover, a metric space
\mathcal{U}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
if and only if so is its ultrapower
\mathcal{U}^{\text{\rm}}
.

B
Geodesics

9.8. Uniqueness of geodesics.

In a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space, pairs of points at distance
<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
are joined by unique geodesics, and these geodesics depend continuously on their endpoint pairs.

Proof.

Fix a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space
\mathcal{U}
.
Fix two points
p^{1},p^{2}\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
such that

|{p^{1}}-\penalty{p^{2}}|_{\mathcal{U}}<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}.

Choose a sequence of approximate midpoints
z_{n}
between
p^{1}
and
p^{2}
;
that is,

|{p^{1}}-\penalty{z_{n}}|,|{p^{2}}-\penalty{z_{n}}|\to\penalty\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{p^{1}}-\penalty{p^{2}}|\quad\text{as}\quad n\to\penalty\infty.

➊

By the law of cosines,
\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p^{1}\,{}^{z_{n}}_{p^{2}}\hskip-0.86108pt)
and
\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p^{2}\,{}^{z_{n}}_{p^{1}}\hskip-0.86108pt)
are arbitrarily small when
n
is sufficiently large.

Let us apply
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
comparison (
9A
) to the quadruple
p^{1}
,
p^{2}
,
z_{n}
,
z_{k}
with large
n
and
k
.
We conclude that
\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{z_{n}}_{z_{k}}\hskip-0.86108pt)
is arbitrarily small when
n,k
are sufficiently large and
p
is either
p^{1}
or
p^{2}
.
By
➊
and the law of cosines, the sequence
z_{n}
converges.

Since
\mathcal{U}
is complete, the sequence
z_{n}
converges to a midpoint between
p^{1}
and
p^{2}
.
By Lemma
2C
we obtain the existence of a geodesic
[p^{1}p^{2}]
.

Now suppose
p^{1}_{n}\to\penalty p^{1}
,
p^{2}_{n}\to\penalty p^{2}
as
n\to\penalty\infty
.
Let
z_{n}
be the midpoint of a geodesic
[p^{1}_{n}p^{2}_{n}]
and
z
be the midpoint of a geodesic
[p^{1}p^{2}]
.

It suffices to show that

|{z_{n}}-\penalty{z}|\to\penalty 0\quad\text{as}\quad n\to\penalty\infty.

➋

By the triangle inequality, the
z_{n}
are approximate midpoints between
p^{1}
and
p^{2}
.
Apply the
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
comparison (
9A
) to the quadruple
p^{1}
,
p^{2}
,
z_{n}
,
z
.
For
p=p^{1}
or
p=p^{2}
, we see that
\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{z_{n}}_{z}\hskip-0.86108pt)
is arbitrarily small when
n
is sufficiently large.
By the law of cosines,
➋
follows.
∎

9.9. Exercise.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a complete length
CAT
space.
Assume
\mathcal{U}
is a topological manifold.
Show that any geodesic in
\mathcal{U}
can be extended
as a two-side infinite local geodesic.

Moreover the same holds for any locally geodesic locally
CAT
space
\mathcal{U}
with nontrivial local homology groups at any point;
the latter holds in particular if
\mathcal{U}
is a homological manifold.

9.10. Exercise.

Assume
\mathcal{U}
is a locally compact geodesic
CAT
space with extendable geodesics;
that is, any geodesic in
\mathcal{U}
can be extended to a both-sided infinite local geodesic.

Show that the space of geodesic directions
\Sigma_{p}^{\prime}
is complete for any
p\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
.

By the uniqueness of geodesics (
9B
),
we have the following.

9.11. Corollary.

Any complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space is
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-geodesic.

9.12. Proposition.

The completion
\bar{\mathcal{U}}
of any geodesic
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space
\mathcal{U}
is a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space.

Moreover,
\mathcal{U}
is a geodesic
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space
if and only if there is a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space
\bar{\mathcal{U}}
that contains a
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-convex dense set isometric to
\mathcal{U}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
9B
,
in order to show that
\bar{\mathcal{U}}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
,
it is sufficient to verify that the completion of a length space is a length space;
this is straightforward.

For the second part, note that the completion
\bar{\mathcal{U}}

contains the original space
\mathcal{U}
as a dense
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-convex subset, and the metric on
\mathcal{U}
coincides with the induced length metric from
\bar{\mathcal{U}}
.
∎

Here is a corollary from Proposition
9B

and Theorem
9B
.

9.13. Corollary.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-geodesic
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space.
Then pairs of points in
\mathcal{U}
at distance less than
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
are joined by unique geodesics, and these geodesics depend continuously on their endpoint pairs.

Moreover for any pair of points
p,q\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
and any value

{\ell}>\sup\left\{\,{\frac{\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}r}{\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}|{p}-\penalty{q}|}}\ {:}\ {0\leqslant\penalty r\leqslant\penalty|{p}-\penalty{q}|}\,\right\}

there are neighborhoods
\Omega_{p}\ni p
and
\Omega_{q}\ni q
such that the map

(x,y,t)\mapsto\penalty\mathrm{path}_{[xy]}(t)

is
{\ell}
-Lipschitz in
\Omega_{p}\times\penalty\Omega_{q}\times\penalty[0,1]
.

Proof.

By Proposition
9B
, any geodesic
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space is isometric to a convex dense subset of a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space.
It remains to apply Theorem
9B
.
∎

C
More comparisons

Here we give a few reformulations of Definition
9A
.

9.14. Theorem.

If
\mathcal{U}
is a
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space, then
the following conditions hold for all triples
p,x,y\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
of perimeter
<2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
:

(a)

(adjacent angle comparison
) for any geodesic
[xy]
and
z\in\penalty\mathopen{]}xy\mathclose{[}
, we have

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(z\,{}^{p}_{x}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(z\,{}^{p}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt)\geqslant\penalty\uppi.

(b)

(point-on-side comparison
)
for any geodesic
[xy]
and
z\in\penalty\mathopen{]}xy\mathclose{[}
, we have

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{p}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt)\geqslant\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{p}_{z}\hskip-0.86108pt),

or equivalently,

|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{z}}|\geqslant\penalty|{p}-\penalty{z}|,

where
[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{x}}{\tilde{y}}]={\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}(pxy)
,
\tilde{z}\in\penalty\mathopen{]}\tilde{x}\tilde{y}\mathclose{[}
,
|{\tilde{x}}-\penalty{\tilde{z}}|=|{x}-\penalty{z}|
.

(c)

(hinge comparison
)
for any hinge
[x\,{}^{p}_{y}]
, the angle

\measuredangle[x\,{}^{p}_{y}]
exists and

\measuredangle[x\,{}^{p}_{y}]\leqslant\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{p}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt),

or equivalently,

\mathop{\tilde{\curlyvee}}\nolimits^{\upkappa}[x\,{}^{p}_{y}]\leqslant\penalty|{p}-\penalty{y}|.

Moreover, if
\mathcal{U}
is
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-geodesic, then the converse holds in each case.

Remark.

In the following proof, the part
9C
\Rightarrow
9C
)
only requires that the
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
comparison (
9A
) hold for any quadruple, and does not require the existence of geodesics at distance
<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
.
The same is true of the parts
9C
\Leftrightarrow
9C
and

9C
\Rightarrow
9C
.
Thus the conditions
9C
,
9C
) and
9C
are valid for any metric space (not necessarily a length space) that satisfies
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
comparison (
9A
).
The converse does not hold; for example, all these conditions are
vacuously true in a
totally disconnected space, while

\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
comparison is not.

Proof;
9C
.
Since the perimeter of
p,x,y
is
<2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
, so is the perimeter of any subtriple of
p,z,x,y
by the triangle inequality.
By Alexandrov’s lemma (
6B
),

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{z}_{x}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{z}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt)<\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{x}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt)\quad\text{or}\quad\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(z\,{}^{p}_{x}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(z\,{}^{p}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt)=\uppi.

In the former case, the
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
comparison (
9A
) applied to the quadruple
p,z,x,y
implies

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(z\,{}^{p}_{x}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(z\,{}^{p}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt)\geqslant\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(z\,{}^{x}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt)=\uppi.

9C
\Leftrightarrow
9C
.

Follows from Alexandrov’s lemma (
6B
).

9C
\Rightarrow
9C
.

By
9C
, for
\bar{p}\in\penalty\mathopen{]}xp]
and
\bar{y}\in\penalty\mathopen{]}xy]
the function
(|{x}-\penalty{\bar{p}}|,|{x}-\penalty{\bar{y}}|)\mapsto\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{\bar{p}}_{\bar{y}}\hskip-0.86108pt)
is nondecreasing in each argument.
In particular,

\measuredangle[x\,{}^{p}_{y}]=\penalty\inf\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{\bar{p}}_{\bar{y}}\hskip-0.86108pt)
.
Thus
\measuredangle[x\,{}^{p}_{y}]
exists and is
at most
\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{p}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt)
.

Converse.
Assume
\mathcal{U}
is
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-geodesic.
Let us first show that in this case
9C
\Rightarrow
9C
.

Indeed, by
9C
and the triangle inequality for angles (
6C
),

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(z\,{}^{p}_{x}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(z\,{}^{p}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt)\geqslant\penalty\measuredangle[z\,{}^{p}_{x}]+\measuredangle[z\,{}^{p}_{y}]\geqslant\penalty\uppi.

It remains to prove the converse for
9C
.

Given a quadruple
p^{1},p^{2},x^{1},x^{2}
whose subtriples have perimeter
<2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
, we must verify the
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
comparison (
9A
).
In
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
, construct the model triangles
[{\tilde{p}^{1}}{\tilde{p}^{2}}{\tilde{x}^{1}}]={\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}(p^{1}p^{2}x^{1})

and
[{\tilde{p}^{1}}{\tilde{p}^{2}}{\tilde{x}^{2}}]=\penalty{\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}(p^{1}p^{2}x^{2})
, lying on either side of a common segment
[\tilde{p}^{1}\tilde{p}^{2}]
.
We may suppose

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p^{1}\,{}^{p^{2}}_{x^{1}}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p^{1}\,{}^{p^{2}}_{x^{2}}\hskip-0.86108pt)\leqslant\penalty\uppi\quad\text{and}\quad\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p^{2}\,{}^{p^{1}}_{x^{1}}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p^{2}\,{}^{p^{1}}_{x^{2}}\hskip-0.86108pt)\leqslant\penalty\uppi,

since otherwise
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
comparison holds trivially.
Then
[\tilde{p}^{1}\tilde{p}^{2}]
and
[\tilde{x}^{1}\tilde{x}^{2}]
intersect, say at
\tilde{q}
.

By assumption, there is a geodesic
[p^{1}p^{2}]
.
Choose
q\in\penalty[p^{1}p^{2}]
corresponding to
\tilde{q}
;
that is,
|{p^{1}}-\penalty{q}|=|{\tilde{p}^{1}}-\penalty{\tilde{q}}|
.
Then

|{x^{1}}-\penalty{x^{2}}|\leqslant\penalty|{x^{1}}-\penalty{q}|+|{q}-\penalty{x^{2}}|\leqslant\penalty|{\tilde{x}^{1}}-\penalty{\tilde{q}}|+|{\tilde{q}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{2}}|=|{\tilde{x}^{1}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{2}}|,

where the second inequality follows from
9C
.
By monotonicity of the function
a\mapsto\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}\{a;b,c\}
(
1A
),

\displaystyle\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p^{1}\,{}^{x^{1}}_{x^{2}}\hskip-0.86108pt)\leqslant\penalty\measuredangle[\tilde{p}^{1}\,{}^{\tilde{x}^{1}}_{\tilde{x}^{2}}]=\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p^{1}\,{}^{p^{2}}_{x^{1}}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p^{1}\,{}^{p^{2}}_{x^{2}}\hskip-0.86108pt).

∎

Let us display a corollary of the proof of
9C
,
namely, monotonicity of the model angle with respect to adjacent sidelengths.

9.15. Angle-sidelength monotonicity.

Suppose
\mathcal{U}
is a
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-geodesic
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space, and

p,x,y\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
have perimeter
<2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
.
Then for
\bar{y}\in\penalty\mathopen{]}xy]
, the function

|{x}-\penalty{\bar{y}}|\mapsto\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{p}_{\bar{y}}\hskip-0.86108pt)

is nondecreasing.

In particular, if
\bar{p}\in\penalty\mathopen{]}xp]
, then

(a)

the function

(|{x}-\penalty{\bar{y}}|,|{x}-\penalty{\bar{p}}|)\mapsto\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{\bar{p}}_{\bar{y}}\hskip-0.86108pt)

is nondecreasing in each argument,

(b)

\measuredangle[x\,{}^{p}_{y}]=\inf\left\{\,{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{\bar{p}}_{\bar{y}}\hskip-0.86108pt)}\ {:}\ {\bar{p}\in\penalty\mathopen{]}xp],\ \bar{y}\in\penalty\mathopen{]}xy]}\,\right\}.

9.16. Exercise.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be
\mathbb{R}^{m}
with the metric defined by a norm.
Show that
\mathcal{U}
is a complete length
CAT
space if and only if
\mathcal{U}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it iso}}}\penalty\mathbb{E}^{m}
.

9.17. Exercise.

Assume
\mathcal{U}
is a geodesic
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
space.
Show that for any two geodesic paths

\upgamma,\upsigma\colon[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{U}

the function

t\mapsto\penalty|{\upgamma(t)}-\penalty{\upsigma(t)}|

is convex.

9.18. Proposition.

Assume
\upkappa<\mathrm{K}
.
Then any complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space is
\text{\rm CAT}({\mathrm{K}})
.

Moreover a space
\mathcal{U}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
if
\mathcal{U}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({\mathrm{K}})
for all
\mathrm{K}>\upkappa
.

Proof.

The first statement follows from Corollary
9B
, the adjacent-angles comparison (
9C
) and the monotonicity of the function
\upkappa\mapsto\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{y}_{z}\hskip-0.86108pt)
(
1A
).

The second statement follows since the function
\upkappa\mapsto\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{y}_{z}\hskip-0.86108pt)
is continuous.
∎

D
Thin triangles

In this section we define thin triangles
and use them to characterize
CAT
spaces.
Inheritance for thin triangles with respect to decomposition
is the main result of this section.
It will lead to two fundamental constructions:
Alexandrov’s patchwork globalization (
9G
)
and Reshetnyak gluing (
9I
).

9.19. Definition of
\bm{\upkappa}
-thin triangles.

Let
[{x^{1}}{x^{2}}{x^{3}}]
be a triangle of perimeter
<2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
in a metric space
and

[{\tilde{x}^{1}}{\tilde{x}^{2}}{\tilde{x}^{3}}]=\penalty{\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}({x^{1}}{x^{2}}{x^{3}})
.
Consider the
natural map

[{\tilde{x}^{1}}{\tilde{x}^{2}}{\tilde{x}^{3}}]\to\penalty[{x^{1}}{x^{2}}{x^{3}}]

that sends a point
\tilde{z}\in\penalty[\tilde{x}^{i}\tilde{x}^{j}]
to the corresponding point
z\in\penalty[x^{i}x^{j}]

(that is, such that
|{\tilde{x}^{i}}-\penalty{\tilde{z}}|=|{x^{i}}-\penalty{z}|
and therefore
|{\tilde{x}^{j}}-\penalty{\tilde{z}}|=|{x^{j}}-\penalty{z}|
).

We say the triangle
[{x^{1}}{x^{2}}{x^{3}}]
is
\upkappa
-thin
if the natural map
[{\tilde{x}^{1}}{\tilde{x}^{2}}{\tilde{x}^{3}}]\to\penalty[{x^{1}}{x^{2}}{x^{3}}]
is short.

9.20. Exercise.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-geodesic
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space.
Let
[{x}{y}{z}]
be a triangle in
\mathcal{U}

and
[{\tilde{x}}{\tilde{y}}{\tilde{z}}]
be its model triangle in
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
.
Prove that the natural map
f\colon[{\tilde{x}}{\tilde{y}}{\tilde{z}}]\to\penalty[{x}{y}{z}]

is distance-preserving if and only if one of the following conditions hold:

(a)

\measuredangle[x\,{}^{y}_{z}]=\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{y}_{z}\hskip-0.86108pt)
,

(b)

|{x}-\penalty{w}|=|{\tilde{x}}-\penalty{\tilde{w}}|
for some
\tilde{w}\in\penalty]\tilde{y}\tilde{z}[
and

w=f(\tilde{w})
,

(c)

|{v}-\penalty{w}|=|{\tilde{v}}-\penalty{\tilde{w}}|
for some

\tilde{v}\in\penalty\mathopen{]}\tilde{x}\tilde{y}\mathclose{[}
,

\tilde{w}\in\penalty\mathopen{]}\tilde{x}\tilde{z}\mathclose{[}

and
v=f(\tilde{v})
,
w=f(\tilde{w})
.

9.21. Proposition.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-geodesic space.
Then
\mathcal{U}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})

if and only if every triangle of perimeter
<2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
in
\mathcal{U}
is
\upkappa
-thin.

Proof.

The if part follows from the point-on-side comparison (
9C
).
The only-if part follows from angle-sidelength monotonicity (
9C
).
∎

9.22. Corollary.

Suppose
\mathcal{U}
is a
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-geodesic
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space.
Then any local geodesic in
\mathcal{U}
of length
<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
is length-minimizing.

Proof.

Suppose
\upgamma\colon[0,\ell]\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
is a local geodesic that is not minimizing, with
\ell<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
.
Choose
a
to be the maximal value
such that
\upgamma
is minimizing on
[0,a]
.
Further choose
b>a
so that
\upgamma
is minimizing on
[a,b]
.

Since triangle
[{\upgamma(0)}{\upgamma(a)}{\upgamma(b)}]
is
\upkappa
-thin, we have

|{\upgamma(a-\upvarepsilon)}-\penalty{\upgamma(a+\upvarepsilon)}|<2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon

for all small
\upvarepsilon>0
,
a contradiction.
∎

Now let us formulate the main result of this section.
The inheritance lemma states that in any metric space, a triangle is
\upkappa
-thin if it decomposes into
\upkappa
-thin triangles.
In contrast,
\upkappa
-thickness of triangles (
8C
) is not inherited in this way.

9.23. Inheritance lemma.

In a metric space, consider a triangle
[{p}{x}{y}]
that
decomposes

into two triangles
[{p}{x}{z}]
and
[{p}{y}{z}]
;
that is,
[{p}{x}{z}]
and
[{p}{y}{z}]
have common side
[pz]
, and the sides
[xz]
and
[zy]
together form the side
[xy]
of
[{p}{x}{y}]
.

If the triangle
[{p}{x}{y}]
has perimeter
<2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}

and both triangles
[{p}{x}{z}]
and
[{p}{y}{z}]
are
\upkappa
-thin, then triangle
[{p}{x}{y}]
is
\upkappa
-thin.

The following model-space lemma is extracted from Lemma 2 in
[
139
]
.

9.24. Lemma.

Let
[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{x}}{\tilde{y}}]
be a triangle in
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
and
\tilde{z}\in\penalty[\tilde{x}\tilde{y}]
.
Consider the solid triangle
\tilde{D}=\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{x}}{\tilde{y}}]
.
Construct points
\dot{p},\dot{x},\dot{z},\dot{y}\in\penalty\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
such that

\displaystyle|{\dot{p}}-\penalty{\dot{x}}|

\displaystyle=|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}}|,

\displaystyle|{\dot{p}}-\penalty{\dot{y}}|

\displaystyle=|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{y}}|,

\displaystyle|{\dot{p}}-\penalty{\dot{z}}|

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{z}}|,

\displaystyle|{\dot{x}}-\penalty{\dot{z}}|

\displaystyle=|{\tilde{x}}-\penalty{\tilde{z}}|,

\displaystyle|{\dot{y}}-\penalty{\dot{z}}|

\displaystyle=|{\tilde{y}}-\penalty{\tilde{z}}|,

where points
\dot{x}
and
\dot{y}
lie on either side of
[\dot{p}\dot{z}]
.
Set

\dot{D}=\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits[{\dot{p}}{\dot{x}}{\dot{z}}]\cup\penalty\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits[{\dot{p}}{\dot{y}}{\dot{z}}].

Then there is a short map
F\colon\tilde{D}\to\penalty\dot{D}
that maps
\tilde{p}
,
\tilde{x}
,
\tilde{y}
and
\tilde{z}
to
\dot{p}
,
\dot{x}
,
\dot{y}
and
\dot{z}
respectively.

Proof.

By Alexandrov’s lemma (
6B
),
there are nonoverlapping triangles

[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{x}}{\tilde{z}_{y}}]\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it iso}}}\penalty[{\dot{p}}{\dot{x}}{\dot{z}}]

and

[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{y}}{\tilde{z}_{x}}]\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it iso}}}\penalty[{\dot{p}}{\dot{y}}{\dot{z}}]

inside triangle
[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{x}}{\tilde{y}}]
.

Connect points in each pair

(\tilde{z},\tilde{z}_{x})
,

(\tilde{z}_{x},\tilde{z}_{y})

and
(\tilde{z}_{y},\tilde{z})

with arcs of circles centered at

\tilde{y}
,
\tilde{p}
, and
\tilde{x}
respectively.
Define
F
as follows.

\diamond

Map
\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{x}}{\tilde{z}_{y}}]
isometrically onto
\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits[{\dot{p}}{\dot{x}}{\dot{y}}]
;
similarly map
\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{y}}{\tilde{z}_{x}}]
onto
\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits[{\dot{p}}{\dot{y}}{\dot{z}}]
.

\diamond

If
w
is in one of the three circular sectors, say at distance
r
from the center of the circle, let
F(w)
be the point on

[\dot{p}\dot{z}]
,

[\dot{x}\dot{z}]
,
or
[\dot{y}\dot{z}]
whose distance from the left-hand endpoint of the segment is
r
.

\diamond

Finally, if
w
lies in the remaining curvilinear triangle
\tilde{z}\tilde{z}_{x}\tilde{z}_{y}
,
set
F(w)=\dot{z}
.

By construction,
F
meets the conditions of the lemma.
∎

Proof of
9D
.

Construct model triangles
[{\dot{p}}{\dot{x}}{\dot{z}}]=\penalty{\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}(pxz)

and
[{\dot{p}}{\dot{y}}{\dot{z}}]=\penalty{\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}(pyz)
so that
\dot{x}
and
\dot{y}
lie on opposite sides of
[\dot{p}\dot{z}]
.

Suppose

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(z\,{}^{p}_{x}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(z\,{}^{p}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt)<\uppi.

Then for some point
\dot{w}\in\penalty[\dot{p}\dot{z}]
, we have

|{\dot{x}}-\penalty{\dot{w}}|+|{\dot{w}}-\penalty{\dot{y}}|<|{\dot{x}}-\penalty{\dot{z}}|+|{\dot{z}}-\penalty{\dot{y}}|=|{x}-\penalty{y}|.

Let
w\in\penalty[pz]
correspond to
\dot{w}
; that is,
|{z}-\penalty{w}|=|{\dot{z}}-\penalty{\dot{w}}|
.
Since
[{p}{x}{z}]
and
[{p}{y}{z}]
are
\upkappa
-thin, we have

|{x}-\penalty{w}|+|{w}-\penalty{y}|<|{x}-\penalty{y}|,

contradicting the triangle inequality.

Thus

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(z\,{}^{p}_{x}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(z\,{}^{p}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt)\geqslant\penalty\uppi.

By Alexandrov’s lemma (
6B
), this is equivalent to

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{p}_{z}\hskip-0.86108pt)\leqslant\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{p}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt).

➊

Let
[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{x}}{\tilde{y}}]={\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}(pxy)

and
\tilde{z}\in\penalty[\tilde{x}\tilde{y}]
correspond to
z
; that is,
|{x}-\penalty{z}|=|{\tilde{x}}-\penalty{\tilde{z}}|
.
Inequality
➊
is equivalent to
|{p}-\penalty{z}|\leqslant\penalty|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{z}}|
.
Hence Lemma
9D
applies. Therefore
there is a short map
F
that sends

[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{x}}{\tilde{y}}]
to
\dot{D}=\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits[{\dot{p}}{\dot{x}}{\dot{z}}]\cup\penalty\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits[{\dot{p}}{\dot{y}}{\dot{z}}]

in such a way that

\tilde{p}\mapsto\penalty\dot{p}
,

\tilde{x}\mapsto\penalty\dot{x}
,

\tilde{z}\mapsto\penalty\dot{z}

and

\tilde{y}\mapsto\penalty\dot{y}
.

By assumption, the natural maps
[{\dot{p}}{\dot{x}}{\dot{z}}]\to\penalty[{p}{x}{z}]
and
[{\dot{p}}{\dot{y}}{\dot{z}}]\to\penalty[{p}{y}{z}]
are short.
By composition, the natural map from
[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{x}}{\tilde{y}}]
to
[{p}{y}{z}]
is short, as claimed.
∎

E
Function comparison

In this section we give analytic and geometric ways of viewing the point-on-side comparison (
9C
) as a convexity condition.

First we obtain a corresponding differential inequality for the distance function in
\mathcal{U}
;
see Section
3F
for the definition.

9.25. Theorem.

Suppose
\mathcal{U}
is a
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-geodesic space.
Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

\mathcal{U}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
,

(b)

for any
p\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
, the function
f=\mathrm{md}^{\upkappa}\circ\penalty\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}{}{}
satisfies

f^{\prime\prime}+\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f\geqslant\penalty 1

in
\mathrm{B}{}(p,\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}})
.

9.26. Corollary.

A geodesic space
\mathcal{U}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
if and only if for any
p\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
, the function
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}^{2}{}{}\colon\mathcal{U}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
is
2
-convex.

Proof of
9E
.

Fix a sufficiently short geodesic
[xy]
in
\mathrm{B}{}(p,\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}})
.
We can assume that the model triangle
[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{x}}{\tilde{y}}]=\penalty{\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}(pxy)
is defined.
Let

\displaystyle\tilde{r}(t)

\displaystyle=|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\mathrm{geod}_{[\tilde{x}\tilde{y}]}(t)}|,

\displaystyle r(t)

\displaystyle=|{p}-\penalty{\mathrm{geod}_{[xy]}(t)}|.

Let
\tilde{f}=\mathrm{md}^{\upkappa}\circ\penalty\tilde{r}
and
f=\mathrm{md}^{\upkappa}\circ\penalty r
.
By
1A
, we have
\tilde{f}^{\prime\prime}=\penalty 1-\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\tilde{f}
.
Clearly
\tilde{f}(t)
and
f(t)
agree at
t=0
and
t=|{x}-\penalty{y}|
.
The point-on-side comparison (
9C
) is the condition
r(t)\leqslant\penalty\tilde{r}(t)
for all
t\in\penalty[0,|{x}-\penalty{y}|]
.
Since
\mathrm{md}^{\upkappa}
is increasing on
[0,\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}})
, then
r\leqslant\penalty\tilde{r}
and
f\leqslant\penalty\tilde{f}
are equivalent.
Thus the claim follows by Jensen’s inequality (
3E
).
∎

9.27. Corollary.

Suppose
\mathcal{U}
is a
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-geodesic
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space.
Then any ball (closed or open) of radius
R<\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}
in
\mathcal{U}
is convex.

Moreover, any open ball of radius
\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}
is convex
and any closed ball of radius
\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}
is
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-convex.

Proof.

Suppose
p\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
,
R\leqslant\penalty\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}/2
, and two points

x
and
y
lie in
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[p,R]
or
\mathrm{B}{}(p,R)
.
By the triangle inequality, if
|{x}-\penalty{y}|<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
, then any
geodesic
[xy]
lies in
\mathrm{B}{}(p,\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}})
.

By the function comparison (
9E
),
the geodesic
[xy]
lies in
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[p,R]
or
\mathrm{B}{}(p,R)
respectively.

Thus any ball (closed or open) of radius
R<\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}
is
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-convex.
This implies convexity unless there is a pair of points in the ball at distance at least
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
.
By the triangle inequality, the latter is possible only for the closed ball of radius
\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}
.
∎

.

.

9.28. Exercise.

Let

be a complete length

and
\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{\upgamma}\colon\mathcal{U}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
be the Busemann function for a half-line
\upgamma\colon[0,\infty)\to\penalty\mathcal{L}
.

(a)

If
\upkappa=0
, then the Busemann function
\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{\upgamma}
is convex.

(b)

If
\upkappa=-1
, then the function
f=\exp\circ\penalty\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{\upgamma}
satisfies

f^{\prime\prime}-f\geqslant\penalty 0.

F
Development

Geometrically, the development construction (
8E
) translates distance comparison into a local convexity statement for subsets of
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
.
Recall that a curve in
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
is
(locally) concave
with respect to
p
if (locally) its supergraph with respect to
p
is a convex subset of
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
; see Definition
8E
.

9.29. Development criterion.

For a
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-geodesic space
\mathcal{U}
,
the following statements hold:

(a)

For any
p\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
and any geodesic
\upgamma\colon[0,T]\to\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(p,\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}})
, suppose the
\upkappa
-development
\tilde{\upgamma}
in
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
of
\upgamma
with respect to
p
is locally concave.
Then
\mathcal{U}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
.

(b)

If
\mathcal{U}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
, then for any geodesic
\upgamma\colon[0,T]\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
and
p\in\penalty\mathcal{U}

such that the triangle
[{p}{\upgamma(0)}{\upgamma(T)}]
has perimeter
<2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
,
the
\upkappa
-development
\tilde{\upgamma}
in
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
of
\upgamma
with respect to
p
is concave.

Proof;
9F
.

Let
\upgamma=\mathrm{geod}_{[xy]}
and
T=|{x}-\penalty{y}|
.
Let
\tilde{\upgamma}\colon[0,T]\to\penalty\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
be the concave
\upkappa
-development based at
\tilde{p}
of
\upgamma
with respect to
p
.
Let us show that the function

t\mapsto\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{p}_{\upgamma(t)}\hskip-0.86108pt)

➊

is nondecreasing.

For a partition
0=t^{0}<t^{1}<\dots\penalty<t^{n}=T
, let

\tilde{y}^{i}=\tilde{\upgamma}(t^{i})\quad\text{and}\quad\uptau^{i}=|{\tilde{y}^{0}}-\penalty{\tilde{y}^{1}}|+|{\tilde{y}^{1}}-\penalty{\tilde{y}^{2}}|+\dots\penalty+|{\tilde{y}^{i-1}}-\penalty{\tilde{y}^{i}}|.

Since
\tilde{\upgamma}
is locally concave,
for a sufficiently fine partition the polygonal line
\tilde{y}^{0}\tilde{y}^{1}\dots\penalty\tilde{y}^{n}
is locally concave with respect to
\tilde{p}
.
Alexandrov’s lemma (
6B
), applied inductively to pairs of triangles
{\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}\{\uptau^{i-1},|{p}-\penalty{\tilde{y}^{0}}|,|{p}-\penalty{\tilde{y}^{i-1}}|\}
and
{\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}\{|{\tilde{y}^{i-1}}-\penalty{\tilde{y}^{i}}|,|{p}-\penalty{\tilde{y}^{i-1}}|,|{p}-\penalty{\tilde{y}^{i}}|\}
, shows that the sequence
\tilde{\measuredangle}^{\upkappa}\{|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{y}^{i}}|;|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{y}^{0}}|,\uptau^{i}\}
is nondecreasing.

Taking finer partitions and passing to the limit, we get

\max\nolimits_{i}\{|\uptau^{i}-t^{i}|\}\to\penalty 0.

Therefore
➊
and
the point-on-side comparison (
9C
) follows.

9F
.

Consider a partition
0=t^{0}<t^{1}<\dots\penalty<t^{n}=T
, and
let
x^{i}=\penalty\upgamma(t^{i})
. Construct a chain of model triangles
[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{x}^{i-1}}{\tilde{x}^{i}}]={\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}(px^{i-1}x^{i})
with the direction of
[\tilde{p}\tilde{x}^{i}]
turning counterclockwise as
i
grows.
By the angle comparison (
9C
),

\measuredangle[\tilde{x}^{i}\,{}^{\tilde{x}^{i-1}}_{\tilde{p}}]+\measuredangle[\tilde{x}^{i}\,{}^{\tilde{x}^{i+1}}_{\tilde{p}}]\geqslant\penalty\uppi.

➋

Since
\upgamma
is a geodesic,

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upgamma=\sum_{i=1}^{n}|{x^{i-1}}-\penalty{x^{i}}|\leqslant\penalty|{p}-\penalty{x^{0}}|+|{p}-\penalty{x^{n}}|.

➌

By repeated application of Alexandrov’s lemma (
6B
), and inequality
➌
,

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\measuredangle[\tilde{p}\,{}^{\tilde{x}^{i-1}}_{\tilde{x}^{i}}]\leqslant\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{x^{0}}_{x^{n}}\hskip-0.86108pt)\leqslant\penalty\uppi.

Then by
➋
, the polygonal line
\tilde{p}\tilde{x}^{0}\tilde{x}^{1}\dots\penalty\tilde{x}^{n}
are concave with respect to
\tilde{p}
.

Note that under finer partitions, the polygonal line
\tilde{x}^{0}\tilde{x}^{1}\dots\penalty\tilde{x}^{n}
approach the development of
\upgamma
with respect to
p
.
Since the polygonal lines are convex, their lengths converge to the length of
\upgamma
.
Hence the result.
∎

G
Patchwork globalization

If
\mathcal{U}
is a
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space, then it is locally
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
.
The converse does not hold even for complete length space.
For example,
\mathbb{S}^{1}
is locally isometric to
\mathbb{R}
, and so
is locally
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
, but it is easy to find a quadruple of points in
\mathbb{S}^{1}
that violates
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
comparison.

The following theorem was essentially proved by Alexandr Alexandrov
[
17
, Satz 9]
;
it gives a global condition on geodesics that is necessary and sufficient for a locally
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space to be globally
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
.
The proof uses thin-triangle decompositions
and the inheritance lemma (
9D
).

9.30. Patchwork globalization theorem.

For any complete length space
\mathcal{U}
, the following two statements are equivalent:

(a)

\mathcal{U}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
.

(b)

\mathcal{U}
is locally
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
; moreover, pairs of points in
\mathcal{U}
at distance
<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
are joined by unique geodesics, and these geodesics depend continuously on their endpoint pairs.

Note that the implication
9G
\Rightarrow
9G
follows from Theorem
9B
.

9.31. Corollary.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a complete length space
and
\Omega\subset\penalty\mathcal{U}
be an open locally
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
subset.
Then for any point
p\in\penalty\Omega
there is
R>0
such that
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[p,R]
is a convex subset of
\mathcal{U}

and
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[p,R]
is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
.

Proof.

Fix
R>0
such that
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
comparison holds in
\mathrm{B}{}(p,R)
.

We may assume that
\mathrm{B}{}(p,R)\subset\penalty\Omega
and
R<\varpi{}^{{\mathrm{K}}}
.
The same argument as in the proof of the theorem on uniqueness of geodesics (
9B
)
shows that any two points in
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[p,\tfrac{R}{2}]
can be joined by a unique geodesic that depends continuously on the endpoints.

The same argument as in the proof of Corollary
9E
shows that
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[p,\tfrac{R}{2}]
is a convex set.
Then
9G
\Rightarrow
9G
of the patchwork globalization theorem implies that
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[p,\tfrac{R}{2}]
is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
.
∎

The proof of patchwork globalization uses the following construction:

9.32. Definition (Line-of-sight map).

Let
p
be a point and
\upalpha
be a curve of finite length in a length space
\mathcal{U}
.
Let
\bar{\upalpha}:[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
be the constant-speed parametrization of
\upalpha
.
If
\upgamma_{t}\colon[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
is a geodesic path from
p
to
\bar{\upalpha}(t)
, we say that the map
[0,1]\times\penalty[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
defined by

(t,s)\mapsto\penalty\upgamma_{t}(s)

is a
line-of-sight map
for
\upalpha
with respect to
p
.

Note that a line-of-sight map is closely related to geodesic homotopy (Section
9M
).

Proof of
9G
.

It only remains to prove
9G
\Rightarrow
9G
.

Let
[pxy]
be a triangle of perimeter
<2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
in
\mathcal{U}
.
According to
9D
and
9C
, it is sufficient to show the triangle
[{p}{x}{y}]
is
\upkappa
-thin.

Since pairs of points at distance
<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
are joined by unique geodesics and these geodesics depend continuously on their endpoint pairs, there is a unique and continuous line-of-sight map for
[xy]
with respect to
p
.

For a partition

0=\penalty t^{0}<\penalty t^{1}<\penalty\dots\penalty\penalty<\penalty t^{N}=1,

let
x^{i,j}=\upgamma_{t^{i}}(t^{j})
.

Since the line-of-sight map is continuous, we may assume each triangle
[{x^{i,j}}{x^{i,j+1}}{x^{i+1,j+1}}]
and
[{x^{i,j}}{x^{i+1,j}}{x^{i+1,j+1}}]
is
\upkappa
-thin
(see Proposition
9D
).

Now we show that the
\upkappa
-thin property propagates to
[{p}{x}{y}]
, by repeated application of the inheritance lemma (
9D
):

\diamond

First, for fixed
i
,
sequentially applying the lemma shows that the triangles

[{x}{x^{i,1}}{x^{i+1,2}}]
,

[{x}{x^{i,2}}{x^{i+1,2}}]
,

[{x}{x^{i,2}}{x^{i+1,3}}]
,
and so on are
\upkappa
-thin.

In particular, for each
i
, the long triangle
[{x}{x^{i,N}}{x^{i+1,N}}]
is
\upkappa
-thin.

\diamond

Applying the lemma again shows that the triangles
[{x}{x^{0,N}}{x^{2,N}}]
,
[{x}{x^{0,N}}{x^{3,N}}]
, and so on are
\upkappa
-thin.

In particular,
[{p}{x}{y}]=[{p}{x^{0,N}}{x^{N,N}}]
is
\upkappa
-thin.
∎

The following exercise implies that if the space is proper, then one can drop the condition on continuous dependence of geodesics in the formulation of patchwork globalization.

9.33. Exercise.

(a)

Suppose pairs of points in a geodesic space
\mathcal{U}
are joined by unique geodesics.
Show that if
\mathcal{U}
is proper, then
these geodesics depend continuously on their endpoint pairs.

(b)

Construct an example of a complete geodesic space
\mathcal{U}
such that
pairs of points in
\mathcal{U}
are joined by unique geodesics, but
these geodesics do not depend continuously on their endpoint pairs.

H
Angles

Recall that
denotes a selective nonprincipal ultrafilter on
\mathbb{N}
, see Section
4B
.

9.34. Angle semicontinuity.

Suppose
\mathcal{U}_{1},\mathcal{U}_{2},\dots\penalty
is a sequence of
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-geodesic
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
spaces
and
\mathcal{U}_{n}\to\penalty\mathcal{U}_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.
Assume that a sequence of hinges
[p_{n}\,{}^{x_{n}}_{y_{n}}]
in
\mathcal{U}_{n}
converges to a hinge
[p_{\text{\rm}}\,{}^{x_{\text{\rm}}}_{y_{\text{\rm}}}]
in
\mathcal{U}_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.
Then

\measuredangle[p_{\text{\rm}}\,{}^{x_{\text{\rm}}}_{y_{\text{\rm}}}]\geqslant\penalty\lim_{n\to\penalty\text{\rm}}\measuredangle[p_{n}\,{}^{x_{n}}_{y_{n}}].

Proof.

By the angle-sidelength monotonicity (
9C
),

\measuredangle[p_{\text{\rm}}\,{}^{x_{\text{\rm}}}_{y_{\text{\rm}}}]=\inf\left\{\,{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p_{\text{\rm}}\,{}^{\bar{x}_{\text{\rm}}}_{\bar{y}_{\text{\rm}}}\hskip-0.86108pt)}\ {:}\ {\bar{x}_{\text{\rm}}\in\penalty\mathopen{]}p_{\text{\rm}}x_{\text{\rm}}],\ \bar{y}_{\text{\rm}}\in\penalty\mathopen{]}p_{\text{\rm}}y_{\text{\rm}}]}\,\right\}.

For fixed
\bar{x}_{\text{\rm}}\in\penalty\mathopen{]}p_{\text{\rm}}x_{\text{\rm}}]

and
\bar{y}_{\text{\rm}}\in\penalty\mathopen{]}p_{\text{\rm}}x_{\text{\rm}}]
,
choose
\bar{x}_{n}\in\penalty\mathopen{]}px_{n}]
and
\bar{y}_{n}\in\penalty\mathopen{]}py_{n}]
so that
\bar{x}_{n}\to\penalty\bar{x}_{\text{\rm}}

and
\bar{y}_{n}\to\penalty\bar{y}_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.
Clearly

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p_{n}\,{}^{\bar{x}_{n}}_{\bar{y}_{n}}\hskip-0.86108pt)\to\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p_{\text{\rm}}\,{}^{\bar{x}_{\text{\rm}}}_{\bar{y}_{\text{\rm}}}\hskip-0.86108pt)

as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.

By the angle comparison (
9C
),
\measuredangle[p_{n}\,{}^{x_{n}}_{y_{n}}]\leqslant\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p_{n}\,{}^{\bar{x}_{n}}_{\bar{y}_{n}}\hskip-0.86108pt)
.
Hence the result.
∎

Now we verify that the first variation formula
holds in the
CAT
setting.
Compare it to the first variation inequality (
6C
) which holds for general metric spaces and to the strong angle lemma (
8G
) for
CBB
spaces.

9.35. Strong angle lemma.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-geodesic
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space.
Then for any hinge
[p\,{}^{q}_{y}]
in
\mathcal{U}
,
we have

\measuredangle[p\,{}^{q}_{y}]=\lim_{\bar{y}\to\penalty p}\left\{\,{\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{q}_{\bar{y}}\hskip-0.86108pt)}\ {:}\ {\bar{y}\in\penalty\mathopen{]}py]}\,\right\}

➊

for any
\upkappa\in\penalty\mathbb{R}
such that
|{p}-\penalty{q}|<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
.

Proof.

By angle-sidelength monotonicity (
9C
), the right-hand side is defined and bigger than or equal to the left-hand side.

By Lemma
6C
, we may take
\upkappa=0
in
➊
.
By the cosine law and the first variation inequality (
6C
),
the right-hand side is less than or equal to the left-hand side.
∎

9.36. First variation.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-geodesic
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space.
For any nontrivial geodesic
[py]
in
\mathcal{U}
and point
q\neq p
such that
|{p}-\penalty{q}|<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
, we have

|{q}-\penalty{\mathrm{geod}_{[py]}(t)}|=|{q}-\penalty{p}|-t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\measuredangle[p\,{}^{q}_{y}]+o(t).

Proof.

The first variation equation is equivalent to the strong angle lemma (
9H
), as follows from the cosine law.
∎

9.37. First variation (both-endpoints version).

Assume that
\mathcal{U}
is a
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-geodesic
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space.
Then for any nontrivial geodesics
[py]
and
[qz]
in
\mathcal{U}
such that
p\neq q
and
|{p}-\penalty{q}|<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
, we have

|{\mathrm{geod}_{[py]}(t)}-\penalty{\mathrm{geod}_{[qz]}(\uptau)}|=|{q}-\penalty{p}|-t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\measuredangle[p\,{}^{q}_{y}]-\uptau{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\measuredangle[q\,{}^{p}_{z}]+o(t+\uptau).

Proof.

By
9C
,

\displaystyle|{\mathrm{geod}_{[py]}(t)}-\penalty{\mathrm{geod}_{[qz]}(\uptau)}|\geqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty|{q}-\penalty{\mathrm{geod}_{[py]}(t)}|-\uptau{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\measuredangle[q\,{}^{\mathrm{geod}_{[py]}(t)}_{z}]+o(\uptau)\geqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty|{q}-\penalty{p}|-t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\measuredangle[p\,{}^{q}_{y}]+o(t)-\uptau{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\measuredangle[q\,{}^{\mathrm{geod}_{[py]}(t)}_{z}]+o(\uptau)=

\displaystyle=|{q}-\penalty{p}|-t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\measuredangle[p\,{}^{q}_{y}]-\uptau{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\measuredangle[q\,{}^{p}_{z}]+o(t+\uptau).

Here the final equality follows from

\lim_{t\to\penalty 0}\measuredangle[q\,{}^{\mathrm{geod}_{[py]}(t)}_{z}]=\measuredangle[q\,{}^{p}_{z}].

➋

The angle semicontinuity (
9H
) implies “
\leqslant\penalty
” in
➋
, and “
\geqslant\penalty
” holds by the triangle inequality for angles, since angle comparison (
9C
) gives

\lim_{t\to\penalty 0}\measuredangle[q\,{}^{p}_{\mathrm{geod}_{[py]}(t)}]=0.

The opposite inequality follows from
9H
and the triangle inequality

|{\mathrm{geod}_{[py]}(t)}-\penalty{\mathrm{geod}_{[qz]}(\uptau)}|\leqslant\penalty|{\mathrm{geod}_{[py]}(t)}-\penalty{m}|+|{m}-\penalty{\mathrm{geod}_{[qz]}(\uptau)}|,

where
m
is the midpoint of
[pq]
.
∎

We have given elementary proofs of the first-variation statements
9H
,
9H
and
9H
.
Note however that the no-conjugate-point theorem
9J
not only provides proofs of these statements but also extends the statements from geodesics in
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
spaces to local geodesics in locally
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
spaces as follows:

9.38. First variation for local geodesics.

Let
\upgamma_{t}\colon[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
be a continuous family of local geodesics in a locally
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
.
Set

\upalpha(t)=\upgamma_{t}(0)
and
\upbeta(t)=\upgamma_{t}(1)
.
Suppose that
\upgamma_{0}
is unit-speed and
\upalpha^{+}(0)
and
\upbeta^{+}(0)
are defined.
Then

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upgamma_{t}=\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upgamma_{0}-(\langle\upalpha^{+}(0),\upgamma_{0}^{+}(0)\rangle+\langle\upbeta^{+}(0),\upgamma_{0}^{-}(1)\rangle){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}t+o(t).

I
Reshetnyak gluing theorem

The following theorem was proved by Yuriy Reshetnyak
[
139
]
, assuming
\mathcal{U}^{1}
,
\mathcal{U}^{2}
are proper and complete.
In the following form, the theorem appears in the book of Martin Bridson and André Haefliger
[
34
]
.

9.39. Reshetnyak gluing theorem.

Suppose

\mathcal{U}^{1}
,
\mathcal{U}^{2}
are
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-geodesic spaces
with isometric complete
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-convex sets
A^{i}\subset\penalty\mathcal{U}^{i}
. Let
\upiota\colon A^{1}\to\penalty A^{2}
be an isometry.
Let
\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{U}^{1}\sqcup_{\upiota}\mathcal{U}^{2}
;
that is,
\mathcal{W}
is the gluing of
\mathcal{U}^{1}
and
\mathcal{U}^{2}
along
\upiota
(see Section
2E
).

Then:

(a)

Both canonical mappings
\jmath_{i}\colon\mathcal{U}^{i}\to\penalty\mathcal{W}
are distance-preserving
and the images
\jmath_{i}(\mathcal{U}^{i})
are
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-convex subsets in
\mathcal{W}
.

(b)

If
\mathcal{U}^{1},\mathcal{U}^{2}
are
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
,
then so is
\mathcal{W}
.

Proof.

Part
9I

follows directly from
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-convexity of the
A^{i}
.

9I
.

According to
9I
,
we can identify
\mathcal{U}^{i}
with its image
\jmath_{i}(\mathcal{U}^{i})
in
\mathcal{W}
;
in this way, the subsets
A^{i}\subset\penalty\mathcal{U}^{i}
will be identified and denoted further by
A
.
Thus
A=\mathcal{U}^{1}\cap\penalty\mathcal{U}^{2}\subset\penalty\mathcal{W}
,
and
A
is
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-convex in
\mathcal{W}
.

Part
9I
can be reformulated as follows:

9.40. Reformulation of
9I
.

Let
\mathcal{W}
be a
length space having two

\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-convex subsets
\mathcal{U}^{1},\mathcal{U}^{2}\subset\penalty\mathcal{W}
such that

\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{U}^{1}\cup\penalty\mathcal{U}^{2}
.
Assume the subset
A=\mathcal{U}^{1}\cap\penalty\mathcal{U}^{2}
is complete and
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-convex in
\mathcal{W}
, and
\mathcal{U}^{1}
,
\mathcal{U}^{2}
are
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
spaces.
Then
\mathcal{W}
is a
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space.

➊

If
\mathcal{W}
is
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-geodesic, then
\mathcal{W}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
.

Indeed,
according to
9D
,
it is sufficient to show that any triangle
[{x^{0}}{x^{1}}{x^{2}}]
of perimeter
<2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}

in
\mathcal{W}
is
\upkappa
-thin.
This is obviously true if all three points
x^{0}
,
x^{1}
,
x^{2}
lie in a single
\mathcal{U}^{i}
.
Thus, without loss of generality, we may assume that
x^{0}\in\penalty\mathcal{U}^{1}
and
x^{1},x^{2}\in\penalty\mathcal{U}^{2}
.

Choose points
z^{1},z^{2}\in\penalty A=\mathcal{U}^{1}\cap\penalty\mathcal{U}^{2}

lying respectively on the sides
[x^{0}x^{1}],[x^{0}x^{2}]
.
Note that all distances between any pair of points from
x^{0}
,
x^{1}
,
x^{2}
,
z^{1}
,
z^{2}
are less than
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
.
Therefore

\diamond

triangle
[{x^{0}}{z^{1}}{z^{2}}]
lies in
\mathcal{U}^{1}
,

\diamond

both triangles
[{x^{1}}{z^{1}}{z^{2}}]
and
[{x^{1}}{z^{2}}{x^{2}}]
lie in
\mathcal{U}^{2}
.

In particular, each triangle
[{x^{0}}{z^{1}}{z^{2}}]
,

[{x^{1}}{z^{1}}{z^{2}}]
,
[{x^{1}}{z^{2}}{x^{2}}]
is
\upkappa
-thin.

Applying the inheritance lemma for thin triangles (
9D
) twice,
we get that
[{x^{0}}{x^{1}}{z^{2}}]

and consequently
[{x^{0}}{x^{1}}{x^{2}}]
is
\upkappa
-thin.

\vartriangle

➋

\mathcal{W}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
if
\upkappa\leqslant\penalty 0
.

By
9I
it suffices to prove that
\mathcal{W}
is geodesic.

For
p^{1}\in\penalty\mathcal{U}^{1}
,
p^{2}\in\penalty\mathcal{U}^{2}
, we may choose a sequence
z_{n}\in\penalty A
such that
|{p^{1}}-\penalty{z_{n}}|+|{p^{2}}-\penalty{z_{n}}|

converges to
|{p^{1}}-\penalty{p^{2}}|
, and
|{p^{1}}-\penalty{z_{n}}|
and
|{p^{2}}-\penalty{z_{n}}|
converge.
Since
A
is complete, it suffices to show
z_{n}
is a Cauchy sequence.
In that case, the limit point
z
of
z_{n}
satisfies
|{p^{1}}-\penalty{z}|+|{p^{2}}-\penalty{z}|=|{p^{1}}-\penalty{p^{2}}|
, so the geodesics
[p^{1}z]
in
\mathcal{U}^{1}
and
[p^{2}z]
in
\mathcal{U}^{2}
together give a geodesic
[p^{1}p^{2}]
in
\mathcal{U}
.

Suppose
z_{n}
is not a Cauchy sequence.
Then there are subsequences
x_{n}
and
y_{n}
of
z_{n}
satisfying
\lim|{x_{n}}-\penalty{y_{n}}|>0
.
Let
m_{n}
be the midpoint of
[x_{n}y_{n}]
.
Since
|{p^{1}}-\penalty{m_{n}}|+|{p^{2}}-\penalty{m_{n}}|\geqslant\penalty|{p^{1}}-\penalty{p^{2}}|
, and
|{p^{1}}-\penalty{x_{n}}|+|{p^{2}}-\penalty{x_{n}}|
and
|{p^{1}}-\penalty{y_{n}}|+|{p^{2}}-\penalty{y_{n}}|

converge to
|{p^{1}}-\penalty{p^{2}}|
, then for any
\upvarepsilon>0
, we may assume (taking subsequences and possibly relabeling
p^{1}
and
p^{2}
)

|{p^{1}}-\penalty{m_{n}}|\geqslant\penalty|{p^{1}}-\penalty{x_{n}}|-\upvarepsilon,\qquad|{p^{1}}-\penalty{m_{n}}|\geqslant\penalty|{p^{1}}-\penalty{y_{n}}|-\upvarepsilon.

Since triangle
[{p^{1}}{x_{n}}{y_{n}}]
is thin, the analogous inequalities hold for the Euclidean model triangle
[{\tilde{p}^{1}}{\tilde{x}_{n}}{\tilde{y}_{n}}]
.
Then there is a nondegenerate limit triangle
[{p}{x}{y}]
in the Euclidean plane satisfying
|{p}-\penalty{x}|=|{p}-\penalty{y}|\leqslant\penalty|{p}-\penalty{m}|
where
m
is the midpoint of
[xy]
. This contradiction proves the claim.

\vartriangle

Finally suppose
\upkappa>0
; by rescaling, take
\upkappa=1
. Consider the Euclidean cones
\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits\mathcal{U}^{i}
(see Section
6E
).
By Theorem
11D
,
\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits\mathcal{U}^{i}
is a
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
space for
i=1,2
.

Geodesics contained in the complement of the tip of
\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits\mathcal{U}^{i}
project to geodesics of length
<\uppi
in
\mathcal{U}^{i}
.
It follows that
\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits A
is convex in
\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits\mathcal{U}^{1}
and
\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits\mathcal{U}^{2}
.
By the cone distance formula,
\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits A
is complete since
A
is complete.

Gluing along
\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits A
and applying
9I
and
9I
for
\upkappa=0
, we find that

\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits\mathcal{W}
is a
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
space. By Theorem
11D
,
\mathcal{W}
is a
\text{\rm CAT}({1})
space.
∎
