J
Space of geodesics

In this section we prove a no-conjugate-point theorem for spaces with upper curvature bounds and derive from it a number of statements about local geodesics.
These statements will be used to prove the Hadamard–Cartan theorem (
9M
) and the lifting globalization theorem (
9K
), in much the same way as the exponential map is used in Riemannian geometry.

9.45. Proposition.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a locally
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space.
Let
\upgamma_{n}\colon[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
be a sequence of local geodesic paths converging to a path
\upgamma_{\infty}\colon[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
.
Then
\upgamma_{\infty}
is a local geodesic path.
Moreover

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upgamma_{n}\to\penalty\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upgamma_{\infty}

as
n\to\penalty\infty
.

Proof.

Fix
t\in\penalty[0,1]
.
By Corollary
9G
, we may choose
R
satisfying
0<R<\varpi{}^{{\mathrm{K}}}
,
and such that
the ball
\mathcal{B}=\mathrm{B}{}(\upgamma_{\infty}(t),R)
is a convex subset of
\mathcal{U}
and forms a
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space.

A local geodesic segment with length less than
R/2
that intersects
\mathrm{B}{}(\upgamma_{\infty}(t),R/2)
cannot leave
\mathcal{B}
, and hence is minimizing by Corollary
9D
.
In particular, for all sufficiently large
n
,
if subsegment of
\upgamma_{n}
has length less than
R/2
and contains
\upgamma_{n}(t)
, then it is a geodesic.

Since
\mathcal{B}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
, geodesic segments in
\mathcal{B}
depend uniquely and continuously on their endpoint pairs by Theorem
9B
.
Thus there is a subinterval
\mathbb{I}
of
[0,1]

that contains a neighborhood of
t
in
[0,1]

and such that
\upgamma_{n}|_{\mathbb{I}}
is minimizing for all large
n
.
It follows that the restriction
\upgamma_{\infty}|_{\mathbb{I}}
is a geodesic,
and therefore
\upgamma_{\infty}
is a local geodesic.
∎

The following theorem was proved by the first author and Richard Bishop
[
6
]
.
In analogy with Riemannian geometry, the main statement of the following theorem could be restated as:

In a space of curvature
\leqslant\penalty\upkappa
, two points cannot be conjugate along a local geodesic of length
<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
.

9.46. No-conjugate-point theorem.

Suppose
\mathcal{U}
is a locally complete, length, locally
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space.
Let
\upgamma\colon[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
be a local geodesic path with length
<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
.
Then for some neighborhoods
\Omega^{0}\ni\upgamma(0)
and
\Omega^{1}\ni\upgamma(1)
,
there is a unique continuous map from the direct product
\Omega^{0}\times\penalty\Omega^{1}\times\penalty[0,1]
to
\mathcal{U}
,

(x,y,t)\mapsto\penalty\upgamma_{xy}(t),

such that

\upgamma_{xy}\colon[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
is a local geodesic path with

\upgamma_{xy}(0)=x
and

\upgamma_{xy}(1)=y
for each
(x,y)\in\penalty\Omega^{0}\times\penalty\Omega^{1}
,
and the family
\upgamma_{xy}
contains
\upgamma
.
Moreover, we can assume that the map

(x,y,t)\mapsto\penalty\upgamma_{xy}(t)\colon\Omega^{0}\times\penalty\Omega^{1}\times\penalty[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{U}

is
{\ell}
-Lipschitz
for any

{\ell}>\max\left\{\,{\tfrac{\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}r}{\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}\ell}}\ {:}\ {0\leqslant\penalty r\leqslant\penalty\ell}\,\right\}
.

9.47. Patchwork along a geodesic.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a locally complete, length, locally
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space,
and
\upalpha\colon[a,b]\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
be a local geodesic.

Then there is a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space
\mathcal{N}

with an open set
\hat{\Omega}\subset\penalty\mathcal{N}
,
a local geodesic
\hat{\upalpha}\colon[a,b]\to\penalty\hat{\Omega}
,
and an open locally distance-preserving map

\Phi\colon\hat{\Omega}\looparrowright\mathcal{U}
such that

\Phi\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}=\upalpha
.

Moreover if
\upalpha
is simple, then one can assume in addition that
\Phi
is an open embedding;
thus
\hat{\Omega}
is locally isometric to a neighborhood of
\Omega=\Phi(\hat{\Omega})
of
\upalpha
.

This lemma and its proof were suggested by Alexander Lytchak.
The proof proceeds by piecing together
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
neighborhoods of points on a curve to construct a new
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space.
Exercise
9O
is inspired by the original idea of the proof of the no-conjugate-point theorem (
9J
) given in
[
6
]
.

Proof.

According to Corollary
9G
,
we can choose
r>0
such that
for any
t\in\penalty[a,b]
the closed ball

\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[\upalpha(t),r]
is a convex set that forms a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space.

Choose balls
\mathcal{B}_{i}=\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[\upalpha(t_{i}),r]

for some partition
a=\penalty t_{0}<t_{1}<\penalty\dots\penalty\penalty<\penalty t_{n}=\penalty b

in such a way that

\mathop{\rm Interior}\nolimits\mathcal{B}_{i}\supset\penalty\upalpha([t_{i-1},t_{i}])

for all
i>0
.
We can assume in addition that
\mathcal{B}_{i-1}\cap\penalty\mathcal{B}_{i+1}\subset\penalty\mathcal{B}_{i}
if
0<i<n
.

Consider the disjoint union
\bigsqcup_{i}\mathcal{B}_{i}=\left\{\,{(i,x)}\ {:}\ {x\in\penalty\mathcal{B}_{i}}\,\right\}
with the minimal equivalence relation
\sim
such that
(i,x)\sim(i-1,x)
for all
i>0
.
Let
\mathcal{N}
be the space obtained by gluing the
\mathcal{B}_{i}
along
\sim
.
Note that
A_{i}=\mathcal{B}_{i}\cap\penalty\mathcal{B}_{i-1}
is convex in
\mathcal{B}_{i}
and in
\mathcal{B}_{i-1}
.
Applying the Reshetnyak gluing theorem (
9I
) several times,
we conclude that
\mathcal{N}
is a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space.

For
t\in\penalty[t_{i-1},t_{i}]
, let
\hat{\upalpha}(t)
be the equivalence class of
(i,\upalpha(t))
in
\mathcal{N}
.
Let
\hat{\Omega}
be the
\upvarepsilon
-neighborhood of
\hat{\upalpha}
in
\mathcal{N}
, where
\upvarepsilon>0
is chosen so that
\mathrm{B}{}(\upalpha(t),\upvarepsilon)\subset\penalty\mathcal{B}_{i}
for all
t\in\penalty[t_{i-1},t_{i}]
.

Define
\Phi\colon\hat{\Omega}\to\penalty\mathcal{U}

by sending the equivalence class of
(i,x)
to
x
.
It is straightforward to check that
\Phi\colon\mathcal{N}\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
,
\hat{\upalpha}\colon[a,b]\to\penalty\mathcal{N}
and
\hat{\Omega}\subset\penalty\mathcal{N}
satisfy the conclusion of the main part of the lemma.

To prove the final statement in the lemma,
we only have to choose
\upvarepsilon>0
so that in addition,
|{\upalpha(\uptau)}-\penalty{\upalpha(\uptau^{\prime})}|>2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon
if
\uptau\leqslant\penalty t_{i-1}
and
t_{i}\leqslant\penalty\uptau^{\prime}
for some
i
.
∎

Proof of
9J
.

Apply patchwork along
\upgamma
(
9J
).
∎

The No-conjugate-point theorem (
9J
) allows us to move a local geodesic
so that its endpoints follow given trajectories.
The following corollary describes how this process might terminate.

9.48. Corollary.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a locally complete, length, locally
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space.
Suppose
\upgamma\colon[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
is a local geodesic with length
<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
. Let
\upalpha^{i}\colon[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
, for
i=0,1
, be curves starting at
\upgamma(0)
and
\upgamma(1)
respectively.

Then there is a uniquely determined pair consisting of an interval
\mathbb{I}
satisfying
0\in\penalty\mathbb{I}\subset\penalty[0,1]
, and a continuous family of local geodesics
\upgamma_{t}\colon[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
for
t\in\penalty\mathbb{I}
, such that

(a)

\upgamma_{0}=\upgamma
,
\upgamma_{t}(0)=\upalpha^{0}(t)
,
\upgamma_{t}(1)=\upalpha^{1}(t)
, and
\upgamma_{t}
has length
<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
,

(b)

if
\mathbb{I}\neq[0,1]
, then
\mathbb{I}=[0,a)
, where either
\upgamma_{t}
converges uniformly to a local geodesic
\upgamma_{a}
of length
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
, or
for some fixed
s\in\penalty[0,1]
the curve
\upgamma_{t}(s):[0,a)\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
is a Lipschitz curve with no limit
as
t\to\penalty a-
.

Proof.
Uniqueness follows from Theorem
9J
.

Let
\mathbb{I}
be the maximal interval for which there is a family
\upgamma_{t}
satisfying condition
9J
.
By Theorem
9J
, such an interval exists and is open in
[0,1]
. Suppose
\mathbb{I}\neq[0,1]
.
Then
\mathbb{I}=[0,a)
for
0<a\leqslant\penalty 1
.

For each fixed
s\in\penalty[0,1]
, define the curve
\upalpha_{s}:[0,a)\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
by
\upalpha_{s}(t)=\penalty\upgamma_{t}(s)
.
By Theorem
9J
,
each
\upalpha_{s}
is locally Lipschitz.

If
\upalpha_{s}
for some value of
s
does not converge as
t\to\penalty a-
, then condition
9J
holds.
If each
\upalpha_{s}
converges as
t\to\penalty a-
, then
\upgamma_{t}
converges as
t\to\penalty a-
, say to
\upgamma_{a}
.
By Proposition
9J
,
\upgamma_{a}
is a local geodesic and

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upgamma_{t}\to\penalty\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upgamma_{a}\leqslant\penalty\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}.

By maximality of
\mathbb{I}
,
\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upgamma_{a}=\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
and so condition
9J
again holds.
∎

9.49. Corollary.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a complete locally
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
length space, and

\upalpha\colon[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
be a path of length
<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
that starts at
p
and ends at
q
.
Then:

(a)

There is a unique homotopy of local geodesic paths
\upgamma_{t}\colon[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{U}

such that
\upgamma_{0}(t)=\upgamma_{t}(0)=p
and
\upgamma_{t}(1)=\upalpha(t)
for any
t
.

(b)

For any
t\in\penalty[0,1]
,

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upgamma_{t}\leqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits(\upalpha|_{[0,t]}),

and equality holds for given
t
if and only if the restriction
\upalpha|_{[0,t]}
is a reparametrization of
\upgamma_{t}
.

Moreover, instead of completeness of
\mathcal{U}
, one can assume that the subspace

W=\left\{\,{x\in\penalty\mathcal{U}}\ {:}\ {|{x}-\penalty{p}|+|{x}-\penalty{q}|\leqslant\penalty\ell}\,\right\}

is complete.

Proof.

By Corollary
9J
, taking
\upalpha^{0}(t)=p
and
\upalpha^{1}(t)=\upalpha(t)
for all
t\in\penalty[0,1]
, there is an interval
\mathbb{I}
such that
9J
holds for all
t\in\penalty\mathbb{I}
, and either
\mathbb{I}=[0,1]
or
\mathbb{I}=[0,a)
for
a\leqslant\penalty 1
.

By patchwork along a curve (
9J
), the values of
t
for which condition
9J
holds form an open subset of
\mathbb{I}
containing
0
; clearly this subset is also closed in
\mathbb{I}
.
Therefore
9J
holds on all of
\mathbb{I}
.

Corollary
9J
implies that

\mathbb{I}=[0,1]
.
Indeed if
\mathbb{I}=[0,a)
, then either
\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upgamma_{t}\to\penalty\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
as
t\to\penalty a-
,
or for some fixed
s\in\penalty[0,1]
the Lipschitz curve
\upgamma_{t}(s)
has no limit as
t\to\penalty a-
.
Since
\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upalpha<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
,
9J
implies that neither of these is possible.
∎

K
Lifting globalization

The Hadamard–Cartan theorem (
9M
) states that
the universal metric cover of a complete locally
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
space is
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
.
The lifting globalization theorem gives an appropriate generalization of the above statement to arbitrary curvature bounds;
it could be also described as a global version of Gauss’s lemma.

9.50. Lifting globalization theorem.

Suppose
\mathcal{U}
is a complete length locally
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space and
p\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
.
Then there is a complete
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
length space
\mathcal{B}
,
with a point
\hat{p}
such that
there is a locally distance-preserving map
\Phi\colon\mathcal{B}\to\penalty\mathcal{U}

such that
\Phi(\hat{p})=p
and the following lifting property holds:
for any path
\upalpha\colon[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
with
\upalpha(0)=p
and
\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upalpha<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}/2
,
there is a unique path
\hat{\upalpha}\colon[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{B}
such that
\hat{\upalpha}(0)=\hat{p}

and
\Phi\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}\equiv\upalpha
.

Note that the lifting property implies that
\Phi(\mathcal{B})\supset\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(p,\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}/2)
and by completeness
\Phi(\mathcal{B})\supset\penalty\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[p,\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}/2]
.
Also, since
\mathcal{B}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
, the closed ball
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[\hat{p},\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}]_{\mathcal{B}}
is a weakly convex set in
\mathcal{B}
(see
9E
);
in particular
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[\hat{p},\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}]_{\mathcal{B}}
is a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space.
Therefore we can assume in addition that
|{\hat{p}}-\penalty{\hat{x}}|\leqslant\penalty\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}/2
for any
\hat{x}\in\penalty\mathcal{B}
;
or equivalently

\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[\hat{p},\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}]_{\mathcal{B}}=\mathcal{B}.

Before proving the theorem we state and prove its corollary.

9.51. Corollary.

Suppose
\mathcal{U}
is a complete length locally
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space.
Then for any
p\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
there is
\uprho_{p}>0

such that
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[p,\uprho_{p}]
is a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space.

Moreover, we can assume that
\uprho_{p}<\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}

for any
p
and the function
p\mapsto\penalty\uprho_{p}
is 1-Lipschitz.

Proof.

Assume
\Phi\colon\mathcal{B}\to\penalty\mathcal{U}

and
\hat{p}\in\penalty\mathcal{B}

are provided by the lifting globalization theorem
(
9K
).

Since
\Phi
is local isometry,
we can choose
r>0
so that the restriction of
\Phi
to
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[\hat{p},r]
is distance-preserving.
By the lifting globalization, the image
\Phi(\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[\hat{p},r])
coincides with the ball

\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[p,r]
.
This proves the first part of the theorem.

To prove the second part, let us choose
\uprho_{p}
to be the maximal value
\leqslant\penalty\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}
such that
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[p,\uprho_{p}]
is a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space.
By Corollary
9E
, the ball

\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[q,\uprho_{p}-|{p}-\penalty{q}|]

is weakly convex in
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[p,\uprho_{p}]
.
Therefore

\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[q,\uprho_{p}-|{p}-\penalty{q}|]

is a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space
for any
q\in\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(p,\uprho_{p})
.
In particular,
\uprho_{q}\geqslant\penalty\uprho_{p}-|{p}-\penalty{q}|
for any
p,q\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
.
Hence the second statement follows.
∎

The proof of the lifting globalization theorem relies heavily on the properties of the space of local geodesic paths discussed in Section
9J
.
The following lemma is a key step in the proof;
it was proved by the first author and Richard Bishop
[
4
]
.

9.52. Radial lemma.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a length locally
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space,
and suppose
p\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
,
R\leqslant\penalty\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
.
Assume the ball
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[p,\bar{R}]
is complete for any
\bar{R}<R
, and there is a unique geodesic path,
\mathrm{path}_{[px]}
, from
p
to any point
x\in\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(p,R)

that depends continuously on
x
.
Then
\mathrm{B}{}(p,\tfrac{R}{2})
is a
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-geodesic
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume
\mathcal{U}=\mathrm{B}{}(p,R)
.

Set
f=\mathrm{md}^{\upkappa}\circ\penalty\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}{}{}
. Let us show that

f^{\prime\prime}+\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f\geqslant\penalty 1.

➊

Fix
z\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
.
We will apply the no-conjugate-point theorem (
9J
) for the unique geodesic path
\upgamma

from
p
to
z
.
The notations
\Omega^{0}
,

\Omega^{1}
,

\upgamma_{xy}
,
\mathcal{N}
,
\hat{x}
,
\hat{y}
will be as in the formulation of the lifting globalization theorem (
9K
);
in particular,
z\in\penalty\Omega^{1}
.

By assumption,

\upgamma_{py}=\mathrm{path}_{[py]}
for any
y\in\penalty\Omega^{1}
.
Consequently,

f(y)=\penalty\mathrm{md}^{\upkappa}|{\hat{p}}-\penalty{\hat{y}}|_{\mathcal{N}}
.
Applying the function comparison (
9E
) in
\mathcal{N}
,
we have that
f^{\prime\prime}+\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f\geqslant\penalty 1
in
\Omega^{1}
;
whence
➊
follows.

\vartriangle

Fix
r<\tfrac{R}{2}
. Proving the following claim takes most of the remaining proof:

➋

\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[p,r]
is a convex set in
\mathcal{U}
.

Choose arbitrary
x,z\in\penalty\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[p,r]
.
First note that
➊
implies the following claim.

➌

If
\upgamma\colon[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{U}

is a local geodesic path from
x
to
z
and

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upgamma<\penalty\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
,
then
\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upgamma\leqslant\penalty 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}r

and
\upgamma
lies completely in
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[p,r]
.

Note that
|{x}-\penalty{z}|<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
.
Thus to prove Claim
9K
, it is sufficient to show that there is a geodesic path from
x
to
z
.
Note that by assumption
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[p,2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}r]
is complete.
Therefore Corollary
9J
implies the following:

➍

Given a path
\upalpha\colon[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
from
x
to
z
with
\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upalpha<2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}r
,
there is a local geodesic path
\upgamma
from
x
to
z
such that

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upgamma\leqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upalpha.

Further, let us prove the following:

➎

There is a unique local geodesic path
\upgamma_{xz}
in
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[p,r]
from
x
to
z
.

Denote by
\Delta_{xz}
the set of all local geodesic paths in
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[p,r]
from
x
to
z
.
By Corollary
9J
, there is a bijection
\Delta_{xz}\to\penalty\Delta_{pp}
.
According to
➊
,

\Delta_{pp}
contains only the constant path.
Claim
9K
follows.

Note that
claims
9K
,

9K

and
9K

imply that
\upgamma_{xz}
is minimizing; hence Claim
9K
.

Further, Claim
9K
and the no-conjugate-point theorem (
9J
) together
imply that the map
(x,z)\mapsto\penalty\upgamma_{xz}
is continuous.

Therefore by the patchwork globalization theorem (
9G
),

\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[p,r]
is a
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-geodesic
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space.

Since

\mathrm{B}{}(p,R)=\bigcup_{r<R}\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[p,r],

then
\mathrm{B}{}(p,R)
is convex in
\mathcal{U}
and

\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
comparison holds for any quadruple in
\mathrm{B}{}(p,R)
.
Therefore
\mathrm{B}{}(p,\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}/2)
is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
.
∎

In the following proof, we construct a space
\mathfrak{G}_{p}
of local geodesic paths that start at
p
.
The space
\mathfrak{G}_{p}
comes with
a marked point
\hat{p}

and the endpoint map
\Phi\colon\mathfrak{G}_{p}\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
.
One can think of
the map
\Phi
as an analog of the exponential map
\exp_{p}
in the Riemannian geometry;
in this case,
the space
\mathfrak{G}_{p}
corresponds to the ball of radius
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
in the tangent space at
p
, equipped with the metric pulled back by
\exp_{p}
.

We are going to set
\mathcal{B}=\mathrm{B}{}(\hat{p},\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}/2)\subset\penalty\mathfrak{G}_{p}
,
and use the radial lemma (
9K
) to prove that
\mathcal{B}
is a
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-geodesic
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space.

Proof of
9K
.

Suppose
\hat{\upgamma}
is a homotopy of local geodesic paths that start at
p
. Thus the map

\hat{\upgamma}\colon(t,\uptau)\mapsto\penalty\hat{\upgamma}_{t}(\uptau)\colon[0,1]\times\penalty[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{U}

is continuous,
and the following holds for each
t
:

\diamond

\hat{\upgamma}_{t}(0)=p
,

\diamond

\hat{\upgamma}_{t}\colon[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
is a local geodesic path in
\mathcal{U}
.

Denote by
\upvartheta(\hat{\upgamma})
the length traced by the ends of
\hat{\upgamma}_{t}
;
that is,
\upvartheta(\hat{\upgamma})
is the length of the path
t\mapsto\penalty\hat{\upgamma}_{t}(1)
.

Let
\mathfrak{G}_{p}
be the set of all local geodesic paths
with length
<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
in
\mathcal{U}
that start at
p
.
Denote by
\hat{p}\in\penalty\mathfrak{G}_{p}
the constant path
\hat{p}(t)\equiv p
.
Given
\upalpha,\upbeta\in\penalty\mathfrak{G}_{p}
, define

|{\upalpha}-\penalty{\upbeta}|_{\mathfrak{G}_{p}}=\inf_{\hat{\upgamma}}\{\upvartheta(\hat{\upgamma})\},

with the exact lower bound taken along all homotopies

\hat{\upgamma}\colon[0,1]\times\penalty[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{U}

such that

\hat{\upgamma}_{0}=\upalpha
,

\hat{\upgamma}_{1}=\upbeta

and
\hat{\upgamma}_{t}\in\penalty\mathfrak{G}_{p}
for all
t\in\penalty[0,1]
.

By the no-conjugate-point theorem (
9J
), we have
|{\upalpha}-\penalty{\upbeta}|_{\mathfrak{G}_{p}}>0
for distinct
\upalpha
and
\upbeta
;
that is,

➏

|{{*}}-\penalty{{*}}|_{\mathfrak{G}_{p}}
is a metric on
\mathfrak{G}_{p}
.

Further, again from the no-conjugate-point theorem (
9J
), we have

➐

The map

\Phi\colon\upxi\mapsto\penalty\upxi(1)\colon\mathfrak{G}_{p}\to\penalty\mathcal{U}

is a local isometry.
In particular,
\mathfrak{G}_{p}
is locally
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
.

Let
\upalpha\colon[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
be a path,
\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upalpha<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
, and
\upalpha(0)=p
.
The homotopy constructed in Corollary
9J
can be regarded as a path in
\mathfrak{G}_{p}
, say
\hat{\upalpha}\colon[0,1]\to\penalty\mathfrak{G}_{p}
,
such that
\hat{\upalpha}(0)=\hat{p}
and
\Phi\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}=\upalpha
;
in particular
\hat{\upalpha}_{t}(1)\equiv\upalpha(t)
for any
t
.
By
9K
,

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits(\hat{\upalpha})_{\mathfrak{G}_{p}}=\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits(\upalpha)_{\mathcal{U}}.

Moreover, it follows that
\upalpha
is a local geodesic path of
\mathcal{U}
if and only if
\hat{\upalpha}
is a local geodesic path of
\mathfrak{G}_{p}
.

Further, from Corollary
9J
,
for any
\upxi\in\penalty\mathfrak{G}_{p}
and path
\hat{\upalpha}\colon[0,1]\to\penalty\mathfrak{G}_{p}
from
\hat{p}
to
\upxi
,
we have

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\hat{\upalpha}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\Phi\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}\geqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upxi=

\displaystyle=\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\hat{\upxi}

where equality holds only if
\hat{\upalpha}
is a reparametrization of
\hat{\upxi}
.
In particular,

|{\hat{p}}-\penalty{\upxi}|_{\mathfrak{G}_{p}}=\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upxi

➑

and

\hat{\upxi}\colon[0,1]\to\penalty\mathfrak{G}_{p}
is the unique geodesic path from
\hat{p}
to
\upxi
.
Clearly, the map
\upxi\mapsto\penalty\hat{\upxi}
is continuous.

By
➑
and Proposition
9J
,

➒

For any
\bar{R}<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
, the closed ball

\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[\hat{p},\bar{R}]
in
\mathfrak{G}_{p}
is complete.

Take
\mathrm{B}{}(\hat{p},\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}/2)
and
\Phi
constructed above.
According to the radial lemma (
9K
),
\mathrm{B}{}(\hat{p},\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}/2)
is a
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-geodesic
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space.
The map
\Phi
extends to its completion
\mathcal{B}=\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[\hat{p},\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}/2]
.
All the remaining statements are already proved.
∎

L
Reshetnyak majorization

9.53. Definition.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a metric space,

\tilde{\upalpha}
be a simple closed curve of finite length in
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
,
and
D\subset\penalty\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
be a closed region bounded by
\tilde{\upalpha}
.
A length-nonincreasing map
F\colon D\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
is called
majorizing
if it is length-preserving on
\tilde{\upalpha}
.

In this case, we say that
D

majorizes
the curve
\upalpha=F\circ\penalty\tilde{\upalpha}
under the map
F
.

The following proposition is a consequence of the definition.

9.54. Proposition.

Let
\upalpha
be a closed curve in a metric space
\mathcal{X}
.
Suppose
D\subset\penalty\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
majorizes
\upalpha
under
F\colon D\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
.
Then any geodesic subarc of
\upalpha
is the image under
F
of a subarc of
\partial_{\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})}D
that is geodesic in the length metric of
D
.

In particular, if
D
is convex, then the corresponding subarc is a geodesic in
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
.

Proof.
For a geodesic subarc
\upgamma\colon[a,b]\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
of
\upalpha=F\circ\penalty\tilde{\upalpha}
, set

\displaystyle\tilde{r}

\displaystyle=|{\tilde{\upgamma}(a)}-\penalty{\tilde{\upgamma}(b)}|_{D},

\displaystyle\tilde{\upgamma}

\displaystyle=(F|_{\partial D})^{-1}\circ\penalty\upgamma,

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle=\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upgamma,

\displaystyle\tilde{s}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\tilde{\upgamma}.

Then

\tilde{r}\geqslant\penalty r=s=\tilde{s}\geqslant\penalty\tilde{r}.

Therefore
\tilde{s}=\tilde{r}
.
∎

9.55. Corollary.

Let
[{p}{x}{y}]
be a triangle of perimeter
<2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
in a metric space
\mathcal{X}
. Assume a convex region
D\subset\penalty\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
majorizes
[{p}{x}{y}]
.
Then
D=\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{x}}{\tilde{y}}]
for a model triangle
[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{x}}{\tilde{y}}]={\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}(pxy)
, and the majorizing map sends
\tilde{p}
,
\tilde{x}
and
\tilde{y}
respectively to
p
,
x
and
y
.

Now we come to the main theorem of this section.

9.56. Majorization theorem.

Any closed curve
\upalpha
with length smaller than
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
in a
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-geodesic
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space is majorized by a convex region in
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
.

This theorem was proved by Yuriy Reshetnyak
[
139
]
;
our proof uses a trick that we learned from the lectures of Werner Ballmann
[
21
]
.
Another proof can be built on Kirszbraun’s theorem (
10D
), but it works only for complete spaces.

The case when
\upalpha
is a triangle, say
[{p}{x}{y}]
, is the base and is nontrivial.
In this case, by Corollary
9L
, the majorizing convex region has to be isometric to
\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{x}}{\tilde{y}}]
, where
[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{x}}{\tilde{y}}]={\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}(pxy)
.
There is a majorizing map for
[{p}{x}{y}]
whose image
W
is the image of the line-of-sight map (definition
9G
) for
[xy]
from
p
,
but as one can see from the following example, the line-of-sight map is not majorizing in general.

Example.
Let
\mathcal{Q}
be a solid quadrangle
[pxzy]
in
\mathbb{E}^{2}
formed by two congruent triangles, which is non-convex at
z
(as in the picture).
Equip
\mathcal{Q}
with the length metric.
Then
\mathcal{Q}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({0})

by Reshetnyak gluing (
9I
).
For triangle
{[{p}{x}{y}]}_{\mathcal{Q}}
in
\mathcal{Q}
and its model triangle
[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{x}}{\tilde{y}}]
in
\mathbb{E}^{2}
,
we have

|{\tilde{x}}-\penalty{\tilde{y}}|=|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\mathcal{Q}}=|{x}-\penalty{z}|+|{z}-\penalty{y}|.

Then the map
F
defined by matching line-of-sight parameters satisfies
F(\tilde{x})=x
and
|{x}-\penalty{F(\tilde{w})}|>|{\tilde{x}}-\penalty{\tilde{w}}|
if
\tilde{w}
is near the midpoint
\tilde{z}
of
[\tilde{x}\tilde{y}]
and lies on
[\tilde{p}\tilde{z}]
.
Indeed, by the first variation formula (
8G
), for
\upvarepsilon=1-s
we have

|{\tilde{x}}-\penalty{\tilde{w}}|=|{\tilde{x}}-\penalty{\tilde{\upgamma}_{\frac{1}{2}}(s)}|=|{x}-\penalty{z}|+o(\upvarepsilon)

and

|{x}-\penalty{F(\tilde{w})}|=|{x}-\penalty{\upgamma_{\frac{1}{2}}(s)}|=|{x}-\penalty{z}|-\upvarepsilon{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\measuredangle[z\,{}^{p}_{x}]+o(\upvarepsilon).

Thus
F
is not majorizing.

In the following proofs,
x^{1}\dots\penalty x^{n}
(
n\geqslant\penalty 3
) denotes a polygonal line
x^{1},\dots\penalty,x^{n}
, and
[x^{1}\dots\penalty x^{n}]
denotes the corresponding (closed) polygon.
For a subset
R
of the ambient metric space,
we denote by
[x^{1}\dots\penalty x^{n}]_{R}
a polygon in the length metric of
R
.

Our first lemma gives a model space construction based on repeated application of Lemma
9D
from the proof of the inheritance.
Recall that convex and concave curves with respect to a point are defined in
8E
.

9.57. Lemma.

In
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
, let

\upbeta
be a curve from
x
to
y

that is concave with respect to
p
.
Let
D
be the subgraph of
\upbeta
with respect to
p
.
Assume

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upbeta+\penalty|{p}-\penalty{x}|+|{p}-\penalty{y}|<2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}.

(a)

Then
\upbeta
forms a geodesic
[xy]_{D}
in
D
and therefore
\upbeta
,
[px]
and
[py]
form a triangle

{[{p}{x}{y}]}_{D}
in the length metric of
D
.

(b)

Let
[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{x}}{\tilde{y}}]
be the model triangle for

{[{p}{x}{y}]}_{D}
.
Then there is a short map
G\colon\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{x}}{\tilde{y}}]\to\penalty D
such that
\tilde{p}\mapsto\penalty p
,
\tilde{x}\mapsto\penalty x
,
\tilde{y}\mapsto\penalty y
, and
G
is length-preserving on each side of
[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{x}}{\tilde{y}}]
.
In particular,
\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{x}}{\tilde{y}}]
majorizes triangle
[pxy]_{D}
in
D
under
G
.

Proof.

We prove the lemma for a polygonal line
\upbeta
;
the general case then follows by approximation.
Namely, since
\upbeta
is concave
it can be approximated by polygonal lines that are concave with respect to
p
,
with their lengths converging to
\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upbeta
.
Passing to a partial limit we will obtain the needed map
G
.

Suppose
\upbeta=x^{0}x^{1}\dots\penalty x^{n}
is a polygonal line with
x^{0}=x
and
x^{n}=y
.
Consider a sequence of polygonal lines
\upbeta_{i}=x^{0}x^{1}\dots\penalty x^{i-1}y_{i}
such that
|{p}-\penalty{y_{i}}|=|{p}-\penalty{y}|
and

\upbeta_{i}
has same length as
\upbeta
;
that is,

|{x^{i-1}}-\penalty{y_{i}}|=|{x^{i-1}}-\penalty{x^{i}}|+|{x^{i}}-\penalty{x^{i+1}}|+\dots\penalty+|{x^{n-1}}-\penalty{x^{n}}|.

Clearly
\upbeta_{n}=\upbeta
.
Sequentially applying Alexandrov’s lemma (
6B
) shows that each of the polygonal lines
\upbeta_{n-1},\upbeta_{n-2},\dots\penalty,\upbeta_{1}
is concave with respect to
p
.
Let
D_{i}
be the subgraph of
\upbeta_{i}
with respect to
p
.
Applying Lemma
9D
gives a short map
G_{i}\colon D_{i}\to\penalty D_{i+1}
that maps
y_{i}\mapsto\penalty y_{i+1}
and does not move
p
and
x
(in fact,
G_{i}
is the identity everywhere except on
\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits[{p}{x^{i-1}}{y_{i}}]
).
Thus the composition

G_{n-1}\circ\penalty\dots\penalty\circ\penalty G_{1}\colon D_{1}\to\penalty D_{n}

is short.
The result follows since
D_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it iso}}}\penalty\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{x}}{\tilde{y}}]
.∎

9.58. Lemma.

Let
[{p}{x}{y}]
be a triangle of perimeter
<2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
in a
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-geodesic
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space
\mathcal{U}
.
In
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
, let
\tilde{\upgamma}
be the
\upkappa
-development of
[xy]
with respect to
p
, where
\tilde{\upgamma}
has basepoint
\tilde{p}
and subgraph
D
.
Consider the map
H\colon D\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
that sends the point with parameter
(t,s)
under the line-of-sight map for
\tilde{\upgamma}
with respect to
\tilde{p}
, to the point with the same parameter under the line-of-sight map
f
for
[xy]
with respect to
p
.
Then
H
is length-nonincreasing.
In particular,
D
majorizes triangle
[{p}{x}{y}]
.

Proof.

Let
\upgamma=\mathrm{geod}_{[xy]}
and
T=|{x}-\penalty{y}|
.
As in the proof of the development criterion (
9F
), take a partition

0=t^{0}<t^{1}<\dots\penalty<t^{n}=T,

and set
x^{i}=\upgamma(t^{i})
.
Construct a chain of model triangles
[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{x}^{i-1}}{\tilde{x}^{i}}]=\penalty{\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}(px^{i-1}{x^{i}})
, with
\tilde{x}^{0}=\tilde{x}
and the direction of
[\tilde{p}\tilde{x}^{i}]
turning counterclockwise as
i
grows.
Let
D_{n}
be the subgraph with respect to
\tilde{p}
of the polygonal line
\tilde{x}^{0}\dots\penalty\tilde{x}^{n}
.

Let
\updelta_{n}
be the maximum radius of a circle inscribed in any of the triangles
[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{x}^{i-1}}{\tilde{x}^{i}}]
.

Now we construct a map
H_{n}\colon D_{n}\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
that increases distances by at most
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\updelta_{n}
.
Suppose
w\in\penalty D_{n}
.
Then
w
lies on or inside some triangle
[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{x}^{i-1}}{\tilde{x}^{i}}]
.
Define
H_{n}(w)
by first mapping
w
to a nearest point on
[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{x}^{i-1}}{\tilde{x}^{i}}]
(choosing one if there are several), followed by the natural map to the triangle
[{p}{x^{i-1}}{x^{i}}]
.

Since triangles in
\mathcal{U}
are
\upkappa
-thin (
9D
), the restriction of
H_{n}
to each triangle
[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{x}^{i-1}}{\tilde{x}^{i}}]
is short.
Then the triangle inequality implies that the restriction of
H_{n}
to

U_{n}=\bigcup_{1\leqslant\penalty i\leqslant\penalty n}[{\tilde{p}}{\tilde{x}^{i-1}}{\tilde{x}^{i}}]

is short with respect to the length metric on
D_{n}
.
Since nearest-point projection from
D_{n}
to
U_{n}
increases the
D_{n}
-distance between two points by at most
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\updelta_{n}
, the map
H_{n}
also increases the
D_{n}
-distance by at most
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\updelta_{n}
.

Consider converging sequences
v_{n}\to\penalty v
and
w_{n}\to\penalty w
such that
v_{n},w_{n}\in\penalty D_{n}
and therefore
v,w\in\penalty D
.
Note that

|{H_{n}(v_{n})}-\penalty{H_{n}(w_{n})}|\leqslant\penalty|{v_{n}}-\penalty{w_{n}}|_{D_{n}}+2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\updelta_{n},

➊

for each
n
.
Since
\updelta_{n}\to\penalty 0
and geodesics in
\mathcal{U}
vary continuously with their endpoints (
9G
), we have
H_{n}(v_{n})\to\penalty H(v)
and
H_{n}(w_{n})\to\penalty H(w)
.
Therefore the left-hand side in
➊
converges to
|{H(v)}-\penalty{H(w)}|
and the right-hand side converges to
|{v}-\penalty{w}|_{D}
, it follows that
H
is short.
∎

Proof of
9L
.

We begin by proving the theorem in case
\upalpha
is polygonal.

First suppose
\upalpha
is a triangle, say
[{p}{x}{y}]
.
By assumption, the perimeter of
[{p}{x}{y}]
is less than

2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
.
This is the base case for the induction.

Let
\tilde{\upgamma}
be the
\upkappa
-development of
[xy]
with respect to
p
, where
\tilde{\upgamma}
has basepoint
\tilde{p}
and subgraph
D
.
By the development criterion (
9F
),
\tilde{\upgamma}
is concave.
By Lemma
9L
, there is a short map
G\colon\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits{\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}(pxy)\to\penalty D
.
Further, by Lemma
9L
,
D
majorizes
[{p}{x}{y}]
under a majorizing map
H\colon D\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
. Clearly
H\circ\penalty G
is a majorizing map for
[{p}{x}{y}]
.

Now we claim that any closed
n
-gon
[x^{1}x^{2}\dots\penalty x^{n}]
of perimeter less than
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
in a
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space is majorized by a convex polygonal region

R_{n}=\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits[\tilde{x}^{1}\tilde{x}^{2}\dots\penalty\tilde{x}^{n}]

under a map
F_{n}
such that
F_{n}\colon\tilde{x}^{i}\mapsto\penalty x^{i}
for each
i
.

Assume the statement is true for
(n-1)
-gons,
n\geqslant\penalty 4
.
Then
[x^{1}x^{2}\dots\penalty x^{n-1}]
is majorized by a convex polygonal region

R_{n-1}=\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits[\tilde{x}^{1}\tilde{x}^{2},\dots\penalty\tilde{x}^{n-1}],

in
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
under a map
F_{n-1}
satisfying
F_{n-1}(\tilde{x}^{i})=x^{i}
for all
i
.
Take
\dot{x}^{n}\in\penalty\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
such that
[{\tilde{x}^{1}}{\tilde{x}^{n-1}}{\dot{x}^{n}}]={\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}(x^{1}x^{n-1}x^{n})

and this triangle lies on the other side of
[\tilde{x}^{1}\tilde{x}^{n-1}]
from
R_{n-1}
.
Let
\dot{R}=\penalty\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits[{\tilde{x}^{1}}{\tilde{x}^{n-1}}{\dot{x}^{n}}]
,
and
\dot{F}\colon\dot{R}\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
be a majorizing map for
[{x^{1}}{x^{n-1}}{x^{n}}]
as provided above.

Set

R=R_{n-1}\cup\penalty\dot{R}
, where
R
carries its length metric.
Since
F_{n}
and
F
agree on
[\tilde{x}^{1}\tilde{x}^{n-1}]
, we may define
F\colon R\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
by

F(x)=\begin{cases}F_{n-1}(x),\quad&x\in\penalty R_{n-1},\\
\dot{F}(x),\quad&x\in\penalty\dot{R}.\\
\end{cases}

Then
F
is length-nonincreasing and is a majorizing map for
[x^{1}x^{2}\dots\penalty x^{n}]
(as in Definition
9L
).

If
R
is a convex subset of
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
, we are done.

If
R
is not convex, the total internal angle of
R
at
\tilde{x}^{1}
or
\tilde{x}^{n-1}
or both is
>\uppi
.
By relabeling we may suppose this holds for
\tilde{x}^{n-1}
.

The region
R
is obtained by gluing
R_{n-1}
to
\dot{R}
by
[x^{1}x^{n-1}]
.
Thus, by Reshetnyak gluing (
9I
),

carrying its length metric is a
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
-space.
Moreover
[\tilde{x}^{n-2}\tilde{x}^{n-1}]\cup\penalty[\tilde{x}^{n-1}\dot{x}^{n}]
is a geodesic of
R
.
Thus
[\tilde{x}^{1}\tilde{x}^{2}\dots\penalty\tilde{x}^{n-2}\dot{x}^{n}]_{R}
is a closed
(n-1)
-gon in
R
, to which the induction hypothesis applies. The resulting short map from a convex region in
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
to
R
, followed by
F
, is the desired majorizing map.

Note that in fact we have proved the following:

➋

Let
F_{n-1}
be a majorizing map for the polygon
[x^{1}x^{2}\dots\penalty x^{n-1}]
,
and
\dot{F}
be a majorizing map for the triangle
[x^{1}x^{n-1}x^{n}]
.
Then there is a majorizing map
F_{n}
for the polygon
[x^{1}x^{2}\dots\penalty x^{n}]

such that

\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits F_{n+1}=\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits F_{n}\cup\penalty\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits\dot{F}.

We now use this claim to prove the theorem for general curves.

Assume
\upalpha\colon[0,\ell]\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
is an arbitrary closed curve with natural parameter.
Choose a sequence of partitions
0=t^{0}_{n}<t^{2}_{n}<\dots\penalty<t^{n}_{n}=\ell

so that:

\diamond

The set
\{t_{n+1}^{i}\}_{i=0}^{n+1}

is obtained from the set
\{t_{n}^{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
by adding one element.

\diamond

For a sequence
\upvarepsilon_{n}\to\penalty 0+
,
we have
t^{i}_{n}-t^{i-1}_{n}<\upvarepsilon_{n}
for all
i
.

Inscribe in
\upalpha
a sequence of polygons
P_{n}
with vertexes
\upalpha(t^{i}_{n})
.
Apply the claim above, to get a sequence of majorizing maps
F_{n}\colon R_{n}\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
.
Note that for all
m>n
we have

\diamond

\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits F_{m}
lies in an
\upvarepsilon_{n}
-neighborhood of
\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits F_{n}
,

\diamond

\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits F_{m}\setminus\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits F_{n}
lies in an
\upvarepsilon_{n}
-neighborhood of
\upalpha
.

It follows that the set

K=\upalpha\cup\penalty\left(\bigcup_{n}\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits F_{n}\right)

is compact.
Therefore the sequence
(F_{n})

has a partial limit as
n\to\penalty\infty
;
say
F
.
Clearly
F
is a majorizing map for
\upalpha
.
∎

If
p_{1}\dots\penalty p_{n}
is a polygon, then values
\upvartheta_{i}=\uppi-\measuredangle[p_{i}\,{}^{p_{i-1}}_{p_{i+1}}]
for all

are called
external angles
of the polygon.
The following exercise is a generalization of Fenchel’s theorem.

9.59. Exercise.

Show that the sum of external angles of any polygon in a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
space cannot be smaller than
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi
.

9.60. Very advanced exercise.

Suppose that a simple polygon
\upbeta

Show that the sum of external angles of

.

Give an example of such a polygon

with the sum of external angles exactly
4{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi
.

The following exercise is the rigidity case
of the majorization theorem.

9.61. Exercise.

Let

.

9.62. Exercise.

Two majorizations
F\colon D\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
and

Show that a closed rectifiable curve in a

The following lemma states, in particular, that in a
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space,
a sharp triangle comparison implies the
presence of an isometric copy of the convex hull of the model triangle.
The latter statement was proved by Alexandr Alexandrov

]
.

9.63. Arm lemma.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-geodesic
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space,
and
P=[x^{0}x^{1}\dots\penalty x^{n+1}]
be a polygon of length
<2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
in
\mathcal{U}
.
Suppose
\tilde{P}=[\tilde{x}^{0}\tilde{x}^{1}\dots\penalty\tilde{x}^{n+1}]
is a convex polygon in
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})

such that

|{\tilde{x}^{i}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{i-1}}|_{\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})}=|{x^{i}}-\penalty{x^{i-1}}|_{\mathcal{U}}\quad\text{and}\quad\measuredangle[x^{i}\,{}^{x^{i-1}}_{x^{i+1}}]\geqslant\penalty\measuredangle[\tilde{x}^{i}\,{}^{\tilde{x}^{i-1}}_{\tilde{x}^{i+1}}]

➌

for all
i
.
Then

(a)

|{\tilde{x}^{0}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{n+1}}|_{\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})}\leqslant\penalty|{x^{0}}-\penalty{x^{n+1}}|_{\mathcal{U}}
.

(b)

Equality holds in
9L
if and only if the map
\tilde{x}^{i}\mapsto\penalty x^{i}
can be extended
to a distance-preserving map of
\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits(\tilde{x}^{0},\tilde{x}^{1}\dots\penalty\tilde{x}^{n+1})
onto
\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits(x^{0},x^{1}\dots\penalty x^{n+1})
.

Proof;
9L
.

By majorization (
9L
),
P
is majorized by a convex region
\tilde{D}
in
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
.
By Proposition
9L
and the definition of angle,

\tilde{D}
is bounded by a convex polygon
\tilde{P}_{R}=[\tilde{y}^{0}\tilde{y}^{1}\dots\penalty\tilde{y}^{n+1}]
that satisfies

\displaystyle|{\tilde{y}^{i}}-\penalty{\tilde{y}^{i\pm 1}}|_{\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})}

\displaystyle=|{x^{i}}-\penalty{x^{i\pm 1}}|_{\mathcal{U}},\qquad|{\tilde{y}^{0}}-\penalty{\tilde{y}^{n+1}}|_{\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})}=|{x^{0}}-\penalty{x^{n+1}}|_{\mathcal{U}},

\displaystyle\measuredangle[\tilde{y}^{i}\,{}^{\tilde{y}^{i-1}}_{\tilde{y}^{i+1}}]\geqslant\penalty\measuredangle[x^{i}\,{}^{x^{i-1}}_{x^{i+1}}]\geqslant\penalty\measuredangle[\tilde{x}^{i}\,{}^{\tilde{x}^{i-1}}_{\tilde{x}^{i+1}}]

for
1\leqslant\penalty i\leqslant\penalty n
; the last inequality follows from
➌
.

The classical arm lemma
[
142
]
gives
|{\tilde{x}^{0}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{n+1}}|\leqslant\penalty|{\tilde{y}^{0}}-\penalty{\tilde{y}^{n+1}}|
.
Since
|{\tilde{y}^{0}}-\penalty{\tilde{y}^{n+1}}|=|{x^{0}}-\penalty{x^{n+1}}|
, part
9L
follows.

9L
.

Suppose equality holds in
9L
.
Then angles at the
j
-th vertex of
\tilde{P}
,
P
, and
\tilde{P}_{R}
are equal for
1\leqslant\penalty j\leqslant\penalty n
, and we may take
\tilde{P}=\tilde{P}_{R}
.

Let
F\colon\tilde{D}\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
be the majorizing map for
P
, where
\tilde{D}
is the convex region bounded by
\tilde{P}
, and
F|_{\tilde{P}}
is length-preserving.

➍

Let
\tilde{x},\tilde{y},\tilde{z}
be three vertexes of
\tilde{P}
, and
x,y,z
be the corresponding vertexes of
P
. If
|{\tilde{x}}-\penalty{\tilde{y}}|=|{x}-\penalty{y}|
,
|{\tilde{y}}-\penalty{\tilde{z}}|=|{x}-\penalty{z}|
and
\measuredangle[\tilde{y}\,{}^{\tilde{x}}_{\tilde{z}}]=\measuredangle[y\,{}^{x}_{z}]
, then
F|_{\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits(\tilde{x},\tilde{y},\tilde{z})}
is distance-preserving.

Indeed, since
F
is majorizing,
F
restricts to distance-preserving maps from
[\tilde{x}\tilde{y}]
to
[xy]
and
[\tilde{y}\tilde{z}]
to
[yz]
.
Suppose
\tilde{p}\in\penalty[\tilde{x}\tilde{y}]

\tilde{q}\in\penalty[\tilde{y}\tilde{z}]

|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{q}}|_{\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})}=|{F(\tilde{p})}-\penalty{F(\tilde{q})}|_{\mathcal{U}}.

➎

This inequality holds in one direction by majorization, and in the other direction by the angle comparison (
9C
).
By the first variation formula (
9H
), it follows that each pair of corresponding angles of triangles
[\tilde{x}\tilde{y}\tilde{z}]
and
[xyz]
are equal.
But then
➎
holds for
p,q
on any two sides of these triangles, so
F
is distance-preserving on every geodesic of
\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits(\tilde{p},\tilde{x},\tilde{y})
.
Hence the claim.

\vartriangle

➏

Suppose
F|_{\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits(\tilde{x}^{0},\tilde{x}^{1},\dots\penalty,\tilde{x}^{k})}
is distance-preserving for
2\leqslant\penalty k\leqslant\penalty n-1
.
Then
F|_{\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits(\tilde{x}^{0},\tilde{x}^{1},\dots\penalty,\tilde{x}^{k+1})}
is distance-preserving.

To verify this claim, let

\tilde{p}=[\tilde{x}^{k-1}\tilde{x}^{k+1}]\cap\penalty[\tilde{x}^{k}\tilde{x}^{0}]\quad\text{and}\quad p=F(p).

Note that the following maps are distance-preserving:

(i)

F|_{\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits(\tilde{x}^{k-1},\tilde{x}^{k},\tilde{x}^{k+1})}
,

(ii)

F|_{\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits(\tilde{x}^{k+1},\tilde{x}^{k-1},\tilde{x}^{0})}
,

(iii)

F|_{\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits(\tilde{x}^{0},\tilde{x}^{k},\tilde{x}^{k+1})}
.

Indeed, (i) follows from
9L
.
Therefore
|{\tilde{x}^{k-1}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{k+1}}|=|{x^{k-1}}-\penalty{x^{k+1}}|
, and so
F
restricts to a distance-preserving map from
[\tilde{x}^{k-1}\tilde{x}^{k+1}]
onto
[x^{k-1}x^{k+1}]
. With the induction hypothesis in

9L
,
it follows that
p=[x^{k-1}x^{k+1}]\cap\penalty[x^{k}x^{0}]
, hence

\measuredangle[\tilde{x}^{k-1}\,{}^{\tilde{x}^{k+1}}_{\tilde{x}^{0}}]=\measuredangle[x^{k-1}\,{}^{x^{k+1}}_{x^{0}}].

➐

Then (ii) follows from
➐
and
9L
. Since
|{\tilde{x}^{k}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{0}}|=|{x^{k}}-\penalty{x^{0}}|
, (iii) follows from
➐
and (i).

Let
\tilde{\upgamma}
be a geodesic of
\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits(\tilde{x}^{0},\tilde{x}^{0},\tilde{x}^{1}\dots\penalty\tilde{x}^{k+1})
.
Then
\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\tilde{\upgamma}<\penalty\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
.
If
\tilde{\upgamma}
does not contain the point
\tilde{p}
, then by the induction hypothesis in

9L
and (i)+(ii)+(iii), we get that
\upgamma=F\circ\penalty\tilde{\upgamma}
is a local geodesic of length
<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
.
By
9D
,
\upgamma
is a geodesic.

By continuity,
F\circ\penalty\tilde{\upgamma}
is a geodesic for all
\tilde{\upgamma}
;
so
9L
follows.

The base of the induction is provided by
9L
.
It finishes the proof of part
9L
.
∎

9.64. Exercise.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a complete length

x^{1},x^{2},x^{3},x^{4}\in\penalty\mathcal{U}

there is a convex quadrangle

[\tilde{x}^{1}\tilde{x}^{2}\tilde{x}^{3}\tilde{x}^{4}]

in
\mathbb{E}^{2}

such that

|{x^{i}}-\penalty{x^{j}}|_{\mathcal{U}}=|{\tilde{x}^{i}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{j}}|_{\mathbb{E}^{2}}

for all

and

.
Show that
\mathcal{U}
contains an isometric copy of the

solid quadrangle

[\tilde{x}^{1}\tilde{x}^{2}\tilde{x}^{3}\tilde{x}^{4}]

M
Hadamard–Cartan theorem

The development of Alexandrov geometry was greatly influenced by the Hadamard–Cartan theorem.
Its original formulation states that if
M
is a complete Riemannian manifold with nonpositive sectional curvature,
then the exponential map at any point
p\in\penalty M
is a covering;
in particular it implies that the universal cover of
M
is diffeomorphic to the Euclidean space of the same dimension.

In this generality, the theorem appeared in the lectures of Élie Cartan
[
46
]
.
For surfaces in the Euclidean space,
the theorem was proved by
Hans von Mangoldt
[
111
]
,
and a few years later independently by Jacques Hadamard
[
78
]
.

Formulations for metric spaces of different generality were proved by
Herbert Busemann
[
44
]
,
Willi Rinow
[
140
]
, and
Michael Gromov
[
75
, p. 119]
.
A detailed proof of Gromov’s statement when
\mathcal{U}
is proper was given by Werner Ballmann
[
20
]
, using Birkhoff’s curve-shortening.
A proof in the non-proper
geodesic case
was given by the first author and Richard Bishop
[
6
]
.
This proof applies more generally, to
convex spaces
(see Exercise
9O
).
It was pointed out by Bruce Kleiner
[
21
]

and independently by Martin Bridson and André Haefliger
[
34
]
that
this proof extends to length spaces, as well as geodesic spaces, giving the following statement:

9.65. Hadamard–Cartan theorem.

Let
\upkappa\leqslant\penalty 0
, and
\mathcal{U}
be a complete, simply connected length locally
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space.
Then
\mathcal{U}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
.

Proof.
Since
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}=\infty
, Theorem
9K
implies that there is a
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space
\mathcal{B}
and a
metric covering

\Phi\colon\mathcal{B}\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
; that is,
\Phi
is a length-preserving covering map.

Since
\mathcal{U}
is simply connected,
\Phi\colon\mathcal{B}\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
is an isometry — hence the result.
∎

To formulate the generalized Hadamard–Cartan theorem,
we need the following definition.

9.66. Definition.

Given
\ell\in\penalty(0,\infty]
,
a metric space
\mathcal{X}
is called

\ell
-simply connected
if it is connected and
any closed curve of length
<\ell

is null-homotopic in the class of curves of length
<\ell
in
\mathcal{X}
.

Note that there is a subtle difference between
simply connected and
\infty
-simply connected spaces;
the first states that any closed curve is null-homotopic while the second means that any rectifiable curve is null-homotopic in the class of rectifiable curves.
However, as follows from Proposition
9M
, for locally
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
spaces these two definitions are equivalent.
This fact makes it possible to deduce the Hadamard–Cartan theorem directly from the generalized Hadamard–Cartan theorem.

9.67. Generalized Hadamard–Cartan theorem.

A complete length space

\mathcal{U}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})

if and only if
\mathcal{U}
is
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-simply connected
and
\mathcal{U}
is locally
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
.

For proper spaces, the generalized Hadamard–Cartan theorem was proved by Brian Bowditch
[
31
]
.
In the proof we need the following lemma.

9.68. Lemma.

Assume
\mathcal{U}
is a complete length locally
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space,

\upvarepsilon>0
,
and
\upgamma_{1},\upgamma_{2}\colon\mathbb{S}^{1}\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
are two closed curves.
Assume

(a)

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upgamma_{i}<2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}-4{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon
for
i=1,2
;

(b)

|{\upgamma_{1}(x)}-\penalty{\upgamma_{2}(x)}|<\upvarepsilon
for any
x\in\penalty\mathbb{S}^{1}
, and the geodesic
[\upgamma_{1}(x)\upgamma_{2}(x)]
is uniquely defined and depends continuously on
x
;

(c)

\upgamma_{1}
is majorized by a convex region in
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
.

Then
\upgamma_{2}
is majorized by a convex region in
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
.

Proof.
Let
D
be a convex region in
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
that majorizes
\upgamma_{1}
under the map
F\colon D\to\penalty\mathcal{U}

(see Definition
9L
).
Denote by
\tilde{\upgamma}_{1}

the curve bounding
D

such that
F\circ\penalty\tilde{\upgamma}_{1}=\upgamma_{1}
.
Since

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\tilde{\upgamma}_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upgamma_{1}<

\displaystyle<2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}-4{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon,

there is a point
\tilde{p}\in\penalty D
such that

|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{\upgamma}(x)}|_{\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})}<\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}-\upvarepsilon

for any
x\in\penalty\mathbb{S}^{1}
.
Denote by
\upalpha_{x}
the concatenation of the paths
F\circ\penalty\mathrm{path}_{[p\tilde{\upgamma}_{1}(x)]_{\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})}}

and
\mathrm{path}_{[\upgamma_{1}(x)\upgamma_{2}(x)]}
in
\mathcal{U}
.
Note that
\upalpha_{x}
depends continuously on
x
, and

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upalpha_{x}<\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\upalpha_{x}(1)=\upgamma_{2}(x)

hold for any
x
.

Let us apply the lifting globalization theorem (
9K
) for
p=\penalty F(\tilde{p})
.
We obtain a
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-geodesic
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space
\mathcal{B}

and a locally distance-preserving map
\Phi\colon\mathcal{B}\to\penalty\mathcal{U}

with
\Phi(\hat{p})=p
for some
\hat{p}\in\penalty\mathcal{B}
, and with the lifting property for the curves starting at
p
with length
<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}/2
.
Applying the lifting property for
\upalpha_{x}
,
we get existence of a curve
\hat{\upgamma}_{2}\colon\mathbb{S}^{1}\to\penalty\mathcal{B}
such that

\upgamma_{2}=\Phi\circ\penalty\hat{\upgamma}_{2}.

Since
\mathcal{B}
is a geodesic
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space, we can apply the majorization theorem (
9L
) for
\hat{\upgamma}_{2}
.
The composition of the obtained majorization with
\Phi
is a majorization of
\upgamma_{2}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
9M
.

The only-if part follows from the Reshetnyak majorization theorem (
9L
).

Let
\upgamma_{t}
,
t\in\penalty[0,1]

be a null-homotopy of curves in
\mathcal{U}
;
that is,
\upgamma_{0}(x)=\penalty p
for some
p\in\penalty\mathcal{U}

and any
x\in\penalty\mathbb{S}^{1}
.
Assume further that
\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upgamma_{t}<\penalty 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
for any
t
.
To prove the if part, it is sufficient to show that
\upgamma_{1}
is majorized by a convex region in
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
if
\mathcal{U}
is locally
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
.

By semicontinuity of length (
2C
),
we can choose
\upvarepsilon>0
sufficiently small that

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upgamma_{t}<2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}-4{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon

for all
t
.

By Corollary
9K
,
we may assume in addition that

\mathrm{B}{}(\upgamma_{t}(x),\upvarepsilon)
is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})

for any
t
and
x
.

Choose a partition
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\dots\penalty<t_{n}=1

so that
|{\upgamma_{t_{i}}(x)}-\penalty{\upgamma_{t_{i-1}}(x)}|<\upvarepsilon

for any
i
and
x
.
According to
9B
,
for any
i
,
the geodesic
[\upgamma_{t_{i}}(x)\upgamma_{t_{i-1}}(x)]
depends continuously on
x
.

Note that
\upgamma_{0}=\upgamma_{t_{0}}
is majorized by a convex region in
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
.
Applying the lemma
n
times, we see that the same holds for
\upgamma_{1}=\penalty\upgamma_{t_{n}}
.
∎

9.69. Proposition.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a complete length locally
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space.
Then
\mathcal{U}
is simply connected if and only if it is
\infty
-simply connected.

Proof; if part.

It is sufficient to show that any closed curve in
\mathcal{U}
is homotopic to a polygon.

Let
\upgamma_{0}
be a closed curve in
\mathcal{U}
.
According to Corollary
9K
,
there is
\upvarepsilon>0
such that

\mathrm{B}{}(\upgamma(x),\upvarepsilon)
is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})

for any
x
.

Choose a polygon
\upgamma_{1}
such that
|{\upgamma_{0}(x)}-\penalty{\upgamma_{1}(x)}|<\upvarepsilon
for any
x
.
By
9B
,

\mathrm{path}_{[\upgamma_{0}(x)\upgamma_{1}(x)]}

is uniquely defined
and depends continuously on
x
.

Hence
\upgamma_{t}(x)=\mathrm{path}_{[\upgamma_{0}(x)\upgamma_{1}(x)]}(t)
gives a homotopy from
\upgamma_{0}
to
\upgamma_{1}
.

Only-if part.
The proof is similar.

Assume
\upgamma_{t}
is a homotopy between two rectifiable curves
\upgamma_{0}
and
\upgamma_{1}
.
Fix
\upvarepsilon>0
so that the ball
\mathrm{B}{}(\upgamma_{t}(x),\upvarepsilon)
is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})

for any
t
and
x
.
Choose a partition
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\dots\penalty<t_{n}=1

so that

|{\upgamma_{t_{i-1}}(x)}-\penalty{\upgamma_{t_{i}}(x)}|<\tfrac{\upvarepsilon}{10}

for any
i
and
x
.
Set
\hat{\upgamma}_{t_{0}}=\upgamma_{0}
,
\hat{\upgamma}_{t_{n}}=\upgamma_{t_{n}}
.
For each
0<i<n
, approximate
\upgamma_{t_{i}}
by a polygon
\hat{\upgamma}_{i}
.

Construct the
geodesic homotopy

from
\hat{\upgamma}_{t_{i-1}}

to
\hat{\upgamma}_{t_{i}}
;
that is,
set

\hat{\upgamma}_{t}=\mathrm{path}_{[\hat{\upgamma}_{t_{i-1}}(x)\hat{\upgamma}_{t_{i}}(x)]}(t)

for
t\in\penalty[t_{i-1},t_{i}]
.
Since
\upvarepsilon
is sufficiently small,
by
9B
, we get that

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\hat{\upgamma}_{t}<10{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\hat{\upgamma}_{t_{i-1}}+\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\hat{\upgamma}_{t_{i}})

for any
t\in\penalty[t_{i-1},t_{i}]
.
In particular,
\hat{\upgamma}_{t}
is rectifiable for all
t
.

Joining the obtained homotopies for all
i
, we obtain a homotopy from
\upgamma_{0}
to
\upgamma_{1}
in the class of rectifiable curves.
∎

, and

.

and

.

N
Convex sets

Recall that according to Corollary
9E
, any ball (closed or open) of radius
R<\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}
in a
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-geodesic
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space is convex.
From the uniqueness of geodesics in
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
spaces (
9B
) we get the following:

9.72. Observation.

Any weakly
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-convex set
in a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space is
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-convex.

9.73. Closest-point projection lemma.

Let
p
be a point in a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space
\mathcal{U}
, and
K\subset\penalty\mathcal{U}
be a closed
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-convex set.
If
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{K}{p}{}<\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}
,
then there is a unique point
p^{*}\in\penalty K
that minimizes the distance to
p
;
that is,
|{p^{*}}-\penalty{p}|=\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{K}{p}{}
.

Proof.

Fix
r
properly between
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{K}{p}{}
and
\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}
.
By the function comparison (
9E
),
the function
f=\mathrm{md}^{\upkappa}\circ\penalty\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}{}{}
is strongly convex in
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[p,r]
.

The lemma follows from Lemma
14A
applied to the subspace
K^{\prime}=\penalty K\cap\penalty\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[p,r]

and the restriction
f|_{K^{\prime}}
.
∎

Let
\mathcal{U}

\mathcal{U}\to\penalty K
