9.75. Advanced exercise.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({1})
space and
K\subset\penalty\mathcal{U}
be a closed
\uppi
-convex set.
Assume
K\subset\penalty\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[p,\tfrac{\uppi}{2}]
for
p\in\penalty K
.
Show that there is a short retraction of
\mathcal{U}
to
K
.

9.76. Proposition.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-geodesic
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space
and
K\subset\penalty\mathcal{U}
be a closed
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-convex set.
Let

f=\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}\circ\penalty\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{K}{}{}.

Then

f^{\prime\prime}+\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f\geqslant\penalty 0

holds in
\mathrm{B}{}(K,\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2})
.

Proof.

It is sufficient to show that Jensen’s inequality (
3E
)
holds on a sufficiently short
geodesic
[pq]
in
\mathrm{B}{}(K,\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2})
.
We may assume that

|{p}-\penalty{q}|+\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{K}{p}{}+\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{K}{q}{}<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}.

➊

For each
x\in\penalty[pq]
,
we need to find a value
h(x)\in\penalty\mathbb{R}

such that

h(p)=f(p),\qquad h(q)=f(q),\qquad h(x)\leqslant\penalty f(x)\qquad

for any
x\in\penalty[pq]
,
and

h^{\prime\prime}+\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}h\geqslant\penalty 0

➋

along
[pq]
.

Denote by
p^{*}
and
q^{*}
the closest-point projections of
p
and
q
on
K
;
they are provided by
9N
.
From
➊
and the triangle inequality,
we have

|{p^{*}}-\penalty{q^{*}}|<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}.

Since
K
is
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
-convex,
K\supset\penalty[p^{*}q^{*}]
;
in particular

\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{K}{x}{}\leqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{[p^{*}q^{*}]}{x}{}

for any
x\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
.

There is a majorizing map
F:D\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
for quadrangle
[pp^{*}q^{*}q]
, as in Definition
9L
and the Reshetnyak majorization theorem (
9L
).
By Proposition
9L
,
the figure
D
is a solid convex quadrangle
[\tilde{p}\tilde{p}^{*}\tilde{q}^{*}\tilde{q}]
in
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
such that

\displaystyle|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{p}^{*}}|_{\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})}

\displaystyle=|{p}-\penalty{p^{*}}|_{\mathcal{U}}

\displaystyle|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{q}}|_{\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})}

\displaystyle=|{p}-\penalty{q}|_{\mathcal{U}}

\displaystyle|{\tilde{q}}-\penalty{\tilde{q}^{*}}|_{\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})}

\displaystyle=|{q}-\penalty{q^{*}}|_{\mathcal{U}}

\displaystyle|{\tilde{p}^{*}}-\penalty{\tilde{q}^{*}}|_{\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})}

\displaystyle=|{p^{*}}-\penalty{q^{*}}|_{\mathcal{U}}.

Given
x\in\penalty[pq]
, denote by
\tilde{x}
the corresponding point on
[\tilde{p}\tilde{q}]
.
Then

\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{[pq]}{x}{}\leqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{[\tilde{p}\tilde{q}]}{\tilde{x}}{}.

Set

h(x)=\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}\circ\penalty\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{[\tilde{p}\tilde{q}]}{\tilde{x}}{}.

By straightforward calculations,
➋
holds
and hence the statement follows.
∎

9.77. Corollary.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space
and
K\subset\penalty\mathcal{U}
be a closed locally convex set.
Then there is an open set
\Omega\supset\penalty K

such that the function

f=\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}\circ\penalty\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{K}{}{}

satisfies

f^{\prime\prime}+\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f\geqslant\penalty 0

in
\Omega
.

Proof.

Fix
p\in\penalty K
.
By Corollary
9E
,

\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[p,r]
is convex for all small
r>0
.

Since
K
is locally convex, there is
r_{p}>0
such that
the intersection

K^{\prime}=K\cap\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(p,r_{p})
is convex.

Note that

\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{K}{x}{}=\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{K^{\prime}}{x}{}

for any
x\in\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(p,\tfrac{r_{p}}{2})
.
Therefore the statement holds for

\Omega=\bigcup_{p\in\penalty K}\mathrm{B}{}(p,\tfrac{r_{p}}{2}).

∎

9.78. Theorem.

Assume
\mathcal{U}
is a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space and
K\subset\penalty\mathcal{U}
is a closed connected locally convex set.
Assume
|{x}-\penalty{y}|<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
for any
x,y\in\penalty K
.
Then
K
is convex.

In particular, if
\upkappa\leqslant\penalty 0
, then any closed connected locally convex set in
\mathcal{U}
is convex.

The following proof is due to Sergei Ivanov
[
86
]
.

Proof.

Since
K
is locally convex,
it is locally path-connected.
Since
K
is connected and locally path connected it is path-connected.

Fix two points
x,y\in\penalty K
.
Let us connect
x
to
y
by a path
\upalpha\colon[0,1]\to\penalty K
.
Since
|{x}-\penalty{\upalpha(s)}|<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
for any
s
,
Theorem
9B
implies that the geodesic
[x\upalpha(s)]

is uniquely defined and depends continuously on
s
.

Let
\Omega\supset\penalty K
be the open set provided by Corollary
9N
.
If
[xy]=\penalty[x\upalpha(1)]
does not completely lie in
K
, then
there is a value
s\in\penalty[0,1]
such that
[x\upalpha(s)]

lies in
\Omega
but does not completely lie in
K
.
By Corollary
9N
,
the function
f=\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}\circ\penalty\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{K}{}{\mathcal{U}}

satisfies the differential inequality

f^{\prime\prime}+\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f\geqslant\penalty 0

➌

along
[x\upalpha(s)]
.

Since

\displaystyle|{x}-\penalty{\upalpha(s)}|

\displaystyle<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}},

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle=f(\upalpha(s))=0,

then the barrier inequality (
3E
)
implies that
f(z)\leqslant\penalty 0
for
z\in\penalty[x\upalpha(s)]
;
that is
[x\upalpha(s)]\subset\penalty K
, a contradiction.
∎

O
Remarks

The following question was known in folklore in the 80’s,
but it seems that in print
it was first mentioned by Michael Gromov
[
73
, 6.B
{}_{1}\mathrm{(f)}
]
.
We do not see any reason why it should be true,
but we also cannot construct a counterexample.

9.79. Open question.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
space and
K\subset\penalty\mathcal{U}
be a compact set.
Is it true that
K
lies in a convex compact set
\bar{K}\subset\penalty\mathcal{U}
?

The question can easily be reduced to the case when
K
is finite;
so far it is not even known if any three points in a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
space lie in a compact convex set.

We expect that
9J
can be extended to all curves, not necessarily local geodesics,
but the proof does not admit a straightforward generalization.

About convex spaces.

A
convex space

\mathcal{X}
is a geodesic space such that the function

t\mapsto\penalty|{\upgamma(t)}-\penalty{\upsigma(t)}|
is convex
for any two geodesic paths
\upgamma,\upsigma:[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
.
A
locally convex space
is a length space in which every point has a neighborhood that is a convex space in the restricted metric.

9.80. Exercise.

Assume
\mathcal{X}
is a convex space
such that the angle of any hinge is defined.
Show that
\mathcal{X}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
.

The following exercise gives an analog of the Hadamard–Cartan theorem for locally convex spaces;
see also
[
6
]
.

9.81. Exercise.

Show that a complete, simply connected, locally convex space is a convex space.

Examples and constructions.

Let us list important sources of examples of
CAT
spaces.
This should be beneficial to the reader despite that we do not provide all the proofs and some proofs are deferred to later chapters.

Riemannian manifolds
with sectional curvature at most
\upkappa
are locally
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
.
This statement follows from the Rauch comparison, and it is one of the main motivations for
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
comparison.

Hilbert spaces
are another motivating example of
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
spaces.

The question of when a Riemannian
manifold-with-boundary
is locally
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
has been completely answered by the first author, David Berg, and Richard Bishop
[
2
]
.
If the sectional curvatures of the interior and the outward sectional curvatures of the boundary do not exceed
\upkappa
then it is locally
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
(where an outward sectional curvature is one that corresponds to a tangent
2
-plane all of whose normal curvature vectors point outward).
In particular, if a Riemannian manifold and its boundary have sectional curvature at most
\upkappa
, then it is locally
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
.

Subsets of
positive reach
in Riemannian manifolds were studied in this context by
the first author, Richard Bishop, and
Alexander Lytchak
[
3
,
110
]
.
In particular, any compact subset of positive reach in a Riemannian manifold is
CAT
;
as usual, we assume that it is equipped with the induced length metric.

It was shown by Alexander Lytchak and Stephan Stadler
[
107
]
that any
simply-connected subset
of a contractible two-dimensional
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
length space (equipped with induced length metric) is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
.
In higher dimensions things are more complicated, even for a Euclidean ambient space;
see
[
11
, Chapter 4]
for related questions (open and solved).

By the Gauss formula smooth saddle surfaces in manifolds with sectional curvature at most
\upkappa
are locally
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
.
The nonsmooth analog of this statement is wide open; this is the so-called Shefel conjecture
[
11
, Chapter 4]
.
However, the following weaker statement was proved by the third author and Stephan Stadler
[
125
]
:
metric-minimizing surfaces
in
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space are locally
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
;
metric minimizing means that it is impossible to decrease its length metric by a small deformation.

Note that any
metric tree
(see Section
4C
) is
\text{\rm CAT}({-\infty})
;
that is, a metric tree is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
for
any

\upkappa\in\penalty\mathbb{R}
.
In particular, any tree (in the graph-theoretical sense) with a length metric such that every edge is isometric to a line segment is
\text{\rm CAT}({-\infty})
.

Suppose
A^{1},\dots\penalty,A^{n}
is an array of convex closed sets in the Euclidean space
\mathbb{E}^{m}
.
Let us prepare
n+1
copy of
\mathbb{E}^{m}
and glue successive pairs of spaces along
A^{1},\dots\penalty,A^{n}
.
The resulting space is called
puff pastry
;
by the Reshetnyak gluing theorem it is
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
.
(This observation was used in one of the most beautiful applications of the Reshetnyak gluing theorem given by Dmitri Burago, Serge Ferleger,
and Alexey Kononenko
[
37
,
38
,
39
,
40
]
.
They use it to study billiards; a short survey on the subject was written by Dmitri Burago
[
35
]
;
see also our book
[
11
]
.)

An if-and-only-if condition on
polyhedral spaces
is given in
12B
.
It implies the so-called Gromov’s flag condition
12C
which provides in particular a flexible way to construct
\text{\rm CAT}({0})

cube complexes
.
(Several applications are mentioned in Section
12D
.)

By
9A
and
5E
, the
ultralimits
, as well as
Gromov–Hausdorff limits
of
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
spaces are
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
.
In particular, if
\mathcal{U}
is a
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
space then its
asymptotic cone
defined as the ultralimit of its rescalings
\tfrac{1}{n}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathcal{U}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
is again
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
.
Unlike in the case of
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
spaces, the use of ultralimits is necessary even if
\mathcal{U}
is a manifold due to the lack of compactness theorem.
Asymptotic cones of
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
spaces and their generalizations play an important role in geometric group theory.

Conformal deformations
of
CAT
spaces were studied by Alexander Lytchak and Stephan Stadler
[
106
]
.
In particular, if
\mathcal{U}
is a
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
space and
f\colon\mathcal{U}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
is continuous, convex
and bounded below,
then the conformally equivalent space with conformal factor
e^{f}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
.

Further,
CAT
spaces behave nicely with respect to some natural constructions.
For example, the product of
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
spaces is again
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
.
Also, the Euclidean cone over a
\text{\rm CAT}({1})
space is
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
.
These are the first examples of the so-called
warped products
that are discussed in Chapter
11
;
a general statement is given in
11E
.
Also, as it was observed by Karl-Theodor Sturm
[
152
, Prop. 3.10]
,
the
space of
L^{2}
-maps
from a measure space to a complete
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
length space is
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
.

Among more conceptual examples, let us mention a result of Brian Clarke
[
53
]
: the
space of Riemannian metrics
on a compact, orientable smooth manifold
with respect to the
L^{2}
-distance is
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
;
a shorter proof of this statement was given by Nicola Cavallucci
[
47
]
.
By a result of Tamás Darvas
[
56
]
, the
space of Kähler potentials
on a compact Kähler manifold is
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
.
The
Teichmüller space
with the Weil–Petersson metric is
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
;
the latter was shown by Sumio Yamada
[
158
]
.

Chapter 10
Kirszbraun revisited

This chapter is based on our paper
[
9
]

and an earlier paper of Urs Lang and Viktor Schroeder

[
97
]
.

A
Short map extension definitions

10.1. Theorem.

A complete length space

\mathcal{L}
is
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
if and only if for any 3-point set
V_{3}
and any 4-point set
V_{4}\supset\penalty V_{3}
in
\mathcal{L}
,
any short map
f\colon V_{3}\to\penalty\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
can be extended to a short map
F\colon V_{4}\to\penalty\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
(so
f=F|_{V_{3}}
).

The only-if part of Theorem
10A
can be obtained as a corollary of Kirszbraun’s theorem (
10D
).
We present another, more elementary proof; using the following analog of Alexandrov’s lemma (
6B
).

We say that two triangles with a common vertex
do not overlap
if their convex hulls intersect only at the common vertex.

10.2. Overlap lemma.

Let
[{\tilde{x}^{1}}{\tilde{x}^{2}}{\tilde{x}^{3}}]
be a triangle in
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
.
Let
\tilde{p}^{1}
,
\tilde{p}^{2}
,
\tilde{p}^{3}
be points in
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
such that, for any permutation
\{i,j,k\}
of
\{1,2,3\}
, we have

(i)

|{\tilde{p}^{i}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{k}}|=|{\tilde{p}^{j}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{k}}|
,

(ii)

\tilde{p}^{i}
and
\tilde{x}^{i}
lie in the same closed halfspace determined by
[\tilde{x}^{j}\tilde{x}^{k}]
,

If no pair of triangles
[{\tilde{p}^{i}}{\tilde{x}^{j}}{\tilde{x}^{k}}]
overlap,
then

\measuredangle{\tilde{p}^{1}}+\measuredangle{\tilde{p}^{2}}+\measuredangle{\tilde{p}^{3}}>2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi,

where
\measuredangle\tilde{p}^{i}:=\measuredangle[\tilde{p}^{i}\,{}^{\tilde{x}^{k}}_{\tilde{x}^{j}}]

for a permutation
\{i,j,k\}
of
\{1,2,3\}
.

Remarks.

If
\upkappa\leqslant\penalty 0
, then the overlap lemma can be proved without using condition (
i
).
This follows since the sum of external angles for the hexagon

[\tilde{p}^{1}\tilde{x}^{2}\tilde{p}^{3}\tilde{x}^{1}\tilde{p}^{2}\tilde{x}^{3}]
and its area is
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi-\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}a
, where
a
denotes the area of the hexagon.

The diagram shows that condition (
i
) is essential
in case
\upkappa>0
.

Proof.
Rotate the triangle
[{\tilde{p}^{3}}{\tilde{x}^{1}}{\tilde{x}^{2}}]
around
\tilde{x}^{1}
to make
[\tilde{x}^{1}\tilde{p}^{3}]
coincide with
[\tilde{x}^{1}\tilde{p}^{2}]
.
Let
\dot{x}^{2}
denote the image of
\tilde{x}^{2}
after rotation.
Note that

\measuredangle[\tilde{x}^{1}\,{}^{\tilde{x}^{3}}_{\dot{x}^{2}}]=\min\{\,\measuredangle[\tilde{x}^{1}\,{}^{\tilde{x}^{2}}_{\tilde{p}^{3}}]+\measuredangle[\tilde{x}^{1}\,{}^{\tilde{p}^{2}}_{\tilde{x}^{3}}],\,2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi-(\measuredangle[\tilde{x}^{1}\,{}^{\tilde{x}^{2}}_{\tilde{p}^{3}}]+\measuredangle[\tilde{x}^{1}\,{}^{\tilde{p}^{2}}_{\tilde{x}^{3}}])\,\}.

By (
ii
),
the triangles

[{\tilde{p}^{3}}{\tilde{x}^{1}}{\tilde{x}^{2}}]

and
[{\tilde{p}^{2}}{\tilde{x}^{3}}{\tilde{x}^{1}}]
do not overlap if and only if

\measuredangle[\tilde{x}^{1}\,{}^{\tilde{x}^{3}}_{\tilde{x}^{2}}]>\measuredangle[\tilde{x}^{1}\,{}^{\tilde{x}^{3}}_{\dot{x}^{2}}].

➊

and

2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi>\measuredangle[\tilde{x}^{1}\,{}^{\tilde{x}^{2}}_{\tilde{p}^{3}}]+\measuredangle[\tilde{x}^{1}\,{}^{\tilde{p}^{2}}_{\tilde{x}^{3}}]+\measuredangle[\tilde{x}^{1}\,{}^{\tilde{x}^{2}}_{\tilde{x}^{3}}]

➋

The condition
➊
holds if and only if

|{\tilde{x}^{2}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{3}}|>|{\dot{x}^{2}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{3}}|
,
which in turn holds if and only if

\displaystyle\measuredangle\tilde{p}^{1}

\displaystyle>\measuredangle[\tilde{p}^{2}\,{}^{\tilde{x}^{3}}_{\dot{x}^{2}}]

➌

\displaystyle=\min\{\measuredangle\tilde{p}^{3}+\measuredangle\tilde{p}^{2},2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi-(\measuredangle\tilde{p}^{3}+\measuredangle\tilde{p}^{2})\}.

The inequality follows since the corresponding hinges have the same pairs of sidelengths.
(The two pictures show that both possibilities for the minimum can occur.)

Now assume
\measuredangle\tilde{p}^{1}+\measuredangle\tilde{p}^{2}+\measuredangle\tilde{p}^{3}\leqslant\penalty 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi
.
Then
➌
implies

\measuredangle\tilde{p}^{i}>\measuredangle\tilde{p}^{j}+\measuredangle\tilde{p}^{k}.

Since no pair of triangles overlap, the same holds
for any permutation
(i,j,k)
of
(1,2,3)
.
Therefore

\measuredangle\tilde{p}^{1}+\measuredangle\tilde{p}^{2}+\measuredangle\tilde{p}^{3}>2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(\measuredangle\tilde{p}^{1}+\measuredangle\tilde{p}^{2}+\measuredangle\tilde{p}^{3}),

a contradiction.
∎

Proof of
10A
; if part.

Assume
\mathcal{L}
is geodesic.
Consider
x^{1},x^{2},x^{3}\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
such that the model triangle

[{\tilde{x}^{1}}{\tilde{x}^{2}}{\tilde{x}^{3}}]={\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}(x^{1}x^{2}x^{3})
is defined.
Choose
p\in\penalty\,{]}x^{1}x^{2}{[}\,
.
Applying the short map extension property with
V_{3}=\{x^{1},x^{2},x^{3}\}
,
V_{4}=\{x^{1},x^{2},x^{3},p\}
and the map
f\colon x^{i}\mapsto\penalty\tilde{x}^{i}
, we obtain the point-on-side comparison (
8C
).

In case
\mathcal{L}
is not geodesic, pass to its ultrapower
\mathcal{L}^{\text{\rm}}
.
Note that the short map extension property survives
for
\mathcal{L}^{\text{\rm}}
and recall that
\mathcal{L}^{\text{\rm}}
is geodesic (see
4C
).
Thus, from above,
\mathcal{L}^{\text{\rm}}
is a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space.
By Proposition
8A
,
\mathcal{L}
is a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space.

Only-if part.

Assume the contrary:

\mathcal{L}
is complete and
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
, and

x^{1},x^{2},x^{3},p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
and

\tilde{x}^{1},\tilde{x}^{2},\tilde{x}^{3}\in\penalty\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
are such that

|{\tilde{x}^{i}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{j}}|\leqslant\penalty|{x^{i}}-\penalty{x^{j}}|
for all
i,j
but there is no point
\tilde{p}\in\penalty\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
such that
|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{i}}|\leqslant\penalty|{p}-\penalty{x^{i}}|
for all
i
.

Note that in this case all comparison triangles
{\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}(px^{i}x^{j})
are defined.
This is always true if
\upkappa\leqslant\penalty 0
.
If
\upkappa>0
, and say
{\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}(px^{1}x^{2})
is undefined, then

\displaystyle|{p}-\penalty{x^{1}}|+|{p}-\penalty{x^{2}}|

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}-|{x^{1}}-\penalty{x^{2}}|\geqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}-|{\tilde{x}^{1}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{2}}|\geqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty|{\tilde{x}^{1}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{3}}|+|{\tilde{x}^{2}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{3}}|.

Then the last inequality must be an equality.
Thus we may extend by taking
\tilde{p}
on
[\tilde{x}^{1}\tilde{x}^{3}]
or
[\tilde{x}^{2}\tilde{x}^{3}]
.

For each
i\in\penalty\{1,2,3\}
, consider a point
\tilde{p}^{i}\in\penalty\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
such that
|{\tilde{p}^{i}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{i}}|
is minimal among points satisfying
|{\tilde{p}^{i}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{j}}|\leqslant\penalty|{p}-\penalty{x^{j}}|
for all
j\neq i
.
Clearly, every
\tilde{p}^{i}
is inside the triangle
[{\tilde{x}^{1}}{\tilde{x}^{2}}{\tilde{x}^{3}}]
(that is, in
\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits(\tilde{x}^{1},\tilde{x}^{2},\tilde{x}^{3})
), and
|{\tilde{p}^{i}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{i}}|>|{p}-\penalty{x^{i}}|
for each
i
.
Since the function
x\mapsto\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}\{x;a,b\}

is increasing, it follows that

(i)

|{\tilde{p}^{i}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{j}}|=|{p}-\penalty{x^{j}}|
for
i\neq j
;

(ii)

no pair of triangles from
[{\tilde{p}^{1}}{\tilde{x}^{2}}{\tilde{x}^{3}}]
,
[{\tilde{p}^{2}}{\tilde{x}^{3}}{\tilde{x}^{1}}]
,
[{\tilde{p}^{3}}{\tilde{x}^{1}}{\tilde{x}^{2}}]
overlap in
[{\tilde{x}^{1}}{\tilde{x}^{2}}{\tilde{x}^{3}}]
.

As follows from the overlap lemma (
10A
),
in this case

\measuredangle[\tilde{p}^{1}\,{}^{\tilde{x}^{2}}_{\tilde{x}^{3}}]+\measuredangle[\tilde{p}^{2}\,{}^{\tilde{x}^{3}}_{\tilde{x}^{1}}]+\measuredangle[\tilde{p}^{3}\,{}^{\tilde{x}^{1}}_{\tilde{x}^{2}}]>2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi.

Since
|{\tilde{x}^{i}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{j}}|\leqslant\penalty|{x^{i}}-\penalty{x^{j}}|
we have

\measuredangle[\tilde{p}^{k}\,{}^{\tilde{x}^{i}}_{\tilde{x}^{j}}]\leqslant\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{x^{i}}_{x^{j}}\hskip-0.86108pt)

if
(i,j,k)
is a permutation of
(1,2,3)
.
Therefore

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{x^{1}}_{x^{2}}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{x^{2}}_{x^{3}}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{x^{3}}_{x^{1}}\hskip-0.86108pt)>2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi,

contradicting the
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
comparison (
8A
).
∎

10.3. Theorem.

Assume any pair of points at distance
<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
in the metric space
\mathcal{U}
are joined by a unique geodesic.
Then
\mathcal{U}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
if and only if
for any
3
-point set
V_{3}
with perimeter
<2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}

and any
4
-point set
V_{4}\supset\penalty V_{3}
in
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
,
any short map
f\colon V_{3}\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
can be extended to a short map
F\colon V_{4}\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
.

Note that the only-if part of Theorem
10A
does not follow directly from Kirszbraun’s theorem, since the desired extension is in
\mathcal{U}
— not its completion.

10.4. Lemma.

Let
x^{1},x^{2},x^{3},y^{1},y^{2},y^{3}\in\penalty\mathbb{M}({\upkappa})

be points such that
|{x^{i}}-\penalty{x^{j}}|\geqslant\penalty|{y^{i}}-\penalty{y^{j}}|
for all
i,j
.
Then there is a short map
\Phi\colon\mathbb{M}({\upkappa})\to\penalty\mathbb{M}({\upkappa})
such that
\Phi(x^{i})=y^{i}
for all
i
;
moreover, one can choose
\Phi
so that

\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits\Phi\subset\penalty\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits(y^{1},y^{2},y^{3}).

We only give an idea of the proof of this lemma;
alternatively, it can be obtained as a corollary of Kirszbraun’s theorem (
10D
)

Idea of the proof.

The map
\Phi
can be constructed as a composition of an isometry of
\mathbb{M}({\upkappa})
and the following folding map:
given a halfspace
H
in
\mathbb{M}({\upkappa})
, consider the map
\mathbb{M}({\upkappa})\to\penalty H

that is the identity on
H
and reflects all points outside of
H
into
H
.
This map is a path isometry; in particular, it is short.

The last part of the lemma can be proved by composing this map with folding maps along the sides of triangle
[{y^{1}}{y^{2}}{y^{3}}]
, and passing to a partial limit.
∎

Proof of
10A
; if part.

The point-on-side comparison (
9C
) follows by
taking
V_{3}=\{\tilde{x},\tilde{y},\tilde{p}\}
and
V_{4}=\{\tilde{x},\tilde{y},\tilde{p},\tilde{z}\}
where
z\in\penalty\mathopen{]}xy\mathclose{[}
.
It is only necessary to observe that
F(\tilde{z})=z
by uniqueness of
[xy]
.

Only-if part.

Let
V_{3}=\{\tilde{x}^{1},\tilde{x}^{2},\tilde{x}^{3}\}
and
V_{4}=\{\tilde{x}^{1},\tilde{x}^{2},\tilde{x}^{3},\tilde{p}\}
.

Set
y^{i}=\penalty f(\tilde{x}^{i})
for all
i
.
We need to find a point
q\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
such that
|{y^{i}}-\penalty{q}|\leqslant\penalty|{\tilde{x}^{i}}-\penalty{\tilde{p}}|
for all
i
.

Let
D
be the convex set in
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
bounded by the model triangle

[{\tilde{y}^{1}}{\tilde{y}^{2}}{\tilde{y}^{3}}]=\penalty{\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}{y^{1}}{y^{2}}{y^{3}}
;
that is,
D=\penalty\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits({\tilde{y}^{1}},{\tilde{y}^{2}},{\tilde{y}^{3}})
.

Note that
|{\tilde{y}^{i}}-\penalty{\tilde{y}^{j}}|=|{y^{i}}-\penalty{y^{j}}|\leqslant\penalty|{\tilde{x}^{i}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{j}}|
for all
i,j
.
Applying Lemma
10A
,
we get a short map

\Phi\colon\mathbb{M}({\upkappa})\to\penalty D
such that

\Phi\colon\tilde{x}^{i}\mapsto\penalty\tilde{y}^{i}
.

Further, by the majorization theorem (
9L
),
there is a short map
F\colon D\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
such that
\tilde{y}^{i}\mapsto\penalty y^{i}
for all
i
.

Thus one can take
q=F\circ\penalty\Phi(\tilde{p})
.
∎

10.5. Exercise.

Assume
\mathcal{X}
is a complete length space that satisfies the following condition:
any 4-point subset admits a distance-preserving map to the Euclidean 3-space.

Prove that
\mathcal{X}
is isometric to a closed convex subset of a Hilbert space.

10.6. Exercise.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{s}
be the metric
on the 5-point set
\{p,q,x,y,z\}
for which
|{p}-\penalty{q}|=s

and all the remaining distances are equal
1
.
For which values
s
does the space
\mathcal{F}_{s}
admit a distance-preserving map into

(a)

a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
space?

(b)

a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
space?

The following exercise describes the first known definition of spaces with curvature bounded below;
it was given by Abraham Wald
[
156
]
.

10.7. Exercise.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a metric space and
\upkappa\leqslant\penalty 0
.
Prove that
\mathcal{L}
is
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
if and only if any quadruple of points
p,q,r,s\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
admits a distance-preserving embedding into
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\mathrm{K}})
for some
\mathrm{K}\geqslant\penalty\upkappa
.

Is the same true for
\upkappa>0
; what is the difference?

B
(1+
n
)-point comparison

The following theorem gives a more sensitive analog of the
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
comparison (
8A
).

10.8. (1+
n
)-point comparison.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space.
Then for any array
(p,x^{1},\dots\penalty,x^{n})
of points in
\mathcal{L}

there is a model array
(\tilde{p},\tilde{x}^{1},\dots\penalty,\tilde{x}^{n})
in
\mathbb{M}^{n}({\upkappa})
such that

(a)

|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{i}}|=|{p}-\penalty{x^{i}}|
for all
i
.

(b)

|{\tilde{x}^{i}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{j}}|\geqslant\penalty|{x^{i}}-\penalty{x^{j}}|
for all
i,j
.

Proof.

It is sufficient to show that given
\upvarepsilon>0
there is an array
(\tilde{p},\tilde{x}^{1},\dots\penalty,\tilde{x}^{n})
in
\mathbb{M}^{n}({\upkappa})
such that

|{\tilde{x}^{i}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{j}}|\geqslant\penalty|{x^{i}}-\penalty{x^{j}}|\quad\text{and}\quad|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{i}}|\lessgtr|{p}-\penalty{x^{i}}|\bigr|\pm\upvarepsilon.

Then one can pass to a limit array for
\upvarepsilon\to\penalty 0+
.

According to
8B
, the set
\mathop{\rm Str}\nolimits(x^{1},\dots\penalty,x^{n})

is dense in
\mathcal{L}
.
Thus there is a point
p^{\prime}\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Str}\nolimits(\tilde{x}^{1},\dots\penalty,\tilde{x}^{n})
such that
|{p^{\prime}}-\penalty{p}|\leqslant\penalty\upvarepsilon
.
According to Corollary
13I
,

\text{\rm T}_{p^{\prime}}
contains a subcone
E
isometric to a Euclidean space
and containing all vectors
\mathrm{log}[{p^{\prime}}{x^{i}}]
.
Passing to a subspace if necessary, we may assume that
\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits E\leqslant\penalty n
.

Mark a point
\tilde{p}\in\penalty\mathbb{M}^{n}({\upkappa})
and choose a distance-preserving map

\upiota\colon E\to\penalty\text{\rm T}_{\tilde{p}}\mathbb{M}^{n}({\upkappa})
.
Let

\tilde{x}^{i}=\exp_{\tilde{p}}\circ\penalty\upiota(\mathrm{log}[{p^{\prime}}{x^{i}}]).

Thus
|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{i}}|=|{p^{\prime}}-\penalty{x^{i}}|
.
Since
|{p}-\penalty{p^{\prime}}|\leqslant\penalty\upvarepsilon
, we get

|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{i}}|\lessgtr|{p}-\penalty{x^{i}}|\pm\upvarepsilon.

From the hinge comparison (
8C
)
we have

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(\tilde{p}\,{}^{\tilde{x}^{i}}_{\tilde{x}^{j}}\hskip-0.86108pt)=\measuredangle[\tilde{p}\,{}^{\tilde{x}^{i}}_{\tilde{x}^{j}}]=\measuredangle[p^{\prime}\,{}^{x^{i}}_{x^{j}}]\geqslant\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p^{\prime}\,{}^{x^{i}}_{x^{j}}\hskip-0.86108pt),

and thus

|{\tilde{x}^{i}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{j}}|\geqslant\penalty|{x^{i}}-\penalty{x^{j}}|.

∎

10.9. Exercise.

Let
(p,x_{1},\dots\penalty,x_{n})
be a point array in a
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
space.
Consider the
n{\times\penalty}n
-matrix
M
with components

m_{i,j}=\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(|{x_{i}}-\penalty{p}|^{2}+|{x_{j}}-\penalty{p}|^{2}-|{x_{i}}-\penalty{x_{j}}|^{2}).

Show that

\bm{s}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}M{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\bm{s}^{\top}\geqslant\penalty 0

➊

for any vector
\bm{s}=(s_{1},\dots\penalty,s_{n})
with nonnegative components.

The above exercise describes the so-called
Lang–Schroeder–Sturm inequality
; it was discovered by Urs Lang and Viktor Schroeder
[
97
]
and rediscovered by Karl-Theodor Sturm
[
150
]
.
It turns out to be weaker than (1+
n
)-point comparison.
An example can be constructed by perturbing the 6-point metric isometric to a regular pentagon with its center, making its sides slightly longer and diagonals slightly shorter
[
101
]
.
In particular, this inequality in general metric spaces (not necessarily length spaces) does not imply the inequality in the following exercise.

10.10. Exercise.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space.
Show that for any points
p,x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4},x_{5}
in
\mathcal{L}
we have

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{x_{1}}_{x_{5}}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{x_{2}}_{x_{1}}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{x_{3}}_{x_{2}}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{x_{4}}_{x_{3}}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{x_{5}}_{x_{4}}\hskip-0.86108pt)\leqslant\penalty 4{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi,

assuming that the left-hand side is defined.

10.11. Exercise.

Give an example of a metric on a finite set that satisfies the comparison inequality

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{0}(p\,{}^{x_{1}}_{x_{2}}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{0}(p\,{}^{x_{2}}_{x_{3}}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{0}(p\,{}^{x_{3}}_{x_{1}}\hskip-0.86108pt)\leqslant\penalty 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi

for any quadruple of points
(p,x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
,
but is not isometric to a subset of an Alexandrov space with curvature
\geqslant\penalty 0
.

10.12. Exercise.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space.
Assume that a point array
(a^{0},a^{1},\dots\penalty,a^{k})
in
\mathcal{L}

is
\upkappa
-strutting
(Definition
15A
)
for a point
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
.
Show that there are points

\tilde{p},\tilde{a}^{0},\dots\penalty,\tilde{a}^{m}
in
\mathbb{M}^{m+1}({\upkappa})
such that

|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{a}^{i}}|=|{p}-\penalty{a^{i}}|\quad\text{and}\quad|{\tilde{a}^{i}}-\penalty{\tilde{a}^{j}}|=|{a^{i}}-\penalty{a^{j}}|

for all
i
and
j
.

C
Helly’s theorem

10.13. Helly’s theorem.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
space
and
\{K_{\upalpha}\}_{\upalpha\in\penalty\mathcal{A}}
be an arbitrary collection of closed bounded convex subsets in
\mathcal{U}
.

If

\bigcap_{\upalpha\in\penalty\mathcal{A}}K_{\upalpha}=\varnothing,

then there is a finite index array
(\upalpha_{1},\upalpha_{2},\dots\penalty,\upalpha_{n})
in
\mathcal{A}
such that

\bigcap_{i}K_{\upalpha_{i}}=\varnothing.

Remarks.

(i)

In general, none of the
K_{\upalpha}
may be compact;
otherwise the statement is trivial.

(ii)

If
\mathcal{U}
is a Hilbert space (not necessarily separable),
then Helly’s theorem is equivalent to the following statement: if a convex bounded set is closed in the ordinary topology then it is compact in the weak topology.
One can define
weak topology
in an arbitrary metric space by taking exteriors of closed ball as prebase.
Then
Helly’s theorem
implies the analogous statement for complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
spaces
(compare to
[
117
]
).

We present the proof of Urs Lang and Viktor Schroeder
[
97
]
.

Proof of
10C
.

Assume the contrary. Then for any finite set
F\subset\penalty\mathcal{A}
,

K_{F}:=\bigcap_{\upalpha\in\penalty F}K_{\upalpha}\neq\varnothing.

We will construct a point
z
such that
z\in\penalty K_{\upalpha}
for each
\upalpha
.
Thus we will arrive at a contradiction since

\bigcap_{\upalpha\in\penalty\mathcal{A}}K_{\upalpha}=\varnothing.

Choose a point
p\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
, and let
r=\sup\{\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{K_{F}}{p}{}\}
where
F
runs over all finite subsets of
\mathcal{A}
.
Let
p^{*}_{F}
be the closest point on
K_{F}
to
p
;
according to the closest-point projection lemma (
9N
),
p^{*}_{F}

exists and is unique.

Take a nested sequence of finite subsets

F_{1}\subset\penalty F_{2}\subset\penalty\dots\penalty
of
\mathcal{A}
, such that
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{K_{F_{n}}}{p}{}\to\penalty r
.

Let us show that the sequence
p^{*}_{F_{n}}
is Cauchy.
If not, then for fixed
\upvarepsilon>0
,
we can choose two subsequences
y^{\prime}_{n}
and
y^{\prime\prime}_{n}
of
p^{*}_{F_{n}}

such that
|{y^{\prime}_{n}}-\penalty{y^{\prime\prime}_{n}}|\geqslant\penalty\upvarepsilon
.
Let
z_{n}
be the midpoint of
[y^{\prime}_{n}y^{\prime\prime}_{n}]
.
From the point-on-side comparison (
8C
),
there is
\updelta>0
such that

|{p}-\penalty{z_{n}}|\leqslant\penalty\max\{|{p}-\penalty{y^{\prime}_{n}}|,|{p}-\penalty{y^{\prime\prime}_{n}}|\}-\updelta.

Thus

\varlimsup_{n\to\penalty\infty}|{p}-\penalty{z_{n}}|<r.

On the other hand, from convexity, each
K_{F_{n}}

contains all
z_{k}
with sufficiently large
k
, a contradiction.

Thus,
p^{*}_{F_{n}}
converges and we can take
z=\lim_{n}p^{*}_{F_{n}}
.
Clearly

|{p}-\penalty{z}|=r.

Repeat the above arguments for the sequence
F_{n}^{\prime}=F_{n}\cup\penalty\{\upalpha\}
.
As a result, we get another point
z^{\prime}
such that
|{p}-\penalty{z}|=|{p}-\penalty{z^{\prime}}|=r
and

z,z^{\prime}\in\penalty K_{F_{n}}
for all
n
.
Thus, if
z\neq z^{\prime}
the midpoint
\hat{z}
of
[zz^{\prime}]
would belong to all

K_{F_{n}}
, and from comparison, we would have
|{p}-\penalty{\hat{z}}|<r
, a contradiction.

Thus,
z^{\prime}=z
; in particular

z\in\penalty K_{\upalpha}
for each
\upalpha\in\penalty\mathcal{A}
.
∎

D
Kirszbraun’s theorem

A slightly weaker version of the following theorem was proved by Urs Lang and Viktor Schroeder
[
97
]
.

10.14. Kirszbraun’s theorem.

Let

\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space,

\mathcal{U}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space,

Q\subset\penalty\mathcal{L}
be arbitrary subset
and
f\colon Q\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
be a short map.
Assume that there is
z\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
such that

f(Q)\subset\penalty\mathrm{B}{}[z,\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}]_{\mathcal{U}}
.
Then
f\colon Q\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
can be extended to a short map

F\colon\mathcal{L}\to\penalty\mathcal{U}

(that is, there is a short map
F\colon\mathcal{L}\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
such that
F|_{Q}=f
).

The condition
f(Q)\subset\penalty\mathrm{B}{}[z,\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}]
trivially holds for any
\upkappa\leqslant\penalty 0
since in this case
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}=\infty
.
The following example shows that this condition is needed for
\upkappa>0
.

Conjecture
10E
(if true) gives an equivalent condition for the existence of a short extension;
it states that the following example is the only obstacle.

10.15. Example.

Let
\mathbb{S}^{m}_{+}
be a closed
m
-dimensional unit hemisphere. Denote its boundary, which is isometric to
\mathbb{S}^{m-1}
, by
\partial\mathbb{S}^{m}_{+}
.
Clearly,
\mathbb{S}^{m}_{+}
is
\text{\rm CBB}({1})
and
\partial\mathbb{S}^{m}_{+}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({1})
, but the identity map
{\partial\mathbb{S}^{m}_{+}}\to\penalty\partial\mathbb{S}^{m}_{+}
cannot be extended to a short map
\mathbb{S}^{m}_{+}\to\penalty\partial\mathbb{S}^{m}_{+}
(there is no place for the pole).

There is also a direct generalization of this example to a hemisphere in a Hilbert space of arbitrary cardinal dimension.

First we prove this theorem in the case
\upkappa\leqslant\penalty 0
(
10D
).
In the proof of the more complicated case
\upkappa>0
, we use the case
\upkappa=0
.
The following lemma is the main ingredient in the proof.

10.16. Finite
\bm{+}
one lemma.

Let
\upkappa\leqslant\penalty 0
,

\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space, and

\mathcal{U}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space.
Suppose

x^{1},x^{2},\dots\penalty,x^{n}
in
\mathcal{L}

and
y^{1},y^{2},\dots\penalty,y^{n}
in
\mathcal{U}

are
such that
|{x^{i}}-\penalty{x^{j}}|\geqslant\penalty|{y^{i}}-\penalty{y^{j}}|
for all
i,j
.

Then for any
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
, there is
q\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
such that
|{y^{i}}-\penalty{q}|\leqslant\penalty|{x^{i}}-\penalty{p}|
for each
i
.

Proof.

It is sufficient to prove the lemma only for
\upkappa=0
and
-1
.
The proofs of these two cases are identical, only the formulas differ.
In the proof, we assume
\upkappa=0
and provide the formulas for
\upkappa=-1
in the footnotes.

From the (1+
n
)-point comparison (
10B
),
there is a model configuration

\tilde{p},\tilde{x}^{1},\tilde{x}^{2},\dots\penalty,\tilde{x}^{n}
in
\mathbb{M}^{n}({\upkappa})
such that

|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{i}}|=|{p}-\penalty{x^{i}}|

and
|{\tilde{x}^{i}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{j}}|\geqslant\penalty|{x^{i}}-\penalty{x^{j}}|

for all

,

.
It follows that we can assume that
\mathcal{L}=\mathbb{M}^{n}({\upkappa})
.

For each

, consider functions

and

defined as follows:

In case

\displaystyle f^{i}=\cosh\circ\penalty\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{y^{i}}{}{},

\displaystyle\tilde{f}^{i}=\cosh\circ\penalty\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\tilde{x}^{i}}{}{}.

[formula]

\displaystyle f^{i}=\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{y^{i}}^{2}{}{},

\displaystyle\tilde{f}^{i}=\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\tilde{x}^{i}}^{2}{}{}.

[formula]

Consider the function arrays

.

Define

\displaystyle=\left\{\,{\bm{v}\in\penalty\mathbb{R}^{k+1}}\ {:}\ {\exists\,\bm{w}\in\penalty\bm{f}(\mathcal{U})\ \text{such that}\ \bm{v}\succcurlyeq\bm{w}}\,\right\},

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm Min}\nolimits\bm{f}(\mathcal{U})

\displaystyle=\left\{\,{\bm{v}\in\penalty\bm{f}(\mathcal{U})}\ {:}\ {\text{if}\ \bm{v}\succcurlyeq\bm{w}\in\penalty\bm{f}(\mathcal{U})\ \text{then}\ \bm{w}=\bm{v}}\,\right\}.

(See Definition
14A
.)
Note it is sufficient to prove that
\bm{\tilde{f}}(\tilde{p})\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Up}\nolimits\bm{f}(\mathcal{U})
.

Clearly,

(f^{i})^{\prime\prime}\geqslant\penalty 1
.
Thus by Theorem

,
the set

is convex.

Arguing by contradiction, let us assume that

.

Then there exists a supporting hyperplane
\upalpha_{1}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x_{1}+\ldots\upalpha_{n}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x_{n}=c
to
\mathop{\rm Up}\nolimits\bm{f}(\mathcal{U})
, separating it from
\bm{\tilde{f}}(\tilde{p})
.
According to Lemma

,

\upalpha_{i}\geqslant\penalty 0
for each

.
So we may assume that

(\upalpha_{1},\upalpha_{2},\dots\penalty,\upalpha_{n})\in\penalty\Delta^{n-1}

(that is,

\upalpha_{i}\geqslant\penalty 0
for each

and
\sum\upalpha_{i}=1

and

\sum_{i}\upalpha_{i}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\tilde{f}^{i}(\tilde{p}))<\inf\left\{\,{\sum_{i}\upalpha_{i}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f^{i}(q)}\ {:}\ {q\in\penalty\mathcal{U}}\,\right\}.

The latter contradicts the following claim.

➊

Given
\bm{\upalpha}=(\upalpha_{1},\upalpha_{2},\dots\penalty,\upalpha_{n})\in\penalty\Delta^{n-1}
,
let

\displaystyle h=\sum_{i}\upalpha_{i}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f^{i}

\displaystyle h\colon\mathcal{U}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle z=\mathop{\rm MinPoint}h\in\penalty\mathcal{U}

\displaystyle\tilde{h}=\sum_{i}\upalpha_{i}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\tilde{f}^{i}

\displaystyle\tilde{h}\colon\mathbb{M}^{n}({\upkappa})\to\penalty\mathbb{R}

Then

h(z)\leqslant\penalty\tilde{h}(\tilde{z})
.

Proof of the claim.

Note that
\bm{d}_{z}h\geqslant\penalty 0
.
Thus, for each
i
, we have

10.17. Kirszbraun’s theorem for nonpositive bound.

Let

\upkappa\leqslant\penalty 0
,

\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space,

\mathcal{U}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space,

Q\subset\penalty\mathcal{L}
be arbitrary subset
and
f\colon Q\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
be a short map.
Then there is a short extension

F\colon\mathcal{L}\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
of
f
;
that is, there is a short map
F\colon\mathcal{L}\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
such that
F|_{Q}=f
.

Remark.

If
\mathcal{U}
is proper, then we do not need Helly’s theorem (
10C
); compactness of closed balls in
\mathcal{U}
is sufficient in this case.

Proof of
10D
.

By Zorn’s lemma, we can assume
that
Q\subset\penalty\mathcal{L}
is a maximal set;
that is,
f\colon Q\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
does not admit a short extension to any larger set
Q^{\prime}\supset\penalty Q
.

Let us argue by contradiction.
Assume that
Q\neq\mathcal{L}
;
choose
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}\setminus Q
.
Then

\bigcap_{x\in\penalty Q}\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[f(x),|{p}-\penalty{x}|]=\varnothing.

Since
\upkappa\leqslant\penalty 0
, the balls are convex;
thus, by Helly’s theorem (
10C
),
one can choose points
x^{1},x^{2},\dots\penalty,x^{n}
in
Q
such that

\bigcap_{i}\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[y^{i},|{x^{i}}-\penalty{p}|]=\varnothing,

➋

where
y^{i}=f(x^{i})
.
Finally note that
➋
contradicts the finite+one lemma (
10D
).∎

Proof of Kirszbraun’s theorem (
10D
).

The case
\upkappa\leqslant\penalty 0
is already proved in
10D
.
Thus it remains to prove the theorem only in case
\upkappa>0
.
After rescaling we can assume that
\upkappa=1

and therefore
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}=\uppi
.

Since
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[z,\uppi/2]_{\mathcal{U}}
is a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space, we can assume
\mathcal{U}=\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[z,\uppi/2]_{\mathcal{U}}
.
In particular,
\mathop{\rm diam}\nolimits\mathcal{U}\leqslant\penalty\uppi
.

Further, any two points
x,y\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
such that
|{x}-\penalty{y}|<\uppi
are joined by a unique geodesic;
if
|{x}-\penalty{y}|=\uppi
, then the concatenation of

[xz]
and
[zy]
as a geodesic from
x
to
y
.
Hence
\mathcal{U}
is geodesic.

We may also assume that
\mathop{\rm diam}\nolimits\mathcal{L}\leqslant\penalty\uppi
.
Otherwise
\mathcal{L}
is one-dimensional (see
8H
);
in this case the result follows since
\mathcal{U}
is geodesic.

Assume the theorem is false. Then
there is a set
Q\subset\penalty\mathcal{L}
,
a short map
f\colon Q\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
, and

p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}\setminus Q
such that

\bigcap_{x\in\penalty Q}\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[f(x),|{x}-\penalty{p}|]=\varnothing.

➌

We will apply
10D
for
\upkappa=0
to the Euclidean cones
\mathring{\mathcal{L}}=\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits\mathcal{L}
and
\mathring{\mathcal{U}}=\penalty\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits\mathcal{U}
.
Note that

\diamond

{\mathring{\mathcal{U}}}
is a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
space (see
11D
),

\diamond

since
\mathop{\rm diam}\nolimits\mathcal{L}\leqslant\penalty\uppi
we have
{\mathring{\mathcal{L}}}
is
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
(see
11D
).

Further, we will view the spaces
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{U}
as unit spheres in
\mathring{\mathcal{L}}
and
\mathring{\mathcal{U}}
respectively.
In the cones
\mathring{\mathcal{L}}
and
\mathring{\mathcal{U}}
we will use
“
|{*}|
” for distance to the tip, denoted by
0
,
“
{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}
” for cone multiplication,
“
\measuredangle(x,y)
” for
\measuredangle[0\,{}^{x}_{y}]
,
and “
\langle x,y\rangle
” for
|x|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|y|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\measuredangle[0\,{}^{x}_{y}]
.
In particular,

\diamond

|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\mathcal{L}}=\measuredangle(x,y)
for any
x,y\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
,

\diamond

|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\mathcal{U}}=\measuredangle(x,y)
for any
x,y\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
,

\diamond

for any
y\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
, we have

\measuredangle(z,y)\leqslant\penalty\tfrac{\uppi}{2}.

➍

Let
\mathring{Q}=\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits Q\subset\penalty\mathring{\mathcal{L}}
and let
\mathring{f}\colon\mathring{Q}\to\penalty\mathring{\mathcal{U}}
be the natural cone extension of
f
;
that is,

y=f(x)

\Rightarrow

t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y=\mathring{f}(t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x)

for
t\geqslant\penalty 0
.
Clearly
\mathring{f}
is short.

Applying
10D
for
\mathring{f}
,
we get a short extension map
\mathring{F}\colon\mathring{\mathcal{L}}\to\penalty\mathring{\mathcal{U}}
.
Let
s=\mathring{F}(p)
.
Then

|{s}-\penalty{\mathring{f}(w)}|\leqslant\penalty|{p}-\penalty{w}|

➎

for any
w\in\penalty\mathring{Q}
.
In particular,
|s|\leqslant\penalty 1
.
Applying
➎

for
w=t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x
and
t\to\penalty\infty
we have

\langle f(x),s\rangle\geqslant\penalty\cos\measuredangle(p,x)

➏

for any
x\in\penalty Q
.

By comparison,
the geodesics
\mathrm{geod}_{[s\ t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}z]}
converge as
t\to\penalty\infty

and the limit is a half-line;
denote it by
\upalpha\colon[0,\infty)\to\penalty\mathring{\mathcal{U}}
.
From
➍
,
the function
t\mapsto\penalty\langle f(x),\upalpha(t)\rangle
is nondecreasing.
From
➏
, for
the necessarily unique point
\bar{s}
on the half-line
\upalpha
such that
|\bar{s}|=1
, we also have

\langle f(x),\bar{s}\rangle\geqslant\penalty\cos\measuredangle(p,x)\quad\text{or, equivalently,}\quad\measuredangle(\bar{s},f(x))\leqslant\penalty\measuredangle(p,f(x))

for any
x\in\penalty Q
,
in contradiction to
➌
.
∎

10.18. Exercise.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
.
Assume there are two point arrays,
(x^{0},x^{1},\dots\penalty,x^{k})
in
\mathcal{U}
and
(\tilde{x}^{0},\tilde{x}^{1},\dots\penalty,\tilde{x}^{k})
in
\mathbb{E}^{m}
, such that

|{x^{i}}-\penalty{x^{j}}|_{\mathcal{U}}=\penalty|{\tilde{x}^{i}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{j}}|_{\mathbb{E}^{m}}
for each
i,j
, and
for any point
z_{0}\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
there is
i>0
such that
|{z_{0}}-\penalty{x_{i}}|\geqslant\penalty|{x_{0}}-\penalty{x_{i}}|
.

Prove that there is a subset
Q\subset\penalty\mathcal{L}
isometric to a convex set in
\mathbb{E}^{m}
and containing all the points
x^{i}
.

10.19. Exercise.

Let

(x^{0},x^{1},\dots\penalty,x^{k})
in
\mathcal{L}
and
(\tilde{x}^{0},\tilde{x}^{1},\dots\penalty,\tilde{x}^{k})
in
\mathbb{E}^{m}

be two point arrays in complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
space
\mathcal{L}
.
Assume that

|{x^{i}}-\penalty{x^{j}}|_{\mathcal{L}}=|{\tilde{x}^{i}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}^{j}}|_{\mathbb{E}^{m}}
for each
i,j

and

\tilde{x}^{0}
lies in the interior of
\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits(\tilde{x}^{1},\dots\penalty,\tilde{x}^{k})
.

Prove that there is a subset
Q\subset\penalty\mathcal{L}
isometric to a convex set in
\mathbb{E}^{m}
and containing all the points
x^{i}
.

The following statement we call
(2
n
+2)-point comparison
.

10.20. Exercise.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space.
Consider
x,y\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
and an array
((p^{1},q^{1})
,
(p^{2},q^{2}),\dots\penalty,(p^{n},q^{n}))
of pairs of points in
\mathcal{U}
, such that there is a model configuration

\tilde{x}
,
\tilde{y}
and array of pairs
((\tilde{p}^{1},\tilde{q}^{1})
,
(\tilde{p}^{2},\tilde{q}^{2}),\dots\penalty,(\tilde{p}^{n},\tilde{q}^{n}))
in
\mathbb{M}^{3}({\upkappa})
with the following properties:

(a)

[{\tilde{x}}{\tilde{p}^{1}}{\tilde{q}^{1}}]={\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}xp^{1}q^{1}

and

[{\tilde{y}}{\tilde{p}^{n}}{\tilde{q}^{n}}]={\tilde{\triangle}}^{\upkappa}yp^{n}q^{n}
,

(b)

the simplex
\tilde{p}^{i}\tilde{p}^{i+1}\tilde{q}^{i}\tilde{q}^{i+1}
is a model simplex
of
p^{i}p^{i+1}q^{i}q^{i+1}

for all
i
;
that is,

|{\tilde{p}^{i}}-\penalty{\tilde{q}^{i}}|=|{p^{i}}-\penalty{q^{i}}|
,

|{\tilde{p}^{i}}-\penalty{\tilde{p}^{i+1}}|=|{p^{i}}-\penalty{p^{i+1}}|
,

,

,
and
|{\tilde{p}^{i+1}}-\penalty{\tilde{q}^{i}}|=|{p^{i+1}}-\penalty{q^{i}}|
.

Then for any choice of
n
points
\tilde{z}^{i}\in\penalty[\tilde{p}^{i}\tilde{q}^{i}]
,
we have

|{\tilde{x}}-\penalty{\tilde{z}^{1}}|+|{\tilde{z}^{1}}-\penalty{\tilde{z}^{2}}|+\dots\penalty+|{\tilde{z}^{n-1}}-\penalty{\tilde{z}^{n}}|+|{\tilde{z}^{n}}-\penalty{\tilde{y}}|\geqslant\penalty|{x}-\penalty{y}|.

E
Remarks

The following problem and its relatives were mentioned by Michael Gromov
[
70
, 1.19]
.

10.21. Open problem.

Find a necessary and sufficient condition for a finite metric space to admit distance-preserving embeddings into

(a)

some length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space,

(b)

some length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space.

A metric on a finite set
\{a^{1},a^{2},\dots\penalty,a^{n}\}
,
can be described by the matrix with components

s^{ij}=|{a^{i}}-\penalty{a^{j}}|^{2},

which we will call the
associated matrix
.
The set of associated matrices of all metrics that admit a distance-preserving map into a
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
or a
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
space
form a convex cone.
The latter follows since the rescalings and products of
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
(or
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
) spaces are
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
(or
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
respectively).
This convexity gives a bit of hope that the cone admits an explicit description.

For the 5-point
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
case, the (2+2)-comparison is a necessary and sufficient condition.
This was proved by Tetsu Toyoda
[
154
]
;
another proof was found by Nina Lebedeva and the third author
[
103
]
.
For the 5-point
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
case, the (1+4)-comparison is a necessary and sufficient condition;
it was proved by Nina Lebedeva and the third author
[
102
]
.
Starting from the 6-point case, only some necessary and some sufficient conditions are known;
for more on the subject see
[
9
,
103
,
101
]
.

The following conjecture (if true) would give the right generality for Kirszbraun’s theorem (
10D
).
It states that the example
10D

is the only obstacle to extending short maps.

10.22. Conjecture.

Assume
\mathcal{L}
is a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({1})
space,

\mathcal{U}
is a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({1})
space,

Q\subset\penalty\mathcal{L}
is a proper subset,
and
f\colon Q\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
is a short map that does not admit a short extension to any bigger set
Q^{\prime}\supset\penalty Q
.
Then:

(a)

Q
is isometric to a sphere in a Hilbert space (of finite or cardinal dimension).
Moreover, there is a point
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
such that
|{p}-\penalty{q}|=\tfrac{\uppi}{2}
for any
q\in\penalty Q
.

(b)

The map
f\colon Q\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
is a distance-preserving map and there is no point
p^{\prime}\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
such that
|{p^{\prime}}-\penalty{q^{\prime}}|=\tfrac{\uppi}{2}
for any
q^{\prime}\in\penalty f(Q)
.

Curvature-free analogs.

Let us present a collection of exercises on curvature-free analogs of Kirszbraun’s theorem.
It is worthwhile to know these results despite they are far from Alexandrov geometry.

10.23. Exercise.

Let
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
be metric spaces,
A\subset\penalty\mathcal{X}
, and
f\colon A\to\penalty\mathcal{Y}
be a short map.
Assume
\mathcal{Y}
is compact and for any finite set
F\subset\penalty\mathcal{X}
there is a short map
F\to\penalty\mathcal{Y}
that agrees with
f
on
F\cap\penalty A
.
Then there is a short map
\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathcal{Y}
that agrees with
f
on
A
.

10.24. Exercise.

We say that a metric space
\mathcal{X}
is
injective

if for an arbitrary metric space
\mathcal{Z}

and a subset
Q\subset\penalty\mathcal{Z}
,
any short map
Q\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
can be extended as a short map
\mathcal{Z}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
.

(a)

Prove that any metric space
\mathcal{X}
admits a distance-preserving embedding into an injective metric space.

(b)

Use this to construct an analog of convex hull in the category of metric spaces; this is called the
injective hull
.

Chapter 11
Warped products

The warped product is a construction that produces
a new metric space, denoted by
\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{f}}\mathcal{F}
,
from two metric spaces base
\mathcal{B}
and fiber
\mathcal{F}
, and a function
f\colon\mathcal{B}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant\penalty 0}
.

Many important constructions such as direct product, cone, spherical suspension, and join can be defined using warped products.

A
Definition

First we define the warped product for length spaces and then we expand the definition to allow for arbitrary fiber
\mathcal{F}
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{F}
be length spaces and
f\colon\mathcal{B}\to\penalty[0,\infty)
be a continuous function.

For any path
\upgamma\colon[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{B}\times\penalty\mathcal{F}
, we write
\upgamma=(\upgamma_{\mathcal{B}},\upgamma_{\mathcal{F}})
where

\upgamma_{\mathcal{B}}
is the projection of
\upgamma
to
\mathcal{B}
,
and
\upgamma_{\mathcal{F}}
is the projection to
\mathcal{F}
.
If
\upgamma_{\mathcal{B}}
and
\upgamma_{\mathcal{F}}
are Lipschitz, set

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits_{f}\upgamma:=\int\limits_{0}^{1}\sqrt{v_{\mathcal{B}}^{2}+(f\circ\penalty\upgamma_{\mathcal{B}})^{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}v_{\mathcal{F}}^{2}}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dt,

➊

where
\int
is Lebesgue integral, and
v_{\mathcal{B}}
and
v_{\mathcal{F}}
are the speeds of
\upgamma_{\mathcal{B}}
and
\upgamma_{\mathcal{F}}
respectively.
(Note that
\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits_{f}\upgamma\geqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upgamma_{\mathcal{B}}
.)

Consider the pseudometric on
\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{f}}\mathcal{F}
defined by

|{x}-\penalty{y}|:=\inf\left\{\,{\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits_{f}\upgamma}\ {:}\ {\upgamma(0)=x,\upgamma(1)=y}\,\right\}

where the exact lower bound is taken for all Lipschitz paths
\upgamma\colon[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{B}\times\penalty\mathcal{F}
.
The corresponding metric space is called the
warped product with base
\mathcal{B}
, fiber
\mathcal{F}
and warping function
f
; it will be denoted by
\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{f}}\mathcal{F}
.

The points in
\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{f}}\mathcal{F}
can be described by corresponding pairs
(p,\upvarphi)\in\penalty\mathcal{B}\times\penalty\mathcal{F}
. Note that if
f(p)=0
for
p\in\penalty\mathcal{B}
, then
(p,\upvarphi)=\penalty(p,\uppsi)
for any
\upvarphi,\uppsi\in\penalty\mathcal{F}
.

We do not claim that every Lipschitz curve in
\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{f}}\mathcal{F}
may be reparametrized as the image of a Lipschitz curve in
\mathcal{B}\times\penalty\mathcal{F}
; in fact this is not true.

11.1. Proposition.

The warped product
\mathcal{\mathcal{B}}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{f}}\mathcal{F}
satisfies:

(a)

The projection
(p,\upvarphi_{0})\mapsto\penalty p
is a submetry.
Moreover, its restriction to any horizontal leaf
\mathcal{B}\times\penalty\{\upvarphi_{0}\}

is an isometry to
\mathcal{B}
.

(b)

If
f(p_{0})\neq 0
, the projection
(p_{0},\upvarphi)\mapsto\penalty\upvarphi
of the vertical leaf
\{p_{0}\}\times\penalty\mathcal{F}
, with its length metric, is a homothety onto
\mathcal{F}
with multiplier
\tfrac{1}{f(p_{0})}
.

(c)

If
f
achieves its (local) minimum at
p_{0}
, then the inclusion of the vertical leaf
\{p_{0}\}\times\penalty\mathcal{F}
in
\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{f}}\mathcal{F}
is (locally) distance-preserving.

Proof.

Claim
11A
follows from the
f
-length formula
➊
.

Also, by
➊
, the projection of

\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{f}}\mathcal{F}
onto
\mathcal{B}\times\penalty\{\upvarphi_{0}\}
given by
(p,\upvarphi)\mapsto\penalty(p,\upvarphi_{0})
is length-nonincreasing; hence
11A
.

Suppose
p_{0}
is a local minimum point of
f
.
Then the projection
(p,\upvarphi)\mapsto\penalty(p_{0},\upvarphi)
of a neighborhood of the vertical leaf
\{p_{0}\}\times\penalty\mathcal{F}
to
\{p_{0}\}\times\penalty\mathcal{F}
is length-nonincreasing.

If
p_{0}
is a global minimum point of
f
, then the same holds for the projection of whole space.
Hence
11A
.
∎

Note that any horizontal leaf is weakly convex, but does not have to be convex even if
\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{f}}\mathcal{F}
is a geodesic space.
The latter follows since vanishing of the warping function
f
allows geodesics to bifurcate into distinct horizontal leaves.
For instance, if there is a geodesic with the ends in the zero set

Z=\left\{\,{(p,\upvarphi)\in\penalty\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{f}}\mathcal{F})}\ {:}\ {f(p)=0}\,\right\},

then there is a geodesic with the same endpoints in each horizontal leaf.

11.2. Proposition.

Suppose
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{F}
are length spaces and
f\colon\mathcal{B}\to\penalty[0,\infty)
is a continuous function.
Then the warped product
\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{f}}\mathcal{F}
is a length space.

Proof.

It is sufficient to show that for any
\upalpha\colon[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{f}}\mathcal{F}
there is a path

\upbeta\colon[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{B}\times\penalty\mathcal{F}
with the same endpoints such that

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upalpha\geqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits_{f}\upbeta.

If
f\circ\penalty\upalpha_{\mathcal{B}}(t)>0
for any
t
, then the vertical projection
\upalpha_{\mathcal{F}}
is defined.
In this case, let
\upbeta(t)=(\upalpha_{\mathcal{B}}(t),\upalpha_{\mathcal{F}}(t))\in\penalty\mathcal{B}\times\penalty\mathcal{F}
.
Clearly

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upalpha=\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits_{f}\upbeta.

If
f\circ\penalty\upalpha_{\mathcal{B}}(t_{0})=0
for some
t_{0}
, let
\upbeta
be the concatenation of three curves in
\mathcal{B}\times\penalty{\mathcal{F}}
,
namely:
(1) the horizontal curve
(\upalpha_{\mathcal{B}}(t),\upvarphi)
for
t\leqslant\penalty t_{0}
,
(2) a vertical path in form
(s,\upvarphi)
to
(s,\uppsi)

and
(3) the horizontal curve
(\upalpha_{\mathcal{B}}(t),\uppsi)
for
t\geqslant\penalty t_{0}
.
By
➊
, the
f
-length of the middle path in the concatenation is vanishing;
therefore the
f
-length of
\upalpha
cannot be smaller than length of
\upalpha_{\mathcal{B}}
,
that is,

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits_{f}\upalpha\geqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upalpha_{\mathcal{B}}=\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits_{f}\upbeta.

The statement follows.
∎

The following theorem states that
distance in a warped product is fiber-independent, in the sense that distances may be calculated by substituting for
\mathcal{F}
a different length space:

11.3. Fiber-independence theorem.

Consider length spaces
\mathcal{B}
,
\mathcal{F}
and
\check{\mathcal{F}}
, and a locally Lipschitz function

f\colon\mathcal{B}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant\penalty 0}
.
Assume
p,q\in\penalty\mathcal{B}
,
\upvarphi,\uppsi\in\penalty\mathcal{F}
and
\check{\upvarphi},\check{\uppsi}\in\penalty\check{\mathcal{F}}
.
Then

\displaystyle|{\upvarphi}-\penalty{\uppsi}|_{\mathcal{F}}

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty|{\check{\upvarphi}}-\penalty{\check{\uppsi}}|_{\check{\mathcal{F}}}

\displaystyle\Downarrow

\displaystyle|{(p,\upvarphi)}-\penalty{(q,\uppsi)}|_{\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{f}}\mathcal{F}}

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty|{(p,\check{\upvarphi})}-\penalty{(q,\check{\uppsi})}|_{\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{f}}\check{\mathcal{F}}}.

In particular,

|{(p,\upvarphi)}-\penalty{(q,\uppsi)}|_{\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{f}}\mathcal{F}}=|{(p,0)}-\penalty{(q,\ell)}|_{\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{f}}\mathbb{R}},

where
\ell=|{\upvarphi}-\penalty{\uppsi}|_{\mathcal{F}}
.

Proof.

Let
\upgamma
be a path in
(\mathcal{B}\times\penalty\mathcal{F})
.

Since
|{\upvarphi}-\penalty{\uppsi}|_{\mathcal{F}}\geqslant\penalty|{\check{\upvarphi}}-\penalty{\check{\uppsi}}|_{\check{\mathcal{F}}}
,
there is a Lipschitz path
\upgamma_{\check{\mathcal{F}}}

from
\check{\upvarphi}
to
\check{\uppsi}
in
\check{\mathcal{F}}
such that

(\mathop{\rm speed}\nolimits\upgamma_{\mathcal{F}})(t)\geqslant\penalty(\mathop{\rm speed}\nolimits\upgamma_{\check{\mathcal{F}}})(t)

for almost all
t\in\penalty[0,1]
.
Consider the path
\check{\upgamma}=(\upgamma_{\mathcal{B}},\upgamma_{\check{\mathcal{F}}})
from
(p,\check{\upvarphi})
to
(q,\check{\uppsi})
in
\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{f}}\check{\mathcal{F}}
.
Clearly

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits_{f}\upgamma\geqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits_{f}\check{\upgamma}.

∎

11.4. Exercise.

Let
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{F}
be length spaces and
f,g\colon\mathcal{B}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant\penalty 0}
be two locally Lipschitz nonnegative functions.
Assume
f(b)\leqslant\penalty g(b)
for any
b\in\penalty\mathcal{B}
.
Show that

\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{f}}\mathcal{F}\leqslant\penalty\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{g}}\mathcal{F}
;
that is, there is a distance-noncontracting map
\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{f}}\mathcal{F}\to\penalty\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{g}}\mathcal{F}
.

B
Extended definitions

The fiber-independence theorem implies that

|{(p,\upvarphi)}-\penalty{(q,\uppsi)}|_{\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{f}}\mathcal{F}}=|{(p,0)}-\penalty{(q,|{\upvarphi}-\penalty{\uppsi}|_{\mathcal{F}})}|_{\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{f}}\mathbb{R}}

for any
(p,\upvarphi),(q,\uppsi)\in\penalty\mathcal{B}\times\penalty\mathcal{F}
.
In particular, if
\upiota\colon A\to\penalty\check{A}
is an isometry between two subsets

A\subset\penalty\mathcal{F}
and
\check{A}\subset\penalty\check{\mathcal{F}}

in length spaces
\mathcal{F}
and
\check{\mathcal{F}}
, and
\mathcal{B}
is a length space, then for any warping function
f\colon\mathcal{B}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant\penalty 0}
,
the map
\upiota
induces an isometry between the sets

\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{f}}A\subset\penalty\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{f}}\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{f}}\check{A}\subset\penalty\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{f}}\check{\mathcal{F}}
.

This observation allows us to define the warped product
\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{f}}\penalty\mathcal{F}
where the fiber
\mathcal{F}
does not carry its length metric.
Indeed we can use Kuratowsky embedding to realize
\mathcal{F}
as a subspace in a length space, say
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
.
Therefore we can take the warped product
\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{f}}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}

and identify
\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{f}}\mathcal{F}
with its subspace consisting of all pairs
(b,\upvarphi)
such that
\upvarphi\in\penalty\mathcal{F}
.
According to the Fiber-independence theorem
11A
, the resulting space does not depend on the choice of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
.

C
Examples

Direct product.

The simplest example is the
direct product

\mathcal{B}\times\penalty\mathcal{F}
, which can also be written as the warped product
\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{1}}\mathcal{F}
.
That is, for
p,q\in\penalty\mathcal{B}
and
\upvarphi,\uppsi\in\penalty\mathcal{F}
, the latter metric simplifies to

|{(p,\upvarphi)}-\penalty{(q,\uppsi)}|=\sqrt{|{p}-\penalty{q}|^{2}+|{\upvarphi}-\penalty{\uppsi}|^{2}}.

Cones.

The
Euclidean cone

\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits\mathcal{F}
over a metric space
\mathcal{F}

can be written as the warped product
[0,\infty)\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}}\mathcal{F}
.
That is, for
s,t\in\penalty[0,\infty)
and
\upvarphi,\uppsi\in\penalty\mathcal{F}
,
the metric is given by the cosine rule

|{(s,\upvarphi)}-\penalty{(t,\uppsi)}|=\sqrt{s^{2}+t^{2}-2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\upalpha},

where
\upalpha=\min\{\uppi,|{\upvarphi}-\penalty{\uppsi}|\}
.
(See Section
6E
.)

Instead of the Euclidean cosine rule,
we may use the cosine rule in
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
:

|{(s,\upvarphi)}-\penalty{(t,\uppsi)}|=\mathop{\tilde{\curlyvee}}\nolimits^{\upkappa}\{\upalpha;s,t\}.

This way we get
\upkappa
-cones
over
\mathcal{F}
, denoted by
\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits^{\upkappa}\mathcal{F}=[0,\infty)\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}}}\mathcal{F}
for
\upkappa\leqslant\penalty 0

and
\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits^{\upkappa}\mathcal{F}=[0,\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}]\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}}}\mathcal{F}
for
\upkappa>0
.

The
1
-cone
\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits^{1}\mathcal{F}
is also called the
spherical suspension
over
\mathcal{F}
;
it is also denoted by
\mathop{\rm Susp}\nolimits\mathcal{F}
.
That is,

\mathop{\rm Susp}\nolimits\mathcal{F}=[0,\uppi]\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{\sin}}\mathcal{F}.

11.5. Exercise.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a length space and
A\subset\penalty\mathcal{F}
.
Show that
\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits^{\upkappa}A
is convex in
\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits^{\upkappa}\mathcal{F}

if and only if
A
is
\uppi
-convex in
\mathcal{F}
.

Doubling.

The doubling space
\mathcal{W}
of a metric space
\mathcal{V}
on a closed subset
A\subset\penalty\mathcal{V}

can be also defined as a special type of warped product.
Consider the fiber
\mathbb{S}^{0}
consisting of two points with distance
2
from each other.
Then

\mathcal{W}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it iso}}}\penalty\mathcal{V}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{A}{}{}}}\mathbb{S}^{0};

that is,

\mathcal{W}
is isometric to the warped product
with base
\mathcal{V}
,
fiber
\mathbb{S}^{0}
and warping function
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{A}{}{}
.

D
1-dimensional base

The following theorems provide conditions for the spaces and functions in a warped product with 1-dimensional base to have curvature bounds.
These theorems are originally due to Valerii Berestovskii
[
23
]
.
They are baby cases of the characterization of curvature bounds in warped products given in
[
8
,
7
]
.

11.6. Theorem.

(a)

If
\mathcal{L}
is a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({1})
space and
\mathop{\rm diam}\nolimits\mathcal{L}\leqslant\penalty\uppi
,
then

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm Susp}\nolimits\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=[0,\uppi]\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{\sin}}\mathcal{L}\quad\text{is}\quad\text{\rm CBB}({1}),

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=[0,\infty)\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}}\mathcal{L}\quad\text{is}\quad\text{\rm CBB}({0}),

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits^{-1}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=[0,\infty)\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{\sinh}}\mathcal{L}\quad\text{is}\quad\text{\rm CBB}({-1}).

Moreover, the converse also holds in each of the three cases.

(b)

If
\mathcal{L}
is a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
space,
then

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}\times\penalty\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle\quad\text{is a complete length $\text{\rm CBB}({0})$ space},

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{\exp}}\mathcal{L}

is a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({-1})
space.

Moreover, the converse also holds in each of the two cases.

(c)

If
\mathcal{L}
is a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({-1})
space,
if and only if the warped product
\mathbb{R}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{\cosh}}\mathcal{L}
is a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({-1})
space.

11.7. Theorem.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a metric space.

(a)

If
\mathcal{L}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({1})
,
then

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm Susp}\nolimits\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=[0,\uppi]\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{\sin}}\mathcal{L}\quad\text{is}\quad\text{\rm CAT}({1}),

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=[0,\infty)\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}}\mathcal{L}\quad\text{is}\quad\text{\rm CAT}({0}),

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits^{-1}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=[0,\infty)\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{\sinh}}\mathcal{L}\quad\text{is}\quad\text{\rm CAT}({-1}).

Moreover, the converse also holds in each of the three cases.

(b)

If
\mathcal{L}
is a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
space,
then

\mathbb{R}\times\penalty\mathcal{L}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({0})

and

\mathbb{R}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{\exp}}\mathcal{L}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({-1})
.
Moreover, the converse also holds in each of the two cases.

(c)

If
\mathcal{L}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({-1})

if and only if

\mathbb{R}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{\cosh}}\mathcal{L}

is
\text{\rm CAT}({-1})
.

In the proof of the above two theorems we will use the following proposition.

11.8. Proposition.

(a)

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm Susp}\nolimits\mathbb{S}^{m-1}

\displaystyle=[0,\uppi]\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{\sin}}\mathbb{S}^{m-1}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it iso}}}\penalty\mathbb{S}^{m},

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits\mathbb{S}^{m-1}

\displaystyle=[0,\infty)\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}}\mathbb{S}^{m-1}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it iso}}}\penalty\mathbb{E}^{m},

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits^{-1}\mathbb{S}^{m-1}

\displaystyle=[0,\infty)\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{\sinh}}\mathbb{S}^{m-1}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it iso}}}\penalty\mathbb{M}^{m}({-1}).

(b)

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}\times\penalty\mathbb{E}^{m-1}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it iso}}}\penalty\mathbb{E}^{m},

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{\exp}}\mathbb{E}^{m-1}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it iso}}}\penalty\mathbb{M}^{m}({-1}).

(c)

\mathbb{R}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{\cosh}}\mathbb{M}^{m-1}({-1})\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it iso}}}\penalty\mathbb{M}^{m}({-1}).

The proof is left to the reader.

Proof of
11D
and
11D
.

Each proof is based on the fiber-independence theorem
11A

and
the corresponding statement in Proposition
11D
.

Let us prove the last statement in
11D
; the remaining statements of this theorem are similar.
Choose an arbitrary quadruple of points

(s,\upvarphi),(t^{1},\upvarphi^{1}),(t^{2},\upvarphi^{2}),(t^{3},\upvarphi^{3})\in\penalty[0,\infty)\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{\sinh}}\mathcal{L}.

Since
\mathop{\rm diam}\nolimits\mathcal{L}\leqslant\penalty\uppi
,
the (1+3)-point comparison (
10B
) provides a quadruple of points
\uppsi,\uppsi^{1},\uppsi^{2},\uppsi^{3}\in\penalty\mathbb{S}^{3}
such that

|{\uppsi}-\penalty{\uppsi^{i}}|_{\mathbb{S}^{3}}=|{\upvarphi}-\penalty{\upvarphi^{i}}|_{\mathcal{L}}

and

|{\uppsi^{i}}-\penalty{\uppsi^{j}}|_{\mathbb{S}^{3}}\geqslant\penalty|{\upvarphi^{i}}-\penalty{\upvarphi^{j}}|_{\mathcal{L}}

for all
i
and
j
.

According to Proposition
11D
,

\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits^{-1}\mathbb{S}^{3}=[0,\infty)\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{\sinh}}\mathbb{S}^{3}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it iso}}}\penalty\mathbb{M}^{4}({-1}).

Consider the quadruple of points

(s,\uppsi),(t^{1},\uppsi^{1}),(t^{2},\uppsi^{2}),(t^{3},\uppsi^{3})\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits^{-1}\mathbb{S}^{3}=\mathbb{M}^{4}({-1}).

By the fiber-independence theorem
11A
,

|{(s,\uppsi)}-\penalty{(t^{i},\uppsi^{i})}|_{[0,\infty)\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{\sinh}}\mathbb{S}^{3}}=|{(s,\upvarphi)}-\penalty{(t^{i},\upvarphi^{i})}|_{[0,\infty)\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{\sinh}}\mathcal{L}}

and

|{(t^{i},\uppsi^{i})}-\penalty{(t^{j},\uppsi^{j})}|_{[0,\infty)\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{\sinh}}\mathbb{S}^{3}}\geqslant\penalty|{(t^{i},\upvarphi^{i})}-\penalty{(t^{j},\upvarphi^{j})}|_{[0,\infty)\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{\sinh}}\mathcal{L}}

for all
i
and
j
.
Since four points of
\mathbb{M}^{4}({-1})
lie in an isometric copy of
\mathbb{M}^{3}({-1})
, it remains to apply Exercise
8A
.

Now let us prove the converse to
11D
.
Choose a quadruple
\upvarphi
,
\upvarphi^{1}
,
\upvarphi^{2}
,
\upvarphi^{3}\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
with all distances smaller than
\tfrac{\uppi}{2}
.
Choose small
s>0
and let
t_{i}
be the hypotenuse in a hyperbolic right triangle with angle
|{\upvarphi}-\penalty{\upvarphi^{i}}|_{\mathcal{L}}
and the adjacent side
s
.
Observe that the
\mathbb{M}({-1})
model angles of the quadruple

(s,\upvarphi)
,
(t^{1},\upvarphi^{1})
,
(t^{2},\upvarphi^{2})
,
(t^{3},\upvarphi^{3})
in
[0,\infty)\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{\sinh}}\mathcal{L}

at
(s,\upvarphi)
are the same as the
\mathbb{M}({1})
model angles of the quadruple
\upvarphi
,
\upvarphi^{1}
,
\upvarphi^{2}
,
\upvarphi^{3}\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
at
\upvarphi
.
Whence
\text{\rm CBB}({1})
comparison holds for
\upvarphi,\upvarphi^{1},\upvarphi^{2},\upvarphi^{3}
;
in particular,
\mathcal{L}
is locally
\text{\rm CBB}({1})
.
It remains to apply the globalization theorem (
8F
).

The proof of
11D
is nearly identical, but one has to apply (2+2)-comparison (
9A
).
∎
