E
Remarks

Let us formulate general results on curvature bounds of warped products
proved by the first author and Richard Bishop
[
8
]
.

11.10. Theorem.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a complete finite-dimensional
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
length space, and
f\colon{\mathcal{B}}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant\penalty 0}
be a locally Lipschitz function.
Denote by
Z\subset\penalty\mathcal{B}
the zero set of the restriction of
f
to the boundary
\partial\mathcal{B}
of
\mathcal{B}
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{W}
is doubling of
\mathcal{B}
along the closure of
\partial\mathcal{B}\setminus Z
, and
\bar{f}\colon\mathcal{W}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant\penalty 0}
is the natural extension of
f
.
Assume that
\mathcal{W}
is
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})

and
\bar{f}^{\prime\prime}+\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\bar{f}\leqslant\penalty 0
.

Suppose
\mathcal{F}
is a complete finite-dimensional
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa^{\prime}})
space.
Then the warped product
\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{f}}\mathcal{F}
is
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
in the following two cases:

(a)

If
Z=\varnothing
and

\upkappa^{\prime}\geqslant\penalty\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f^{2}(b)

for any
b\in\penalty\mathcal{B}
.

(b)

If
Z\neq\varnothing
and

|d_{z}f|^{2}\leqslant\penalty\upkappa^{\prime}

for any
z\in\penalty Z
.

We mention that in the setting of this theorem,
f
necessarily vanishes only at boundary points if
f
is not identically
0
.

11.11. Theorem.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a
complete

\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
length space, and the function
f\colon\mathcal{B}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant\penalty 0}
satisfy
f^{\prime\prime}+\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f\geqslant\penalty 0
,
where
f
is Lipschitz on bounded sets or
B
is locally compact.
Denote by
Z\subset\penalty\mathcal{B}
the zero set of
f
.
Suppose
\mathcal{F}
is a
complete
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa^{\prime}})
space.
Then the warped product
\mathcal{B}\mathrel{{\times\penalty}_{f}}\mathcal{F}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
in the following two cases:

(a)

If
Z=\varnothing
and
\upkappa^{\prime}\leqslant\penalty\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f^{2}(b)
for any
b\in\penalty\mathcal{B}
.

(b)

If
Z\neq\varnothing
and
[(d_{z}f){\uparrow}_{[{z}{b}]}]^{2}\geqslant\penalty\upkappa^{\prime}
for any minimizing geodesic
[zb]
from
Z
to a point
b\in\penalty\mathcal{B}
and

\upkappa^{\prime}\leqslant\penalty\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f^{2}(b)
for any
b\in\penalty\mathcal{B}
such that
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{Z}{b}{}\geqslant\penalty\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}
.

Chapter 12
Polyhedral spaces

A
Definitions

12.1. Definition.

A length space
\mathcal{P}
is called a

piecewise
\mathbb{M}({\upkappa})

if it admits a finite triangulation
\uptau

such that an arbitrary simplex
\upsigma
in
\uptau
is isometric to a simplex in the model space
\mathbb{M}^{\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits\upsigma}({\upkappa})
.

By
triangulation
of a piecewise

\mathbb{M}({\upkappa})
space
we will understand a triangulation as in the definition.
If we do not wish to specify
\upkappa
, we will say that
\mathcal{P}
is a
polyhedral space
.

By rescaling we can assume that
\upkappa=1
,
0
, or
-1
.

(a)

Piecewise
\mathbb{M}({1})
spaces will also be
called

spherical polyhedral spaces
;

(b)

Piecewise
\mathbb{M}({0})
spaces will also be
called

Euclidean polyhedral spaces
;

(c)

Piecewise
\mathbb{M}({-1})
spaces will also be
called

hyperbolic polyhedral spaces
.

Note that according to the above definition,
all polyhedral spaces are compact.
However,
most of the statements below admit straightforward generalizations
to
locally polyhedral space
;
that is, complete length spaces,
any point of which admits a closed neighborhood isometric to a polyhedral space.
The latter class of spaces includes in particular infinite covers of polyhedral spaces.

The dimension of a polyhedral space
\mathcal{P}

is defined as the maximal dimension of a simplex
in one (and therefore any) triangulation of
\mathcal{P}
.

Links.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a polyhedral space
and
\upsigma
be a simplex in its triangulation
\uptau
.

The simplexes that contain
\upsigma

form an abstract simplicial complex called the
link
of
\upsigma
,
denoted by
\mathop{\rm Link}\nolimits_{\upsigma}
.
If
m=\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits\upsigma
,
then the set of vertexes of
\mathop{\rm Link}\nolimits_{\upsigma}

is formed by the
(m+1)
-simplexes that contain
\upsigma
;
the set of its edges are formed by the
(m+2)
-simplexes
that contain
\upsigma
, and so on.

The link
\mathop{\rm Link}\nolimits_{\upsigma}

can be identified with the subcomplex of
\uptau

formed by all the simplexes
\upsigma^{\prime}

such that
\upsigma\cap\penalty\upsigma^{\prime}=\varnothing

but both
\upsigma
and
\upsigma^{\prime}
are faces of a simplex of
\uptau
.

The points in
\mathop{\rm Link}\nolimits_{\upsigma}
can be identified with the normal directions to
\upsigma
at a point in the interior of
\upsigma
.
The angle metric between directions makes
\mathop{\rm Link}\nolimits_{\upsigma}
into a spherical polyhedral space.
We will always consider the link with this metric.

Tangent space and space of directions.

Let
\uptau
be a triangulation of a polyhedral space
\mathcal{P}
.
If a point
p\in\penalty\mathcal{P}

lies in the interior of a
k
-simplex
\upsigma
of
\uptau

then the tangent space
\text{\rm T}_{p}\mathcal{P}

is naturally isometric to

\mathbb{E}^{k}\times\penalty(\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits\mathop{\rm Link}\nolimits_{\upsigma}).

Equivalently, the space of directions
\Sigma_{p}

can be isometrically identified with the

k
-th spherical suspension over
\mathop{\rm Link}\nolimits_{\upsigma}
;
that is,

\Sigma_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it iso}}}\penalty\mathop{\rm Susp}\nolimits^{k}(\mathop{\rm Link}\nolimits_{\upsigma}).

If
\mathcal{P}
is an
m
-dimensional polyhedral space,
then for any
p\in\penalty\mathcal{P}

the space of directions
\Sigma_{p}
is a spherical polyhedral space
of dimension at most
m-1
.

In particular,
for any point
p
in the interior of a simplex
\upsigma
,
the isometry class of
\mathop{\rm Link}\nolimits_{\upsigma}
and
k=\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits\upsigma

determine the isometry class of
\Sigma_{p}
and the other way around.

A small neighborhood of
p
is isometric to a neighborhood of the tip of the
\upkappa
-cone over
\Sigma_{p}
.
In fact, if this property holds at any point of a compact length space
\mathcal{P}

then
\mathcal{P}
is a piecewise

\mathbb{M}({\upkappa})
space
[
100
]
.

B
Curvature bounds

Recall that
\ell
-simply connected spaces are defined in
9M
.

The following theorem provides a combinatorial description of polyhedral spaces with curvature bounded above.

12.2. Theorem.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a piecewise

\mathbb{M}({\upkappa})
space and
\uptau
be a triangulation of
\mathcal{P}
. Then

(a)

\mathcal{P}
is locally
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})

if and only if any connected component of the link of any simplex
\upsigma
in
\uptau

is
(2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi)
-simply connected.
Equivalently, if and only if any closed local geodesic in
\mathop{\rm Link}\nolimits_{\upsigma}
has length at least
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi
.

(b)

\mathcal{P}
is a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space
if and only if
\mathcal{P}
is
(2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}})
-simply connected and any connected component of the link of any simplex
\upsigma
in
\uptau

is
(2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi)
-simply connected.

Proof.

We will prove only the if part;
the only-if part is evident by the generalized Hadamard–Cartan theorem (
9M
) and Theorem
11D
.

Let us apply induction on
\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits\mathcal{P}
.
The
base
case
\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits\mathcal{P}=0
is evident.

Induction Step.

Assume that the theorem is proved in the case
\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits\mathcal{P}<\penalty m
. Suppose
\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits\mathcal{P}=m
.

Fix a point
p\in\penalty\mathcal{P}
.
A neighborhood of
p

is isometric to a neighborhood of the tip in the
\upkappa
-cone over

\Sigma_{p}
.
By Theorem
11D
,
it is sufficient to show that

➊

\Sigma_{p}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({1})
.

Note that
\Sigma_{p}
is a spherical polyhedral space
and its links are isometric to links of
\mathcal{P}
.
By the induction hypothesis,
\Sigma_{p}
is locally
\text{\rm CAT}({1})
.
Applying the generalized Hadamard–Cartan theorem (
9M
),
we get
12B
.

Part
12B
follows from the generalized Hadamard–Cartan theorem.
∎

12.3. Exercise.

Show that any metric tree is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
for any
\upkappa
.

12.4. Exercise.

Show that if in a Euclidean polyhedral space
\mathcal{P}

any two points can be connected by a unique geodesic,
then
\mathcal{P}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
.

The following theorem provides a combinatorial description of polyhedral spaces with curvature bounded below.

12.5. Theorem.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a piecewise

\mathbb{M}({\upkappa})
space and
\uptau
be a triangulation of
\mathcal{P}
.
Then
\mathcal{P}
is
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
if and only if the following conditions hold.

(a)

\uptau
is
pure
;
that is, any simplex in
\uptau
is a face of some simplex of dimension exactly
m
.

(b)

The link of any simplex of dimension
m-1
is formed by a single point or two points.

(c)

Any link of any simplex of dimension
m-2

has diameter at most
\uppi
.

(d)

The link of any simplex of dimension
\leqslant\penalty m-2
is connected.

Remarks.

Condition
12B

can be reformulated in the following way:

12B
\,{}^{\prime}\!

Any path
\upgamma\colon[0,1]\to\penalty\mathcal{P}
can be approximated by paths

\upgamma_{n}

that cross only simplexes of dimension
m
and
m-1
.

Further, modulo the other conditions,
condition
12B

is equivalent to the following:

12B
\,{}^{\prime}\!

The link of any simplex of dimension
m-2
is
isometric to a circle of length
\leqslant\penalty 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi

or a closed real interval of length
\leqslant\penalty\uppi
.

Proof.

We will prove the if part.
The only-if part is similar and is left to the reader.

We apply induction on
m
.
The
base
case
m=1
follows from the assumption
12B
.

Step.

Assume that the theorem is proved for polyhedral spaces of dimesnion less than
m
.
Suppose
\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits\mathcal{P}=m
.

According to the globalization theorem (
8F
),
it is sufficient to show that

\mathcal{P}
is locally
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
.

Fix
p\in\penalty\mathcal{P}
.
Note that a spherical neighborhood of
p

is isometric
to a spherical neighborhood of the tip of the tangent
\upkappa
-cone

\text{\rm T}_{p}^{\upkappa}=\penalty\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits^{\upkappa}(\Sigma_{p}).

Hence it is sufficient to show that

➋

\text{\rm T}_{p}^{\upkappa}
is
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
for any
p\in\penalty\mathcal{P}
.

By Theorem
11D
,
the latter is equivalent to

➌

\mathop{\rm diam}\nolimits\Sigma_{p}\leqslant\penalty\uppi
and
\Sigma_{p}
is
\text{\rm CBB}({1})
.

If
m=2
, then
12B
follows from
12B
.

To prove the case
m\geqslant\penalty 3
,
note that
\Sigma_{p}
is an
(m-1)
-dimensional spherical polyhedral space and all the conditions of the theorem hold for
\Sigma_{p}
.
It remains to apply the induction hypothesis.∎

12.6. Exercise.

Assume
\mathcal{P}

\mathbb{M}({\upkappa})

Show that

(a)

if

and

.

C
Flag complexes

12.7. Definition.

A simplicial complex
\mathcal{S}

is
flag
if whenever
\{v_{0},\dots\penalty\penalty,v_{k}\}

is a set of distinct vertexes of
\mathcal{S}

that are pairwise joined by edges, then the vertexes
v_{0},\dots\penalty,v_{k}

span a
k
-simplex in
\mathcal{S}
.

If the above condition is satisfied for
k=2
,
then we say
\mathcal{S}
satisfies
the
no-triangle condition
.

Note that every flag complex is determined by its 1-skeleton.

12.8. Proposition.

A simplicial complex
\mathcal{S}
is flag if and only if

\mathcal{S}
, as well as all the links of all its simplexes,
satisfy the no-triangle condition.

From the definition of flag complex
we get the following:

12.9. Observation.

Any link of a flag complex is flag.

Proof of Proposition
12C
.

By Observation
12C
, the no-triangle condition holds
for any flag complex and all its links.

Now assume a complex
\mathcal{S}
and all its links satisfy
the no-triangle condition.
It follows that
\mathcal{S}
includes a 2-simplex for each triangle.
Applying the same observation for each edge we get that
\mathcal{S}

includes a 3-simplex for any complete graph with 4 vertexes.
Repeating this observation
for triangles,
4-simplexes,
5-simplexes
and so on we get that
\mathcal{S}
is flag.
∎

Right-angled triangulation.

A triangulation of a spherical polyhedral space
is called
right-angled

if each simplex of the triangulation is isometric
to a spherical simplex all of whose angles are right.
Similarly, we say that a simplicial complex
is equipped with a
right-angled spherical metric

if it is a length metric and each simplex is isometric
to a spherical simplex all of whose angles are right.

Spherical polyhedral
\text{\rm CAT}({1})
spaces glued from right-angled simplexes
admit the following characterization
discovered by Michael Gromov
[
75
, p. 122]
.

12.10. Flag condition.

Assume that a spherical polyhedral space
\mathcal{P}

admits a right-angled triangulation
\uptau
.
Then
\mathcal{P}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({1})

if and only if
\uptau
is flag.

Proof; only-if part.

Assume there are three vertexes
v_{1},v_{2}
and
v_{3}
of
\uptau

that are pairwise joined by edges
but do not span a simplex.
Note that in this case

\measuredangle[v_{1}\,{}^{v_{2}}_{v_{3}}]=\measuredangle[v_{2}\,{}^{v_{3}}_{v_{1}}]=\measuredangle[v_{3}\,{}^{v_{1}}_{v_{1}}]=\uppi.

Equivalently,

➊

The concatenation of the geodesics
[v_{1}v_{2}]
,
[v_{2}v_{3}]
and
[v_{3}v_{1}]

forms a closed local geodesic in
\mathcal{P}
.

Now assume that
\mathcal{P}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({1})
.
Then by
11D
,

\mathop{\rm Link}\nolimits_{\upsigma}\mathcal{P}
is a compact length
\text{\rm CAT}({1})
space for every simplex
\upsigma

in
\uptau
.

Each of these links is a right-angled spherical complex
and
by Theorem
12B
,
none
of these links can contain a geodesic circle of length less than
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi
.

Therefore Proposition
12C
and
12C

imply the only-if part.

If part.

By Observation
12C
and Theorem
12B
,
it is sufficient to show that any closed local geodesic
\upgamma

in a flag complex
\mathcal{S}
with right-angled metric has length at least
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi
.

Fix a flag complex
\mathcal{S}
.
Recall that the
star
of a vertex
v
(briefly
\overline{\mathop{\rm Star}\nolimits}_{v}
)
is formed by all the simplexes containing
v
. Similarly,
\mathop{\rm Star}\nolimits_{v}
, the open star of
v
, is the union of all simplexes containing
v
with faces opposite
v
removed.

Choose a simplex
\upsigma
that contains a point of
\upgamma
.
Let
v
be a vertex of
\upsigma
.
Set
f(t)=\cos|{v}-\penalty{\upgamma(t)}|
.
Note that

f^{\prime\prime}(t)+f(t)=0

if
f(t)>0
.
Since the zeroes of sine are
\uppi
apart,

\upgamma

spends time
\uppi
on every visit to
\mathop{\rm Star}\nolimits_{v}
.

After leaving
\mathop{\rm Star}\nolimits_{v}
,
the local geodesic
\upgamma
must enter another simplex,
say
\upsigma^{\prime}
,
which has a vertex
v^{\prime}
not joined to
v
by an edge.

Since
\uptau
is flag, the open stars
\mathop{\rm Star}\nolimits_{v}
and
\mathop{\rm Star}\nolimits_{v^{\prime}}

do not overlap.
The same argument as above shows that
\upgamma
spends time
\uppi
on every visit to
\mathop{\rm Star}\nolimits_{v^{\prime}}
.
Therefore the total length of
\upgamma
is at least
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi
.
∎

12.11. Exercise.

Show that the barycentric subdivision of any simplicial complex is flag.
Conclude that any finite simplicial complex is homeomorphic to a compact length
\text{\rm CAT}({1})
space.

12.12. Exercise.

Let
p
be a point in a product of metric trees.
Show that a closed geodesic in the space of directions
\Sigma_{p}
has length either
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi
or at least
3{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi
.

12.13. Exercise.

Assume that a spherical polyhedral space
\mathcal{P}

admits a triangulation
\uptau
such that all edgelengths of all simplexes in
\uptau
are at least
\tfrac{\uppi}{2}

Show that
\mathcal{P}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({1})

if
\uptau
is flag.

12.14. Exercise.

Let
\upvarphi_{1},\upvarphi_{2},\dots\penalty,\upvarphi_{k}\colon\mathcal{U}\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
be commuting short retractions of
a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
space;
that is,

\diamond

\upvarphi_{i}\circ\penalty\upvarphi_{i}=\upvarphi_{i}
for each
i
;

\diamond

\upvarphi_{i}\circ\penalty\upvarphi_{j}=\upvarphi_{j}\circ\penalty\upvarphi_{i}
for any
i
and
j
;

\diamond

|{\upvarphi_{i}(x)}-\penalty{\upvarphi_{i}(y)}|_{\mathcal{U}}\leqslant\penalty|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\mathcal{U}}
for each
i
and any
x,y\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
.

Set
A_{i}=\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits\upvarphi_{i}
for all
i
.
Note that each
A_{i}
is a weakly convex set.

Assume
\Gamma
is a finite graph
(without loops and multiple edges)
with edges labeled by
1,2,\dots\penalty,n
.
Denote by
\mathcal{U}^{\Gamma}
the space obtained by taking
a copy of
\mathcal{U}
for each vertex of
\Gamma
and
gluing two such copies along
A_{i}
if the corresponding vertexes are joined by an edge labeled by
i
.

Show that
\mathcal{U}^{\Gamma}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({0})

The space of trees.

The following construction is given by
Louis Billera,
Susan Holmes,
and Karen Vogtmann
[
27
]
.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{n}
be the set of all metric trees with
n
end-vertexes
labeled by
a_{1},\dots\penalty,a_{n}
.
To describe one tree in
\mathcal{T}_{n}
we may fix a topological tree
\uptau
with end vertexes
a_{1},\dots\penalty,a_{n}
and all the other vertexes of degree 3,
and prescribe the lengths of
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}n-3
edges.
If the length of an edge is
0
, we assume that edge degenerates;
such a tree can be also described using a different topological tree
\uptau^{\prime}
.
The subset of
\mathcal{T}_{n}
corresponding to the given topological tree
\uptau
can be identified with a convex closed cone in
\mathbb{R}^{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}n-3}
.
Equip each such subset with the metric induced from
\mathbb{R}^{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}n-3}
and consider the length metric on
\mathcal{T}_{n}
induced by these metrics.

12.15. Exercise.

Show that
\mathcal{T}_{n}
with the described metric is
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
.

Cubical complexes.

The definition of a cubical complex
mostly repeats the definition of a simplicial complex,
with simplexes replaced by cubes.

Formally, a
cubical complex
is defined as a subcomplex
of the unit cube in Euclidean space of large dimension;
that is, a collection of faces of the cube, that with each face contains all its sub-faces.
Each cube face in this collection
will be called a
cube
of the cubical complex.

Note that according to this definition,
any cubical complex is finite,
that is, contains a finite number of cubes.

The union of all the cubes in a cubical complex
\mathcal{Q}
will be called its
underlying space
;
it will be denoted by
\mathcal{Q}
or by
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to6.22226pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to6.22226pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.91115pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.11119pt{}}}$\hss}}}\mathcal{Q}

if we need to emphasize that we are talking about a topological space,
not a complex.
A homeomorphism from
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to6.22226pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to6.22226pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.91115pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.11119pt{}}}$\hss}}}\mathcal{Q}
to a topological space
\mathcal{X}
is called a
cubulation of

\mathcal{X}
.

The underlying space of a cubical complex
\mathcal{Q}
will be always considered with the length metric
induced from
\mathbb{R}^{N}
.
In particular, with this metric,
each cube of
\mathcal{Q}
is isometric to the unit cube of the same dimension.

It is straightforward to construct a triangulation
of
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to6.22226pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to6.22226pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.91115pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.11119pt{}}}$\hss}}}\mathcal{Q}

such that each simplex is isometric to a Euclidean simplex.
In particular,
\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to6.22226pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to6.22226pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.91115pt{}}}$\hss}}}{\hbox to0.0pt{{$\underline{{\hbox to4.11119pt{}}}$\hss}}}\mathcal{Q}
is a Euclidean polyhedral space.

The link of each cube in a cubical complex admits a natural right-angled triangulation;
each simplex corresponds to an adjusted cube.

12.16. Exercise.

Show that a cubical complex
\mathcal{Q}
is locally
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
if and only if the link of each vertex in
\mathcal{Q}
is flag.

D
Remarks

The condition on polyhedral
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
spaces given in Theorem
12B
might look easy to use,
but in fact, it is hard to check even in simple cases.
For example, the description of those coverings of
\mathbb{S}^{3}
that branch at three
great circles and are
\text{\rm CAT}({1})
requires quite a bit of work;
an answer is given by Ruth Charney and Michael Davis
[
48
]
.

Analogs of the flag condition for spherical Coxeter simplexes
could resolve the following problem.

12.17. Braid space problem.

Consider
\mathbb{C}^{n}
with coordinates
z_{1},\dots\penalty,z_{n}
.
Let us remove from
\mathbb{C}^{n}
the complex hyperplanes
z_{i}=z_{j}
for all
i\neq j
,
pass to the universal cover, and consider the completion
\mathcal{B}_{n}

of the obtained space.

Is it true that
\mathcal{B}_{n}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
for any
n
?

The above question has an affirmative answer for
n\leqslant\penalty 3
and is open for all
n\geqslant\penalty 4

[
48
,
121
]
.

Recall that by the Hadamard–Cartan theorem (
9M
),
any complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
space is contractible.
Therefore any complete length locally
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
space
is
aspherical
;
that is, has contractible universal cover.

This observation can be used together with Exercise
12C
to construct examples of exotic aspherical spaces;
for example, compact topological manifolds with universal cover not homeomorphic to a Euclidean space.
A survey on the subject is given by Michael Davis
[
57
]
; a more elementary introduction to the subject is given by the authors
[
11
, Chapter 3]
.

The flag condition also leads to the so-called
hyperbolization
procedure, a flexible tool for constructing aspherical spaces;
a good survey on the subject is given by Ruth Charney and Michael Davis
[
49
]
.

The
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
property of a cube complex admits interesting (and useful) geometric descriptions if one replaces the
\ell^{2}
-metric with a natural
\ell^{1}
- or
\ell^{\infty}
-metric on each cube.
The following statement was proved by Brian Bowditch
[
32
]
.

12.18. Theorem.

The following three conditions are equivalent.

(a)

A cube complex
Q
equipped with
\ell^{2}
-metric is
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
.

(b)

A cube complex
Q
equipped with
\ell^{\infty}
-metric is
injective
; that is, for any metric space
\mathcal{X}
with a subset
A
, any short map
A\to\penalty(Q,\ell^{\infty})
can be extended to a short map
\mathcal{X}\to\penalty(Q,\ell^{\infty})
.

(c)

A cube complex
Q
equipped with
\ell^{1}
-metric is
median
; that is, for any three points
x,y,z
there is a unique point
m
(called the
median
of
x
,
y
, and
z
) that lies on some geodesics
[xy]
,
[xz]
and
[yz]
.

Part III
Structure and tools

Chapter 13
First order differentiation

A
Ultratangent space

The following theorem is often used together with the
observation that the ultralimit of any sequence of length spaces is geodesic (see
4C
).

13.1. Theorem.

(a)

If
\mathcal{L}
is a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space and
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
, then
\text{\rm T}^{\text{\rm}}_{p}
is
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
.

(b)

If
\mathcal{U}
is a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space and
p\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
, then
\text{\rm T}^{\text{\rm}}_{p}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
.

The proofs of both parts are nearly identical.

Proof;
13A
.

Since
\mathcal{L}
is a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space, then its blowup
n{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathcal{L}

(see Section
6H
)
is a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa/{n^{2}}})
space.
By Proposition
8A
, the
-blowup
\text{\rm}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathcal{L}
is
\text{\rm CBB}({0})

and so is
\text{\rm T}_{p}^{\text{\rm}}
as a metric component of
\text{\rm}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathcal{L}
.

13A
.

Since
\mathcal{U}
is a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space, then its blowup
n{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathcal{U}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa/{n^{2}}})
.
By Proposition
9A
,
\text{\rm}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathcal{U}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({0})

and so is
\text{\rm T}_{p}^{\text{\rm}}
as a metric component of
\text{\rm}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathcal{U}
.
∎

Recall that the tangent space
\text{\rm T}_{p}
can be considered as a subset of
\text{\rm T}^{\text{\rm}}_{p}
(see
6H
).
Therefore we have the following:

13.2. Corollary.

(a)

If
\mathcal{L}
is a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space and
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
, then
\text{\rm T}_{p}
is
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
.
Moreover,
\text{\rm T}_{p}
satisfies the (1+n)-point comparison (
10B
).

(b)

If
\mathcal{U}
is a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space and
p\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
, then
\text{\rm T}_{p}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
.
Moreover,
\text{\rm T}_{p}
satisfies the (2n+2)-comparison (
10D
).

13.3. Proposition.

Assume
\mathcal{Z}
is a complete length
CBB
or
CAT
space
and
f\colon\mathcal{Z}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
is a semiconcave locally Lipscitz subfunction.
Then for any
p\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
, the ultradifferential
\bm{d}^{\text{\rm}}_{p}\colon\text{\rm T}^{\text{\rm}}_{p}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
is a concave function.

Proof.

Fix a geodesic
[x^{\text{\rm}}y^{\text{\rm}}]
in
\text{\rm T}^{\text{\rm}}_{p}
.

It is sufficient to show that for any subarc
[\bar{x}^{\text{\rm}}\bar{y}^{\text{\rm}}]
of
[x^{\text{\rm}}y^{\text{\rm}}]

that does not contain the ends
there is a sequence of geodesics
[\bar{x}^{n}\bar{y}^{n}]
in
n{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathcal{Z}
converging to
[\bar{x}^{\text{\rm}}\bar{y}^{\text{\rm}}]
.

Choose any sequences
\bar{x}^{n},\bar{y}^{n}\in\penalty n{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathcal{Z}
such that
\bar{x}^{n}\to\penalty\bar{x}^{\text{\rm}}
,
\bar{y}^{n}\to\penalty\bar{y}^{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.
We can assume that there is a geodesic
[\bar{x}^{n}\bar{y}^{n}]_{n{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathcal{Z}}
for any
n
; see
8B
and
9B
.
Note that
[\bar{x}^{n}\bar{y}^{n}]

converges to
[\bar{x}^{\text{\rm}}\bar{y}^{\text{\rm}}]

as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.
The latter holds trivially in the
CAT
case,
and the
CBB
case follows from
8G
.
∎

B
Length property of tangent space

13.4. Theorem.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space and
p\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
.
Then
\text{\rm T}_{p}\mathcal{U}
is a length space.

This theorem together with
13A
imply the following.

13.5. Corollary.

For any point
p
in a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space, the tangent space
\text{\rm T}_{p}
is a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
space.

Proof of Theorem
13B
.

Since
\text{\rm T}_{p}=\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits\Sigma_{p}
, it is sufficient to show that for any hinge
[p\,{}^{x}_{y}]
such that

\measuredangle[p\,{}^{x}_{y}]<\uppi
and any
\upvarepsilon>0
, there is
z\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
such that

\measuredangle[p\,{}^{x}_{z}]<\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\measuredangle[p\,{}^{x}_{y}]+\upvarepsilon\quad\text{and}\quad\measuredangle[p\,{}^{y}_{z}]<\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\measuredangle[p\,{}^{x}_{y}]+\upvarepsilon.

➊

Fix a small
\updelta>0
.
Let
\bar{x}\in\penalty\mathopen{]}px]
and
\bar{y}\in\penalty\mathopen{]}py]
denote the points such that

|{p}-\penalty{\bar{x}}|=|{p}-\penalty{\bar{y}}|=\updelta
.
Let
z
denote the midpoint between
\bar{x}
and
\bar{y}
.

Since
\updelta
is small, we can assume that

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{\bar{x}}_{\bar{y}}\hskip-0.86108pt)<\measuredangle[p\,{}^{x}_{y}]+\upvarepsilon.

By Alexandrov’s lemma (
6B
), we have

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{\bar{x}}_{z}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{\bar{y}}_{z}\hskip-0.86108pt)<\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{\bar{x}}_{\bar{y}}\hskip-0.86108pt).

By construction,

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{\bar{x}}_{z}\hskip-0.86108pt)=\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{\bar{y}}_{z}\hskip-0.86108pt).

Applying the angle comparison (
9C
), we get
➊
.
∎

The following example was constructed by Stephanie Halbeisen
[
79
]
.
It shows that an analogous statement does not hold for
CBB
spaces.
If the dimension is finite, such examples do not exist; for proper spaces the question is open, see
13J
.

13.6. Example.

There is a complete length
CBB
space
\check{\mathcal{L}}

with a point
p\in\penalty\check{\mathcal{L}}
such that the space of directions
\Sigma_{p}\check{\mathcal{L}}
is not a
\uppi
-length space, and therefore the tangent space
\text{\rm T}_{p}\check{\mathcal{L}}
is not a length space.

Construction.

Let
\mathbb{H}
be a Hilbert space formed by infinite sequences of real numbers
\bm{x}=(x_{0},x_{1},\dots\penalty)
with the
\ell^{2}
-norm

|\bm{x}|^{2}=\sum_{i}(x_{i})^{2}
.
Fix
\upvarepsilon=0.001
and consider two functions
f,\check{f}:\mathbb{H}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
:

f(\bm{x})=|\bm{x}|,

\check{f}(\bm{x})=\max\left\{|\bm{x}|,\max_{n\geqslant\penalty 1}\{(1+\upvarepsilon){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x_{n}-\tfrac{1}{n}\}\right\}.

Both of these functions are convex and Lipschitz, therefore their graphs in
\mathbb{H}\times\penalty\mathbb{R}
equipped with its length metric form infinite-dimensional Alexandrov spaces, say
\mathcal{L}
and
\check{\mathcal{L}}
(this is proved formally in
13B
).

Let
p
be the origin of
\mathbb{H}\times\penalty\mathbb{R}
.
Note that
\check{\mathcal{L}}\cap\penalty\mathcal{L}
is a starshaped subset of
\mathbb{H}
with center at
p
.
Further,
\check{\mathcal{L}}\setminus\mathcal{L}
consists of a countable number of disjoint sets

\Omega_{n}=\left\{\,{(\bm{x},\check{f}(\bm{x}))\in\penalty\check{\mathcal{L}}}\ {:}\ {(1+\upvarepsilon){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x_{n}-\tfrac{1}{n}>|\bm{x}|}\,\right\}.

Note that
|{\Omega_{n}}-\penalty{p}|>\tfrac{1}{n}
for each
n
.
It follows that for any geodesic
[pq]
in
\check{\mathcal{L}}
,
a small subinterval
[p\bar{q}]\subset\penalty[pq]

is a straight line segment in
\mathbb{H}\times\penalty\mathbb{R}
,
and also a geodesic in
\mathcal{L}
.
Thus we can treat
\Sigma_{p}\mathcal{L}
and
\Sigma_{p}\check{\mathcal{L}}
as one set, with two angle metrics
\measuredangle
and
\check{\measuredangle}
.
Let us denote by
\measuredangle_{\mathbb{H}\times\penalty\mathbb{R}}
the angle in
\mathbb{H}\times\penalty\mathbb{R}
.

The space
\mathcal{L}
is isometric to the Euclidean cone
over
\Sigma_{p}\mathcal{L}
with vertex at
p
;

\Sigma_{p}\mathcal{L}
is isometric to a sphere in Hilbert space with radius
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
.
In particular,
\measuredangle
is the length metric of
\measuredangle_{\mathbb{H}\times\penalty\mathbb{R}}
on
\Sigma_{p}{\mathcal{L}}
.

Therefore in order to show that
\check{\measuredangle}
does not define a length metric on
\Sigma_{p}{\mathcal{L}}
,
it is sufficient to construct a pair of directions
(\upxi_{+},\upxi_{-})
such that

\check{\measuredangle}(\upxi_{+},\upxi_{-})<\measuredangle(\upxi_{+},\upxi_{-}).

Set
\bm{e}_{0}=(1,0,0,\dots\penalty)
,
\bm{e}_{1}=(0,1,0,\dots\penalty),\dots\penalty\in\penalty\mathbb{H}
.
Consider the following two half-lines in
\mathbb{H}\times\penalty\mathbb{R}
:

\upgamma_{+}(t)=\tfrac{t}{\sqrt{2}}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(\bm{e}_{0},1)\quad\text{and}\quad\upgamma_{-}(t)=\tfrac{t}{\sqrt{2}}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(-\bm{e}_{0},1),\quad t\in\penalty[0,+\infty).

They form unit-speed geodesics in both
\mathcal{L}
and
\check{\mathcal{L}}
.
Let
\upxi_{\pm}
be the directions of
\upgamma_{\pm}
at
p
.
Denote by
\upsigma_{n}
the half-planes in
\mathbb{H}

spanned by
\bm{e}_{0}
and
\bm{e}_{n}
;
that is,
\upsigma_{n}=\penalty\left\{\,{x{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\bm{e}_{0}+y{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\bm{e}_{n}}\ {:}\ {y\geqslant\penalty 0}\,\right\}
.
Consider a sequence of
2
-dimensional sectors
Q_{n}=\check{\mathcal{L}}\cap\penalty(\upsigma_{n}\times\penalty\mathbb{R})
.
For each
n
, the sector
Q_{n}
intersects
\Omega_{n}
and is bounded by two geodesic half-lines
\upgamma_{\pm}
.
Note that
Q_{n}\mathbin{\begin{picture}\put(-1.0,1.0){$\longrightarrow$}\put(11.0,-1.5){\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\text{\tiny\rm GH}}}\end{picture}}\penalty Q
, where
Q
is a solid Euclidean angle
in
\mathbb{E}^{2}
with angle measure
\upbeta<\measuredangle(\upxi_{+},\upxi_{-})=\tfrac{\uppi}{\sqrt{2}}
.
Indeed,
Q_{n}
is path-isometric to the subset of
\mathbb{E}^{3}
described by

\displaystyle y\geqslant\penalty 0\quad\text{and}\quad

\displaystyle z=\max\left\{\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}},(1+\upvarepsilon){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y-\tfrac{1}{n}\right\}

with length metric.
Thus its limit
Q
is path-isometric to the subset of
\mathbb{E}^{3}
described by

\displaystyle y\geqslant\penalty 0\quad\text{and}\quad

\displaystyle z=\max\left\{\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}},(1+\upvarepsilon){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y\right\}

with length metric.
In particular, for any
t,\uptau\geqslant\penalty 0
,

\displaystyle|{\upgamma_{+}(t)}-\penalty{\upgamma_{-}(\uptau)}|_{\check{\mathcal{L}}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty\lim_{n\to\penalty\infty}|{\upgamma_{+}(t)}-\penalty{\upgamma_{-}(\uptau)}|_{Q_{n}}=

\displaystyle=\mathop{\tilde{\curlyvee}}\nolimits^{0}\{\upbeta;t,\uptau\}.

That is,
\check{\measuredangle}(\upxi_{+},\upxi_{-})\leqslant\penalty\upbeta<\measuredangle(\upxi_{+},\upxi_{-})
.∎

13.7. Lemma.

Let
\mathbb{H}
be a Hilbert space,

f\colon\mathbb{H}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a convex Lipschitz function
and
S\subset\penalty\mathbb{H}\times\penalty\mathbb{R}
be the graph of
f

equipped with the length metric.
Then
S
is
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
.

Proof.

Recall that for a subset
X\subset\penalty\mathbb{H}\times\penalty\mathbb{R}
,
we will denote by
|{*}-\penalty{*}|_{X}
the
length metric on
X
.

By the theorem of Sergei Buyalo
[
45
]
, sharpened by the authors in
[
10
]
,
any convex hypersurface in a Euclidean space, equipped with the length metric, is non-negatively curved.
Thus it is sufficient to show that for any 4-point set
\{x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\}\subset\penalty S
,
there is a finite-dimensional subspace
E\subset\penalty\mathbb{H}\times\penalty\mathbb{R}

such that
\{x_{i}\}\in\penalty E
and
|{x_{i}}-\penalty{x_{j}}|_{S\cap\penalty E}
is arbitrary close to
|{x_{i}}-\penalty{x_{j}}|_{S}
.

Clearly
|{x_{i}}-\penalty{x_{j}}|_{S\cap\penalty E}\geqslant\penalty|{x_{i}}-\penalty{x_{j}}|_{S}
;
thus it is sufficient to show that for given
\upvarepsilon>0
one can choose
E
so that

|{x_{i}}-\penalty{x_{j}}|_{S\cap\penalty E}<|{x_{i}}-\penalty{x_{j}}|_{S}+\upvarepsilon.

[formula]

For each pair
(x_{i},x_{j})
, choose a polygonal line
\upbeta_{ij}
connecting
x_{i},x_{j}
that lies under
S
(that is, outside of
\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits S
) in
\mathbb{H}\times\penalty\mathbb{R}

and has length at most
|{x_{i}}-\penalty{x_{j}}|_{S}+\upvarepsilon
.
Let
E
be the affine hull of all the vertexes in all
\upbeta_{ij}
.
Thus

|{x_{i}}-\penalty{x_{j}}|_{S\cap\penalty E}\leqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upbeta_{ij}

and

➋
.

follows.∎

13.8. Exercise.

Construct a non-compact complete geodesic
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
space that contains no half-lines.

C
Rademacher theorem

At the end of this section we give an extension of the Rademacher theorem (see
Section
3D
) to
CBB
and
CAT
spaces (
13C
); it was proved by Alexander Lytchak
[
108
]
.
The following proposition is the 1-dimensional case of the extended Rademacher theorem.

Recall that differentiable curves are defined in
6F
.

13.9. Proposition.

Let
\upalpha\colon\mathbb{I}\to\penalty\mathcal{Z}
be a locally Lipschitz curve in a complete length space.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.
Then
\upalpha
is differentiable almost everywhere.

The following two lemmas provide sufficient conditions for existence of the one-sided derivative of a curve in
CBB
and
CAT
spaces.
The proofs of both lemmas are similar.

13.10. Lemma.

Let
\upalpha\colon\mathbb{I}\to\penalty\mathcal{L}
be a
1
-Lipschitz curve in a
CBB
space.
Suppose that for some
t_{0}\in\penalty\mathbb{I}
and any
\upvarepsilon>0
, there is a point
p
such that
|{\upalpha(t_{0})}-\penalty{p}|<\upvarepsilon
and

\varliminf_{t\to\penalty t_{0}+}\frac{\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}\circ\penalty\upalpha(t)-\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}\circ\penalty\upalpha(t_{0})}{t-t_{0}}>1-\upvarepsilon.

Then the right derivative
\upalpha^{+}(t_{0})
is defined and
|\upalpha^{+}(t_{0})|=1
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
t_{0}=0
.
Set
x=\penalty\upalpha(0)
.
Fix a sequence of points
p_{n}\to\penalty x
such that

\varliminf_{t\to\penalty 0+}\frac{|{p_{n}}-\penalty{\upalpha(t)}|-|{p_{n}}-\penalty{x}|}{t}\to\penalty 1

as
n\to\penalty\infty
.

Observe that there are sequences
\updelta_{n}\to\penalty 0+
and
t_{n}\to\penalty 0+
such that

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{\upalpha(s)}_{p_{n}}\hskip-0.86108pt)>\uppi-\updelta_{n}\quad\text{and}\quad(1-\updelta_{n}){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s<|{\upalpha(s)}-\penalty{x}|\leqslant\penalty s

➊

for any
s\in\penalty(0,t_{n}]
.

For each
n
, choose
q_{n}\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Str}\nolimits(x)
sufficiently close
\upalpha(t_{n})
that the inequality

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{q_{n}}_{p_{n}}\hskip-0.86108pt)>\uppi-\updelta_{n}

still holds (see Definition
8B
).

Set
\upgamma_{n}=\mathrm{geod}_{[xq_{n}]}
.
By comparison,

\displaystyle\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{\upalpha(s)}_{\upgamma_{n}(s)}\hskip-0.86108pt)

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi-\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{p_{n}}_{\upgamma_{n}(s)}\hskip-0.86108pt)-\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{\upalpha(s)}_{p_{n}}\hskip-0.86108pt)\leqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi-\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{q_{n}}_{p_{n}}\hskip-0.86108pt)-\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{\upalpha(s)}_{p_{n}}\hskip-0.86108pt)<

\displaystyle<2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\updelta_{n}.

Therefore
➊
implies that

|{\upgamma_{n}(s)}-\penalty{\upalpha(s)}|<10{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\updelta_{n}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(s)

if
s
is a sufficiently small and positive.
That is,
\upalpha^{+}(0)
is defined (see Definition
6F
).
∎

13.11. Lemma.

Let
\upalpha\colon\mathbb{I}\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
be a
1
-Lipschitz curve in a
CAT
space.
Suppose that for some
t_{0}\in\penalty\mathbb{I}
and any
\upvarepsilon>0
there is a point
q
such that
|{\upalpha(t_{0})}-\penalty{q}|<\upvarepsilon
and

\varlimsup_{t\to\penalty t_{0}+}\frac{\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{q}\circ\penalty\upalpha(t)-\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{q}\circ\penalty\upalpha(t_{0})}{t-t_{0}}<-1+\upvarepsilon.

Then the right derivative
\upalpha^{+}(t_{0})
is defined and
|\upalpha^{+}(t_{0})|=1
.

Proof.

Without loss of generatiy we may assume that
t_{0}=0
.
Set
x=\penalty\upalpha(0)
.
Fix a sequence of points
q_{n}\to\penalty x
such that

\varliminf_{t\to\penalty 0+}\frac{|{q_{n}}-\penalty{\upalpha(t)}|-|{q_{n}}-\penalty{x}|}{t}\to\penalty-1

as
n\to\penalty\infty
.

Observe that there are sequences
\updelta_{n}\to\penalty 0+
and
t_{n}\to\penalty 0+
such that

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{\upalpha(s)}_{q_{n}}\hskip-0.86108pt)<\updelta_{n}\quad\text{and}\quad(1-\updelta_{n}){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s<|{\upalpha(s)}-\penalty{x}|\leqslant\penalty s

➋

for any
s\in\penalty(0,t_{n}]
.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
|{x}-\penalty{q_{n}}|<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
for any
n
;
in particular, the geodesic
\upgamma_{n}=\mathrm{geod}_{[xq_{n}]}
is uniquely defined.

By comparison,

\displaystyle\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{\upalpha(s)}_{\upgamma_{n}(s)}\hskip-0.86108pt)

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{\upalpha(s)}_{q_{n}}\hskip-0.86108pt)<

\displaystyle<\updelta_{n}.

Therefore
➋
implies that

|{\upgamma_{n}(s)}-\penalty{\upalpha(s)}|<10{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\updelta_{n}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s

if
s
is a sufficiently small and positive.
That is,
\upalpha^{+}(0)
is defined (see Definition
6F
).
∎

Proof of
13C
.

By the standard Rademacher theorem, we may assume that
\upalpha
has an arc-length parametrization.
In particular,
\upalpha
is 1-Lipschitz.

Recall that by Theorem
3D
,

\mathop{\rm speed}\nolimits_{s}\upalpha\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it a.e.}}}1.

➌

Fix a countable dense set
T\subset\penalty\mathbb{I}
;
given
t\in\penalty T
, let

h_{t}(s)=|{\upalpha(t)}-\penalty{\upalpha(s)}|.

Note that
h_{t}
is
1
-Lipschitz for each
t\in\penalty T
.
Therefore, by the standard Rademacher theorem and countability of
T
for almost all
s\in\penalty\mathbb{I}
,
h_{t}^{\prime}(s)
is defined for all
t\in\penalty T
.

Let

w^{+}(s):=\varlimsup_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in\penalty T\\
t\to\penalty s-\end{subarray}}\{h^{\prime}_{t}(s)\}.

Let us show that

w^{+}(s)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it a.e.}}}1.

➍

Note that once this is proved, Lemma
13C
implies the proposition in the
CBB
case.

For a small
\upvarepsilon>0
, denote by
N_{\upvarepsilon}^{+}
the set of all points
s\in\penalty\mathbb{I}
such that
w^{+}(s)<1-\upvarepsilon
.
Note that the sets
N_{\upvarepsilon}^{+}
are measurable.

Suppose
N_{\upvarepsilon}^{+}
has positive measure.
Let
s_{0}\in\penalty N_{\upvarepsilon}^{+}
be a Lebesgue point of
\upalpha
.
We may assume that
\mathop{\rm speed}\nolimits_{s_{0}}\upalpha=1
and
h_{t}^{\prime}(s_{0})
is defined for any
t\in\penalty T
.
Suppose
t\in\penalty T
is sufficiently close to
s_{0}
and
t<s_{0}
.
Since
\mathop{\rm speed}\nolimits_{s_{0}}\upalpha=1
, we have

h_{t}(s_{0})\geqslant\penalty(s_{0}-t){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(1-\upvarepsilon^{2}).

➎

Further, there is a set
A\subset\penalty[t,s_{0}]
with measure at least
(1-\upvarepsilon){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|s_{0}-t|
such that

h_{t}^{\prime}(s)<1-\upvarepsilon

for any
s\in\penalty A_{n}
.
Since
h_{t}
is
1
-Lipschitz, we have

\displaystyle h_{t}(s_{0})

\displaystyle=\int_{[t,s_{0}]\setminus A}h_{t}^{\prime}(s){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\bm{d}s+\int_{A}h_{t}^{\prime}(s){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\bm{d}s\leqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty(s_{0}-t){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}[\upvarepsilon+(1-\upvarepsilon)^{2}].

The latter contradicts
➎
.
Thus
w^{+}(s)\geqslant\penalty 1-\upvarepsilon
almost everywhere.
Since
\upvarepsilon>0
is arbitrary,
➍
follows.

In the same way we can show that

w^{-}(s)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it a.e.}}}-1,

➏

where

w^{-}(s):=\varliminf_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in\penalty T\\
t\to\penalty s+\end{subarray}}\{h^{\prime}_{t}(s)\}.

Then Lemma
13C
implies the proposition in the
CAT
case.
∎

13.12. Extended Rademacher theorem.

Let
f\colon\mathbb{E}^{m}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathcal{Z}
be a locally Lipschitz submap from a Euclidean space to a complete length space
\mathcal{Z}
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.
Then the differential
\bm{d}_{x}f
is defined at almost all points
x\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
.

Moreover the differential
\bm{d}_{x}f
is
linear
at almost all
x
in the following sense:
the image
\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits f
is a convex subcone of
\text{\rm T}_{f(x)}\mathcal{Z}
, and
there is an isometry
\upiota
from
\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits f
to a Euclidean space such that the composition
\upiota\circ\penalty\bm{d}_{x}f
is linear.

The proof is a reduction to the 1-dimensional case (
13C
) by standard arguments
[
93
,
112
]
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
is bounded and
f
is Lipschitz.

Fix a countable dense set of vectors
\{v_{i}\}
in
\mathbb{E}^{m}
.
Fix
v_{i}
and a point
p\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
.
By Proposition
13C
, the value
\bm{d}_{x}f(v_{i})
is defined at
x=p+t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}v_{i}
for almost all
t
such that
x\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
.
It follows that
\bm{d}_{x}f(v_{i})

is defined for every
i
on a set
A
of full measure in
\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
.
Since the metric differential of
f
is defined almost everywhere (
3D
), we have that
\bm{d}_{x}f(v)
is defined for any
v
on a set
B
of full measure in
\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
.

Applying the definitions of metric differential and differential (
3D
and
6G
), we obtain that the image of
\bm{d}_{x}f
is a weakly convex set in
\text{\rm T}_{f(x)}
.
It follows that
\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits\bm{d}_{x}f
is
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
or
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
if the space
\mathcal{Z}
is
CBB
or
CAT
respectively.
It remains to apply Exercise
8C
or
9C
if the space
\mathcal{Z}
is
CBB
or
CAT
respectively.
∎

Differential

Let
\mathcal{U}

\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space and
p,q\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
.
Assume
|{p}-\penalty{q}|<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
.
Show that

(\bm{d}_{q}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}{}{})(v)=-\langle{\uparrow}_{[{q}{p}]},v\rangle.

13.14. Exercise.

Let
\mathcal{L}
