\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space and
p,q\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
be distinct points.
Assume
q\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Str}\nolimits(p)
or
p\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Str}\nolimits(q)

Show that

(\bm{d}_{q}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}{}{})(v)=-\langle{\uparrow}_{[{q}{p}]},v\rangle.

13.15. Lemma.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a complete length
CAT
space,

f\colon\mathcal{U}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a locally Lipschitz semiconcave subfunction,
and
p\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
.
Then
\bm{d}_{p}f
is a Lipschitz concave function on
\text{\rm T}_{p}\mathcal{U}
.

Proof.

Recall that the tangent space
\text{\rm T}_{p}=\text{\rm T}_{p}\mathcal{U}
can be considered as a subspace of the ultratangent space
\text{\rm T}_{p}^{\text{\rm}}
(
6H
).
Since
\text{\rm T}_{p}^{\text{\rm}}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
,
13B
implies that
\text{\rm T}_{p}
is a convex set in
\text{\rm T}_{p}^{\text{\rm}}
.

By
13A
,
\bm{d}_{p}^{\text{\rm}}f
is a concave function on
\text{\rm T}_{p}^{\text{\rm}}
.
It remains to apply that
\bm{d}_{p}f=(\bm{d}_{p}^{\text{\rm}}f)|_{\text{\rm T}_{p}}
(
6G
).
∎

As it is shown in Halbeisen’s example (Section
13B
),
a
CBB
space might have tangent spaces that are not length spaces;
thus concavity of the differential
\bm{d}_{p}f
of a semiconcave function
f
is meaningless.
Nevertheless, as the following lemma says, the differential
\bm{d}_{p}f
of a semiconcave function always satisfies a weaker property similar to concavity (compare to
[
136
, Prop. 136]
and
[
120
, 4.2]
).
In the finite dimensional case,
\bm{d}_{p}f
is concave.

13.16. Lemma.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a complete length space,

f\colon\mathcal{Z}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a locally Lipschitz semiconcave subfunction,
and
p\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.
Then for any
u,v\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}
, we have

s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sqrt{|u|^{2}+2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle u,v\rangle+|v|^{2}}\geqslant\penalty(\bm{d}_{p}f)(u)+(\bm{d}_{p}f)(v),

where

s=\sup\left\{\,{(\bm{d}_{p}f)(\upxi)}\ {:}\ {\upxi\in\penalty\Sigma_{p}}\,\right\}.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{Z}
is
CAT
, then the statement follows from
13D
.
Indeed, let
z
be the midpoint of a geodesic
[uv]_{\text{\rm T}_{p}}
.
Observe that

2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|z|=\penalty\sqrt{|u|^{2}+2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle u,v\rangle+|v|^{2}}.

Since
\bm{d}_{p}f
is concave, we have that

2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\bm{d}_{p}f(z)\geqslant\penalty\bm{d}_{p}f(u)+\bm{d}_{p}f(v).

It remains to choose
\upxi\in\penalty\Sigma_{p}
so that
\upxi{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|z|=z
and observe that
s\geqslant\penalty\bm{d}_{p}(\upxi)
.

Now assume
\mathcal{Z}
is
CBB
.
We can assume that
\upalpha=\measuredangle(u,v)>0
, otherwise the statement is trivial.
Moreover, we can assume that
\exp_{p}(t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}u)

and
\exp_{p}(t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}v)
are defined for all small
t>0
;
the latter follows since geodesic space of directions
\Sigma^{\prime}_{p}
is dense in
\Sigma_{p}
.

Prepare a model configuration of five points:
\tilde{p}
,
\tilde{u}
,
\tilde{v}
,
\tilde{q}
,
\tilde{w}\in\penalty\mathbb{E}^{2}
such that

\diamond

\measuredangle[\tilde{p}\,{}^{\tilde{u}}_{\tilde{v}}]=\upalpha
,

\diamond

|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{u}}|=|u|
,

\diamond

|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{v}}|=|v|
,

\diamond

\tilde{q}
lies on an extension of
[\tilde{p}\tilde{v}]
so that
\tilde{v}
is the midpoint of
[\tilde{p}\tilde{q}]
,

\diamond

\tilde{w}
is the midpoint between
\tilde{u}
and
{\tilde{v}}
.

Note that

|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{w}}|=\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sqrt{|u|^{2}+2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle u,v\rangle+|v|^{2}}.

Assume that
\mathcal{Z}
is geodesic.

For all small
t>0
, construct points
u_{t},v_{t},q_{t},w_{t}\in\penalty\mathcal{Z}
as follows:

(a)

v_{t}=\exp_{p}(t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}v)
,
q_{t}=\exp_{p}(2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}v)

(b)

u_{t}=\exp_{p}(t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}u)
.

(c)

w_{t}
is the midpoint of
[u_{t}v_{t}]
.

Clearly
|{p}-\penalty{u_{t}}|=t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|u|
,
|{p}-\penalty{v_{t}}|=t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|v|
,
|{p}-\penalty{q_{t}}|=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|v|
.
Since
\measuredangle(u,v)
is defined,
we have
|{u_{t}}-\penalty{v_{t}}|=t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{\tilde{u}}-\penalty{\tilde{v}}|+o(t)

and
|{u_{t}}-\penalty{q_{t}}|=t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{\tilde{u}}-\penalty{\tilde{q}}|+o(t)

(see Theorem
8C
and Section
8A
).

From the point-on-side and hinge comparisons (
8C
+
8C
), we have

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(v_{t}\,{}^{p}_{w_{t}}\hskip-0.86108pt)\geqslant\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(v_{t}\,{}^{p}_{u_{t}}\hskip-0.86108pt)\geqslant\penalty\measuredangle[\tilde{v}\,{}^{\tilde{p}}_{\tilde{u}}]+\tfrac{o(t)}{t}

and

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(v_{t}\,{}^{q_{t}}_{w_{t}}\hskip-0.86108pt)\geqslant\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(v_{t}\,{}^{q_{t}}_{u_{t}}\hskip-0.86108pt)\geqslant\penalty\measuredangle[\tilde{v}\,{}^{\tilde{q}}_{\tilde{u}}]+\tfrac{o(t)}{t}.

Clearly,

\measuredangle[\tilde{v}\,{}^{\tilde{p}}_{\tilde{u}}]+\measuredangle[\tilde{v}\,{}^{\tilde{q}}_{\tilde{u}}]=\uppi
.
From the adjacent angle comparison (
8C
),

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(v_{t}\,{}^{p}_{u_{t}}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(v_{t}\,{}^{u_{t}}_{q_{t}}\hskip-0.86108pt)\leqslant\penalty\uppi
.
Hence

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(v_{t}\,{}^{p}_{w_{t}}\hskip-0.86108pt)\to\penalty\measuredangle[\tilde{v}\,{}^{\tilde{p}}_{\tilde{w}}]
as
t\to\penalty 0+

and thus

|{p}-\penalty{w_{t}}|=t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{w}}|+o(t).

Since
f
is
\uplambda
-concave, we have

\displaystyle 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f(w_{t})

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty f(u_{t})+f(v_{t})+\tfrac{\uplambda}{4}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{u_{t}}-\penalty{v_{t}}|^{2}=

\displaystyle=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f(p)+t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}[(\bm{d}_{p}f)(u)+(\bm{d}_{p}f)(v)]+o(t).

Applying
\uplambda
-concavity of
f
, we have

(\bm{d}_{p}f)({\uparrow}_{[{p}{w_{t}}]})\geqslant\penalty\frac{t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}[(\bm{d}_{p}f)(u)+(\bm{d}_{p}f)(v)]+o(t)}{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{w}}|+o(t)}.

➊

The lemma follows.

Finally, if
\mathcal{Z}
is not geodesic one needs to make two adjustments in the above construction.
Namely:

(i)

For the geodesic
[u_{t}v_{t}]
to be defined, in (
b
) one has to take
u_{t}\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Str}\nolimits(v_{t})
,
u_{t}\approx\penalty\exp_{p}(t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}u)
;
more precisely,

|{u_{t}}-\penalty{\exp_{p}(t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}u)}|=o(t).

Thus instead of
|{p}-\penalty{u_{t}}|=t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|u|
we have

|{p}-\penalty{u_{t}}|=t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|u|+o(t),

and this is sufficient for the rest of proof.

(ii)

The direction
{\uparrow}_{[{p}{w_{t}}]}
might be undefined.
Thus in the estimate
➊
, instead of
{\uparrow}_{[{p}{w_{t}}]}
one should take
{\uparrow}_{[{p}{w^{\prime}_{t}}]}
for some point
w_{t}^{\prime}\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Str}\nolimits(p)
near
w_{t}
(that is,
|{w_{t}}-\penalty{w_{t}^{\prime}}|=o(t)
).

∎

E
Definition of gradient

13.17. Definition of gradient.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a length space with defined angles and

f\colon\mathcal{X}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a subfunction.
Suppose for a point

p\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
the differential
\bm{d}_{p}f\colon\text{\rm T}_{p}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
is defined.

A tangent vector
g\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}
is called a

gradient of
f
at
p

(briefly,
g=\penalty\nabla_{p}f
) if

(a)

(\bm{d}_{p}f)(w)\leqslant\penalty\langle g,w\rangle
for any
w\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}
, and

(b)

(\bm{d}_{p}f)(g)=\langle g,g\rangle.

13.18. Example.

Consider the Euclidean plane with standard
(x,y)
-coordinates.
Then the function
f\colon(x,y)\mapsto\penalty-|x|-|y|
is concave;
its gradient field is sketched on the figure.

If a point does not lie on an axis, then its gradient has length
\sqrt{2}
and
takes one of four values
(\pm 1,\pm 1)
depending on the quadrant of the point.
At the origin the gradient vanish,
and on the on the remaining parts of the
x
-axis and
y
-axis it is
(\pm 1,0)
and
(0,\pm 1)
respectively.

13.19. Exercise.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
space.
Show that

\nabla_{p}(-\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{q})={\uparrow}_{[{p}{q}]}

for any pair of distinct points
p,q\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
.

13.20. Existence and uniqueness of the gradient.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a complete length space
and
f\colon\mathcal{Z}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a
locally Lipschitz
and
semiconcave subfunction.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.
Then for any point
p\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
, there is a unique gradient
\nabla_{p}f\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}
.

Proof; uniqueness.

If
g,g^{\prime}\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}
are two gradients of
f
,
then

\displaystyle\langle g,g\rangle

\displaystyle=(\bm{d}_{p}f)(g)\leqslant\penalty\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle,

\displaystyle\langle g^{\prime},g^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=(\bm{d}_{p}f)(g^{\prime})\leqslant\penalty\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle.

Therefore,

|{g}-\penalty{g^{\prime}}|^{2}=\langle g,g\rangle-2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle g,g^{\prime}\rangle+\langle g^{\prime},g^{\prime}\rangle\leqslant\penalty 0.

It follows that
g=g^{\prime}
.

Existence.

Note first that if
\bm{d}_{p}f\leqslant\penalty 0
, then one can take
\nabla_{p}f=0
.

Otherwise, if
s=\sup\left\{\,{(\bm{d}_{p}f)(\upxi)}\ {:}\ {\upxi\in\penalty\Sigma_{p}}\,\right\}>0
,
it is sufficient to show that there is
\overline{\upxi}\in\penalty\Sigma_{p}
such that

(\bm{d}_{p}f)\left(\overline{\upxi}\right)=s.

➊

Indeed, suppose
\overline{\upxi}
exists.
Then applying Lemma
13D
for
u=\overline{\upxi}
,
v=\upvarepsilon{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}w
with
\upvarepsilon\to\penalty 0+
,
we get

(\bm{d}_{p}f)(w)\leqslant\penalty\langle w,s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\overline{\upxi}\rangle

for any
w\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}
;
that is,
s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\overline{\upxi}
is the gradient at
p
.

Take a sequence of directions
\upxi_{n}\in\penalty\Sigma_{p}
, such that
(\bm{d}_{p}f)(\upxi_{n})\to\penalty s
.
Applying Lemma
13D
for
u=\upxi_{n}
,
v=\upxi_{m}
, we get

s\geqslant\penalty\frac{(\bm{d}_{p}f)(\upxi_{n})+(\bm{d}_{p}f)(\upxi_{m})}{\sqrt{2+2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\measuredangle(\upxi_{n},\upxi_{m})}}.

Therefore
\measuredangle(\upxi_{n},\upxi_{m})\to\penalty 0
as
n,m\to\penalty\infty
;
that is, the sequence
\upxi_{n}
is Cauchy.
Clearly
\overline{\upxi}=\lim_{n}\upxi_{n}
satisfies
➊
.
∎

13.21. Exercise.

Let
p
be a point in a complete length
CBB
space.
Show that

|\nabla_{x}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}|=1

for
x
in a dense G-delta subset.

F
Calculus of gradient

The next lemma states that the gradient points
in the direction of maximal slope;
moreover, if the slope in the given direction is almost maximal, then it is almost the direction of the gradient.

13.22. Lemma.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a complete length space,

f\colon\mathcal{Z}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
be locally Lipschitz and semiconcave,
and
p\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.

Assume
|\nabla_{p}f|>0
;
let
\overline{\upxi}=\tfrac{1}{|\nabla_{p}f|}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nabla_{p}f
.
Then:

(a)

If for some
v\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}
, we have

|v|\leqslant\penalty 1+\upvarepsilon\quad\text{and}\quad(\bm{d}_{p}f)(v)>|\nabla_{p}f|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(1-\upvarepsilon),

then

|{\overline{\upxi}}-\penalty{v}|<100{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sqrt{\upvarepsilon}.

(b)

If
v_{n}\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}
is a sequence of vectors such that

\varlimsup_{n\to\penalty\infty}|v_{n}|\leqslant\penalty 1\quad\text{and}\quad\varliminf_{n\to\penalty\infty}(\bm{d}_{p}f)(v_{n})\geqslant\penalty|\nabla_{p}f|,

then

\lim_{n\to\penalty\infty}v_{n}=\overline{\upxi}.

(c)

\overline{\upxi}
is the unique maximum direction for the restriction
\bm{d}_{p}f|_{\Sigma_{p}}
.
In particular,

|\nabla_{p}f|=\sup\left\{\,{\bm{d}_{p}f}\ {:}\ {\upxi\in\penalty\Sigma_{p}f}\,\right\}.

Proof.

According to the definition of gradient,

\displaystyle|\nabla_{p}f|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(1-\upvarepsilon)

\displaystyle<(\bm{d}_{p}f)(v)\leqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty\langle v,\nabla_{p}f\rangle=

\displaystyle=|v|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|\nabla_{p}f|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\measuredangle(\nabla_{p}f,v).

Thus

|v|>1-\upvarepsilon

and

\cos\measuredangle(\nabla_{p}f,v)>\tfrac{1-\upvarepsilon}{1+\upvarepsilon}.

Hence
13F
.

Statements
13F
and
13F
follow directly from
13F
).
∎

As a corollary of the above lemma and Proposition
3F
we obtain the following:

13.23. Chain rule.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a complete length space,

f\colon\mathcal{Z}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a semiconcave function,
and
\upvarphi\colon\mathbb{R}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a nondecreasing semiconcave function.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.
Then
\upvarphi\circ\penalty f
is semiconcave and
\nabla_{x}(\upvarphi\circ\penalty f)=\upvarphi^{+}(f(x)){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nabla_{x}f
for any
x\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
.

The following inequalities describe an important property of the “gradient
vector field”.

13.24. Lemma.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a complete length space,

f\colon\mathcal{Z}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
satisfy
f^{\prime\prime}+\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f\leqslant\penalty\uplambda
for some
\upkappa,\uplambda\in\penalty\mathbb{R}
.
Let
[pq]\subset\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
.
and
\ell=|{p}-\penalty{q}|
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.
Then

\langle{\uparrow}_{[{p}{q}]},\nabla_{p}f\rangle\geqslant\penalty\frac{{f(q)}-{f(p){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}\ell}-\uplambda{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{md}^{\upkappa}\ell}{\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}\ell}.

In particular,

(a)

if
\upkappa=0
,

\langle{\uparrow}_{[{p}{q}]},\nabla_{p}f\rangle\geqslant\penalty\left({f(q)}-{f(p)}-\tfrac{\uplambda}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell^{2}\right)/\ell;

(b)

if
\upkappa=1
,
\uplambda=0
we have

\langle{\uparrow}_{[{p}{q}]},\nabla_{p}f\rangle\geqslant\penalty\left(f(q)-f(p){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\ell\right)/\sin\ell;

(c)

if
\upkappa=-1
,
\uplambda=0
we have

\langle{\uparrow}_{[{p}{q}]},\nabla_{p}f\rangle\geqslant\penalty\left(f(q)-f(p){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cosh\ell\right)/\sinh\ell.

Proof of
13F
.

Note that

\mathrm{geod}_{[pq]}(0)=p,\quad\mathrm{geod}_{[pq]}(\ell)=q,\quad(\mathrm{geod}_{[pq]})^{+}(0)=\penalty{\uparrow}_{[{p}{q}]}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\langle{\uparrow}_{[{p}{q}]},\nabla_{p}f\rangle

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty d_{p}f({\uparrow}_{[{p}{q}]})=

\displaystyle=(f\circ\penalty\mathrm{geod}_{[pq]})^{+}(0)\geqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty\frac{{f(q)}-{f(p){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}\ell}-\uplambda{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{md}^{\upkappa}\ell}{\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}\ell}.

∎

The following corollary states that the gradient vector field is monotonic in a sense similar to the definition of
monotone operators

[
132
]
.

13.25.
\bm{\uplambda}
-Monotonicity of gradient.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a complete length space,

f\colon\mathcal{Z}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
be locally Lipschitz and
\uplambda
-concave
and
[pq]\subset\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.
Then

\langle{\uparrow}_{[{p}{q}]},\nabla_{p}f\rangle+\langle{\uparrow}_{[{q}{p}]},\nabla_{q}f\rangle\geqslant\penalty-\uplambda{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{p}-\penalty{q}|.

Proof.

Add two inequalities from
13F
.
∎

13.26. Lemma.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a complete length space,

f,g\colon\mathcal{Z}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
,
and
p\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f\cap\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits g
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.

Then

|{\nabla_{p}f}-\penalty{\nabla_{p}g}|_{\text{\rm T}_{p}}^{2}\leqslant\penalty s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(|\nabla_{p}f|+|\nabla_{p}g|),

where

s=\sup\left\{\,{|(\bm{d}_{p}f)(\upxi)-(\bm{d}_{p}g)(\upxi)|}\ {:}\ {\upxi\in\penalty\Sigma_{p}}\,\right\}.

In particular, if
f_{n}\colon\mathcal{Z}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
is a sequence of locally Lipschitz and semiconcave subfunctions,

p\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f_{n}
for each
n
,
and
\bm{d}_{p}f_{n}
converges uniformly on
{\Sigma_{p}}
,
then the sequence
\nabla_{p}f_{n}\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}
converges.

Proof.

Clearly for any
v\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}
, we have

|(\bm{d}_{p}f)(v)-(\bm{d}_{p}g)(v)|\leqslant\penalty s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|v|.

From the definition of gradient (
13E
) we have:

\displaystyle(\bm{d}_{p}f)(\nabla_{p}g)\leqslant\penalty\langle\nabla_{p}f,\nabla_{p}g\rangle,

\displaystyle(\bm{d}_{p}g)(\nabla_{p}f)\leqslant\penalty\langle\nabla_{p}f,\nabla_{p}g\rangle,

\displaystyle(\bm{d}_{p}f)(\nabla_{p}f)=\langle\nabla_{p}f,\nabla_{p}f\rangle,

\displaystyle(\bm{d}_{p}g)(\nabla_{p}g)=\langle\nabla_{p}g,\nabla_{p}g\rangle.

Therefore,

\displaystyle|{\nabla_{p}f}-\penalty{\nabla_{p}g}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\langle\nabla_{p}f,\nabla_{p}f\rangle+\langle\nabla_{p}g,\nabla_{p}g\rangle-2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\nabla_{p}f,\nabla_{p}g\rangle\leqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty(\bm{d}_{p}f)(\nabla_{p}f)+(\bm{d}_{p}g)(\nabla_{p}g)-

\displaystyle\quad-(\bm{d}_{p}f)(\nabla_{p}g)-(\bm{d}_{p}g)(\nabla_{p}f)\leqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(|\nabla_{p}f|+|\nabla_{p}g|).

∎

13.27. Exercise.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space,
the function

f\colon\mathcal{L}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
be semiconcave and locally Lipschitz,
and

\upalpha\colon\mathbb{I}\to\penalty\mathcal{L}
be a Lipschitz curve.
Show that

\langle\nabla_{\upalpha(t)}f,\upalpha^{+}(t)\rangle=(\bm{d}_{\upalpha(t)}f)(\upalpha^{+}(t))

for almost all
t\in\penalty\mathbb{I}
.

G
Semicontinuity of |gradient|

In this section we collect a few consequences of the following lemma.

13.28. Ultralimit of |gradient|.

Assume that

\diamond

(\mathcal{Z}_{n})
is a sequence of complete length spaces and
(\mathcal{Z}_{n},p_{n})\to\penalty(\mathcal{Z}_{\text{\rm}},p_{\text{\rm}})
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.
Suppose that all
\mathcal{Z}_{n}
are either
CBB
or
CAT
.

\diamond

f_{n}\colon\mathcal{Z}_{n}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
and
f_{\text{\rm}}\colon\mathcal{Z}_{\text{\rm}}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
are locally Lipschitz and
\uplambda
-concave, and
f_{n}\to\penalty f_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.

\diamond

x_{n}\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f_{n}
and
x_{n}\to\penalty x_{\text{\rm}}\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.

Then

|\nabla_{x_{\text{\rm}}}f_{\text{\rm}}|\leqslant\penalty\lim_{n\to\penalty\text{\rm}}|\nabla_{x_{n}}f_{n}|.

Remarks.
The inequality might be strict.
For example, consider
\mathcal{Z}_{n}=\penalty\mathbb{R}
,
f_{n}(x)=-|x|
and
x_{n}\to\penalty 0+
.

From the convergence of gradient curves (proved later in
16E
),
one can deduce the following slightly stronger statement.

13.29. Proposition.

Assume that

\diamond

\mathcal{Z}_{n}
is a sequence of complete length spaces and
(\mathcal{Z}_{n},p_{n})\to\penalty(\mathcal{Z}_{\text{\rm}},p_{\text{\rm}})
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.
Suppose that all
\mathcal{Z}_{n}
are either
CBB
or
CAT
.

\diamond

f_{n}\colon\mathcal{Z}_{n}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
and
f_{\text{\rm}}\colon\mathcal{Z}_{\text{\rm}}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
are locally Lipschitz and
\uplambda
-concave and
f_{n}\to\penalty f_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.

Then

|\nabla_{x_{\text{\rm}}}f_{\text{\rm}}|=\inf\{\lim_{n\to\penalty\text{\rm}}|\nabla_{x_{n}}f_{n}|\},

where infimum is taken for all sequences
x_{n}\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f_{n}
such that
x_{n}\to\penalty x_{\text{\rm}}\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.

Proof of
13G
.

Fix an
\upvarepsilon>0
and choose
y_{\text{\rm}}\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f_{\text{\rm}}
sufficiently close to
x_{\text{\rm}}
that

|\nabla_{x_{\text{\rm}}}f_{\text{\rm}}|-\upvarepsilon<\frac{f_{\text{\rm}}(y_{\text{\rm}})-f_{\text{\rm}}(x_{\text{\rm}})}{|{x_{\text{\rm}}}-\penalty{y_{\text{\rm}}}|}.

Choose
y_{n}\in\penalty\mathcal{Z}_{n}
such that
y_{n}\to\penalty y_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.
Since
|{x_{\text{\rm}}}-\penalty{y_{\text{\rm}}}|
is sufficiently small, the
\uplambda
-concavity of
f_{n}
implies that

|\nabla_{x_{\text{\rm}}}f_{\text{\rm}}|-2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon<(\bm{d}_{x_{n}}f_{n})({\uparrow}_{[{x_{n}}{y_{n}}]})

for
-almost all
n
.
Hence

|\nabla_{x_{\text{\rm}}}f_{\text{\rm}}|-2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon\leqslant\penalty\lim_{n\to\penalty\text{\rm}}|\nabla_{x_{n}}f_{n}|.

Since
\upvarepsilon>0
is arbitrary, the proposition follows.
∎

Note that the distance-preserving map
\upiota\colon\mathcal{Z}\hookrightarrow\penalty\mathcal{Z}^{\text{\rm}}
induces an embedding

\bm{d}_{p}\upiota\colon\text{\rm T}_{p}\mathcal{Z}\hookrightarrow\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{\rm}}.

Thus, we can (and will) consider
\text{\rm T}_{p}\mathcal{Z}
as a subcone of
\text{\rm T}_{p}\mathcal{Z}^{\text{\rm}}
.

13.30. Corollary.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a complete length space
and
f\colon\mathcal{Z}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a locally Lipschitz semiconcave subfunction.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.
Then

\nabla_{x}f=\nabla_{x}f^{\text{\rm}}.

for any point
x\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
.

Proof.

Note that

\begin{matrix}\mathcal{Z}&\subset\penalty&\mathcal{Z}^{\text{\rm}}\\
\cup\penalty&&\cup\penalty\\
\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f&\subset\penalty&\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f^{\text{\rm}}\end{matrix}.

Applying
13G
for
\mathcal{Z}_{n}=\mathcal{Z}
and
x_{n}=x
, we get that
|\nabla_{x}f|\geqslant\penalty|\nabla_{x}f^{\text{\rm}}|
.

On the other hand,
f=f^{\text{\rm}}|_{\mathcal{Z}}
, hence
\bm{d}_{p}f=\bm{d}_{p}f^{\text{\rm}}|_{\text{\rm T}_{p}\mathcal{Z}}
.
Thus from
13F
,

|\nabla_{x}f|\leqslant\penalty|\nabla_{x}f^{\text{\rm}}|
.
Therefore

|\nabla_{x}f|=|\nabla_{x}f^{\text{\rm}}|

➊

for any
x\in\penalty\mathcal{Z}
.

Further,

\displaystyle|\nabla_{x}f|^{2}

\displaystyle=(\bm{d}_{x}f)(\nabla_{x}f)=

\displaystyle=\bm{d}_{x}f^{\text{\rm}}(\nabla_{x}f)\leqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty\langle\nabla_{x}f^{\text{\rm}},\nabla_{x}f\rangle=

\displaystyle=|\nabla_{x}f^{\text{\rm}}|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|\nabla_{x}f|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\measuredangle(\nabla_{x}f^{\text{\rm}},\nabla_{x}f).

Together with
➊
, this implies
\measuredangle(\nabla_{x}f^{\text{\rm}},\nabla_{x}f)=0
and the statement follows.
∎

13.31. Semicontinuity of |gradient|.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a complete length space
and
f\colon\mathcal{Z}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a locally Lipschitz semiconcave subfunction.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.
Then the function
x\mapsto\penalty|\nabla_{x}f|
is lower-continuous;
that is, for any sequence
x_{n}\to\penalty x\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
, we have

|\nabla_{x}f|\leqslant\penalty\varliminf_{n\to\penalty\infty}|\nabla_{x_{n}}f|.

Proof.

According to
13G
,
|\nabla_{x}f|=|\nabla_{x}f^{\text{\rm}}|
.
Applying
13G
for
x_{n}\to\penalty x
, we obtain

\lim_{n\to\penalty\text{\rm}}|\nabla_{x_{n}}f|\geqslant\penalty|\nabla_{x}f^{\text{\rm}}|=|\nabla_{x}f|.

The same holds for an arbitrary subsequence of
x_{n}
— hence the result. ∎

H
Polar vectors

Here we give a corollary of Lemma
13F
.
It will be used to prove basic properties of the
tangent space.

13.32. Anti-sum lemma.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
CBB
space and
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
.

Given two vectors
u,v\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}
, there is a unique vector
w\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}
such that

\langle u,x\rangle+\langle v,x\rangle+\langle w,x\rangle\geqslant\penalty 0

for any
x\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}
, and

\langle u,w\rangle+\langle v,w\rangle+\langle w,w\rangle=0.

If
\text{\rm T}_{p}
were a length space, then the lemma would follow from the existence of the gradient (
13E
), applied to the function
\text{\rm T}_{p}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
defined by
x\mapsto\penalty-(\langle u,x\rangle+\langle v,x\rangle)
.
However, the tangent space
\text{\rm T}_{p}
might be not a length space; see Halbeisen’s example
13B
.

Applying the above lemma for
u=v
, we have the following statement.

13.33. Existence of polar vector.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
CBB
space
and
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
.
Given a vector
u\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}
, there is a unique vector
u^{*}\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}
such that
\langle u^{*},u^{*}\rangle+\langle u,u^{*}\rangle=0
and

u^{*}
is
polar
to
u
;
that is,

\langle u^{*},x\rangle+\langle u,x\rangle\geqslant\penalty 0
for any
x\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}
.

In particular, for any vector
u\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}
there is a polar vector
u^{*}\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}
such that

|u^{*}|\leqslant\penalty|u|
.

Milka’s lemma provides a refinement of this statement;
it states that in the finite-dimensional
CBB
space, any tangent vector
u
has a polar vector
u^{*}
such that
|u^{*}|=|u|
.

13.34. Example.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be the upper half space of the Euclidean space
\mathbb{E}^{n}
;
that is,
\mathcal{L}=\{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in\penalty\mathbb{E}^{n}\mid x_{n}\geqslant\penalty 0\}
.
It is a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
space.
For
p=0
, the tangent space
\text{\rm T}_{p}
can be canonically identified with
\mathcal{L}
.
Then any vector
u=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}

a unique polar vector such that
|u^{*}|=|u|
which is
u^{*}=(-v_{1},\ldots,-v_{n-1},v_{n})
.
However, if
v_{n}\neq 0
, then
u
has other polar vectors, in particular
(-v_{1},\ldots,-v_{n-1},0)
.

It is instructive to solve the following exercise before reading the proof of
13H
.

13.35. Exercise.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space and
a,b,p

be mutually distinct points in
\mathcal{L}
.
Prove that

(\bm{d}_{p}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{a}{}{})(\nabla_{p}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{b}{}{})\leqslant\penalty\cos\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{a}_{b}\hskip-0.86108pt).

Proof of
13H
.

Choose two sequences of points
a_{n},b_{n}\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Str}\nolimits(p)
such that
{\uparrow}_{[{p}{a_{n}}]}\to\penalty u/|u|
and
{\uparrow}_{[{p}{b_{n}}]}\to\penalty v/|v|
as
n\to\penalty\infty
.
Consider a sequence of functions

f_{n}=|u|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{a_{n}}{}{}+|v|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{b_{n}}{}{}.

According to Exercise
13D
,

(\bm{d}_{p}f_{n})(x)=-|u|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle{\uparrow}_{[{p}{a_{n}}]},x\rangle-|v|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle{\uparrow}_{[{p}{b_{n}}]},x\rangle.

Thus we have the following uniform convergence for all
x\in\penalty\Sigma_{p}
:

(\bm{d}_{p}f_{n})(x)\xrightarrow[n\to\penalty\infty]{}-\langle u,x\rangle-\langle v,x\rangle.

According to Lemma
13F
,
the sequence
\nabla_{p}f_{n}
converges.
Let

w=\lim_{n\to\penalty\infty}\nabla_{p}f_{n}.

By the definition of gradient,

\displaystyle\langle w,w\rangle

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\penalty\infty}\langle\nabla_{p}f_{n},\nabla_{p}f_{n}\rangle=

\displaystyle\langle w,x\rangle

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\penalty\infty}\langle\nabla_{p}f_{n},x\rangle\geqslant\penalty

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\penalty\infty}(\bm{d}_{p}f_{n})(\nabla_{p}f_{n})=

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty\lim_{n\to\penalty\infty}(\bm{d}_{p}f_{n})(x)=

\displaystyle=-\langle u,w\rangle-\langle v,w\rangle,

\displaystyle=-\langle u,x\rangle-\langle v,x\rangle.

∎

I
Linear subspace of tangent space

13.36. Definition.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space,
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
and
u,v\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}
.
We say that vectors
u
and
v
are
opposite
to each other, (briefly,
u+v=0
) if
|u|=|v|=0
or
\measuredangle(u,v)=\uppi
and
|u|=|v|
.

The subcone

\mathop{\rm Lin}\nolimits_{p}=\left\{\,{v\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}}\ {:}\ {\exists\ w\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}\quad\text{such that}\quad w+v=0}\,\right\}

will be called the
linear subcone
of
\text{\rm T}_{p}
.

The reason for the term “linear” will become evident in Theorem
13I
.

13.37. Proposition.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
CBB
space and
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
.
Given two vectors
u,v\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}
, the following statements are equivalent:

(a)

u+v=0
;

(b)

\langle u,x\rangle+\langle v,x\rangle=0
for any
x\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}
;

(c)

\langle u,\upxi\rangle+\langle v,\upxi\rangle=0
for any
\upxi\in\penalty\Sigma_{p}
.

Proof.

The condition
u+v=0
is equivalent to

\langle u,u\rangle=-\langle u,v\rangle=\langle v,v\rangle;

thus

13I
\Rightarrow
13I
.
Since
\text{\rm T}_{p}
is isometric to a subset of
\text{\rm T}^{\text{\rm}}_{p}
,
the splitting theorem (
16G
) applied to
\text{\rm T}_{p}^{\text{\rm}}

gives
13I
\Rightarrow
13I
.

The equivalence
13I
\Leftrightarrow
13I
is trivial.
∎

13.38. Proposition.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
CBB
space and
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
.
Then for any three vectors
u,v,w\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}
, if
u+v=0
and
u+w=0
then
v=w
.

Proof.

By Proposition
13I
, both
v
and
w
satisfy the condition in corollary
13H
.
Hence the result.∎

Let
u\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Lin}\nolimits_{p}
; that is
u+v=0
for some
v\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}
.
Given
s<0
, let

s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}u:=(-s){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}v.

This way we define multiplication of any vector in
\mathop{\rm Lin}\nolimits_{p}
by any real number (positive and negative).
Proposition
13I
implies that such multiplication is uniquely defined.

13.39. Theorem.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space and
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
.
Then
\mathop{\rm Lin}\nolimits_{p}
is a subcone of
\text{\rm T}_{p}
isometric to a Hilbert space.

Before proving the theorem,
let us give a corollary.

13.40. Corollary.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space
and
p\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Str}\nolimits(x_{1},\dots\penalty,x_{n})
.
Then there is a subcone
E\subset\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}
that is isometric to a Euclidean space such that
\mathrm{log}[{p}{x_{i}}]\in\penalty E
for every
i
.

Proof.

By the definition of
\mathop{\rm Str}\nolimits(x_{1},\dots\penalty,x_{n})
(
8B
),
\mathrm{log}[{p}{x_{i}}]\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Lin}\nolimits_{p}
for each
i
.
It remains to apply Theorem
13I
.
∎

The main difficulty in the proof of Theorem
13I
comes from the fact that in general
\text{\rm T}_{p}
is not a length space;
see Halbeisen’s example (
13B
).
If the tangent space were a length space, the statement would follow directly from the splitting theorem (
16G
).
In fact the proof of Theorem
13I

is very circuitous — we use the construction of the gradient, as well as the splitting theorem, namely its corollary (
16G
).
Thus in order to understand our proof one needs to read most of Chapter
16
.

Proof of
13I
.

First we show that
\mathop{\rm Lin}\nolimits_{p}
is a complete geodesic
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
space.

Recall that
\text{\rm T}^{\text{\rm}}_{p}
is a complete geodesic
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
space (see
4C
and
13A
) and
\mathop{\rm Lin}\nolimits_{p}
is a closed subset of
\text{\rm T}^{\text{\rm}}_{p}
.
Thus, it is sufficient to show that the metric on
\mathop{\rm Lin}\nolimits_{p}
inherited from
\text{\rm T}^{\text{\rm}}_{p}
is a length metric.

Fix two vectors
x,y\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Lin}\nolimits_{p}
.
Let
u
and
v
be such that
u+\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x=0

and
v+\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y=0
.
Apply Lemma
13H

to the vectors
u
and
v
;
let
w\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}
denote the obtained tangent vector.

➊

w
is a midpoint of
[xy]
.

Indeed, according to Lemma
13H
,

\displaystyle|w|^{2}

\displaystyle=-\langle w,u\rangle-\langle w,v\rangle=

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle w,x\rangle+\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle w,y\rangle.

Therefore

\displaystyle|{x}-\penalty{w}|^{2}+|{w}-\penalty{y}|^{2}

\displaystyle=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|w|^{2}+|x|^{2}+|y|^{2}-2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle w,x\rangle-2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle w,y\rangle=

\displaystyle=|x|^{2}+|y|^{2}-\langle w,x\rangle-\langle w,y\rangle\leqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty|x|^{2}+|y|^{2}+\langle u,x\rangle+\langle v,x\rangle+\langle u,y\rangle+\langle v,y\rangle=

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|x|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|y|^{2}-\langle x,y\rangle=

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{x}-\penalty{y}|^{2}.

Thus
|{x}-\penalty{w}|=|{w}-\penalty{y}|=\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{x}-\penalty{y}|
and
13I
follows.

\vartriangle

Note that for any
v\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Lin}\nolimits_{p}
there is a line
\ell
that contains
v
and
0
.
Therefore by
16G
,
\mathop{\rm Lin}\nolimits_{p}
is isometric to a Hilbert space.
∎

J
Comments

13.41. Open question.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a proper length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space.
Is it true that for any
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
, the tangent space
\text{\rm T}_{p}
is a length space?

Chapter 14
Dimension of CAT spaces

In this chapter we discuss constructions introduced by Bruce Kleiner
[
94
]
.

The material of this chapter is used mostly for
CAT
spaces,
but the results in section
14A
find applications for finite-dimensional
CBB
spaces as well.

A
The case of complete geodesic spaces

The following construction gives a
k
-dimensional submanifold
for a given “nondegenerate” array of
k+1
strongly convex functions.

14.1. Definition.

For two real arrays
\bm{v}
,
\bm{w}\in\penalty\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
,

\bm{v}=(v^{0},v^{1},\dots\penalty,v^{k})

and

\bm{w}=(w^{0},w^{1},\dots\penalty,w^{k})
,
we will write

\bm{v}\succcurlyeq\bm{w}
if
v^{i}\geqslant\penalty w^{i}
for each
i
.

Given a subset
Q\subset\penalty\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
,
denote by
\mathop{\rm Up}\nolimits Q

the smallest upper set containing
Q
,
and by

\mathop{\rm Min}\nolimits Q
the set of minimal elements of
Q
with respect to
\succcurlyeq
;
that is,

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm Up}\nolimits Q

\displaystyle=\left\{\,{\bm{v}\in\penalty\mathbb{R}^{k+1}}\ {:}\ {\exists\,\bm{w}\in\penalty Q\ \text{such that}\ \bm{v}\succcurlyeq\bm{w}}\,\right\},

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm Min}\nolimits Q

\displaystyle=\left\{\,{\bm{v}\in\penalty Q}\ {:}\ {\text{if}\ \bm{v}\succcurlyeq\bm{w}\in\penalty Q\ \text{then}\ \bm{w}=\bm{v}}\,\right\}.

14.2. Definition.

Let
\bm{f}=(f^{0},f^{1},\dots\penalty,f^{k})\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
be a function array on a metric space
\mathcal{X}
.
The set

\mathop{\rm Web}\nolimits\bm{f}:=\bm{f}^{-1}\left[\mathop{\rm Min}\nolimits\bm{f}(\mathcal{X})\right]\subset\penalty\mathcal{X}

will be called the
web
of
\bm{f}
.

Given an array
\bm{f}=(f^{0},f^{1},\dots\penalty,f^{k})
,
we denote by
\bm{f}^{{-}i}
the subarray of
\bm{f}
with
f^{i}
removed;
that is,

\bm{f}^{{-}i\,}:=(f^{0},\dots\penalty,f^{i-1},f^{i+1},\dots\penalty,f^{k}).

Clearly

\mathop{\rm Web}\nolimits\bm{f}^{{-}i}\subset\penalty\mathop{\rm Web}\nolimits\bm{f}
.
Define the
inner web
of
\bm{f}

as

\mathop{\rm InWeb}\nolimits\bm{f}=\mathop{\rm Web}\nolimits\bm{f}\setminus\left(\bigcup_{i}\mathop{\rm Web}\nolimits\bm{f}^{{-}i}\right).

We say that a function array is
nondegenerate

if
\mathop{\rm InWeb}\nolimits\bm{f}\neq\varnothing
.

Example.

If
\mathcal{X}
is a geodesic space,
then
\mathop{\rm Web}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{x}{}{},\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{y}{}{})
is the union of all geodesics from
x
to
y
, and

\mathop{\rm InWeb}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{x}{}{},\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{y}{}{})=\mathop{\rm Web}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{x}{}{},\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{y}{}{})\setminus\{x,y\}.

Barycenters.

Let us denote by
\Delta^{k}\subset\penalty\mathbb{R}^{k+1}

the
standard
k
-simplex
;
that is,
\bm{x}=(x^{0},x^{1},\dots\penalty,x^{k})\in\penalty\Delta^{k}
if
\sum_{i=0}^{k}x^{i}=1
and
x^{i}\geqslant\penalty 0
for all
i
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a metric space
and
\bm{f}=(f^{0},f^{1},\dots\penalty,f^{k})\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
be a function array.
Consider the map
\mathfrak{S}_{\bm{f}}\colon\Delta^{k}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
defined by

\mathfrak{S}_{\bm{f}}(\bm{x})=\mathop{\rm MinPoint}\sum_{i=0}^{k}x^{i}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f^{i},

where
\mathop{\rm MinPoint}f
denotes a point of minimum of
f
.
The map
\mathfrak{S}_{\bm{f}}
will be called a
barycentric simplex
of
\bm{f}
.
Note that for a general function array
\bm{f}
,
the value
\mathfrak{S}_{\bm{f}}(\bm{x})
might be undefined or nonuniquely defined.

It is clear from the definition that
\mathfrak{S}_{\bm{f}^{{-}i}}

coincides with the restriction of
\mathfrak{S}_{\bm{f}}
to the corresponding facet of
\Delta^{k}
.

14.3. Theorem.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a complete geodesic space
and
\bm{f}=\penalty(f^{0},f^{1},\dots\penalty,f^{k})\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}^{k+1}

be an array of strongly convex and locally Lipschitz functions.
Then
\bm{f}
defines a
C^{\frac{1}{2}}
-embedding

\mathop{\rm Web}\nolimits\bm{f}\hookrightarrow\penalty\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
.

Moreover,

(a)

W=\mathop{\rm Up}\nolimits[\bm{f}(\mathcal{X})]
is a convex closed subset of
\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
,
and

S=\partial_{\mathbb{R}^{k+1}}W
is a convex hypersurface in
\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
.

(b)

\bm{f}(\mathop{\rm Web}\nolimits\bm{f})=\mathop{\rm Min}\nolimits W\subset\penalty S

and

\bm{f}(\mathop{\rm InWeb}\nolimits\bm{f})=\mathop{\rm Interior}\nolimits_{S}(\mathop{\rm Min}\nolimits W).

(c)

The barycentric simplex

\mathfrak{S}_{\bm{f}}\colon\Delta^{k}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
is a uniquely defined Lipshitz map and
\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits\mathfrak{S}_{\bm{f}}=\mathop{\rm Web}\nolimits\bm{f}
.
In particular,
\mathop{\rm Web}\nolimits\bm{f}
is compact.

(d)

Let us equip
\Delta^{k}
with the metric induced by the
\ell^{1}
-norm on
\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
.
Then the Lipschitz constant of
\mathfrak{S}_{\bm{f}}\colon\Delta^{k}\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
can be estimated in terms of
positive lower bounds on
(f^{i})^{\prime\prime}

and Lipschitz constants of
f^{i}

in a neighborhood of
\mathop{\rm Web}\nolimits\bm{f}
for all
i
.

In particular, by
14A
and
14A
,
\mathop{\rm InWeb}\nolimits\bm{f}
is
C^{\frac{1}{2}}
-homeomorphic to an open set of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

The proof is preceded by a few preliminary statements.

14.4. Lemma.

Suppose
\mathcal{X}
is a complete geodesic space and
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
is a locally Lipschitz, strongly convex function. Then the minimum point
of
f

is uniquely defined.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that
f
is
1
-convex.
In particular, the following claim holds:

➊

if
z
is a midpoint of the geodesic
[xy]
, then

s\leqslant\penalty f(z)\leqslant\penalty\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f(x)+\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f(y)-\tfrac{1}{8}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{x}-\penalty{y}|^{2},

where
s
is the infimum of
f
.

Uniqueness.

Assume that
x
and
y
are distinct minimum points of
f
.
From
14A
we have

f(z)<f(x)=f(y),

a contradiction.

Existence.

Fix a point
p\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
, and
let
{\ell}\in\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a Lipschitz constant of
f
in a neighborhood of
p
.

Choose a geodesic
[px]
;
consider the function
\upvarphi\colon t\mapsto\penalty f\circ\penalty\mathrm{geod}_{[px]}(t)
.
Clearly
\upvarphi
is
1
-convex and
\upvarphi^{+}(0)\geqslant\penalty-{\ell}
.
Setting
a=|{p}-\penalty{x}|
, we have

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle=\upvarphi(a)\geqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty f(p)-{\ell}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}a+\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}a^{2}\geqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty f(p)-\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}{{\ell}^{2}}.

In particular,

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle:=\inf\left\{\,{f(x)}\ {:}\ {x\in\penalty\mathcal{X}}\,\right\}\geqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty f(p)-\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}{{\ell}^{2}}.

Choose a sequence of points
p_{n}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
such that
f(p_{n})\to\penalty s
.
Applying
14A
for
x=\penalty p_{n}
,
y=\penalty p_{m}
, we see that
p_{n}
is a Cauchy sequence.
Thus the sequence
p_{n}
converges to a minimum point of
f
.
∎

14.5. Definition.

Let
Q
be a closed subset of
\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
.
A vector
\bm{x}=\penalty(x^{0},x^{1},\dots\penalty,x^{k})\in\penalty\mathbb{R}^{k+1}

is
subnormal
to
Q
at a point
\bm{v}\in\penalty Q

if

\langle\bm{x},\bm{w}-\bm{v}\rangle:=\sum_{i}x^{i}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(w^{i}-v^{i})\geqslant\penalty 0

for any
\bm{w}\in\penalty Q
.

14.6. Lemma.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a complete geodesic space
and
\bm{f}=\penalty(f^{0},f^{1},\dots\penalty,f^{k})\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}^{k+1}

be an array of strongly convex and locally Lipschitz functions.
Let
W=\mathop{\rm Up}\nolimits\bm{f}(\mathcal{X})
.
Then:

(a)

W
is a closed convex set, bounded below with respect to
\succcurlyeq
.

(b)

If
\bm{x}
is a subnormal vector to
W
, then
\bm{x}\succcurlyeq\bm{0}
.

(c)

S=\partial_{\mathbb{R}^{k+1}}W
is a complete convex hypersurface in
\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
.

Proof.

Denote by
\bar{W}
the closure of
W
.

Convexity of all
f^{i}
implies that
for any two points
p,q\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
and
t\in\penalty[0,1]
we have

(1-t){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\bm{f}(p)+t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\bm{f}(q)\succcurlyeq\bm{f}\circ\penalty\mathrm{path}_{[pq]}(t),

➋

where
\mathrm{path}_{[pq]}
denotes a geodesic path from
p
to
q
.
Therefore
W
, as well as
\bar{W}
, are convex sets in
\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
.

Let

w^{i}=\min\left\{\,{f^{i}(x)}\ {:}\ {x\in\penalty\mathcal{X}}\,\right\}.

By Lemma
14A
,
w^{i}
is finite for each
i
.
Evidently,
\bm{w}=(w^{0},w^{1},\dots\penalty,w^{k})
is a lower bound of
\bar{W}
with respect to
\succcurlyeq
.

It is clear that
W
has nonempty interior,
and
W\neq\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
since
W
is bounded below.
Therefore
S=\partial_{\mathbb{R}^{k+1}}W=\partial_{\mathbb{R}^{k+1}}\bar{W}

is a complete convex hypersurface in
\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
.

Since
\bar{W}
is closed and bounded below, we also have

\bar{W}=\mathop{\rm Up}\nolimits[\mathop{\rm Min}\nolimits\bar{W}].

➌

Choose an arbitrary
\bm{v}\in\penalty S
.
Let
\bm{x}\in\penalty\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
be a subnormal vector to
\bar{W}
at
\bm{v}
.
In particular,

\langle\bm{x},\bm{y}\rangle\geqslant\penalty 0

for any
\bm{y}\succcurlyeq\bm{0}
;
that is,
\bm{x}\succcurlyeq\bm{0}
.

Further, according to Lemma
14A
,
the function

p\mapsto\penalty\langle\bm{x},\bm{f}(p)\rangle=\sum_{i}x^{i}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f^{i}(p)

has a uniquely defined minimum point, say
p
.
Clearly

\bm{v}\succcurlyeq\bm{f}(p)\quad\text{and}\quad\bm{f}(p)\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Min}\nolimits W.

➍

Note that for any
\bm{u}\in\penalty\bar{W}
there is
\bm{v}\in\penalty S
such that
\bm{u}\succcurlyeq\bm{v}
.
Therefore
➍
implies

\bar{W}\subset\penalty\mathop{\rm Up}\nolimits[\mathop{\rm Min}\nolimits W]\subset\penalty W.

Hence

\bar{W}=W
; that is,
W
is closed.
∎

Proof of
14A
;

+
14A
.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that all

1
-convex.

Given

;
that is,

In particular,

➎

Given

and

.
Since

\displaystyle h_{\bm{v}}(q)

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty h_{\bm{v}}(p)+\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{p}-\penalty{q}|^{2},

\displaystyle h_{\bm{w}}(p)

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty h_{\bm{w}}(q)+\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{p}-\penalty{q}|^{2}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle|{p}-\penalty{q}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sup_{x\in\penalty\mathcal{X}}\{|h_{\bm{v}}(x)-h_{\bm{w}}(x)|\}\leqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\max_{i}\{|v^{i}-w^{i}|\}.

In particular,

\Phi
is

-continuous.
Hence

is a

-embedding.

As in Lemma

,

and

.
Then

is a convex hypersurface in

.
Clearly

.
From the definition of inner web, we have

if and only if

\bm{v}\in\penalty S
and
for any

there is

such that

.
That is,

.

Since

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle=|{p}-\penalty{q}|.

Note the following:

\diamond

B
The case of CAT spaces

Let
\bm{a}=(a^{0},a^{1},\dots\penalty,a^{k})
be a point array in a metric space
\mathcal{U}
.
Recall that

\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{}

denotes the distance map

(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{a^{0}}{}{},\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{a^{1}}{}{},\dots\penalty,\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{a^{k}}{}{})\colon\mathcal{U}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}^{k+1},

which can be also regarded as a function array.
The
radius
of the point array
\bm{a}
is defined to be the radius of the set
\{a^{0},a^{1},\dots\penalty,a^{k}\}
;
that is,

\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\bm{a}=\inf\left\{\,{r>0}\ {:}\ {\exists z\in\penalty\mathcal{U}\ \text{such that}\ a^{i}\in\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(z,r)\ \text{for any}\ i}\,\right\}.

Fix
\upkappa\in\penalty\mathbb{R}
.
Let
\bm{a}=(a^{0},a^{1},\dots\penalty,a^{k})
be a point array of radius
<\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}

in a metric space
\mathcal{U}
.
Consider the function array
\bm{f}=(f^{0},f^{1},\dots\penalty,f^{k})

where

f^{i}(x)=\penalty\mathrm{md}^{\upkappa}|{a^{i}}-\penalty{x}|.

Assuming the barycentric simplex
\mathfrak{S}_{\bm{f}}
is defined,
then
\mathfrak{S}_{\bm{f}}
is called the
\upkappa
-barycentric simplex
for the point array
\bm{a}
;
it will be denoted by
\mathfrak{S}_{\bm{a}}^{\upkappa}
.
The points
a^{0},a^{1},\dots\penalty,a^{k}
are called

vertexes
of the
\upkappa
-barycentric simplex.
Note that once we say the
\upkappa
-barycentric simplex is defined,
we automatically assume that
\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\bm{a}<\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}
.

14.7. Theorem.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space
and
\bm{a}=\penalty(a^{0},a^{1},\dots\penalty a^{k})
be a point array with radius
<\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}
.
Then:

(a)

The
\upkappa
-barycentric simplex
\mathfrak{S}_{\bm{a}}^{\upkappa}\colon\Delta^{k}\to\penalty\mathcal{U}

is defined.
Moreover,
\mathfrak{S}_{\bm{a}}^{\upkappa}
is a Lipschitz map,
and if
\Delta^{k}
is equipped with the
\ell^{1}
-metric, then its Lipschitz constant can be estimated in terms of
\upkappa
and the radius of
\bm{a}
(in particular it does not depend on
k
).

(b)

\mathop{\rm Web}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{})=\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits\mathfrak{S}_{\bm{a}}^{\upkappa}
.
Moreover, if a closed convex set
K\subset\penalty\mathcal{U}
contains all
a^{i}
, then
\mathop{\rm Web}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{})\subset\penalty K
.

(c)

The restriction
1
1

1

Recall that
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}^{{-}0}}{}{}
denotes the array
(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{a^{1}}{}{},\dots\penalty,\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{a^{k}}{}{})
.

\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}^{{-}0}}{}{}|_{\mathop{\rm InWeb}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{})}
is an open
C^{\frac{1}{2}}
-embedding in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
.
Thus there is an inverse of

\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}^{{-}0}}{}{}|_{\mathop{\rm InWeb}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{})}
, say
\Phi\colon\mathbb{R}^{k}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathcal{U}
.

The subfunction
f=\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{a^{0}}{}{}\circ\penalty\Phi
is semiconvex and locally Lipschitz.
Moreover, if
\upkappa\leqslant\penalty 0
, then
f
is convex.

In particular,
\mathop{\rm Web}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{})
is a compact set and

\mathop{\rm InWeb}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{})
is
C^{\frac{1}{2}}
-homeomorphic to an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

14.8. Definition.

The submap
\Phi\colon\mathbb{R}^{k}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathcal{X}
of Theorem
14B

will be called the
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{}

with
brace

\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{a^{0}}{}{}
.
The terminology invokes Theorem

.

14.9. Definition.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space
and
\bm{a}=\penalty(a^{0},a^{1},\dots\penalty a^{k})
be a point array with radius
<\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}
.
If
\mathop{\rm InWeb}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{})
is nonempty, then the point array
\bm{a}
is called
nondegenerate
.

Lemma
14B
will provide examples of nondegenerate point arrays,
which can be used in
14B
.

14.10. Corollary.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space,

\bm{a}=\penalty(a^{0},a^{1},\dots\penalty a^{m})
be a nondegenerate point array
of radius
<\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}
in
\mathcal{U}

and
\upsigma=\mathfrak{S}_{\bm{a}}^{\upkappa}
be the corresponding
\upkappa
-baricentric simplex.
Then for some
\bm{x}\in\penalty\Delta^{m}
,
the differential
\bm{d}_{\bm{x}}\upsigma
is linear
and the image
\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits\bm{d}_{\bm{x}}\upsigma

forms a subcone isometric to an
m
-dimensional Euclidean space in the tangent cone
\text{\rm T}_{\upsigma(\bm{x})}
.

Proof.

Denote the distance map
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}^{{-}0}}{}{}
by
\uptau\colon\mathcal{U}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

According to Theorem
14B
,

\upsigma
is Lipschitz
and the distance map
\uptau

gives an open embedding of

\mathop{\rm InWeb}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{})=\upsigma(\Delta^{m})\setminus\upsigma(\partial\Delta^{m})
.
Note that
\uptau
is Lipschitz.
According to Rademacher’s theorem (
13C
),
the differential

\bm{d}_{\bm{x}}(\uptau\circ\penalty\upsigma)

is linear for almost all
\bm{x}\in\penalty\Delta^{m}
.
Further, since
\mathop{\rm InWeb}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{})\neq\penalty\varnothing
,
the area formula
[
91
]
implies that
\bm{d}_{\bm{x}}(\uptau\circ\penalty\upsigma)
is surjective on a set of positive masure of points
\bm{x}\in\penalty\Delta^{m}
.

Note that
\bm{d}_{\bm{x}}(\uptau\circ\penalty\upsigma)=(\bm{d}_{\upsigma(\bm{x})}\uptau)\circ\penalty(\bm{d}_{\bm{x}}\upsigma)
.
Applying Rademacher’s theorem again, we have linearity of

\bm{d}_{\bm{x}}\upsigma
for almost all
\bm{x}\in\penalty\Delta^{m}
;
at these points
\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits\bm{d}_{\bm{x}}\upsigma
forms a subcone isometric to a Euclidean space in
\text{\rm T}_{\upsigma(\bm{x})}
.
Clearly the dimension of
\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits\bm{d}_{\bm{x}}(\uptau\circ\penalty\upsigma)
is at least as big as the dimension of
\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits\bm{d}_{\bm{x}}\upsigma
.
Hence the result.
∎

Proof of
14B
.

Fix
z\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
and
r<\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}

such that
|{z}-\penalty{a^{i}}|<r
for all
i
.
Note that the set
K\cap\penalty\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[z,r]
is convex, closed, and contains all
a^{i}
.
Applying the theorem on short retract (Exercise
9N
),
we get the second part of
14B
.

The remaining statements are proved first in the case
\upkappa\leqslant\penalty 0
,
and then the remaining case
\upkappa>0
is reduced to the case
\upkappa=0
.

Case
\upkappa\leqslant\penalty 0
.

Consider the function array
f^{i}=\mathrm{md}^{\upkappa}\circ\penalty\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{a^{i}}{}{}
.
From the definition of web (
14A
),
it is clear that
\mathop{\rm Web}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{})=\mathop{\rm Web}\nolimits\bm{f}
.
Further, by the definition of
\upkappa
-barycentric simplex,

\mathfrak{S}_{\bm{a}}^{\upkappa}=\mathfrak{S}_{\bm{f}}
.

All the functions
f^{i}
are strongly convex (see
9E
).
Therefore
14B
,
14B
, and the first statements in
14B
follow from Theorem
14A
.

Case
\upkappa>0
.

Applying rescaling, we may assume
\upkappa=1
,
so
\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}=\varpi{}^{{1}}=\penalty\uppi
.

Let
\mathring{\mathcal{U}}=\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits\mathcal{U}
.
By
11D
,
\mathring{\mathcal{U}}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
.
Let us denote by
\upiota
the natural embedding of
\mathcal{U}
as the unit sphere in
\mathring{\mathcal{U}}
, and by

\mathop{\rm proj}\nolimits\colon\mathring{\mathcal{U}}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathcal{U}
the submap
defined by
\mathop{\rm proj}\nolimits(v)=\upiota^{-1}(v/|v|)
for all

v\neq 0
.
Note that there is
z\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
and
\upvarepsilon>0
such that
the set

K_{\upvarepsilon}=\left\{\,{v\in\penalty\mathring{\mathcal{U}}}\ {:}\ {\langle\upiota(z),v\rangle\geqslant\penalty\upvarepsilon}\,\right\}

contains all
\upiota(a^{i})
.
Then

0\notin K_{\upvarepsilon}
,
and
the set
K_{\upvarepsilon}
is closed and convex.
The latter follows from Exercise
9E
,
since
v\mapsto\penalty-\langle\upiota(z),v\rangle
is a Busemann function.

Denote by
\upiota(\bm{a})
the point array
(\upiota(a^{0}),\upiota(a^{1}),\dots\penalty,\upiota(a^{k}))
in
\mathring{\mathcal{U}}
.
From the case
\upkappa=0
,
we get that
\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits\mathfrak{S}_{\upiota(\bm{a})}^{0}\subset\penalty K_{\upvarepsilon}
.
In particular,
\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits\mathfrak{S}_{\upiota(\bm{a})}^{0}\not\ni 0
and thus
\mathop{\rm proj}\nolimits\circ\penalty\mathfrak{S}_{\upiota(\bm{a})}^{0}
is defined.
Direct calculations show

\mathfrak{S}_{\bm{a}}^{1}=\mathop{\rm proj}\nolimits\circ\penalty\mathfrak{S}_{\upiota(\bm{a})}^{0}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathop{\rm Web}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{})=\mathop{\rm proj}\nolimits[\mathop{\rm Web}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\upiota(\bm{a})}{}{})].

Thus the case
\upkappa=1
of the theorem is reduced to the case
\upkappa=0
,
which is proved already.
∎

14.11. Lemma.

Let
\bm{a}=\penalty(a^{0},a^{1},\dots\penalty a^{k})
be a point array of radius
<\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}

in a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space
\mathcal{U}
,
and
B^{i}=\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[a^{i},r^{i}]
for an array of positive reals
(r^{0},r^{1},\dots\penalty,r^{k})
.
Assume that

\bigcap_{i}B^{i}=\varnothing
,
but

\bigcap_{i\neq j}B^{i}\neq\varnothing

for any
j
.
Then
\bm{a}
is nondegenerate.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\mathcal{U}
is geodesic and
\mathop{\rm diam}\nolimits\mathcal{U}<\penalty\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
.
If not, choose
z\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
and
r<\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}
so that

|{z}-\penalty{a^{i}}|\leqslant\penalty r

for each
i
,
and consider
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[z,r]
instead of
\mathcal{U}
.
The latter can be done since
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[z,r]
is convex and closed,
so
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[z,r]
is a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space
and
\mathop{\rm Web}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{})\subset\penalty\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[z,r]
;
see
9E
and
14B
.

By Theorem
14B
,
\mathop{\rm Web}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{})
is a compact set;
therefore there is a point
p\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Web}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{})

minimizing the function

f(x)=\max_{i}\{\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{B^{i}}{x}{}\}=\max\{0,|{a^{0}}-\penalty{x}|-r^{0},\dots\penalty,|{a^{k}}-\penalty{x}|-r^{k}\}.

By the definition of web (
14A
),

p
is also the minimum point of
f
on
\mathcal{U}
.
Let us prove the following claim:

➊

p\notin B^{j}
for any
j
.

Indeed,
assume the contrary; that is,

p\in\penalty B^{j}

➋

for some
j
.
Then
p
is a point of local minimum for the function

h^{j}(x)=\max_{i\neq j}\{\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{B^{i}}{x}{}\}.

Hence

\max_{i\neq j}\{\measuredangle[p\,{}^{x}_{a^{i}}]\}\geqslant\penalty\tfrac{\uppi}{2}

for any
x\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
.
From the angle comparison (
9C
), it follows that

p
is a global minimum of
h^{j}
and hence

p\in\penalty\bigcap_{i\neq j}B^{i}.

The latter and
➋
contradict
\bigcap_{i}B^{i}=\varnothing
.

\vartriangle

From the definition of web, it also follows that

\mathop{\rm Web}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}^{{-}j}}{}{})\subset\penalty\bigcup_{i\neq j}B^{i}.

Indeed, if
q\in\penalty\bigcap_{i\neq j}B^{i}
and
q^{\prime}\notin\bigcup_{i\neq j}B^{i}
,
then
|{a_{i}}-\penalty{q}|<\penalty|{a_{i}}-\penalty{q^{\prime}}|
for any
i\neq j
therefore
q^{\prime}\notin\mathop{\rm Web}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}^{{-}j}}{}{})
.
Therefore the claim implies that

p\notin\mathop{\rm Web}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}^{{-}j}}{}{})
for each
j
;
that is,
p\in\penalty\mathop{\rm InWeb}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{})
.
∎

C
Dimension

See Chapter
7
for definitions of various dimension-like invariants of metric spaces.

We start with two examples.

The first example shows that the dimension of complete length
CAT
spaces is not local;
that is, such spaces might have open sets with different linear dimensions.

Such an example can be constructed by gluing at one point two Euclidean spaces of different dimensions.
According to Reshetnyak’s gluing theorem (
9I
), this construction gives a
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
space.

The second example provides a complete length
CAT
space
with topological dimension 1 and arbitrary large Hausdorff dimension.
Thus for complete length
CAT
spaces, one should not expect any relations between topological and Hausdorff dimensions except for the one provided by Szpilrajn’s theorem (
7A
).

To construct the second type of example,
note that the completion of any metric tree has topological dimension 1 and is
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
for any
\upkappa
.
Start with a binary tree
\Gamma
, and a sequence
\upvarepsilon_{n}>0
such that
\sum_{n}\upvarepsilon_{n}<\infty
.
Define the metric on
\Gamma

by prescribing the length of an edge from level
n
to level
n+1
to be
\upvarepsilon_{n}
.
For an appropriately chosen sequence
\upvarepsilon_{n}
, the completion of
\Gamma
will contain a Cantor set of arbitrarily large Hausdorff dimension.

The following is a version of a theorem proved by Bruce Kleiner
[
94
]
, with an improvement made by Alexander Lytchak
[
108
]
.

14.12. Theorem.

For any complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space
\mathcal{U}
, the following statements are equivalent:

(a)

\mathop{\rm LinDim}\nolimits\mathcal{U}\geqslant\penalty m
.

(b)

For some
z\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
there is an array of
m+1
balls
B^{i}=\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(a^{i},r^{i})
with
a^{0},a^{1},\dots\penalty,a^{m}\in\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(z,\frac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2})

such that

\bigcap_{i}B^{i}=\varnothing\quad\text{and}\quad\bigcap_{i\neq j}B^{i}\neq\varnothing\quad\text{for each $j$}.

(c)

There is a
C^{\frac{1}{2}}
-embedding
\Phi\colon\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[1]_{\mathbb{E}^{m}}\hookrightarrow\penalty\mathcal{U}
;
that is,
\Phi
is bi-Hölder with exponent
\tfrac{1}{2}
.

(d)

There is a closed separable set
K\subset\penalty\mathcal{U}
such that

\mathop{\rm TopDim}\nolimits K\geqslant\penalty m.

Remarks.

Theorem
14D
gives a stronger version of part
14C
in the finite-dimensional case.
Namely, a complete length
CAT
space with linear dimension
m

admits a bi-Lipschitz embedding
\Phi
of an open set of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
.
Moreover, the Lipschitz constants of
\Phi
can be made arbitrarily close to
1
.

14.13. Corollary.

For any separable complete length
CAT
space
\mathcal{U}
, we have

\mathop{\rm TopDim}\nolimits\mathcal{U}=\mathop{\rm LinDim}\nolimits\mathcal{U}.

Any simplicial complex can be equipped with a length metric
such that each
k
-simplex
is isometric to the standard simplex

\Delta^{k}=\left\{\,{(x_{0},\dots\penalty,x_{k})\in\penalty\mathbb{R}^{k+1}}\ {:}\ {x_{i}\geqslant\penalty 0,\quad x_{0}+\dots\penalty+x_{k}=1}\,\right\}

with the metric induced by the
\ell^{1}
-norm on
\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
.
This metric will be called the
\ell^{1}
-metric
on the simplicial complex.

14.14. Lemma.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space
and
\uprho\colon\mathcal{U}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a continuous positive function.
Then there is a simplicial complex
\mathcal{N}
equipped with
\ell^{1}
-metric,
a locally Lipschitz map
\Phi\colon\mathcal{U}\to\penalty\mathcal{N}
,
and a Lipschitz map
\Psi\colon\mathcal{N}\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
such that:

(a)

The displacement of the composition
\Psi\circ\penalty\Phi\colon\mathcal{U}\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
is bounded by
\uprho
;
that is,

|{x}-\penalty{\Psi\circ\penalty\Phi(x)}|<\uprho(x)

for any
x\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
.

(b)

If
\mathop{\rm LinDim}\nolimits\mathcal{U}\leqslant\penalty m

then the
\Psi
-image of any closed simplex in
\mathcal{N}

coincides with the image of its
m
-skeleton.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that for any
x
we have
\uprho(x)<\penalty\uprho_{0}

for fixed
\uprho_{0}<\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}
.

By Stone’s theorem, any metric space is paracompact.
Thus, we can choose a locally finite covering
\left\{\,{\Omega_{\upalpha}}\ {:}\ {\upalpha\in\penalty\mathcal{A}}\,\right\}
of
\mathcal{U}
such that
\Omega_{\upalpha}\subset\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(x,\tfrac{1}{3}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uprho(x))
for any
x\in\penalty\Omega_{\upalpha}
.

Denote by
\mathcal{N}
the nerve of the covering
\{\Omega_{\upalpha}\}
;
that is,
\mathcal{N}
is an abstract simplicial complex with
vertex set
\mathcal{A}
,
such that

\{\upalpha^{0},\upalpha^{1},\dots\penalty,\upalpha^{n}\}\subset\penalty\mathcal{A}

are vertexes of a simplex if and only if

\Omega_{\upalpha^{0}}\cap\penalty\Omega_{\upalpha^{1}}\cap\penalty\dots\penalty\cap\penalty\Omega_{\upalpha^{n}}\neq\varnothing
.

Fix a Lipschitz partition of unity

\upvarphi_{\upalpha}\colon\mathcal{U}\to\penalty[0,1]
subordinate to
\{\Omega_{\upalpha}\}
.
Consider the map
\Phi\colon\mathcal{U}\to\penalty\mathcal{N}
such that the barycentric coordinate of
\Phi(p)
is
\upvarphi_{\upalpha}(p)
.
Note that
\Phi
is locally Lipschitz.
Clearly the
\Phi
-preimage of any open simplex in
\mathcal{N}
lies in
\Omega_{\upalpha}
for some
\upalpha\in\penalty\mathcal{A}
.

For each
\upalpha\in\penalty\mathcal{A}
,
choose
x_{\upalpha}\in\penalty\Omega_{\upalpha}
.
Let us extend the map
\upalpha\mapsto\penalty x_{\upalpha}

to a map
\Psi\colon\mathcal{N}\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
that is
\upkappa
-barycentric on each simplex.
According to Theorem
14B
, this extension exists,

\Psi
is Lipschitz,
and its Lipschitz constant depends only on
\uprho_{0}
and
\upkappa
.

14C
.

Fix
x\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
. Denote by
\Delta
the minimal simplex that contains
\Phi(x)
,
and let
\upalpha^{0},\upalpha^{1},\dots\penalty,\upalpha^{n}
be the vertexes of
\Delta
.
Note that
\upalpha
is a vertex of
\Delta
if and only if
\upvarphi_{\upalpha}(x)>0
.
Thus

|{x}-\penalty{x_{\upalpha^{i}}}|<\tfrac{1}{3}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uprho(x)

for any
i
.
Therefore

\mathop{\rm diam}\nolimits\Psi(\Delta)\leqslant\penalty\max_{i,j}\{|{x_{\upalpha^{i}}}-\penalty{x_{\upalpha^{j}}}|\}<\tfrac{2}{3}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uprho(x).

In particular,

|{x}-\penalty{\Psi\circ\penalty\Phi(x)}|\leqslant\penalty|{x}-\penalty{x_{\upalpha^{0}}}|+\mathop{\rm diam}\nolimits\Psi(\Delta)<\uprho(x).

14C
.

Assume the contrary;
that is,
\Psi(\mathcal{N})
is not included in the
\Psi
-image of the
m
-skeleton of
\mathcal{N}
.
Then for some
k>m
,
there is a
k
-simplex
\Delta^{k}
in
\mathcal{N}

such that the barycentric simplex
\upsigma=\Psi|_{\Delta^{k}}
is nondegenerate;
that is,

W=\Psi(\Delta^{k})\setminus\Psi(\partial\Delta^{k})\neq\varnothing.

Applying Corollary
14B

gives
\mathop{\rm LinDim}\nolimits\mathcal{U}\geqslant\penalty k
, a contradiction.
∎

Proof of
14C
;
14C
\Rightarrow
14C
\Rightarrow
14C
.

The implication
14C
\Rightarrow
14C
follows from Lemma
14B

and Theorem
14B
, and
14C
\Rightarrow
14C
is trivial.

14C
\Rightarrow
14C
.

According to Theorem
7A
,
there is a continuous map
f\colon K\to\penalty\mathbb{R}^{m}
with a stable value.
By the Tietze extension theorem, it is possible to extend
f

to a continuous map
F\colon\mathcal{U}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

Fix
\upvarepsilon>0
.
Since
F
is continuous, there is a continuous positive function
\uprho
defined on
\mathcal{U}
such that

|{x}-\penalty{y}|<\uprho(x)\quad\Rightarrow\quad|F(x)-F(y)|<\tfrac{1}{3}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon.

Apply Lemma
14C
to
\uprho
.
For the resulting simplicial complex
\mathcal{N}

and the maps
\Phi\colon\mathcal{U}\to\penalty\mathcal{N}
,
\Psi\colon\mathcal{N}\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
, we have

|F\circ\penalty\Psi\circ\penalty\Phi(x)-F(x)|<\tfrac{1}{3}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon

for any
x\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
.

According to Lemma
3B
,
there is a locally Lipschitz map
F_{\upvarepsilon}\colon\mathcal{U}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}^{m+1}

such that
|F_{\upvarepsilon}(x)-F(x)|<\tfrac{1}{3}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon
for any
x\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
.

Note that

\Phi(K)
is contained in a countable subcomplex of
\mathcal{N}
, say
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
.
Indeed, since
K
is separable, there is a countable dense collection of points
\{x_{n}\}
in
K
.
Denote by
\Delta_{n}
the minimal simplex of
\mathcal{N}
that contains
\Phi(x_{n})
.
Then
\Phi(K)\subset\penalty\bigcup_{i}\Delta_{n}
.

Arguing by contradiction,
assume
\mathop{\rm LinDim}\nolimits\mathcal{U}<m
.
By
14C
,
the image
F_{\upvarepsilon}\circ\penalty\Psi\circ\penalty\Phi(K)
lies in the
F_{\upvarepsilon}
-image of the
(m-1)
-skeleton of
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
;
In particular, it can be covered by a countable collection of Lipschitz images of
(m-1)
-simplexes.
Hence

\bm{0}\in\penalty\mathbb{R}^{m}
is not a stable value of the restriction
F_{\upvarepsilon}\circ\penalty\Psi\circ\penalty\Phi|_{K}
.
Since
\upvarepsilon>0
is arbitrary,
then
\bm{0}\in\penalty\mathbb{R}^{m}
is not a stable value of
f
— a contradiction.

14C
\Rightarrow
14C
.

Choose
q\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
such that
\text{\rm T}_{q}
contains a subcone
E
isometric to
m
-dimensional Euclidean space.
Note that one can choose
\upvarepsilon>0

and a point arrray
(\dot{a}^{0},\dot{a}^{1},\dots\penalty,\dot{a}^{m})
in
E\subset\penalty\text{\rm T}_{q}

such that

\bigcap_{i}\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[\dot{a}^{i},1+\upvarepsilon]=\varnothing

and
\bigcap_{i\neq j}\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[\dot{a}^{i},1-\penalty\upvarepsilon]\neq\penalty\varnothing
for each
j
.

For each
i
choose a geodesic
\upgamma^{i}

from
q
that goes almost in the directions of
\dot{a}^{i}
.
Choose small
\updelta>0
and take the point
a^{i}
on
\upgamma^{i}
at distance
\updelta{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|\dot{a}^{i}|
from
q
.
We get a point array

(a^{0},a^{1},\dots\penalty,a^{m})
in
\mathcal{U}

such that
\bigcap_{i}\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[a^{i},\updelta]=\penalty\varnothing

and
\bigcap_{i\neq j}\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[a^{i},\updelta]\neq\varnothing
for each
j
.
Since
\updelta>0
can be chosen arbitrarily small,

14C
follows.
∎

D
Finite-dimensional spaces

Recall that a web embedding and its brace are defined in
14B
.

14.15. Theorem.

Suppose
\mathcal{U}
is a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space such that

\mathop{\rm LinDim}\nolimits\mathcal{U}=m
,
and
\bm{a}=(a^{0},a^{1},\dots\penalty a^{m})
is a point array in
\mathcal{U}

with radius
<\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}
.
Then
the
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{}
-web embedding
\Phi\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathcal{U}
with brace
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{a^{0}}{}{}
is locally Lipschitz.

Note that if
\bm{a}
is degenerate,
that is, if
\mathop{\rm InWeb}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{})=\varnothing
,
then
the domain of
\Phi
is empty, and hence the conclusion of the theorem trivially holds.

14.16. Lemma.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space,
and
\bm{a}=\penalty(a^{0},a^{1},\dots\penalty a^{k})
be a point array with radius
<\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}
.
Then for any
p\in\penalty\mathop{\rm InWeb}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{})
,
there is
\upvarepsilon>0
such that
if for some
q\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Web}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{})
and
b\in\penalty\mathcal{U}

we have

|{p}-\penalty{q}|<\upvarepsilon,\quad|{p}-\penalty{b}|<\upvarepsilon,\quad\text{and}\quad\measuredangle[q\,{}^{b}_{a^{i}}]<\tfrac{\uppi}{2}+\upvarepsilon

➊

for each
i
,
then the array
(b,a^{0},a^{1},\dots\penalty,a^{m})
is nondegenerate.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\mathcal{U}
is geodesic and
\mathop{\rm diam}\nolimits\mathcal{U}<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
.
If not, consider instead of
\mathcal{U}
,
a ball
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[z,r]\subset\penalty\mathcal{U}

for some
z\in\penalty\mathcal{U}

and
r<\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}

such that
|{z}-\penalty{a^{i}}|\leqslant\penalty r
for each
i
.

From the angle comparison (
9C
), it follows that

p\in\penalty\mathop{\rm InWeb}\nolimits\bm{a}
if and only if both of the following conditions hold:

1.

\max_{i}\{\measuredangle[p\,{}^{a^{i}}_{u}]\}\geqslant\penalty\tfrac{\uppi}{2}
for any
u\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
,

2.

for each
i
there is
u^{i}\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
such that
\measuredangle[p\,{}^{a^{j}}_{u^{i}}]<\tfrac{\uppi}{2}
for all
j\neq i
.

Due to the semicontinuity of angles (
9H
),
there is
\upvarepsilon>0
such that for any
x\in\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(p,10{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon)
we have

\measuredangle[x\,{}^{a^{j}}_{u^{i}}]<\tfrac{\uppi}{2}-10{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon\quad\text{for all}\quad j\neq i.

➋

Now assume that for sufficiently small
\upvarepsilon>0

there are points
b\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
and
q\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Web}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{})
such that
➊
holds.
According to Theorem
14B
,
for all small
\upvarepsilon>0
we have

\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\{b,a^{0},a^{1},\dots\penalty,a^{k}\}<\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}.

Fix a sufficiently small
\updelta>0

and let

v^{i}=\mathrm{geod}_{[qu^{i}]}(\tfrac{1}{3}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\updelta)\quad\text{and}\quad w^{i}=\mathrm{geod}_{[v^{i}b]}(\tfrac{2}{3}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\updelta).

Clearly

\displaystyle|{b}-\penalty{w^{i}}|

\displaystyle=|{b}-\penalty{v^{i}}|-\tfrac{2}{3}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\updelta\leqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty|{b}-\penalty{q}|-\tfrac{1}{3}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\updelta.

Further, the inequalities
➋
and
➊
imply

\displaystyle|{a^{j}}-\penalty{w^{i}}|

\displaystyle<|{a^{j}}-\penalty{v^{i}}|+\tfrac{2}{3}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\updelta<

\displaystyle<|{a^{i}}-\penalty{q}|-\upvarepsilon{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\updelta<

\displaystyle<|{a^{i}}-\penalty{q}|

for all
i\neq j
.

Set
B^{i}=\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[a^{i},|{a^{i}}-\penalty{q}|]
and
B^{m+1}=\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[b,|{a^{i}}-\penalty{q}|-\tfrac{1}{3}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\updelta]
.
Clearly

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\bigcap_{i\neq m+1}B^{i}=\{q\},

\displaystyle\bigcap_{i\neq j}B^{i}\ni w^{j}\quad\text{for}\quad j\neq m+1,

\displaystyle\bigcap_{i}B^{i}=\{q\}\cap\penalty B^{m+1}=\varnothing.

Lemma
14B
finishes the proof.
∎

Proof of
14D
.

Suppose
\Phi
is not locally Lipshitz.; that is, there are sequences
\bm{y}_{n},\bm{z}_{n}\to\penalty\bm{x}\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits\Phi
such that

\frac{|{\Phi(\bm{y}_{n})}-\penalty{\Phi(\bm{z}_{n}}|)}{|\bm{y}_{n}-\bm{z}_{n}|}\to\penalty\infty\quad\text{as}\quad n\to\penalty\infty.

➌

Set
p=\Phi(\bm{x})
,

q_{n}=\Phi(\bm{y}_{n})
,
and
b_{n}=\Phi(\bm{z}_{n})
.
By
14B
,
p
,
q_{n}
,
b_{n}\in\penalty\mathop{\rm InWeb}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{})

and
q_{n},b_{n}\to\penalty p
as
n\to\penalty\infty
.
Choose
\upvarepsilon>0
; note that
➌
implies

\measuredangle[q_{n}\,{}^{a^{i}}_{b_{n}}]<\tfrac{\uppi}{2}+\upvarepsilon

for all
i>0
and all large
n
.
Further, according to
14B
, the subfunction

(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}^{0}}{}{})\circ\penalty\Phi
is locally Lipschitz.
Therefore we also have

\measuredangle[q_{n}\,{}^{a^{0}}_{b_{n}}]<\tfrac{\uppi}{2}+\upvarepsilon

for all large
n
.
According to Lemma
14D
, the point array
b_{n},a^{0},\dots\penalty,a^{k}
for large
n
is nondegenerate.

Applying Corollary
14B
,
we have a contradiction.
∎

E
Remarks

The following conjecture was formulated by Bruce Kleiner
[
94
]
, see also
[
73
, p. 133]
.
For separable spaces, it follows from Corollary
14C
.

14.17. Conjecture.

For any complete length
CAT
space
\mathcal{U}
, we have

\mathop{\rm TopDim}\nolimits\mathcal{U}=\mathop{\rm LinDim}\nolimits\mathcal{U}.

Chapter 15
Dimension of CBB spaces

As the main dimension-like invariant, we will use the linear dimension
\mathop{\rm LinDim}\nolimits
;
see Definition
7B
. In other words, by default
dimension
means
linear dimension
.

A
Struts and rank

Our definitions of strut
and distance chart
differ from the one in
[
43
]
;
it is closer to Perelman’s definitions
[
124
,
123
]
.

The term “strut” seems to have the closest meaning to the original Russian term used by Yuriy Burago, Grigory Perelman, and Michael Gromov
[
43
]
.
In the official translation,
it appears as “burst”,
and in the authors’ translation it was “strainer”.
Neither seems intuitive,
so we decided to switch to “strut”.

15.1. Definition of struts.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
CBB
space.
We say that a point array
(a^{0},a^{1},\dots\penalty,a^{k})
in
\mathcal{L}

is
\upkappa
-strutting
for a point
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
if
\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{a^{i}}_{a^{j}}\hskip-0.86108pt)>\tfrac{\uppi}{2}
for all
i\neq j
.

Recall that the packing number is defined in
2B
.
The following definition is motivated by the observation that
k=\penalty\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\uppi/2}(\mathbb{S}^{k-1})-1
for any integer
k>0
.

15.2. Definition.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
CBB
space
and
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
.
Let us define rank of
\mathcal{L}
at
p
as

\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits_{p}=\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits_{p}\mathcal{L}:=\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\uppi/2}\Sigma_{p}-1.

Thus
\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits
takes values in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant\penalty 0}\cup\penalty\{\infty\}
.

15.3. Proposition.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space
and
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
.
Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(a)

\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits_{p}\geqslant\penalty k
,

(b)

there is a point array
(a^{0},a^{1},\dots\penalty,a^{k})

that is
\upkappa
-strutting at
p
.

Proof of
15A
,
15A
\Rightarrow
15A
.

For each
i
,
choose a point
\acute{a}^{i}\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Str}\nolimits(p)
sufficiently close to
a^{i}
(so
[p\acute{a}^{i}]
exists for each
i
).
One can choose
\acute{a}^{i}
so that we still have

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{\acute{a}^{i}}_{\acute{a}^{j}}\hskip-0.86108pt)>\tfrac{\uppi}{2}
for all
i\neq j
.

From hinge comparison (
8C
),

\measuredangle({\uparrow}_{[{p}{\acute{a}^{j}}]},{\uparrow}_{[{p}{\acute{a}^{j}}]})\geqslant\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{\acute{a}^{i}}_{\acute{a}^{j}}\hskip-0.86108pt)>\tfrac{\uppi}{2}

for all
i\neq j
.
In particular,
\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\uppi/2}\Sigma_{p}\geqslant\penalty k+1
.

15A
\Rightarrow
15A
.

Assume
(\upxi^{0},\upxi^{1},\dots\penalty,\upxi^{k})
is an array of directions in
\Sigma_{p}
, such that
\measuredangle(\upxi^{i},\upxi^{j})>\tfrac{\uppi}{2}
if
i\neq j
.

Without loss of generality,
we may assume that each direction
\upxi^{i}
is geodesic;
that is, for each
i
there is a geodesic
\upgamma^{i}
in
\mathcal{L}
such that
\upgamma^{i}(0)=\penalty p
and
\upxi^{i}=(\upgamma^{i})^{+}(0)
.
From the definition of angle, it follows that for sufficiently small
\upvarepsilon>0
the array of points
a^{i}=\upgamma^{i}(\upvarepsilon)
satisfies
15A
.
∎

15.4. Corollary.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
CBB
space and
k\in\penalty\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant\penalty 0}
.
Then the set of all points in
\mathcal{L}

with rank
\geqslant\penalty k
is open.

Proof.
Given an array of points
\bm{a}=(a^{0},\dots\penalty,a^{k})
in
\mathcal{L}
, consider
the set
\Omega_{\bm{a}}
of all points
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
such that array
\bm{a}

is
\upkappa
-strutting for a point
p
.
Clearly
\Omega_{\bm{a}}
is open.

According to Proposition
15A
, the set of points in
\mathcal{L}

with rank
\geqslant\penalty k
can be presented as

\bigcup_{\bm{a}}\Omega_{\bm{a}},

where the union is taken over all
k
-arrays
\bm{a}
of points in
\mathcal{L}
.
Hence the result.
∎

B
Right-inverse theorem

Suppose that
\bm{a}=(a^{1},\dots\penalty,a^{k})
is a point array in a metric space
\mathcal{L}
.
Recall that the map
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{}\colon\mathcal{L}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}^{n}
is defined by

\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{p}{}:=(|{a^{1}}-\penalty{p}|,\dots\penalty,|{a^{n}}-\penalty{p}|).

15.5. Right-inverse theorem.

Suppose
\mathcal{L}
is a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space,

p,b\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
,
and
\bm{a}=(a^{1},\dots\penalty,a^{k})
is a point array in
\mathcal{L}
.

Assume that
(b,a^{1},a^{2},\dots\penalty,a^{k})
is
\upkappa
-strutting for
p
.
Then the distance map
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{}\colon\mathcal{L}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}^{k}
has a right inverse defined in a neighborhood of
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{p}\in\penalty\mathbb{R}^{k}
;
that is, there is a submap
\Phi\colon\mathbb{R}^{k}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathcal{L}
such that
\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits\Phi\ni\penalty\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{p}
and

\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{[\Phi(\bm{x})]}=\bm{x}
for any
