\bm{x}\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits\Phi
.
Moreover,

(a)

The map
\Phi
can be chosen to be
C^{\frac{1}{2}}
-continuous (that is, Hölder continuous with exponent
\tfrac{1}{2}
) and such that

\Phi(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{p})=p.

(b)

The distance map
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{}\colon\mathcal{L}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}^{k}
is locally co-Lipschitz (in particular, open) in a neighborhood of
p
.

Part
15B
of the theorem
is closely related to
[
43
, Theorem 5.4]
by Yuriy Burago, Grigory Perelman, and Michael Gromov,
but the proof presented here is different.
Yet another proof can be built on
[
109
, Proposition 4.3]
by Alexander Lytchak.

Proof.

Fix
\upvarepsilon,r,\uplambda>0
such that the following conditions hold:

(i)

Each distance function
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{a^{i}}{}{}
and
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{b}{}{}
is
\tfrac{\uplambda}{2}
-concave in
\mathrm{B}{}(p,r)
.

(ii)

For any
q\in\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(p,r)
, we have
\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(q\,{}^{a^{i}}_{a^{j}}\hskip-0.86108pt)>\tfrac{\uppi}{2}+\upvarepsilon
for all
i\neq j
and
\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(q\,{}^{b}_{a^{i}}\hskip-0.86108pt)>\penalty\tfrac{\uppi}{2}+\upvarepsilon
for all
i
.
In addition,
\upvarepsilon<\tfrac{1}{10}
.

Given
\bm{x}=(x^{1},x^{2},\dots\penalty,x^{k})\in\penalty\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
consider the function

f_{\bm{x}}\colon\mathcal{L}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
defined by

f_{\bm{x}}=\min_{i}\{h_{\bm{x}}^{i}\}+\upvarepsilon{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{b}{}{},

where
h_{\bm{x}}^{i}(q)=\min\{0,|{a^{i}}-\penalty{q}|-x^{i}\}
.
Note that for any
\bm{x}\in\penalty\mathbb{R}^{k}
, the function
f_{\bm{x}}
is
(1+\upvarepsilon)
-Lipschitz and
\uplambda
-concave in
\mathrm{B}{}(p,r)
.
Denote by
\upalpha_{\bm{x}}(t)
the
f_{\bm{x}}
-gradient curve (see Chapter
16
) that starts at
p
.

➊

If for some
\bm{x}\in\penalty\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
t_{0}\leqslant\penalty\tfrac{r}{2}
we have

|\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{p}-\bm{x}|\leqslant\penalty\tfrac{\upvarepsilon^{2}}{10}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}t_{0}
, then

\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{[\upalpha_{\bm{x}}(t_{0})]}=\bm{x}
.

First note that
15B
follows if for any
q\in\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(p,r)
, we have

(i)

(\bm{d}_{q}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{a^{i}}{}{})(\nabla_{q}f_{\bm{x}})<-\tfrac{1}{10}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon^{2}
if
|{a^{i}}-\penalty{q}|>x^{i}
and

(ii)

(\bm{d}_{q}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{a^{i}}{}{})(\nabla_{q}f_{\bm{x}})>\tfrac{1}{10}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon^{2}
if

|{a^{i}}-\penalty{q}|-x^{i}=\min_{j}\{|{a^{j}}-\penalty{q}|-\penalty x^{j}\}<0.

Indeed, since
t_{0}\leqslant\penalty\tfrac{r}{2}
, then
\upalpha_{\bm{x}}(t)\in\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(p,r)
for all
t\in\penalty[0,t_{0}]
.
Consider the following real-to-real functions:

\displaystyle\upvarphi(t)

\displaystyle:=\max_{i}\{|{a^{i}}-\penalty{\upalpha_{\bm{x}}(t)}|-x^{i}\},

➋

\displaystyle\uppsi(t)

\displaystyle:=\min_{i}\{|{a^{i}}-\penalty{\upalpha_{\bm{x}}(t)}|-x^{i}\}.

Then from (
i
),
we have
\upvarphi^{+}<-\tfrac{1}{10}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon^{2}

if
\upvarphi>0
and
t\in\penalty[0,t_{0}]
.
Similarly,
from (
ii
),
we have
\uppsi^{+}>\tfrac{1}{10}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon^{2}

if
\uppsi<0
and
t\in\penalty[0,t_{0}]
.
Since
|\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{p}-\bm{x}|\leqslant\penalty\tfrac{\upvarepsilon^{2}}{10}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}t_{0}
, it follows that
\upvarphi(0)\leqslant\penalty\tfrac{\upvarepsilon^{2}}{10}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}t_{0}
and
\uppsi(0)\geqslant\penalty-\tfrac{\upvarepsilon^{2}}{10}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}t_{0}
.
Thus
\upvarphi(t_{0})\leqslant\penalty 0
and
\uppsi(t_{0})\geqslant\penalty 0
.
On the other hand, from
➋
we have
\upvarphi(t_{0})\geqslant\penalty\uppsi(t_{0})
.
That is,
\upvarphi(t_{0})=\uppsi(t_{0})=0
; hence
15B
follows.

Thus, to prove
15B
, it remains to prove (
i
) and (
ii
).
First let us prove it assuming that
\mathcal{L}
is geodesic.

Note that

(\bm{d}_{q}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{b}{}{})({\uparrow}_{[{q}{a^{i}}]})\leqslant\penalty\cos\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(q\,{}^{b}_{a^{j}}\hskip-0.86108pt)<-\tfrac{\upvarepsilon}{2}

➌

for all
i
, and

(\bm{d}_{q}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{a^{j}}{}{})({\uparrow}_{[{q}{a^{i}}]})\leqslant\penalty\cos\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(q\,{}^{a^{i}}_{a^{j}}\hskip-0.86108pt)<-\tfrac{\upvarepsilon}{2}

➍

for all
j\neq i
.
Further,
➍
implies

(\bm{d}_{q}h_{\bm{x}}^{j})({\uparrow}_{[{q}{a^{i}}]})\leqslant\penalty 0.

➎

for all
i\neq j
.
The assumption in (
i
) implies

\bm{d}_{q}f_{\bm{x}}=\min_{j\neq i}\{\bm{d}_{q}h_{\bm{x}}^{j}\}+\upvarepsilon{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(\bm{d}_{q}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{b}{}{}).

Thus

\displaystyle-(\bm{d}_{q}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{a^{i}}{}{})(\nabla_{q}f_{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty\langle{\uparrow}_{[{q}{a^{i}}]},\nabla_{q}f_{\bm{x}}\rangle\geqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty(\bm{d}_{q}f_{\bm{x}})({\uparrow}_{[{q}{a^{i}}]})=

\displaystyle=\min_{i\neq j}\{(\bm{d}_{q}h_{\bm{x}}^{i})({\uparrow}_{[{q}{a^{i}}]})\}+\upvarepsilon{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(\bm{d}_{q}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{b}{}{})({\uparrow}_{[{q}{a^{i}}]}).

Therefore (
i
) follows from
➌
and
➎
.

The assumption in (
ii
) implies that
f_{\bm{x}}(q)=h_{\bm{x}}^{i}(q)+\upvarepsilon{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{b}{}{}
and

\bm{d}_{q}f_{\bm{x}}\leqslant\penalty\bm{d}_{q}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{a^{i}}{}{}+\upvarepsilon{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(\bm{d}_{p}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{b}{}{}).

Therefore,

\displaystyle(\bm{d}_{q}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{a^{i}}{}{})(\nabla_{q}f_{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty\bm{d}_{q}f_{\bm{x}}(\nabla_{q}f_{\bm{x}})\geqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty\left[(\bm{d}_{q}f_{\bm{x}})({\uparrow}_{[{q}{b}]})\right]^{2}\geqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty\left[\min_{i}\{\cos\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(q\,{}^{b}_{a^{i}}\hskip-0.86108pt)\}-\upvarepsilon^{2}\right]^{2}.

Thus (
ii
) follows from
➌
, since
\upvarepsilon<\tfrac{1}{10}
.

Therefore
15B
holds if
\mathcal{L}
is geodesic.
If
\mathcal{L}
is not geodesic,
perform the above estimate in
\mathcal{L}^{\text{\rm}}
, the ultrapower of
\mathcal{L}
.
(Recall that according to
4C
,
\mathcal{L}^{\text{\rm}}
is geodesic.)
This completes the proof of
15B
.

\vartriangle

Set
t_{0}(\bm{x})=\tfrac{10}{\upvarepsilon^{2}}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{p}-\bm{x}|
,
giving equality in
15B
.
Define the submap
\Phi
by

\Phi\colon{\bm{x}}\mapsto\penalty\upalpha_{\bm{x}}\circ\penalty t_{0}(\bm{x}),\quad\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits\Phi=\mathrm{B}{}(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{p},\tfrac{\upvarepsilon^{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}r}{20})\subset\penalty\mathbb{R}^{k}.

It follows from
15B
that

\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{[\Phi(\bm{x})]}=\bm{x}
for any
\bm{x}\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits\Phi
.

Clearly
t_{0}(p)=0
; thus
\Phi(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{p})=p
.
Further, by construction of
f_{\bm{x}}
,

|f_{\bm{x}}(q)-f_{\bm{y}}(q)|\leqslant\penalty|\bm{x}-\bm{y}|,

for any
q\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
.
Therefore, according to Lemma
16C
,
\Phi
is
C^{\frac{1}{2}}
-continuous.
Thus
15B
.

Further, note that

|{p}-\penalty{\Phi(\bm{x})}|\leqslant\penalty(1+\upvarepsilon){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}t_{0}(\bm{x})\leqslant\penalty\tfrac{11}{\upvarepsilon^{2}}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{p}-\bm{x}|

➏

holds.

The above construction may be repeated for any
p^{\prime}\in\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(p,\tfrac{r}{4})
,
\upvarepsilon^{\prime}=\upvarepsilon
, and
r^{\prime}=\tfrac{r}{2}
.
The inequality
➏
for the resulting map
\Phi^{\prime}
implies that for any
p^{\prime},q\in\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(p,\tfrac{r}{4})

there is
q^{\prime}\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
such that
\Phi^{\prime}(q)=\Phi^{\prime}(q^{\prime})
and

|{p^{\prime}}-\penalty{q^{\prime}}|\leqslant\penalty\tfrac{11}{\upvarepsilon^{2}}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{p^{\prime}}-\bm{x}|.

That is, the distance map
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{}
is locally
\tfrac{11}{\upvarepsilon^{2}}
-co-Lipschitz in
\mathrm{B}{}(p,\tfrac{r}{4})
.
∎

C
Dimension theorem

The following theorem is the main result of this section.

15.6. Theorem.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space,

q\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
,

R>0

and
m\in\penalty\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant\penalty 0}
.
Then the following statements are equivalent:

(A)

\mathop{\rm LinDim}\nolimits\mathcal{L}\geqslant\penalty m
.

(B)

There is a point
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
that admits a
\upkappa
-strutting array
(b,a^{1},\dots\penalty,a^{m})\in\penalty\mathcal{L}^{m+1}
.

(C)

Let
\mathop{\rm Euk}\nolimits^{m}
be the set
of all points
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
such that there is a distance-preserving embedding
\mathbb{E}^{m}\hookrightarrow\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}

that preserves the cone structure
(see Section
6E
).
Then
\mathop{\rm Euk}\nolimits^{m}
contains a dense G-delta set in
\mathcal{L}
.

(D)

There is a
C^{\frac{1}{2}}
-embedding

\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[1]_{\mathbb{E}^{m}}\hookrightarrow\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(q,R);

that is, a bi-Hölder embedding with exponent
\tfrac{1}{2}
.

(E)

\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\upvarepsilon}\mathrm{B}{}(q,R)>\frac{c}{\upvarepsilon^{m}}

for fixed
c>0
and any
\upvarepsilon>0
.

In particular:

(i)

If
\mathop{\rm LinDim}\nolimits\mathcal{L}=\infty
, then all the statements
15C
,
15C
, and
15C
are satisfied for all
m\in\penalty\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant\penalty 0}
.

(ii)

If the statement
15C
or
15C
is satisfied for some choice of
q\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
and
R>0
, then it also is satisfied for any other choice of
q
and
R
.

For finite-dimensional spaces, Theorem
15E
gives a stronger version
of the theorem above.

The above theorem with the exception of statement
15C
was proved by Conrad Plaut
[
135
]
.
At that time, it was not known whether

\mathop{\rm LinDim}\nolimits\mathcal{L}=\infty\quad\Rightarrow\quad\mathop{\rm TopDim}\nolimits\mathcal{L}=\infty

for any complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space
\mathcal{L}
.
The latter implication was proved by Grigory Perelman and the third author
[
122
]
;
it was done by combining an idea of Conrad Plaut with the technique of gradient flow.
Part
15C
is somewhat stronger.

To prove Theorem
15C
we will need the following three propositions.

15.7. Proposition.

Let
p
be a point in a a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space

\mathcal{L}
.
Assume there is a distance-preserving embedding
\upiota\colon\mathbb{E}^{m}\hookrightarrow\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}\mathcal{L}

that preserves the cone structure.
Then either

(a)

\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits\upiota=\text{\rm T}_{p}\mathcal{L}
, or

(b)

there is a point
p^{\prime}
arbitrarily close to
p
such that there is a distance-preserving embedding
\upiota^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{E}^{m+1}\hookrightarrow\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p^{\prime}}\mathcal{L}

that preserves the cone structure.

Proof.

Assume
\upiota(\mathbb{E}^{m})
is a proper subset of
\text{\rm T}_{p}\mathcal{L}
.
Equivalently, there is a direction
\upxi\in\penalty\Sigma_{p}\setminus\upiota(\mathbb{S}^{m-1})
,
where
\mathbb{S}^{m-1}\subset\penalty\mathbb{E}^{m}
is the unit sphere.

Fix
\upvarepsilon>0
so that
\measuredangle(\upxi,\upsigma)>\upvarepsilon
for any
\upsigma\in\penalty\upiota(\mathbb{S}^{m-1})
.
Choose a maximal
\upvarepsilon
-packing in
\upiota(\mathbb{S}^{m-1})
;
that is, an array
(\upzeta^{1},\upzeta^{2},\dots\penalty,\upzeta^{n})
of directions in
\upiota(\mathbb{S}^{m-1})
such that
n=\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\upvarepsilon}\mathbb{S}^{m-1}
and
\measuredangle(\upzeta^{i},\upzeta^{j})>\upvarepsilon
for any
i\neq j
.

Choose an array
(x,z^{1},z^{2},\dots\penalty,z^{n})
of points in
\mathcal{L}
such that

{\uparrow}_{[{p}{x}]}\approx\penalty\upxi
,
{\uparrow}_{[{p}{z^{i}}]}\approx\penalty\upzeta^{i}
;
here we write “
\approx\penalty
” for “sufficiently close”.
We can choose this array so that

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{x}_{z^{i}}\hskip-0.86108pt)>\upvarepsilon
for all
i

and
\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{z^{i}}_{z^{j}}\hskip-0.86108pt)>\upvarepsilon
for all
i\neq j
.
Applying Corollary
13I
, there is a point
p^{\prime}
arbitrarily close to
p

such that all directions
{\uparrow}_{[{p^{\prime}}{x}]}
,
{\uparrow}_{[{p^{\prime}}{z^{1}}]}
,
{\uparrow}_{[{p^{\prime}}{z^{2}}]},\dots\penalty,{\uparrow}_{[{p^{\prime}}{z^{n}}]}

belong to an isometric copy of
\mathbb{S}^{k-1}
in
\Sigma_{p^{\prime}}
.
In addition, we may assume that
\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p^{\prime}\,{}^{x}_{z^{i}}\hskip-0.86108pt)>\upvarepsilon
and
\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p^{\prime}\,{}^{z^{i}}_{z^{j}}\hskip-0.86108pt)>\upvarepsilon
.
From the hinge comparison (
8C
),

\measuredangle({\uparrow}_{[{p^{\prime}}{x}]},{\uparrow}_{[{p^{\prime}}{z^{i}}]})>\upvarepsilon

and
\measuredangle({\uparrow}_{[{p^{\prime}}{z^{i}}]},{\uparrow}_{[{p^{\prime}}{z^{j}}]})>\upvarepsilon
;
that is,

\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\upvarepsilon}\mathbb{S}^{k-1}\geqslant\penalty n+1>\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\upvarepsilon}\mathbb{S}^{m-1}.

Hence
k>m
.
∎

15.8. Proposition.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space. Then
for any two points
p,\bar{p}\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
and any
R,\bar{R}>0
, there is a constant
\updelta=\penalty\updelta(\upkappa,R,\bar{R},|{p}-\penalty{\bar{p}}|)>0
such that

\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\updelta{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon}\mathrm{B}{}(\bar{p},\bar{R})\geqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\upvarepsilon}\mathrm{B}{}(p,R).

Proof.
According to
8F
, we can assume that
\upkappa\leqslant\penalty 0
.

Let
n=\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\upvarepsilon}\mathrm{B}{}(p,R)
and
{\{x^{1},\dots\penalty,x^{n}}\}
be a maximal
\upvarepsilon
-packing in
\mathrm{B}{}(p,R)
;
that is,
|{x^{i}}-\penalty{x^{j}}|>\upvarepsilon
for all
i\neq j
.
Without loss of generality, we may assume the
x^{i}
are in
\mathop{\rm Str}\nolimits(\bar{p})
.
Thus, for each
i
there is a unique geodesic
[\bar{p}x^{i}]
(see
8B
).
Choose a factor
1>s>0
so that
\bar{R}>s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(|{p}-\penalty{\bar{p}}|+R)
.
For each
i
, take
\bar{x}^{i}\in\penalty[\bar{p}x^{i}]
so that

|{\bar{p}}-\penalty{\bar{x}^{i}}|=s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(|{p}-\penalty{x^{i}}|)
.
From
8C
,

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}\left(\bar{p}\,{}^{\bar{x}^{i}}_{\bar{x}^{j}}\hskip-0.86108pt\right){}\geqslant\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(\bar{p}\,{}^{x^{i}}_{x^{j}}\hskip-0.86108pt).

The cosine law gives a constant
\updelta=\penalty\updelta(\upkappa,R,\bar{R},|{p}-\penalty{\bar{p}}|)>0
such that

|{\bar{x}^{i}}-\penalty{\bar{x}^{j}}|>\updelta{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(|{x^{i}}-\penalty{x^{j}}|)>\updelta{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon

for all
i\neq j
.
Hence the statement follows.
∎

15.9. Proposition.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space,

r<\penalty\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}

and
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
.
Assume that

\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\upvarepsilon}\mathrm{B}{}(p,r)>\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\upvarepsilon}\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[r]_{\mathbb{M}^{m}({\upkappa})}

➊

for
\upvarepsilon>0
.
Then there is a G-delta set
A\subset\penalty\mathcal{L}

that is dense in a neighborhood of
p
and
such that
\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits\mathop{\rm Lin}\nolimits_{q}>m
for any
q\in\penalty A
.

Proof.

Choose a maximal
\upvarepsilon
-packing in
\mathrm{B}{}(p,r)
,
that is, an array
(x^{1},x^{2},\dots\penalty,x^{n})
of points in
\mathrm{B}{}(p,r)
such that
n=\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\upvarepsilon}\mathrm{B}{}(p,r)
and
|{x^{i}}-\penalty{x^{j}}|>\upvarepsilon
for any
i\neq j
.
Choose a neighborhood
\Omega\ni p

such that
|{q}-\penalty{x^{i}}|<r
for any
q\in\penalty\Omega
and all
i
.
Let

A=\Omega\cap\penalty\mathop{\rm Str}\nolimits(x^{1},x^{2},\dots\penalty,x^{n}).

According to Theorem
8B
,
A
is a G-delta set that is dense in
\Omega
.

Assume
k=\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits\mathop{\rm Lin}\nolimits_{q}\leqslant\penalty m
for
q\in\penalty A
.
Consider an array
(v^{1},v^{2},\dots\penalty,v^{n})
of vectors in
\mathop{\rm Lin}\nolimits_{q}
,
where
v^{i}=\mathrm{log}[{q}{x^{i}}]
.
Clearly

|v^{i}|=|{q}-\penalty{x^{i}}|<r,

and from the hinge comparison (
8C
)
we have

\mathop{\tilde{\curlyvee}}\nolimits^{\upkappa}[0\,{}^{v^{i}}_{v^{j}}]\geqslant\penalty|{x^{i}}-\penalty{x^{j}}|>\upvarepsilon.

Note that the ball
\mathrm{B}{}(0,r)_{\mathop{\rm Lin}\nolimits_{q}}
equipped with the metric
\uprho(v,w)=\penalty\mathop{\tilde{\curlyvee}}\nolimits^{\upkappa}[0\,{}^{v}_{w}]
is isometric to

\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[r]_{\mathbb{M}^{k}({\upkappa})}
.
Thus

\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\upvarepsilon}\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[r]_{\mathbb{M}^{k}({\upkappa})}\geqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\upvarepsilon}\mathrm{B}{}(p,r),

which contradicts
k\leqslant\penalty m
and
➊
.
∎

The proof of Theorem
15C
is essentially done in
15C
,
15C
,
15C
,
15D
,

15B
;
now we assemble the proof from these parts.

Proof of
15C
.

We will prove the implications

15C

\Rightarrow

15C

\Rightarrow

15C

\Rightarrow

15C

\Rightarrow

15C

\Rightarrow

15C

\Rightarrow

15C
.

The implication
15C
\Rightarrow
15C
is trivial.

15C
\Rightarrow
15C
.

Choose a point
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
such that
\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits\mathop{\rm Lin}\nolimits_{p}\geqslant\penalty m
.
Clearly one can choose an array
(\upxi^{0},\upxi^{1},\dots\penalty,\upxi^{m})
of directions in
\mathop{\rm Lin}\nolimits_{p}
such that
\measuredangle(\upxi^{i},\upxi^{j})>\penalty\tfrac{\uppi}{2}
for all
i\neq j
.
Choose an array
(x^{0},x^{1},\dots\penalty,x^{m})
of points in
\mathcal{L}
such that each
{\uparrow}_{[{p}{x^{i}}]}
is sufficiently close to
\upxi^{i}
;
in particular, we have
\measuredangle[p\,{}^{x^{i}}_{x^{j}}]>\tfrac{\uppi}{2}
.
Choose points
a^{i}\in\penalty\mathopen{]}px^{i}\mathclose{]}
sufficiently close to
p
.
This can be done so that each
\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{a^{i}}_{a^{j}}\hskip-0.86108pt)
is arbitrarily close to
\measuredangle[p\,{}^{a^{i}}_{a^{j}}]
,
in particular
\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{a^{i}}_{a^{j}}\hskip-0.86108pt)>\tfrac{\uppi}{2}
.
Finally, set
b=a^{0}
.

15C
\Rightarrow
15C
.

Let
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
be a point that admits a
\upkappa
-strutting array
(b,a^{1},\dots\penalty,a^{m})

of points in
\mathcal{L}
.
The right-inverse theorem (
15B
)
implies that the distance map
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{}\colon\mathcal{L}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}^{m}
,

\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{}\colon x\mapsto\penalty(|{a^{1}}-\penalty{x}|,|{a^{2}}-\penalty{x}|,\dots\penalty,|{a^{n}}-\penalty{x}|),

is open in a neighborhood of
p
.
Since the distance map
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{}
is Lipschitz,
for any
r>0
, there is
c>0
such that

\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\upvarepsilon}\mathrm{B}{}(p,r)>\frac{c}{\upvarepsilon^{m}}

for any
\upvarepsilon>0
.
Applying
15C
, we get a similar inequality for any other ball in
\mathcal{L}
;
that is, for any
q\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
and
R>0
, there is
c^{\prime}>0
such that

\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\upvarepsilon}\mathrm{B}{}(q,R)>\frac{c^{\prime}}{\upvarepsilon^{m}}.

15C
\Rightarrow
15C
.

Note that for any
q^{\prime}\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
and
R^{\prime}>|{q}-\penalty{q^{\prime}}|+R
we have

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\upvarepsilon}\mathrm{B}{}(q^{\prime},R^{\prime})

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\upvarepsilon}\mathrm{B}{}(q,R)\geqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty\frac{c}{\upvarepsilon^{m}}>

\displaystyle>\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\upvarepsilon}\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[R^{\prime}]_{\mathbb{M}^{m-1}({\upkappa})}

for all sufficiently small
\upvarepsilon>0
.
Applying
15C
,

\mathop{\rm Euk}\nolimits^{m}

contains a G-delta set that is dense in a neighborhood of any point
q^{\prime}\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
.

15C
\Rightarrow
15C
.

Since
\mathop{\rm Euk}\nolimits^{m}
contains a dense G-delta set in
\mathcal{L}
, we can choose
p\in\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(q,R)
with a distance-preserving cone embedding
\upiota\colon\mathbb{E}^{m}\hookrightarrow\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}
.

Repeating the construction in
15C
\Rightarrow
15C
,
we get a
\upkappa
-strutting array
(p,a^{1},\dots\penalty,a^{m})
for
p
.

Applying the right-inverse theorem (
15B
),
we obtain a
C^{\frac{1}{2}}
-submap

\Phi\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(q,R)

that is a right inverse for
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{}\colon\mathcal{L}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}^{m}
and such that
\Phi(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{p})=p
.
In particular,
\Phi
is a
C^{\frac{1}{2}}
-embedding of
\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits\Phi
.

15C
\Rightarrow
15C
.

This proof is valid for general metric spaces;
it is based on general relations between topological dimension, Hausdorff measure and
\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\upvarepsilon}
.

Let
W\subset\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(q,R)
be the image of the embedding
\Phi
.
Since
\mathop{\rm TopDim}\nolimits W=\penalty m
,
Szpilrajn’s theorem (
7A
) implies that

\mathop{\rm HausMes}\nolimits_{m}W>0.

Given
\upvarepsilon>0
, consider a maximal
\upvarepsilon
-packing of
W
,
that is, an array
(x^{1},x^{2},\dots\penalty,x^{n})
of points in
W
such that
n=\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\upvarepsilon}W
and
|{x^{i}}-\penalty{x^{j}}|>\upvarepsilon
for all
i\neq j
.
Note that
W
is covered by balls
\mathrm{B}{}(x^{i},2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon)
.

By the definition of Hausdorff measure,

\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\upvarepsilon}W\geqslant\penalty\frac{c}{\upvarepsilon^{m}}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathop{\rm HausMes}\nolimits_{m}W

for a fixed constant
c>0
and all small
\upvarepsilon>0
.
Hence
15C
follows.

∎

D
Inverse function theorem

15.10. Inverse function theorem.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be an
m
-dimensional complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space
and
p,b,a^{1},a^{2},\dots\penalty,a^{m}\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
.

Assume that the point array
\bm{a}=(b,a^{1},\dots\penalty,a^{m})
is
\upkappa
-strutting for
p
.
Then
there are
R>0
and
\upvarepsilon>0
such that:

(a)

For all
i\neq j
and any
q\in\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(p,R)
we have

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(q\,{}^{a^{i}}_{a^{j}}\hskip-0.86108pt)>\tfrac{\uppi}{2}+\upvarepsilon\quad\text{and}\quad\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(q\,{}^{b}_{a^{i}}\hskip-0.86108pt)>\tfrac{\uppi}{2}.

(b)

The restriction of the distance map

\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{}\colon x\mapsto\penalty(|{a^{1}}-\penalty{x}|,\dots\penalty,|{a^{m}}-\penalty{x}|)

to the ball
\mathrm{B}{}(p,R)

is an open
[\upvarepsilon,\sqrt{m}]
-bi-Lipschitz embedding
\mathrm{B}{}(p,R)\hookrightarrow\penalty\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

(c)

The value
R
depends only on
\upkappa
,
|{p}-\penalty{a^{i}}|
,
|{a^{i}}-\penalty{a^{j}}|
, and
|{b}-\penalty{a^{i}}|

for all
i
and
j
.

15.11. Definition.

Suppose
\mathcal{L}
is an
m
-dimensional complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space.
If a point array
(b,a^{1},a^{2},\dots\penalty,a^{m})

and the value
R
satisfy the conditions in Theorem
15D
,
then the restriction

\bm{x}=\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{}|_{\mathrm{B}{}(p,R)}

is called a
distance chart
,
the restrictions
x^{i}=\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{a^{i}}{}{}|_{\mathrm{B}{}(p,R)}
are called
coordinates
,
and the restriction
y=\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{b}{}{}|_{\mathrm{B}{}(p,R)}
is called the
strut
of the distance chart.

15.12. Lemma.

Let
p
be a point in an
m
-dimensional complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space
\mathcal{L}
.
Assume for the directions
\upxi,\upzeta^{1},\upzeta^{2},\dots\penalty,\upzeta^{k}\in\penalty\Sigma_{p}
the following conditions hold:

(a)

\measuredangle(\upxi,\upzeta^{i})>\tfrac{\uppi}{2}-\upvarepsilon
for all
i
,

(b)

\measuredangle(\upzeta^{i},\upzeta^{j})>\tfrac{\uppi}{2}+\upvarepsilon
for all
i\neq j
.

Then
k\leqslant\penalty m
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that all
\upxi,\upzeta^{1},\upzeta^{2},\dots\penalty,\upzeta^{k}
are geodesic directions;
let
\upxi={\uparrow}_{[{p}{x}]}
and
\upzeta^{i}={\uparrow}_{[{p}{z^{i}}]}
for all
i
.
Fix a small
r>0
, and
let
\bar{x}\in\penalty\mathopen{]}px]
and
\bar{z}^{i}\in\penalty\mathopen{]}pz^{i}]
be points
such that

|{p}-\penalty{\bar{x}}|=|{p}-\penalty{\bar{z}^{1}}|=\dots\penalty=|{p}-\penalty{\bar{z}^{k}}|=r.

From the definition of angle,
if
r
is sufficiently small we have

\diamond

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{\bar{x}}_{\bar{z}^{i}}\hskip-0.86108pt)>\tfrac{\uppi}{2}-\upvarepsilon
for all
i
,
and
\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{\bar{z}^{i}}_{\bar{z}^{j}}\hskip-0.86108pt)>\tfrac{\uppi}{2}+\upvarepsilon
for all
i\neq j
.

Choose a point
p^{\prime}\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Str}\nolimits(\bar{x},\bar{z}^{1},\bar{z}^{2},\dots\penalty,\bar{z}^{k})
sufficiently close to
p
that the above conditions still hold for
p^{\prime}
; that is,

➊

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p^{\prime}\,{}^{\bar{x}}_{\bar{z}^{i}}\hskip-0.86108pt)>\tfrac{\uppi}{2}-\upvarepsilon
for all
i
, and
\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p^{\prime}\,{}^{\bar{z}^{i}}_{\bar{z}^{j}}\hskip-0.86108pt)>\tfrac{\uppi}{2}+\upvarepsilon
for all
i\neq j
.

Set
\acute{\upxi}={\uparrow}_{[{p^{\prime}}{\bar{x}}]}
and
\acute{\upzeta}^{i}={\uparrow}_{[{p^{\prime}}{\bar{z}^{i}}]}
for each
i
.
By the hinge comparison (
8C
),

➋

\measuredangle(\acute{\upxi},\acute{\upzeta}^{i})>\tfrac{\uppi}{2}-\upvarepsilon
for all
i
, and
\measuredangle(\acute{\upzeta}^{i},\acute{\upzeta}^{j})>\tfrac{\uppi}{2}+\upvarepsilon
for all
i\neq j
.

According to Corollary
13I
, all directions
\acute{\upxi},\acute{\upzeta}^{1},\acute{\upzeta}^{2},\dots\penalty,\acute{\upzeta}^{k}
lie in an isometric copy of the standard
n
-sphere in
\Sigma_{p^{\prime}}
. Clearly
n\leqslant\penalty m-1
.
Thus it remains to prove the following claim, which is a partial case of the lemma.

➌

If
\upxi,\upzeta^{1},\upzeta^{2},\dots\penalty,\upzeta^{k}\in\penalty\mathbb{S}^{m-1}
,

|{\upxi}-\penalty{\upzeta^{i}}|>\tfrac{\uppi}{2}-\upvarepsilon
for all
i
, and

|{\upzeta^{i}}-\penalty{\upzeta^{j}}|>\tfrac{\uppi}{2}+\upvarepsilon
for all
i\neq j
,
then
k\leqslant\penalty m
.

For each
i
,
let

\bar{\upzeta}^{i}

be the closest point to
\upzeta^{i}

in

\Xi=\mathbb{S}^{m-1}\setminus\mathrm{B}{}(\upxi,\tfrac{\uppi}{2})\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it iso}}}\penalty\mathbb{S}^{m-1}_{+}

(if
\upzeta\in\penalty\Xi
, then
\bar{\upzeta}^{i}=\upzeta^{i}
).
By straightforward calculations, we have

|{\bar{\upzeta}^{i}}-\penalty{\bar{\upzeta}^{j}}|\geqslant\penalty|{\upzeta^{i}}-\penalty{\upzeta^{j}}|-\upvarepsilon>\tfrac{\uppi}{2}.

Thus it is sufficient to show the following claim:

➍

\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\frac{\uppi}{2}}\mathbb{S}^{m-1}_{+}=m.

Clearly,
\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\frac{\uppi}{2}}\mathbb{S}^{m-1}_{+}\geqslant\penalty m
.

The opposite inequality is proved by induction on
m
.
The base case
m=1
is obvious.
Assume
(\bar{\upzeta}^{1},\bar{\upzeta}^{2},\dots\penalty,\bar{\upzeta}^{k})
is an array of points in
\mathbb{S}^{m-1}_{+}
with
|{\bar{\upzeta}^{i}}-\penalty{\bar{\upzeta}^{j}}|>\tfrac{\uppi}{2}
.
Without loss of generality, we can also assume that
\bar{\upzeta}^{k}\in\penalty\partial\mathbb{S}^{m-1}_{+}
.
For each
i<k
,
let
\check{\upzeta}^{i}={\uparrow}_{[{\bar{\upzeta}^{k}}{\bar{\upzeta}^{i}}]}\in\penalty\Sigma_{\bar{\upzeta}^{k}}\mathbb{S}^{m-1}_{+}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it iso}}}\penalty\mathbb{S}^{m-2}_{+}
.
By the hinge comparison (
8C
),
\measuredangle(\check{\upzeta}^{i},\check{\upzeta}^{j})>\tfrac{\uppi}{2}

for all
i<j<k
.
Thus from the induction hypothesis we have
k-1\leqslant\penalty{m-1}
.
∎

Proof of
15D
;
15D
.

Fix
\upvarepsilon>0
such that

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{a^{i}}_{a^{j}}\hskip-0.86108pt)>\penalty\tfrac{\uppi}{2}+\upvarepsilon
and
\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{b}_{a^{i}}\hskip-0.86108pt)>\penalty\tfrac{\uppi}{2}+\upvarepsilon
for all
i\neq j
.
Choose
R>0
sufficiently small that

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(q\,{}^{a^{i}}_{a^{j}}\hskip-0.86108pt)>\tfrac{\uppi}{2}+\upvarepsilon
and
\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(q\,{}^{b}_{a^{i}}\hskip-0.86108pt)>\tfrac{\uppi}{2}+\upvarepsilon
for all
i\neq j
and any
q\in\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(p,R)
.
Clearly,
15D
holds for
\mathrm{B}{}(p,R)
.

15D
.

Note that the distance map
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{}
is Lipschitz
and its restriction
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\bm{a}}{}{}|_{\mathrm{B}{}(p,R)}
is open;
the latter follows from the right-inverse theorem (
15B
).
Thus to prove
15D
, it is sufficient to show that

\max_{i}\left\{\,\bigl||{a^{i}}-\penalty{x}|-|{a^{i}}-\penalty{y}|\bigr|\,\right\}>\tfrac{\upvarepsilon}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{x}-\penalty{y}|

➎

for any
x,y\in\penalty{\mathrm{B}{}(p,R)}
.

According to Lemma
15D
,

\measuredangle[x\,{}^{y}_{b}]\leqslant\penalty\tfrac{\uppi}{2}-\upvarepsilon\quad\text{or}\quad\measuredangle[x\,{}^{y}_{a^{i}}]\leqslant\penalty\tfrac{\uppi}{2}-\upvarepsilon\quad\text{for some}\quad i.

In the latter case,
since
|{x}-\penalty{y}|<2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}R
and
R
is small,
the hinge comparison (
8C
) implies

|{a^{i}}-\penalty{x}|-|{a^{i}}-\penalty{y}|>\tfrac{\upvarepsilon}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{x}-\penalty{y}|\quad\text{for some}\quad i.

➏

If
\measuredangle[x\,{}^{y}_{b}]\leqslant\penalty\tfrac{\uppi}{2}-\upvarepsilon
, then
switching
x
and
y
, we get

|{a^{j}}-\penalty{y}|-|{a^{j}}-\penalty{x}|>\tfrac{\upvarepsilon}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{x}-\penalty{y}|\quad\text{for some}\quad j.

➐

Then
➏
and
➐
imply
➎
.

Finally, part
15D

follows since the angle
\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(q\,{}^{a^{i}}_{a^{j}}\hskip-0.86108pt)

depends continuously on
\upkappa
,
|{q}-\penalty{a^{i}}|
,
|{q}-\penalty{a^{j}}|
and
|{a^{i}}-\penalty{a^{j}}|
.
∎

E
Finite-dimensional spaces

The next theorem is a refinement of
15C
for the finite-dimensional case;
it was essentially proved by Yuriy Burago, Grigory Perelman, and Michael Gromov
[
43
]
.

15.13. Theorem.

Suppose
\mathcal{L}
is a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space,

m
is a nonnegative integer,

0<R\leqslant\penalty\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
, and

q\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
.
Then the following statements are equivalent:

(a)

\mathop{\rm LinDim}\nolimits\mathcal{L}=m
.

(b)

m
is the maximal integer such that there is a point
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
that admits a
\upkappa
-strutting array
(b,a^{1},\dots\penalty,a^{m})
.

(c)

\text{\rm T}_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it iso}}}\penalty\mathbb{E}^{m}
for any point
p
in a dense G-delta set of
\mathcal{L}
.

(d)

There is an open bi-Lipschitz embedding

\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[1]_{\mathbb{E}^{m}}\hookrightarrow\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(q,R)\subset\penalty\mathcal{L}.

(e)

For any
\upvarepsilon>0
,

\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\upvarepsilon}\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[R]_{\mathbb{M}^{m}({\upkappa})}\geqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\upvarepsilon}\mathrm{B}{}(q,R).

moreover, there is
c=c(q,R)>0
such that

\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\upvarepsilon}\mathrm{B}{}(q,R)>\frac{c}{\upvarepsilon^{m}}.

Using theorems
15C
and
15E
,
one can show that linear dimension is equal to many different types of dimension, such
as
small
and
big inductive dimension

and
upper
and
lower box-counting dimension

(also known as
Minkowski dimension
),

homological dimension
and so on.

The next two corollaries follow from
15E
.

15.14. Corollary.

Any finite-dimensional complete length
CBB
space is proper and geodesic.

15.15. Corollary.

Let
(\mathcal{L}_{n})
be a sequence of length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
spaces and
\mathcal{L}_{n}\to\penalty\mathcal{L}_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.
Assume
\mathop{\rm LinDim}\nolimits L_{n}\leqslant\penalty m
for all
n
.
Then
\mathop{\rm LinDim}\nolimits L_{\text{\rm}}\leqslant\penalty m
.

15.16. Corollary.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space.
Then for any open
\Omega\subset\penalty\mathcal{L}
, we have

\mathop{\rm LinDim}\nolimits\mathcal{L}=\mathop{\rm LinDim}\nolimits\Omega=\mathop{\rm TopDim}\nolimits\Omega=\mathop{\rm HausDim}\nolimits\Omega,

where
\mathop{\rm TopDim}\nolimits
and
\mathop{\rm HausDim}\nolimits
denote topological dimension (
7A
) and Hausdorff dimension (
7A
) respectively.

In particular,
\mathcal{L}
is dimension-homogeneous; that is, all open sets have the same linear dimension.

Proof of
15E
.

The equality

\mathop{\rm LinDim}\nolimits\mathcal{L}=\mathop{\rm LinDim}\nolimits\Omega

follows from
15C
\&
15C
.

If
\mathop{\rm LinDim}\nolimits\mathcal{L}=\infty
, then
applying
15C
for
\mathrm{B}{}(q,R)\subset\penalty\Omega
, we find that there is a compact subset
K\subset\penalty\Omega
having an arbitrarily large
\mathop{\rm TopDim}\nolimits K
. Therefore

\mathop{\rm TopDim}\nolimits\Omega=\infty.

By Szpilrajn’s theorem (
7A
),

\mathop{\rm HausDim}\nolimits K\geqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm TopDim}\nolimits K
.
Thus we also have

\mathop{\rm HausDim}\nolimits\Omega=\infty.

If
\mathop{\rm LinDim}\nolimits\mathcal{L}=m<\infty
, then the first inequality in
15E

implies that

\mathop{\rm HausDim}\nolimits\mathrm{B}{}(q,R)\leqslant\penalty m.

According to Corollary
15E
,

\mathcal{L}
is proper and in particular has countable base.
Thus applying Szpilrajn’s theorem again, we have

\mathop{\rm TopDim}\nolimits\Omega\leqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm HausDim}\nolimits\Omega\leqslant\penalty m.

Finally,
15E
implies that
m\leqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm TopDim}\nolimits\Omega
.
∎

Proof of
15E
.

The equivalence
15E
\Leftrightarrow
15E
follows from
15C
.

15E
\Rightarrow
15E
.

If
\mathop{\rm LinDim}\nolimits\mathcal{L}=m
, then by Theorem
15C
,

\mathop{\rm Euk}\nolimits^{m}
contains a dense G-delta set in
\mathcal{L}
.
From
15C
, it follows that
\text{\rm T}_{p}
is isometric to
\mathbb{E}^{m}
for any
p\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Euk}\nolimits^{m}
.

15E
\Rightarrow
15E
.

This is proved in exactly the same way as implication
15C
\Rightarrow
15C
of theorem
15C
,
but applying the existence of a distance chart (
15D
)
instead of the right-inverse theorem (
15B
).

15E
\Rightarrow
15E
.

From
15E
, it follows that there is a point
p\in\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(q,R)
and
r>0
such that

\mathrm{B}{}(p,r)\subset\penalty\mathcal{L}
is bi-Lipschitz homeomorphic to a bounded open set of
\mathbb{E}^{m}
.
Thus there is
c>0
such that

\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\upvarepsilon}\mathrm{B}{}(p,r)>\frac{c}{\upvarepsilon^{m}}.

➊

Applying
15C
shows that inequality
➊
, with different constants, holds for any other ball, in particular for
\mathrm{B}{}(q,R)
.

Applying
15C
gives the first inequality in
15E
.

15E
\Rightarrow
15E
.

From Theorem
15C
, we have
\mathop{\rm LinDim}\nolimits\mathcal{L}\geqslant\penalty m
.
Applying Theorem
15C
again, if
\mathop{\rm LinDim}\nolimits\mathcal{L}\geqslant\penalty m+1
then for some
c>0
and any
\upvarepsilon>0
,

\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\upvarepsilon}\mathrm{B}{}(q,R)\geqslant\penalty\frac{c}{\upvarepsilon^{m+1}}.

But

\frac{c^{\prime}}{\upvarepsilon^{m}}\geqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm pack}\nolimits_{\upvarepsilon}\mathrm{B}{}(q,R)

for any
\upvarepsilon>0
,
a contradiction.
∎

15.17. Exercise.

Suppose
\mathcal{L}
is a complete length
CBB
space and
\Sigma_{p}\mathcal{L}
is compact for any
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
.
Prove that
\mathcal{L}
is finite-dimensional.

F
One-dimensional spaces

15.18. Theorem.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a one-dimensional complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space.
Then
\mathcal{L}
is isometric to a connected complete Riemannian one-dimensional manifold with possibly non-empty boundary.

Proof.

Clearly
\mathcal{L}
is connected.
It remains to show the following:

➊

For any point
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}

there is
\upvarepsilon>0
such that
\mathrm{B}{}(p,\upvarepsilon)

is isometric to either
[0,\upvarepsilon)
or
(-\upvarepsilon,\upvarepsilon)
.

First let us show:

➋

If
p\in\penalty\mathopen{]}xy\mathclose{[}
for
x
,
y\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
and
\upvarepsilon<\min\{|{p}-\penalty{x}|,|{p}-\penalty{y}|\},

then
\mathrm{B}{}(p,\upvarepsilon)\subset\penalty\mathopen{]}xy\mathclose{[}
.
In particular,

\mathrm{B}{}(p,\upvarepsilon)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it iso}}}\penalty(-\upvarepsilon,\upvarepsilon)
.

Assume the contrary;
that is, there is

z\in\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(p,\upvarepsilon)\setminus\mathopen{]}xy\mathclose{[}.

Consider a geodesic
[pz]
, and let
q\in\penalty[pz]\cap\penalty[xy]
be the point that maximizes the distance
|{p}-\penalty{q}|
.
At
q
, we have three distinct directions:
to
x
,
y
, and
z
.
Moreover,
\measuredangle[q\,{}^{x}_{y}]=\uppi
.
Thus, according to Proposition
15C
,

\mathop{\rm LinDim}\nolimits\mathcal{L}>1
, a contradiction.

\vartriangle

Now we assume no geodesic includes
p
as a non endpoint.
Since
\mathop{\rm LinDim}\nolimits\mathcal{L}=\penalty 1
there is a point
y\neq p
.

Fix a positive value
\upvarepsilon<|{p}-\penalty{y}|
.
Let us show:

➌

\mathrm{B}{}(p,\upvarepsilon)\subset\penalty[py]
;
in particular,
\mathrm{B}{}(p,\upvarepsilon)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it iso}}}\penalty[0,\upvarepsilon)
.

Assume the contrary;
let
z\in\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(p,\upvarepsilon)\setminus[py]
.

Choose a point
w\in\penalty\mathopen{]}py\mathclose{[}
such that

|{p}-\penalty{w}|+|{p}-\penalty{z}|<\upvarepsilon.

Consider geodesic
[wz]
, and let
q\in\penalty[py]\cap\penalty[wz]
be the point that maximizes the distance
|{w}-\penalty{q}|
.
Since no geodesic includes
p
as a non endpoint, we have
p\neq q
.
As above,
\measuredangle[q\,{}^{p}_{y}]=\uppi

and
{\uparrow}_{[{q}{z}]}
is distinct from
{\uparrow}_{[{q}{p}]}
and
{\uparrow}_{[{q}{p}]}
.
Thus, according to Proposition
15C
,

\mathop{\rm LinDim}\nolimits\mathcal{L}>1
, a contradiction.

\vartriangle

Clearly
\text{\ref{clm:1-dim-mid}}+\text{\ref{clm:1-dim-end}}\Rightarrow\text{\ref{clm:1-dim-all}}
;
hence the result.
∎

Chapter 16
Gradient flow

Gradient flow could be considered as a nonsmooth version of first-order ordinary differential equations.
It provides a universal tool in Alexandrov geometry with most significant applications to
CBB
spaces.

The theory of gradient flows of semiconvex functions on Hilbert spaces (which are of course both
\text{\rm CBB}({0}){}
and
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
) is classical, see for example
[
33
]
.

The technique of gradient flows in the context of comparison geometry takes its roots in
Sharafutdinov’s retraction
,
introduced by Vladimir Sharafutdinov
[
146
]
.
It has been used widely in comparison geometry
since then.
In
CBB
spaces, it was first used by Grigory Perelman and the third author
[
122
,
128
]
.
A bit later, independently Jürgen Jost and Uwe Mayer
[
87
,
113
]

used the gradient flow in
CAT
spaces.
Later, Alexander Lytchak unified and generalized these two approaches
to a wide class of metric spaces
[
109
]
.
It was developed yet further by Shin-ichi Ohta
[
120
]
and by Giuseppe Sevaré
[
145
]
.
It is based on the more analytic approach suitable for the study of synthetic spaces with lower Ricci bounds was developed by Luigi Ambrosio, Nicola Gigli and Giuseppe Sevaré in a general metric and metric measure setting

[
19
]
.

The following exercise is a stripped-down version of Sharfutdinov’s retraction;
it gives the idea behind gradient flow.

16.1. Exercise.

Assume that a one-parameter family of convex sets
K_{t}\subset\penalty\mathbb{E}^{m}
is nested;
that is,
K_{t_{1}}\supset\penalty K_{t_{2}}
if
t_{1}\leqslant\penalty t_{2}
.
Show that there is a family of short maps
\upvarphi_{t}\colon\mathbb{E}^{m}\to\penalty K_{t}

such that
\upvarphi_{t}|_{K_{t}}=\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits
for any
t
and
\upvarphi_{t_{2}}\circ\penalty\upvarphi_{t_{1}}=\upvarphi_{t_{2}}
if
t_{1}\leqslant\penalty t_{2}
.

A
Gradient-like curves

Gradient-like curves will be used later in the construction of gradient curves.
The latter are a special reparametrization of gradient-like curves.

16.2. Definition.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a complete length space
and
f\colon\mathcal{Z}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
be locally Lipschitz semiconcave subfunction.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.

A Lipschitz curve
\hat{\upalpha}\colon[s_{\min},s_{\max})\to\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
will be called an
f
-gradient-like curve
if

\hat{\upalpha}^{+}=\tfrac{1}{|\nabla_{\hat{\upalpha}}f|}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nabla_{\hat{\upalpha}}f;

that is, for any
s\in\penalty[s_{\min},s_{\max})
, the right derivative
\hat{\upalpha}^{+}(s)
is defined and

\hat{\upalpha}^{+}(s)=\tfrac{1}{|\nabla_{\hat{\upalpha}(s)}f|}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nabla_{\hat{\upalpha}(s)}f.

Note that this definition implies that
|\nabla_{p}f|>0
for any point
p
on
\hat{\upalpha}
.

The following theorem gives a seemingly weaker condition that is equivalent to the definition of gradient-like curve.

16.3. Theorem.

Suppose
\mathcal{Z}
is a complete length space,

f\colon\mathcal{Z}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
is a locally Lipschitz semiconcave subfunction,
and

|\nabla_{p}f|>0
for any
p\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
.
Assume that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.

A curve
\hat{\upalpha}\colon[s_{\min},s_{\max})\to\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
is an
f
-gradient-like curve if and only if it is
1
-Lipschitz and

\varliminf_{s\to\penalty s_{0}+}\frac{f\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}(s)-f\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}(s_{0})}{s-s_{0}}\geqslant\penalty|\nabla_{\hat{\upalpha}(s_{0})}f|

➊

for almost all
s_{0}\in\penalty[s_{\min},s_{\max})
.

Proof.
The only-if part follows directly from the definition.
To prove the if part, note that for any
s_{0}\in\penalty[s_{\min},s_{\max})
we have

\displaystyle\varliminf_{s\to\penalty s_{0}+}\frac{f\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}(s)-f\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}(s_{0})}{s-s_{0}}

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty\varliminf_{s\to\penalty s_{0}+}\frac{1}{s-s_{0}}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\int\limits_{s_{0}}^{s}|\nabla_{\hat{\upalpha}(\textit{\c{s}})}f|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\bm{d}\textit{\c{s}}\geqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty|\nabla_{\hat{\upalpha}(s_{0})}f|;

the first inequality follows from
➊

and the second from lower semicontinuity of the function
x\mapsto\penalty|\nabla_{x}f|
,
see
13G
.
From
13F
, we have

\hat{\upalpha}^{+}(s_{0})=\tfrac{1}{|\nabla_{\hat{\upalpha}(s_{0})}f|}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nabla_{\hat{\upalpha}(s_{0})}f.

Hence the result.
∎

Recall that second-order differential inequalities are understood in a barrier sense; see Section
3E
.

16.4. Theorem.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a complete length space
and

f\colon\mathcal{Z}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
be
locally Lipschitz and
\uplambda
-concave.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.
Assume
\hat{\upalpha}\colon[0,s_{\max})\to\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
is an
f
-gradient-like curve.
Then

(f\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha})^{\prime\prime}\leqslant\penalty\uplambda

everywhere on
[0,s_{\max})
.

Closely related statements were proved independently by Uwe Mayer
[
113
, 2.36]
and Shin-ichi Ohta
[
120
, 5.7]
.

Before the proof, let us formulate and prove a corollary.

16.5. Corollary.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a complete length space,

f\colon\mathcal{Z}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a locally Lipschitz and semiconcave function,
and
\hat{\upalpha}\colon[0,s_{\max})\to\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
be an
f
-gradient-like curve.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.
Then the function
s\mapsto\penalty|\nabla_{\hat{\upalpha}(s)}f|

is right-continuous;
that is, for any
s_{0}\in\penalty[0,s_{\max})
we have

|\nabla_{\hat{\upalpha}(s_{0})}f|=\lim_{s\to\penalty s_{0}+}|\nabla_{\hat{\upalpha}(s)}f|.

Proof.
Applying
16A
locally, we have that
f\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}(s)
is semiconcave.
The statement follows since

(f\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha})^{+}(s)=(\bm{d}_{p}f)\left(\tfrac{1}{|\nabla_{\hat{\upalpha}(s)}f|}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nabla_{\hat{\upalpha}(s)}f\right)=|\nabla_{\hat{\upalpha}(s)}f|.

∎

Proof of
16A
.
For any
s>s_{0}
,

\displaystyle(f\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha})^{+}(s_{0})

\displaystyle=|\nabla_{\hat{\upalpha}(s_{0})}f|\geqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty(d_{\hat{\upalpha}(s_{0})}f)({\uparrow}_{[{\hat{\upalpha}(s_{0})}{\hat{\upalpha}(s)}]})\geqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty\frac{f\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}(s)-f\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}(s_{0})}{|{\hat{\upalpha}(s)}-\penalty{\hat{\upalpha}(s_{0})}|}-\tfrac{\uplambda}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{\hat{\upalpha}(s)}-\penalty{\hat{\upalpha}(s_{0})}|.

Let
\uplambda_{+}=\max\{0,\uplambda\}
.
Since
s-s_{0}\geqslant\penalty|{\hat{\upalpha}(s)}-\penalty{\hat{\upalpha}(s_{0})}|
, for any
s>s_{0}
we have

(f\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha})^{+}(s_{0})\geqslant\penalty\frac{f\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}(s)-f\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}(s_{0})}{s-s_{0}}-\tfrac{\uplambda_{+}}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(s-s_{0}).

➋

Thus
f\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}
is
\uplambda_{+}
-concave.
That finishes the proof for
\uplambda\geqslant\penalty 0
.
For
\uplambda<0
we get only that
f\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}
is
0
-concave.

Note that
|{\hat{\upalpha}(s)}-\penalty{\hat{\upalpha}(s_{0})}|=s-s_{0}-o(s-s_{0})
. Thus

(f\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha})^{+}(s_{0})\geqslant\penalty\frac{f\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}(s)-f\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}(s_{0})}{s-s_{0}}-\tfrac{\uplambda}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(s-s_{0})+o(s-s_{0}).

➌

Together,
➋
and
➌
imply that
f\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}
is
\uplambda
-concave.
∎

16.6. Proposition.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space,
p,q\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
.
Assume
\hat{\upalpha}\colon[s_{\min},s_{\max})\to\penalty\mathcal{L}
is a
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}
-gradient-like curve such that
\hat{\upalpha}(s)\to\penalty z\in\penalty\mathopen{]}pq\mathclose{[}
as
s\to\penalty s_{\max}+
.
Then
\hat{\upalpha}
is a unit-speed geodesic that lies in
[pq]
.

Proof.

Clearly,

\tfrac{d^{+}}{dt}|{q}-\penalty{\hat{\upalpha}(t)}|\geqslant\penalty-1.

➍

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\tfrac{d^{+}}{dt}|{p}-\penalty{\hat{\upalpha}(t)}|

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty(\bm{d}_{\hat{\upalpha}(t)}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}{}{})({\uparrow}_{[{\hat{\upalpha}(t)}{q}]})\geqslant\penalty

➎

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty-\cos\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(\hat{\upalpha}(t)\,{}^{p}_{q}\hskip-0.86108pt).

Inequalities
➍
and
➎
imply that the function
t\mapsto\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(q\,{}^{\hat{\upalpha}(t)}_{p}\hskip-0.86108pt)
is nondecreasing.
Hence the result.
∎

B
Gradient curves

In this section we define gradient curves
and tie them tightly to gradient-like curves
which were introduced in Section
16A
.

16.7. Definition.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a complete length space
and
f\colon\mathcal{Z}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a locally Lipschitz and semiconcave subfunction.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.

A locally Lipschitz curve
\upalpha\colon[t_{\min},t_{\max})\to\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
will be called an
f
-gradient curve
if

\upalpha^{+}=\nabla_{\upalpha}f;

that is, for any
t\in\penalty[t_{\min},t_{\max})
,
\upalpha^{+}(t)
is defined and

\upalpha^{+}(t)=\nabla_{\upalpha(t)}f
.

The following exercise describes a global geometric property of a gradient curve without direct reference to its function.
It uses the notion of
self-contracting curves
introduced by Aris Daniilidis, Olivier Ley, Stéphane Sabourau
[
55
]
.

16.8. Exercise.

Let

\mathcal{Z}
be a complete length space,

f\colon\mathcal{Z}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
a concave locally Lipschitz function,
and
\upalpha\colon\mathbb{I}\to\penalty\mathcal{Z}
an
f
-gradient curve.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.

Show that
\upalpha
is
self-contracting
; that is,

t_{1}\leqslant\penalty t_{2}\leqslant\penalty t_{3}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad|{\upalpha(t_{1})}-\penalty{\upalpha(t_{3})}|_{\mathcal{Z}}\geqslant\penalty|{\upalpha(t_{2})}-\penalty{\upalpha(t_{3})}|_{\mathcal{Z}}.

The next lemma states that gradient and gradient-like curves are special reparametrizations of each other.

16.9. Lemma.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a complete length space
and

f\colon\mathcal{Z}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a locally Lipschitz semiconcave subfunction
such that
|\nabla_{p}f|>0
for any
p\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.

Assume that
\upalpha\colon[0,t_{\max})\to\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
is a locally Lipschitz curve
and
\hat{\upalpha}\colon[0,s_{\max})\to\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
is its reparametrization by arc-length,
so
\upalpha=\penalty\hat{\upalpha}\circ\penalty\upsigma
for a homeomorphism
\upsigma\colon[0,t_{\max})\to\penalty[0,s_{\max})
.
Then

\displaystyle\upalpha^{+}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\upalpha}f

\displaystyle\Updownarrow

\displaystyle\hat{\upalpha}^{+}=\frac{1}{|\nabla_{\hat{\upalpha}}f|}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nabla_{\hat{\upalpha}}f\quad

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\upsigma^{-1}(s)=\int\limits_{0}^{s}\frac{\bm{d}\textit{\c{s}}}{(f\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha})^{\prime}(\textit{\c{s}})}.

Proof;
(\Downarrow)
.

According to
3D
,

\displaystyle\upsigma^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it a.e.}}}|\upalpha^{+}(t)|=

➊

\displaystyle=|\nabla_{\upalpha(t)}f|.

Note that

\displaystyle(f\circ\penalty\upalpha)^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it a.e.}}}(f\circ\penalty\upalpha)^{+}(t)=

\displaystyle=|\nabla_{\upalpha(t)}f|^{2}.

Setting
s=\upsigma(t)
, we have

\displaystyle(f\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha})^{\prime}(s)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it a.e.}}}\frac{(f\circ\penalty\upalpha)^{\prime}(t)}{\upsigma^{\prime}(t)}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it a.e.}}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it a.e.}}}|\nabla_{\upalpha(t)}f|=

\displaystyle=|\nabla_{\hat{\upalpha}(s)}f|.

From
16A
, it follows that
\hat{\upalpha}(t)
is an
f
-gradient-like curve;
that is,

\hat{\upalpha}^{+}=\frac{1}{|\nabla_{\hat{\upalpha}}f|}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nabla_{\hat{\upalpha}}f.

In particular,
(f\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha})^{+}(s)=|\nabla_{\hat{\upalpha}^{+}(s)}f|
, and by
➊
,

\displaystyle\upsigma^{-1}(s)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{0}^{s}\frac{\bm{d}\textit{\c{s}}}{|\nabla_{\hat{\upalpha}(\textit{\c{s}})}f|}=

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{0}^{s}\frac{\bm{d}\textit{\c{s}}}{(f\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha})^{\prime}(\textit{\c{s}})}.

(\Uparrow)
.
Clearly,

\displaystyle\upsigma(t)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{0}^{t}(f\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha})^{+}(\upsigma(\textit{\c{t}})){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\bm{d}\textit{\c{t}}=

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{0}^{t}|\nabla_{\upalpha(\textit{\c{t}})}f|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\bm{d}\textit{\c{t}}.

According to
16A
, the function
s\mapsto\penalty|\nabla_{\hat{\upalpha}(s)}f|
is right-continuous.
Therefore so is the function
t\mapsto\penalty|\nabla_{\hat{\upalpha}\circ\penalty\upsigma(t)}f|=|\nabla_{\upalpha(t)}f|
.
Hence, for any
t_{0}\in\penalty[0,t_{\max})
we have

\displaystyle\upsigma^{+}(t_{0})

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to\penalty t_{0}+}\frac{1}{t-t_{0}}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\int\limits_{t_{0}}^{t}|\nabla_{\upalpha(\textit{\c{t}})}f|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\bm{d}\textit{\c{t}}=

\displaystyle=|\nabla_{\upalpha(t_{0})}f|.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\upalpha^{+}(t_{0})

\displaystyle=\upsigma^{+}(t_{0}){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\hat{\upalpha}^{+}(\upsigma(t_{0}))=

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\upalpha(t_{0})}f.

∎

16.10. Exercise.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a complete length space, and

f\colon\mathcal{Z}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a semiconcave locally Lipschitz
function.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.
Assume
\upalpha\colon\mathbb{I}\to\penalty\mathcal{Z}
is a Lipschitz curve such that

\displaystyle\upalpha^{+}(t)

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty|\nabla_{\upalpha(t)}f|,

\displaystyle(f\circ\penalty\upalpha)^{+}(t)

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty|\nabla_{\upalpha(t)}f|^{2}

for almost all
t
.
Show that
\upalpha
is an
f
-gradient curve.

16.11. Exercise.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a complete length space and
f\colon\mathcal{Z}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a concave locally Lipschitz function.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.
Show that
\upalpha\colon\mathbb{R}\to\penalty\mathcal{Z}
is an
f
-gradient curve if and only if

|{x}-\penalty{\upalpha(t_{1})}|_{\mathcal{Z}}^{2}-|{x}-\penalty{\upalpha(t_{0})}|_{\mathcal{Z}}^{2}\leqslant\penalty 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(t_{1}-t_{0}){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(f\circ\penalty\upalpha(t_{1})-f(x))

for any
t_{1}>t_{0}
and
x\in\penalty\mathcal{Z}
.

C
Distance estimates

16.12. First distance estimate.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a complete length space, and

f\colon\mathcal{Z}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a locally Lipschitz

\uplambda
-concave function.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.
Let
\upalpha,\upbeta\colon[0,t_{\max})\to\penalty\mathcal{Z}
be two
f
-gradient curves.
Then

|{\upalpha(t)}-\penalty{\upbeta(t)}|\leqslant\penalty e^{\uplambda{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}t}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{\upalpha(0)}-\penalty{\upbeta(0)}|

for any
t
.

Moreover, the statement holds for a locally Lipschitz
\uplambda
-concave subfunction
f\colon\mathcal{Z}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
if there is a geodesic
[\upalpha(t)\,\upbeta(t)]
in
\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
for any
t
.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{Z}
is not geodesic, then pass to its ultrapower
\mathcal{Z}^{\text{\rm}}
.

Fix a choice of geodesic
[\upalpha(t)\,\upbeta(t)]
for each
t
.

Setting
\ell(t)=|{\upalpha(t)}-\penalty{\upbeta(t)}|
, from the first variation inequality (
6C
) and the estimate in
13F
we get

\ell^{+}(t)\leqslant\penalty-\langle{\uparrow}_{[{\upalpha(t)}{\upbeta(t)}]},\nabla_{\upalpha(t)}f\rangle-\langle{\uparrow}_{[{\upbeta(t)}{\upalpha(t)}]},\nabla_{\upbeta(t)}f\rangle\leqslant\penalty\uplambda{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell(t).

Here one has to apply the first variation inequality for distance to the midpoint
m
of
[\upalpha(t)\,\upbeta(t)]
, and apply the triangle inequality.
Hence the result.
∎

16.13. Second distance estimate.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a complete length space,

\upvarepsilon>0
,
and
f,g\colon\mathcal{Z}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
be two
\uplambda
-concave locally Lipschitz functions such that
|f-g|<\upvarepsilon
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.
Assume

\upalpha,\upbeta\colon[0,t_{\max})\to\penalty\mathcal{Z}
are respectively
f
- and
g
-gradient curves.
Let
\ell\colon t\mapsto\penalty|{\upalpha(t)}-\penalty{\upbeta(t)}|
.
Then

\ell^{+}\leqslant\penalty\uplambda{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell+\tfrac{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon}{\ell}.

In particular, if
\upalpha(0)=\upbeta(0)
and
t_{\max}<\infty
then

|{\upalpha(t)}-\penalty{\upbeta(t)}|\leqslant\penalty c{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sqrt{\upvarepsilon{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}t}

for a constant
c=c(t_{\max},\uplambda)
.

Moreover, the same conclusion holds for locally Lipschitz
\uplambda
-concave subfunctions
f,g\colon\mathcal{Z}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
if for any
t\in\penalty[0,t_{\max})
there is a geodesic
[\upalpha(t)\,\upbeta(t)]
in
\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f\cap\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits g
.

Proof.
Set
\ell=\ell(t)=|{\upalpha(t)}-\penalty{\upbeta(t)}|
.
Fix
t
, and let
p=\upalpha(t)
and
q=\upbeta(t)
.
From the first variation formula and
13F
,

\displaystyle\ell^{+}

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty-\langle{\uparrow}_{[{p}{q}]},\nabla_{p}f\rangle-\langle{\uparrow}_{[{q}{p}]},\nabla_{q}g\rangle\leqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty-{\left({f(q)}-{f(p)}-\uplambda{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\tfrac{\ell^{2}}{2}\right)}/{\ell}-{\left({g(p)}-{g(q)}-\uplambda{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\tfrac{\ell^{2}}{2}\right)}/{\ell}\leqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty\uplambda{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell+\tfrac{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upvarepsilon}{\ell}.

By integrating, we get the second statement.
∎

D
Existence and uniqueness

In general, the “past” of gradient curves can not be determined by the “present”.
For example, consider the concave function
f\colon\mathbb{R}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
,
f(x)=\penalty-|x|
. The
two curves
\upalpha(t)=\min\{0,t\}
with
\upbeta(t)=0

are
f
-gradient with
\upalpha(t)=\penalty\upbeta(t)=\penalty 0
for all
t\geqslant\penalty 0
;
however
\upalpha(t)\neq\penalty\upbeta(t)
for all
t<0
.
Another example can be given as follows.

16.14. Example.

Let
f
be as in
13E
;
that is,
f\colon(x,y)\mapsto\penalty-|x|-|y|
be the concave function on the
(x,y)
-plane;
its gradient field is sketched on the figure.

Let
\upalpha
be an
f
-gradient curve that starts at
p=(x,y)
for
x>y>0
.
Then

\upalpha(t)=\begin{cases}(x-t,y-t)&\text{for}\quad 0\leqslant\penalty t\leqslant\penalty x-y,\\
(x-t,0)&\text{for}\quad x-y\leqslant\penalty t\leqslant\penalty x,\\
(0,0)&\text{for}\quad x\leqslant\penalty t.\end{cases}

In particular, gradient curves can merge even in the region where
|\nabla f|\neq\penalty 0
.
Hence their past cannot be uniquely determined from their present.

The next theorem shows that the future gradient curve is determined by its present.

16.15. Picard’s theorem.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a complete length space,

f\colon\mathcal{Z}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a semiconcave subfunction.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.
Assume
\upalpha,\upbeta\colon[0,t_{\max})\to\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
are two
f
-gradient curves
such that
\upalpha(0)=\upbeta(0)
.
Then
\upalpha(t)=\upbeta(t)
for any
t\in\penalty[0,t_{\max})
.

Proof.

Follows from the first distance estimate (
16C
).
∎

16.16. Local existence.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a complete length space
and
f\colon\mathcal{Z}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
be locally Lipschitz
\uplambda
-concave subfunction.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.
Then for any
p\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
,

(a)

if
|\nabla_{p}f|>0
, then for some
\upvarepsilon>0
,
there is an
f
-gradient-like curve
\hat{\upalpha}\colon[0,\upvarepsilon)\to\penalty\mathcal{Z}
that starts at
p
(that is,
\hat{\upalpha}(0)=\penalty p
);

(b)

for some
\updelta>0
, there is an
f
-gradient curve
\upalpha\colon[0,\updelta)\to\penalty\mathcal{Z}
that starts at
p
(that is
\upalpha(0)=p
).

This theorem was proved by Grigory Perelman and the third author
[
122
]
;
we present a simplified proof given by Alexander Lytchak
[
109
]
.

Proof.

If
|\nabla_{p}f|=0
, then the constant curve
\upalpha(t)=p
is
f
-gradient.

Otherwise, choose
\upvarepsilon>0

such that
\mathrm{B}{}(p,\upvarepsilon)\subset\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
,
the restriction
f|_{\mathrm{B}{}(p,\upvarepsilon)}
is Lipschitz,
and
|\nabla_{x}f|>\upvarepsilon
for all
x\in\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(p,\upvarepsilon)
;
the latter is possible due to semicontinuity of |gradient| (
13G
).

The curves
\hat{\upalpha}
and
\upalpha
will be constructed in the following three steps.
First we construct an
f^{\text{\rm}}
-gradient-like curve
\hat{\upalpha}_{\text{\rm}}\colon[0,\upvarepsilon)\to\penalty\mathcal{Z}^{\text{\rm}}
as an
-limit of a certain sequence of broken geodesics in
\mathcal{Z}
.
Second, we parametrize
\hat{\upalpha}_{\text{\rm}}
as in
16B
, to obtain an
f^{\text{\rm}}
-gradient curve
\upalpha_{\text{\rm}}
in
\mathcal{Z}^{\text{\rm}}
.
Third, applying Picard’s theorem (
16D
) together with Lemma
4B
, we obtain that
\upalpha_{\text{\rm}}
lies in
\mathcal{Z}\subset\penalty\mathcal{Z}^{\text{\rm}}
and therefore one can take
\upalpha=\upalpha_{\text{\rm}}
and
\hat{\upalpha}=\hat{\upalpha}_{\text{\rm}}
.

Note that if
\mathcal{Z}
is proper, then
\mathcal{Z}
is a metric component of
\mathcal{Z}^{\text{\rm}}
and
f=f^{\text{\rm}}|_{\mathcal{Z}}
.
Thus, in this case, the third step is not necessary.

Step 1.

Given
n\in\penalty\mathbb{N}
,
by an open-closed argument,
we can construct a unit-speed curve
\hat{\upalpha}_{n}\colon[0,\upvarepsilon]\to\penalty\mathcal{Z}
starting at
p
, with a partition of
[0,\upvarepsilon)
into a countable number of half-open intervals
[\upsigma_{i},\bar{\upsigma}_{i})

such that for each
i
we have

(i)

\hat{\upalpha}_{n}([\upsigma_{i},\bar{\upsigma}_{i}])
is a geodesic and
\bar{\upsigma}_{i}-\upsigma_{i}<\tfrac{1}{n}
,

(ii)

f\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}_{n}(\bar{\upsigma}_{i})-f\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}_{n}(\upsigma_{i})>(\bar{\upsigma}_{i}-\upsigma_{i}){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(|\nabla_{\hat{\upalpha}_{n}(\upsigma_{i})}f|-\tfrac{1}{n}).

Passing to a subsequence of
\hat{\upalpha}_{n}
such that
f\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}_{n}
uniformly converges, let

h(s)=\lim_{n\to\penalty\infty}f\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}_{n}(s).

Let
\hat{\upalpha}_{\text{\rm}}=\lim_{n\to\penalty\text{\rm}}\hat{\upalpha}_{n}
;
it is a curve in
\mathcal{Z}^{\text{\rm}}
that starts at
p\in\penalty\mathcal{Z}\subset\penalty\mathcal{Z}^{\text{\rm}}
.

Clearly
\hat{\upalpha}_{\text{\rm}}
is
1
-Lipschitz.
From (
ii
) and
13G
, we have

(f^{\text{\rm}}\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}_{\text{\rm}})^{+}(\upsigma)\geqslant\penalty|\nabla_{\hat{\upalpha}_{\text{\rm}}(\upsigma)}f^{\text{\rm}}|.

According to
16A
,
\hat{\upalpha}_{\text{\rm}}\colon[0,\upvarepsilon)\to\penalty\mathcal{Z}^{\text{\rm}}
is an
f^{\text{\rm}}
-gradient-like curve.

Step 2.

Clearly
h(s)=f^{\text{\rm}}\circ\penalty\upalpha_{\text{\rm}}
.
Therefore, according to
16A
,
h
is
\uplambda
-concave.
Thus we can define a homeomorphism
\upsigma\colon[0,\updelta]\to\penalty[0,\upvarepsilon]
by

{\upsigma^{-1}(s)}=\int\limits_{0}^{s}\frac{\bm{d}\textit{\c{s}}}{h^{\prime}(\textit{\c{s}})},

➊

According to
16B
,
\upalpha(t)=\hat{\upalpha}\circ\penalty\upsigma(t)
is an
f^{\text{\rm}}
-gradient curve in
\mathcal{Z}^{\text{\rm}}
.

Step 3.

Clearly,
\nabla_{p}f=\nabla_{p}f^{\text{\rm}}
for any
p\in\penalty\mathcal{Z}\subset\penalty\mathcal{Z}^{\text{\rm}}
;
more formally, if
\upiota\colon\mathcal{Z}\hookrightarrow\penalty\mathcal{Z}^{\text{\rm}}
is the natural embedding, then

(\bm{d}_{p}\upiota)(\nabla_{p}f)=\nabla_{p}f^{\text{\rm}}
.
Thus it is sufficient to show that
\upalpha_{\text{\rm}}
lies in
\mathcal{Z}
.
Assume the contrary; then according to
4B
, there is a subsequence
\hat{\upalpha}_{n_{k}}
such that

\hat{\upalpha}_{\text{\rm}}\not=\hat{\upalpha}^{\prime}_{\text{\rm}}:=\lim_{k\to\penalty\text{\rm}}\hat{\upalpha}_{n_{k}}.

Clearly
h(s)=f^{\text{\rm}}\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}_{\text{\rm}}=f^{\text{\rm}}\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}^{\prime}_{\text{\rm}}
.
Thus for
\upsigma\colon[0,\updelta]\to\penalty[0,\upvarepsilon]
defined by
➊
,
we have that both curves

\hat{\upalpha}_{\text{\rm}}\circ\penalty\upsigma
and
\hat{\upalpha}^{\prime}_{\text{\rm}}\circ\penalty\upsigma
are
f^{\text{\rm}}
-gradient.
From Picard’s theorem (
16D
), we have
\hat{\upalpha}_{\text{\rm}}\circ\penalty\upsigma=\hat{\upalpha}^{\prime}_{\text{\rm}}\circ\penalty\upsigma
.
Therefore
\hat{\upalpha}_{\text{\rm}}=\hat{\upalpha}^{\prime}_{\text{\rm}}
, a contradiction.
∎

E
Convergence

16.17. Ultralimit of gradient curves.

Assume

\diamond

\mathcal{Z}_{n}
is a sequence of complete spaces,
\mathcal{Z}_{n}\to\penalty\mathcal{Z}_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
, and
p_{n}\to\penalty p_{\upomega}
for a sequence of points
p_{n}\in\penalty\mathcal{Z}_{n}
,

\diamond

all spaces
\mathcal{Z}_{n}
are either
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
or
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
,

\diamond

f_{n}\colon\mathcal{Z}_{n}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
are
{\ell}
-Lipschitz and
\uplambda
-concave,

f_{n}\to\penalty f_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
, and
p_{\text{\rm}}\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f_{\text{\rm}}
.

Then:

(a)

f_{\text{\rm}}
is
\uplambda
-concave.

(b)

If
|\nabla_{p_{\text{\rm}}}f_{\text{\rm}}|>0
, then there is
\upvarepsilon>0
such that, the
f_{n}
-gradient-like curves
\hat{\upalpha}_{n}\colon[0,\upvarepsilon)\to\penalty\mathcal{Z}_{n}
are defined for
-almost all
n
.
Moreover, a curve
\hat{\upalpha}_{\text{\rm}}\colon[0,\upvarepsilon)\to\penalty\mathcal{Z}_{\text{\rm}}
is a gradient-like curve that starts at
p_{\text{\rm}}
if and only if

\hat{\upalpha}_{n}(s)\to\penalty\hat{\upalpha}_{\text{\rm}}(s)
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
for all
s\in\penalty[0,\upvarepsilon)
.

(c)

For some
\updelta>0
, the
f_{n}
-gradient curves
\upalpha_{n}\colon[0,\updelta)\to\penalty\mathcal{Z}_{n}
are defined for
-almost all
n
.
Moreover, a curve
\upalpha_{\text{\rm}}\colon[0,\updelta)\to\penalty\mathcal{Z}_{\text{\rm}}
is a gradient curve that starts at
p_{\text{\rm}}
if and only if

\upalpha_{n}(t)\to\penalty\upalpha_{\text{\rm}}(t)
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
for all
t\in\penalty[0,\updelta)
.

Note that according to Exercise
4E
, part
16E
does not hold for general metric spaces.
The idea of the proof is the same as in the proof of local existence (
16D
).

Proof of
16E
;
16E
.

Fix a geodesic
\upgamma_{\text{\rm}}\colon\mathbb{I}\to\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f_{\text{\rm}}
;
we need to show that the function

t\mapsto\penalty f_{\text{\rm}}\circ\penalty\upgamma_{\text{\rm}}(t)-\tfrac{\uplambda}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}t^{2}

➊

is concave.

Since the
f_{n}
are
{\ell}
-Lipschitz, so is
f_{\text{\rm}}
.
Therefore it is sufficient to prove concavity in the interior of
\mathbb{I}
.
In particular, we can assume that
\upgamma_{\text{\rm}}
is sufficiently short and can be extended behind its ends
p_{\text{\rm}}
and
q_{\text{\rm}}
as a minimizing geodesic.
If
\mathcal{Z}
is
CBB
, then by Theorem
8B
,
\upgamma_{\text{\rm}}
is the unique geodesic connecting
p_{\text{\rm}}
to
q_{\text{\rm}}
.
The same holds true if
\mathcal{Z}
is
CAT
by the uniqueness of geodesics (
9B
).

Construct two sequences of points
p_{n},q_{n}\in\penalty\mathcal{Z}_{n}
such that
p_{n}\to\penalty p_{\text{\rm}}
and
q_{n}\to\penalty q_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.
Applying either
8B
or
9B
,
we can assume that for each
n
there is a geodesic
\upgamma_{n}
from
p_{n}
to
q_{n}
in
\mathcal{Z}_{n}
.

Since
f_{n}
is
\uplambda
-concave, the function

t\mapsto\penalty f_{n}\circ\penalty\upgamma_{n}(t)-\tfrac{\uplambda}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}t^{2}

is concave.

The
-limit of the sequence
\upgamma_{n}
is a geodesic in
\mathcal{Z}_{\text{\rm}}
from
p_{\text{\rm}}
to
q_{\text{\rm}}
.
By uniqueness of such geodesics, we have that
\upgamma_{n}\to\penalty\upgamma_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.
Passing to the limit, we have
➊
.

If part of
16E
.

Take
\upvarepsilon>0
so small that
\mathrm{B}{}(p_{\text{\rm}},\upvarepsilon)\subset\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f_{\text{\rm}}
and
|\nabla_{x_{\text{\rm}}}f_{\text{\rm}}|>\penalty 0
for any
x_{\text{\rm}}\in\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(p_{\text{\rm}},\upvarepsilon)
(this is possible by
13G
).

Clearly
\hat{\upalpha}_{\text{\rm}}
is
1
-Lipschitz.
From
13G
, we get

(f_{\text{\rm}}\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}_{\text{\rm}})^{+}(s)\geqslant\penalty|\nabla_{\hat{\upalpha}_{\text{\rm}}(s)}f^{\text{\rm}}|.

According to
16A
,
\hat{\upalpha}_{\text{\rm}}\colon[0,\upvarepsilon)\to\penalty\mathcal{Z}^{\text{\rm}}
is an
f_{\text{\rm}}
-gradient-like curve.

If part of
16E
.

Assume first that
|\nabla_{p_{\text{\rm}}}f_{\text{\rm}}|>0
,
so we can apply the if part of
16E
.
Let
h_{n}=f_{n}\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}_{n}\colon[0,\upvarepsilon)\to\penalty\mathbb{R}

and
h_{\text{\rm}}=f_{\text{\rm}}\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}_{\text{\rm}}
.
From
16A
, the
h_{n}
are
\uplambda
-concave, and clearly
h_{n}\to\penalty h_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.
Let us define reparametrizations

\displaystyle{\upsigma_{n}^{-1}(s)}

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{0}^{s}\frac{\bm{d}\textit{\c{s}}}{h_{n}^{\prime}(\textit{\c{s}})},

\displaystyle{\upsigma_{\text{\rm}}^{-1}(s)}

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{0}^{s}\frac{\bm{d}\textit{\c{s}}}{h_{\text{\rm}}^{\prime}(\textit{\c{s}})}.

The
\uplambda
-convexity of the
h_{n}
implies that
\upsigma_{n}\to\penalty\upsigma_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
.
By
16B
,

\upalpha_{n}=\hat{\upalpha}_{n}\circ\penalty\upsigma_{n}
.
Applying the if part of
16E
together with Lemma
16B
,
we get that
\upalpha_{\text{\rm}}=\hat{\upalpha}_{\text{\rm}}\circ\penalty\upsigma_{\text{\rm}}
is gradient curve.

The remaining case
|\nabla_{p_{\text{\rm}}}f_{\text{\rm}}|=0
can be reduced to the one above using the following trick.
Consider the sequence of spaces
\mathcal{Z}_{n}^{\times\penalty}=\mathcal{Z}_{n}\times\penalty\mathbb{R}
,
with the sequence of subfunctions
f^{\times\penalty}_{n}\colon\mathcal{Z}_{n}^{\times\penalty}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
defined by

f^{\times\penalty}_{n}(p,t)=f_{n}(p)+t.

Applying either
11D
or
11D
, we have that

\mathcal{Z}_{n}^{\times\penalty}
is a
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa_{-}})
space for
\upkappa_{-}=\min\{\upkappa,0\}
, or
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa_{+}})
space for
\upkappa_{+}=\max\{\upkappa,0\}
.
Note that the
f_{n}^{\times\penalty}
are
\uplambda_{+}
-concave
for
\uplambda_{+}=\max\{\uplambda,0\}
.
Now let
\mathcal{Z}_{\text{\rm}}^{\times\penalty}=\mathcal{Z}_{\text{\rm}}\times\penalty\mathbb{R}
,
and
f^{\times\penalty}_{\text{\rm}}(p,t)=f_{\text{\rm}}(p)+t
.

Clearly

\mathcal{Z}_{n}^{\times\penalty}\to\penalty\mathcal{Z}_{\text{\rm}}^{\times\penalty}
,

f_{n}^{\times\penalty}\to\penalty f_{\text{\rm}}^{\times\penalty}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
,
and
|\nabla_{x}f^{\times\penalty}_{\text{\rm}}|>0
for any
x\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f_{\text{\rm}}^{\times\penalty}
.
Thus for the sequence
f_{n}^{\times\penalty}\colon\mathcal{Z}_{n}^{\times\penalty}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
,
we can apply the if part of
16E
.
It remains to note that the curve
\upalpha^{\times\penalty}_{\text{\rm}}(t)=(\upalpha_{\text{\rm}}(t),t)
is an
f^{\times\penalty}_{\text{\rm}}
-gradient curve in
\mathcal{Z}^{\times\penalty}_{\text{\rm}}

if and only if
\upalpha_{\text{\rm}}(t)
is an
f_{\text{\rm}}
-gradient curve.

Only-if part of
16E
and
16E
.

The only-if part of
16E
follows from
the if part of
16E
and Picard’s theorem (
16D
).
Applying Lemma
16B
, we get the only-if part of
16E
.
∎

Directly from local existence (
16D
) and the distance estimates (
16C
), we obtain the following.

16.18. Global existence.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{Z}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a locally Lipschitz and
\uplambda
-concave subfunction on a complete length space
\mathcal{Z}
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.
Then for any
p\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
, there is
t_{\max}\in\penalty(0,\infty]
such that
there is an
f
-gradient curve
\upalpha\colon[0,t_{\max})\to\penalty\mathcal{Z}
with
\upalpha(0)=p
.
Moreover, for any sequence
t_{n}\to\penalty t_{\max}-
, the sequence
\upalpha(t_{n})
does not have a limit point in
\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
.

The following theorem guarantees the existence of gradient curves for all times for the special type of semiconcave functions that play important role in the theory.
It follows from
16E
,

16A
and
16B
.

16.19. Theorem.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a complete length space
and
f\colon\mathcal{Z}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
satisfies

f^{\prime\prime}+\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f\leqslant\penalty\uplambda

for real constants
\upkappa
and
\uplambda
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.
Then
f
has
complete gradient
;
that is, for any
x\in\penalty\mathcal{Z}
there is a
f
-gradient curve
\upalpha\colon[0,\infty)\to\penalty\mathcal{Z}
that starts at
x
.

F
Gradient flow

In this section we define gradient flow for semiconcave subfunctions
and reformulate theorems obtained earlier in this chapter using this new terminology.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a complete length space
and
f\colon\mathcal{Z}\mathrel{\mathrel{\circ}\penalty\joinrel\hskip-1.49994pt\to\penalty}\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a locally Lipschitz semiconcave subfunction.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.
For any
t\geqslant\penalty 0
, we write
\mathop{\rm Flow}\nolimits^{t}_{f}(x)=y
if there is an
f
-gradient curve
\upalpha
such that
\upalpha(0)=x
and
\upalpha(t)=y
.
The partially defined map
\mathop{\rm Flow}\nolimits^{t}_{f}
from
\mathcal{Z}
to itself is called the
f
-gradient flow
for time
t
.

From
16C
,
it follows that for any
t\geqslant\penalty 0
, the domain of definition of
\mathop{\rm Flow}\nolimits^{t}_{f}
is an open subset of
\mathcal{Z}
;
that is,
\mathop{\rm Flow}\nolimits^{t}_{f}
is a submap.
Moreover, if
f
is defined on all of
\mathcal{Z}
and
f^{\prime\prime}+\mathrm{K}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f\leqslant\penalty\uplambda
for constants
\mathrm{K},\uplambda\in\penalty\mathbb{R}
,
then according to
16E
,
\mathop{\rm Flow}\nolimits^{t}_{f}(x)
is defined for all pairs
(x,t)\in\penalty\mathcal{Z}\times\penalty\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant\penalty 0}
.

Clearly
\mathop{\rm Flow}\nolimits^{t_{1}+t_{2}}_{f}=\mathop{\rm Flow}\nolimits_{f}^{t_{1}}\circ\penalty\mathop{\rm Flow}\nolimits_{f}^{t_{2}}
;
in other words, gradient flow is given by an action of the semigroup
(\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant\penalty 0},+)
.

From the first distance estimate (
16C
),
we have the following:

16.20. Proposition.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a complete length
CBB
or
CAT
space
and
f\colon\mathcal{Z}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a semiconcave function.
Then the map
x\mapsto\penalty\mathop{\rm Flow}\nolimits^{t}_{f}(x)
is locally Lipschitz.

Moreover, if
f
is
\uplambda
-concave, then
\mathop{\rm Flow}\nolimits^{t}_{f}
is
e^{\uplambda{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}t}
-Lipschitz.

The next proposition states that gradient flow is stable under Gromov–Hausdorff convergence.
The proposition follows directly from the proposition on ultralimit of gradient curves
16E
.

16.21. Proposition.

Supose
\mathcal{Z}_{\infty},\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2},\dots\penalty
are complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space,
\mathcal{Z}_{n}\xrightarrow{\uptau}\mathcal{Z}_{\infty}
, and
f_{n}\colon\mathcal{Z}_{n}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
is a sequence of

\uplambda
-concave functions that converges to
f_{\infty}\colon\mathcal{Z}_{\infty}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
.
Then

\mathop{\rm Flow}\nolimits_{f_{n}}^{t}\colon\mathcal{Z}_{n}\to\penalty\mathcal{Z}_{n}
converges to
\mathop{\rm Flow}\nolimits_{f_{\infty}}^{t}\colon\mathcal{Z}_{\infty}\to\penalty\mathcal{Z}_{\infty}
.

G
Line splitting theorem

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a metric space and
A,B\subset\penalty\mathcal{X}
.
We will write

\mathcal{X}=A\oplus B

if there are projections
\mathop{\rm proj}\nolimits_{A}\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty A

and

\mathop{\rm proj}\nolimits_{B}\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty B

such that

|{x}-\penalty{y}|^{2}=|{\mathop{\rm proj}\nolimits_{A}(x)}-\penalty{\mathop{\rm proj}\nolimits_{A}(y)}|^{2}+|{\mathop{\rm proj}\nolimits_{B}(x)}-\penalty{\mathop{\rm proj}\nolimits_{B}(y)}|^{2}

for any two points
x,y\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
.

Note that if

\mathcal{X}=A\oplus B

then

\diamond

A
intersects
B
at a single point,

\diamond

both sets
A
and
B
are convex sets in
\mathcal{X}
.

Recall that a line in a metric space is a both-sided infinite geodesic; thus it minimizes the length on each segment.

16.22. Line splitting theorem.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
space
and
\upgamma
be a line in
\mathcal{L}
.
Then

\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\oplus\upgamma(\mathbb{R})

for a subset
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\subset\penalty\mathcal{L}
.

For smooth
2
-dimensional surfaces,
this theorem was proved by Stefan Cohn-Vossen
[
54
]
.
For Riemannian manifolds of higher dimensions
it was proved by Victor Toponogov
[
153
]
.
Then it was generalized by Anatoliy Milka
[
116
]

to Alexandrov spaces; nearly the same proof is used in
[
36
, 1.5]
.

Further generalizations of the splitting theorem for Riemannian manifolds with nonnegative Ricci curvature were obtained by Jeff Cheeger and Detlef Gromoll
[
52
]
.
This was further generalized by Jeff Cheeger and Toby Colding for limits of Riemannian manifolds with almost nonnegative Ricci curvature
[
50
]
and to their synthetic generalizations, so-called
RCD spaces
, by Nicola Gigli
[
68
,
67
]
.
Jost-Hinrich Eschenburg obtained an analogous result for Lorentzian manifolds
[
63
]
, that is, pseudo-Riemannian manifolds of signature
(1,n)
.

We present a proof that uses gradient flow for Busemann functions.
It is close in spirit to the proof given in
[
52
]
.

Before going into the proof, let us state a few corollaries of the theorem.

16.23. Corollary.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
space.
Then there is an isometric splitting

\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\oplus H

where
H\subset\penalty\mathcal{L}
is a subset isometric to a Hilbert space, and
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\subset\penalty\mathcal{L}
is a convex subset that contains no line.

16.24. Corollary.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a finite-dimensional complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
cone and
v_{+},v_{-}\in\penalty\mathcal{K}
be a pair of opposite vectors
(that is,
v_{+}+v_{-}=0
, see Definiton
13I
).
Then there is an isometry
\upiota\colon K\to\penalty K^{\prime}\times\penalty\mathbb{R}
such that

\upiota:v_{\pm}\mapsto\penalty(0^{\prime},\pm|v_{\pm}|)
, where
K^{\prime}
is a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
space having a cone structure with tip
0^{\prime}
.

16.25. Corollary.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be an
m
-dimensional complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({1})
space,
2\leqslant\penalty m<\infty
, and
\mathop{\rm rad}\nolimits\mathcal{L}=\uppi
.
Then
