\mathcal{L}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it iso}}}\penalty\mathbb{S}^{m}.

The following lemma is closely relevant to the first distance estimate (
16C
); its proof goes along the same lines.

16.26. Lemma.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
space.
Suppose
f\colon\mathcal{L}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a concave 1-Lipschitz function.
Consider two
f
-gradient curves
\upalpha
and
\upbeta
.
Then for any
t,s\geqslant\penalty 0
we have

\displaystyle|{\upalpha(s)}-\penalty{\upbeta(t)}|^{2}\leqslant\penalty|{p}-\penalty{q}|^{2}+2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(f(p)-f(q)){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(s-t)+(s-t)^{2},

where
p=\upalpha(0)
and
q=\upbeta(0)
.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{L}
is not geodesic, then pass to its ultrapower
\mathcal{L}^{\text{\rm}}
.

Since
f
is 1-Lipschitz,
|\nabla f|\leqslant\penalty 1
.
Therefore

f\circ\penalty\upbeta(t)\leqslant\penalty f(q)+t

for any
t\geqslant\penalty 0
.

Set
\ell(t)=|{p}-\penalty{\upbeta(t)}|
.
Applying
13F
and the first variation inequality (
6C
), we get

\displaystyle\ell^{2}(t)^{+}

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\left(f\circ\penalty\upbeta(t)-f(p)\right)\leqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\left(f(q)+t-f(p)\right).

Therefore

\ell^{2}(t)-\ell^{2}(0)\leqslant\penalty 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\left(f(q)-f(p)\right){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}t+t^{2}.

It proves the needed inequality in case
s=0
.
Combining it with the first distance estimate (
16C
), we get the result in case
s\leqslant\penalty t
.
The case
s\geqslant\penalty t
follows by switching the roles of
s
and
t
.
∎

Proof of
16G
.
Consider two Busemann functions,
\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{+}
and
\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{-}
, asociated with half-lines
\upgamma:[0,\infty)\to\penalty\mathcal{L}
and
\upgamma:(-\infty,0]\to\penalty\mathcal{L}
respectively; that is,

\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{\pm}(x)=\lim_{t\to\penalty\infty}|{\upgamma(\pm t)}-\penalty{x}|-t.

According to Exercise
8D
,
both functions
\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{\pm}
are concave.

Fix
x\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
.
Note that since
\upgamma
is a line, we have

\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{+}(x)+\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{-}(x)\geqslant\penalty 0.

On the other hand, by
8D
,

f(t)=\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{x}^{2}{}{}\circ\penalty\upgamma(t)

is
2
-concave.
In particular,
f(t)\leqslant\penalty t^{2}+at+b
for some constants
a,b\in\penalty\mathbb{R}
.
Passing to the limit as
t\to\penalty\pm\infty
, we have

\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{+}(x)+\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{-}(x)\leqslant\penalty 0.

Hence

\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{+}(x)+\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{-}(x)=0

for any
x\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
.
In particular, the functions
\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{\pm}
are
affine
;
that is, they are convex and concave at the same time.

It follows that for any
x
,

\displaystyle|\nabla_{x}\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{\pm}|

\displaystyle=\sup\left\{\,{\bm{d}_{x}\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{\pm}(\upxi)}\ {:}\ {\upxi\in\penalty\Sigma_{x}}\,\right\}=

\displaystyle=\sup\left\{\,{-\bm{d}_{x}\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{\mp}(\upxi)}\ {:}\ {\upxi\in\penalty\Sigma_{x}}\,\right\}\equiv

\displaystyle\equiv 1.

By Exercise
16B
, a

1
-Lipschitz curve
\upalpha
such that
\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{\pm}(\upalpha(t))=t+c
is a
\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{\pm}
-gradient curve.
In particular,
\upalpha(t)
is a
\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{+}
-gradient curve if and only if
\upalpha(-t)
is a
\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{-}
-gradient curve.
It follows that for any
t>0
, the
\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{\pm}
-gradient flows commute;
that is,

\mathop{\rm Flow}\nolimits_{\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{+}}^{t}\circ\penalty\mathop{\rm Flow}\nolimits_{\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{-}}^{t}=\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits_{\mathcal{L}}.

Setting

\mathop{\rm Flow}\nolimits^{t}=\left[\begin{matrix}\mathop{\rm Flow}\nolimits_{\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{+}}^{t}&\hbox{if}\ t\geqslant\penalty 0\\
\mathop{\rm Flow}\nolimits_{\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{-}}^{t}&\hbox{if}\ t\leqslant\penalty 0\end{matrix}\right.

defines an
\mathbb{R}
-action on
\mathcal{L}
.

Consider the level set
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}=\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{+}^{-1}(0)=\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{-}^{-1}(0)
;
it is a closed convex subset of
\mathcal{L}
, and therefore forms an Alexandrov space.
Consider the map
h\colon\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\times\penalty\mathbb{R}\to\penalty\mathcal{L}
defined by
h\colon(x,t)\mapsto\penalty\mathop{\rm Flow}\nolimits^{t}(x)
.
Note that
h
is onto.
Applying Lemma
16G
for
\mathop{\rm Flow}\nolimits_{\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{+}}^{t}
and
\mathop{\rm Flow}\nolimits_{\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{-}}^{t}
shows that
h
is short and non-contracting at the same time; that is,
h
is an isometry.
∎

H
Radial curves

The radial curves are specially reparametrized gradient curves for distance functions.
This parametrization makes them behave like unit-speed geodesics in a natural comparison sense (
16I
).

16.27. Definition.

Assume
\mathcal{L}
is a complete length
CBB
space,

\upkappa\in\penalty\mathbb{R}
,
and
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
.
A curve

\upsigma\colon[s_{\min},s_{\max})\to\penalty\mathcal{L}

is called a

(p,\upkappa)
-radial curve

if

s_{\min}=\penalty|{p}-\penalty{\upsigma(s_{\min})}|\in\penalty(0,\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2})

and
\upsigma
satisfies the differential equation

\upsigma^{+}(s)=\penalty\frac{\mathrm{tg}^{\upkappa}|{p}-\penalty{\upsigma(s)}|}{\mathrm{tg}^{\upkappa}s}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nabla_{\upsigma(s)}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}{}{}

➊

for any
s\in\penalty[s_{\min},s_{\max})
, where
\mathrm{tg}^{\upkappa}x:=\frac{\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}x}{\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}x}
.

If
x=\upsigma(s_{\min})
, we say that
\upsigma

starts
at
x
.

Note that according to the definition,
s_{\max}\leqslant\penalty\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}
.

In the the next section, we will see that
(p,\upkappa)
-radial curves
work best for
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
spaces.

16.28. Definition.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
CBB
space
and
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
.
A unit-speed geodesic
\upgamma\colon\mathbb{I}\to\penalty\mathcal{L}
is called a

p
-radial geodesic
if

|{p}-\penalty{\upgamma(s)}|\equiv s
.

The proofs of the following two propositions follow directly from the definitions.

16.29. Proposition.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
CBB
space
and
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
.
Assume
\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}\geqslant\penalty s_{\max}
.
Then any
p
-radial geodesic

\upgamma\colon[s_{\min},s_{\max})\to\penalty\mathcal{L}

is a
(p,\upkappa)
-radial curve.

16.30. Proposition.

Suppose
\mathcal{L}
is a complete length
CBB
space,

p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}

and
\upsigma\colon[s_{\min},s_{\max})\to\penalty\mathcal{L}
is a
(p,\upkappa)
-radial curve.
Then for any
s\in\penalty[s_{\min},s_{\max})
,
we have
|{p}-\penalty{\upsigma(s)}|\leqslant\penalty s
.

Moreover,
if for some
s_{0}
we have
|{p}-\penalty{\upsigma(s_{0})}|=s_{0}
,
then the restriction
\upsigma|_{[s_{\min},s_{0}]}
is a
p
-radial geodesic.

16.31. Existence and uniqueness.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
CBB
space,

\upkappa\in\penalty\mathbb{R}
,

p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
,
and
x\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
.
Assume

0<|{p}-\penalty{x}|<\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}
.
Then there is a unique
(p,\upkappa)
-radial curve
\upsigma\colon[|{p}-\penalty{x}|,\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2})\to\penalty\mathcal{L}

that starts at
x
;
that is,
\upsigma(|{p}-\penalty{x}|)=x
.

Proof; existence.

Let

\mathrm{itg}^{\upkappa}\colon[0,\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2})\to\penalty\mathbb{R},\quad\mathrm{itg}^{\upkappa}(t)=\int\limits_{0}^{t}\mathrm{tg}^{\upkappa}\textit{\c{t}}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\bm{d}\textit{\c{t}}.

Clearly
\mathrm{itg}^{\upkappa}
is smooth and increasing.
From
3F
it follows that the composition

f=\mathrm{itg}^{\upkappa}\circ\penalty\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}{}{}

is semiconcave in
\mathrm{B}{}(p,\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2})
.

According to
16D
, there is an
f
-gradient curve
\upalpha\colon[0,t_{\max})\to\penalty\mathcal{L}
defined on the maximal interval such that
\upalpha(0)=x
.

Now consider a solution
\uptau(t)
for the initial value problem
\uptau^{\prime}=\penalty(\mathrm{tg}^{\upkappa}\uptau)^{2}
,
\uptau(0)=r
.
Note that
\uptau(t)
is also a gradient curve for the function
\mathrm{itg}^{\upkappa}
defined on
[0,\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2})
.
Direct calculations show that the composition
\upalpha\circ\penalty\uptau^{-1}

is a
(p,\upkappa)
-radial curve.

Uniqueness.
Assume
\upsigma^{1},\upsigma^{2}
are two
(p,\upkappa)
-radial curves that start at
x
.
Then the compositions
\upsigma^{i}\circ\penalty\uptau
both give
f
-gradient curves.
By Picard’s theorem (
16D
), we have

\upsigma^{1}\circ\penalty\uptau\equiv\upsigma^{2}\circ\penalty\uptau
.
Therefore
\upsigma^{1}(s)=\upsigma^{2}(s)

for any
s\geqslant\penalty r
such that both sides are defined.
∎

I
Radial comparisons

In this section we show that radial curves behave in a comparison sense like unit-speed geodesics.
Recall that notation
\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}\{a;b,c\}
is introduced in
1A
.

16.32. Radial monotonicity.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space and

p,q
be distinct points in
\mathcal{L}
.
Let
\upsigma\colon[s_{\min},\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2})\to\penalty\mathcal{L}

be a
(p,\upkappa)
-radial curve.
Then the function

s\mapsto\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}\{|{q}-\penalty{\upsigma(s)}|;|{p}-\penalty{q}|,s\}

is nonincreasing in its domain of definition.

Radial monotonicity implies the following by straightforward calculations.

16.33. Corollary.

Let
\upkappa\leqslant\penalty 0
,

\mathcal{L}
be a complete
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space,
and
p,q\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
.
Let
\upsigma\colon[s_{\min},\infty)\to\penalty\mathcal{L}
be a
(p,\upkappa)
-radial curve.
Then for any
w\geqslant\penalty 1
,
the function

s\mapsto\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}\{|{q}-\penalty{\upsigma(s)}|;|{p}-\penalty{q}|,w{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s\}

is nonincreasing in its domain of definition.

16.34. Radial comparison.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space
and
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
.
Let
\uprho\colon[r_{\min},\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2})\to\penalty\mathcal{L}

and
\upsigma\colon[s_{\min},\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2})\to\penalty\mathcal{L}

be two
(p,\upkappa)
-radial curves.
Let

\upvarphi_{\min}=\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}\left(p\,{}^{\uprho(r_{\min})}_{\upsigma(s_{\min})}\hskip-0.86108pt\right).

Then for any
r\in\penalty[r_{\min},\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2})
and
s\in\penalty[s_{\min},\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2})
,
we have

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}\{|{\uprho(r)}-\penalty{\upsigma(s)}|;r,s\}\leqslant\penalty\upvarphi_{\min},

or equivalently,

|{\uprho(r)}-\penalty{\upsigma(s)}|\leqslant\penalty\mathop{\tilde{\curlyvee}}\nolimits^{\upkappa}\{\upvarphi_{\min};r,s\}.

We prove Theorems
16I
and
16I
simultaneously.
The proof is an application of
13F
plus trigonometric manipulations.
We give a proof first in the simplest case
\upkappa=0
,
and then in the harder case
\upkappa\neq 0
.
The arguments for both cases are nearly the same,
but the case
\upkappa\neq 0
requires an extra twist.

Proof of
16I
and
16I
in case
\upkappa=0
.

Set

\displaystyle R=R(r)

\displaystyle=|{p}-\penalty{\uprho(r)}|,

\displaystyle S=S(s)

\displaystyle=|{p}-\penalty{\upsigma(s)}|,

\displaystyle\ell=\ell(r,s)

\displaystyle=|{\uprho(r)}-\penalty{\upsigma(s)}|,

\displaystyle\upvarphi=\upvarphi(r,s)

\displaystyle=\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{0}\{\ell(r,s);r,s\}.

It will be sufficient to prove the following inequalities:

\tfrac{\partial^{+}}{\partial r}\upvarphi(s_{\min},r)\leqslant\penalty 0,\qquad\tfrac{\partial^{+}}{\partial s}\upvarphi(s,r_{\min})\leqslant\penalty 0

[formula]

s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\tfrac{\partial^{+}}{\partial s}\upvarphi+r{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\tfrac{\partial^{+}}{\partial r}\upvarphi\leqslant\penalty 0.

[formula]

The radial monotonicity follows from
(*)^{0}_{\upvarphi}
.
The radial comparison follows from
(*)^{0}_{\upvarphi}
and
(**)^{0}_{\upvarphi}
.
Indeed, one can connect
(s_{\min},r_{\min})
and
(s_{0},r_{0})
in
[s_{\min},\infty)\times\penalty[r_{\min},\infty)

by a concatenation of a coordinate segment and a segment defined by
r/s=r_{0}/s_{0}
as in the figure.
According to
(*)^{0}_{\upvarphi}
and
(**)^{0}_{\upvarphi}
,
\upvarphi
does not increase while the pair
(r,s)
moves along this concatenation with nondecreasing
r
and
s
.
Thus
\upvarphi(r_{0},s_{0})\leqslant\penalty\upvarphi(r_{\min},s_{\min})=\upvarphi_{\min}
.

Let us rewrite the inequalities
(*)^{0}_{\upvarphi}
and
(**)^{0}_{\upvarphi}
in an equivalent form:

\displaystyle\tfrac{\partial^{+}}{\partial s}\ell(s,r_{\min})

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty\cos\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{0}\{r_{\min};s,\ell\},

[formula]

\displaystyle\tfrac{\partial^{+}}{\partial r}\ell(s_{\min},r)

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty\cos\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{0}\{s_{\min};r,\ell\},

s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\tfrac{\partial^{+}}{\partial s}\ell+r{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\tfrac{\partial^{+}}{\partial r}\ell\leqslant\penalty s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{0}\{r;s,\ell\}+r{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{0}\{s;r,\ell\}=\ell.

[formula]

Let

f=\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}^{2}{}{}.

[formula]

Clearly
f
is
1
-concave, and

\uprho^{+}(r)=\tfrac{1}{r}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nabla_{\uprho(r)}f\quad\text{and}\quad\upsigma^{+}(s)=\tfrac{1}{s}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nabla_{\upsigma(s)}f.

[formula]

Thus from
13F
, we have

\tfrac{\partial^{+}}{\partial r}\ell=-\tfrac{1}{r}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\nabla_{\uprho(r)}f,{\uparrow}_{[{\uprho(r)}{\upsigma(s)}]}\rangle\leqslant\penalty\frac{{\ell^{2}}+{R^{2}}-{S^{2}}}{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}r}.

[formula]

Since
R(r)\leqslant\penalty r
and
S(s_{\min})=s_{\min}
, then

\displaystyle\tfrac{\partial^{+}}{\partial r}\ell(r,s_{\min})

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty\frac{{\ell^{2}}+r^{2}-s_{\min}^{2}}{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}r}=

[formula]

\displaystyle=\cos\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{0}\{s_{\min};r,\ell\},

which is the first inequality in
(*)^{0}_{\ell}
.
By switching
\uprho
and
\upsigma
we obtain the second inequality in
(*)^{0}_{\ell}
.
Further, adding
(C)^{0}
and its mirror-inequality for
\frac{\partial^{+}}{\partial s}\ell
, we have

r{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\tfrac{\partial^{+}}{\partial r}\ell+s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\tfrac{\partial^{+}}{\partial s}\ell\leqslant\penalty\frac{{\ell^{2}}+{R^{2}}-{S^{2}}}{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell}+\frac{{\ell^{2}}+{S^{2}}-{R^{2}}}{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell}=\ell,

[formula]

namely
(**)^{0}_{\ell}
.
∎

Proof of
16I
and
16I
in case
\upkappa\neq 0
.

As before, let

\displaystyle R=R(r)

\displaystyle=|{p}-\penalty{\uprho(r)}|,

\displaystyle\ell

\displaystyle=\ell(r,s)=|{\uprho(r)}-\penalty{\upsigma(s)}|,

\displaystyle S=S(s)

\displaystyle=|{p}-\penalty{\upsigma(s)}|,

\displaystyle\upvarphi

\displaystyle=\upvarphi(r,s)=\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}\{\ell(r,s);r,s\}.

It suffices to prove the following three inequalities:

\displaystyle\tfrac{\partial^{+}}{\partial r}\upvarphi(s_{\min},r)\leqslant\penalty 0,

\displaystyle\tfrac{\partial^{+}}{\partial s}\upvarphi(s,r_{\min})\leqslant\penalty 0,

[formula]

\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}S{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\tfrac{\partial^{+}}{\partial s}\upvarphi+\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}r{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}R{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\tfrac{\partial^{+}}{\partial r}\upvarphi\leqslant\penalty 0.

[formula]

Then radial monotonicity follows from
(*)^{\pm}_{\upvarphi}
.
The radial comparison follows from
(*)^{0}_{\upvarphi}
and
(**)^{\pm}_{\upvarphi}
.
Indeed, the functions
s\mapsto\penalty\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}S
and
r\mapsto\penalty\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}r{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}R
are Lipschitz.
Thus there is a solution for the differential equation

(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})=(\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}S,\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}r{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}R)

with any initial data
(r_{0},s_{0})\in\penalty[r_{\min},\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2})\times\penalty[s_{\min},\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2})
.
(Unlike the case
\upkappa=0
, the solution cannot be written explicitly.)
Since
\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}S
,
\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}r{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}R>0
, this solution
(r(t),s(t))
must meet one of the coordinate rays

\{r_{\min}\}\times\penalty[s_{\min},\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2})
or
[r_{\min},\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2})\times\penalty\{s_{\min}\}
.
That is, one can connect the pair
(s_{\min},r_{\min})
to
(s_{0},r_{0})
by a concatenation of a coordinate segment (vertical or horizontal) and part of the solution
(r(t),s(t))
.
According to
(*)^{\pm}_{\upvarphi}
and
(**)^{\pm}_{\upvarphi}
, the value of
\upvarphi
does not increase while the pair
(r,s)
moves along this concatenation in direction of increasing
r
and
s
.
Thus
\upvarphi(r_{0},s_{0})\leqslant\penalty\upvarphi(r_{\min},s_{\min})
.

As before, we rewrite the inequalities
(*)^{\pm}_{\upvarphi}
and
(**)^{\pm}_{\upvarphi}
in terms of
\ell
:

\displaystyle\tfrac{\partial^{+}}{\partial s}\ell(s,r_{\min})

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty\cos\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}\{r_{\min};s,\ell\},

[formula]

\displaystyle\tfrac{\partial^{+}}{\partial r}\ell(s_{\min},r)

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty\cos\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}\{s_{\min};r,\ell\},

\displaystyle\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}s

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}S{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\tfrac{\partial^{+}}{\partial s}\ell+\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}r{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}R{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\tfrac{\partial^{+}}{\partial r}\ell\leqslant\penalty

[formula]

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}S{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}\{r;s,\ell\}+\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}r{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}R{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}\{s;r,\ell\}.

Let

f=-\tfrac{1}{\upkappa}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}\circ\penalty\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}{}{}=\mathrm{md}^{\upkappa}\circ\penalty\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}{}{}-\tfrac{1}{\upkappa}.

[formula]

Clearly
f^{\prime\prime}+\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f\leqslant\penalty 0
and

\displaystyle\uprho^{+}(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathrm{tg}^{\upkappa}r{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}R}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nabla_{\uprho(r)}f,

[formula]

\displaystyle\upsigma^{+}(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathrm{tg}^{\upkappa}s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}S}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nabla_{\upsigma(s)}f.

Thus from
13F
, we have

\displaystyle\tfrac{\partial^{+}}{\partial r}\ell

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\mathrm{tg}^{\upkappa}r{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}R}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\nabla_{\uprho(r)}f,{\uparrow}_{[{\uprho(r)}{\upsigma(s)}]}\rangle\leqslant\penalty

[formula]

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty\frac{1}{\mathrm{tg}^{\upkappa}r{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}R}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\frac{\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}S-\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}R{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}\ell}{\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}\ell}=

\displaystyle=\frac{\frac{\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}S}{\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}R}-\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}\ell}{\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{tg}^{\upkappa}r{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}\ell}.

Note that for all
\upkappa\neq 0
,
the function
x\mapsto\penalty\frac{1}{\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}x}
is increasing.
Thus, since
R(r)\leqslant\penalty r
and
S(s_{\min})=s_{\min}
, we have

\displaystyle\tfrac{\partial^{+}}{\partial r}\ell(r,s_{\min})

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty\frac{\frac{\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}s_{\min}}{\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}r}-\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}\ell}{\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{tg}^{\upkappa}r{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}\ell}=

[formula]

\displaystyle=\frac{{\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}s_{\min}}-\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}\ell{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}r}{\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}r{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}\ell}=

\displaystyle=\cos\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}\{s_{\min};r,\ell\},

which is the first inequality in
(*)^{\pm}_{\ell}
for
\upkappa\neq 0
.
By switching
\uprho
and
\upsigma
we obtain the second inequality in
(*)^{\pm}_{\ell}
.
Further, adding
(C)^{\pm}
and its mirror-inequality for
\tfrac{\partial^{+}}{\partial s}\ell
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}r{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}R{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\tfrac{\partial^{+}}{\partial r}\ell

\displaystyle+\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}S{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\tfrac{\partial^{+}}{\partial s}\ell\leqslant\penalty

[formula]

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty\frac{{\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}S}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}r-\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}\ell{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}R{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}r}{\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}\ell}+

\displaystyle+\frac{{\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}R}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}s-\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}\ell{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}S{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}s}{\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}\ell}=

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}r{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}R{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\frac{\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}s-\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}\ell{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}r}{\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}r{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}\ell}+

\displaystyle+\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}S{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\frac{\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}r-\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}\ell{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}s}{\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}\ell}=

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}r{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}R{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}\{r;s,\ell\}+

\displaystyle+\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathrm{cs}^{\upkappa}S{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}\{s;r,\ell\},

which is
(**)^{\pm}_{\ell}
.∎

16.35. Exercise.

\mathcal{L}

.

.

[xy]

.

J
Gradient exponent

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space,

p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
,
and
\upxi\in\penalty\Sigma_{p}
.
Consider a sequence of points
x_{n}\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
such that
{\uparrow}_{[{p}{x_{n}}]}\to\penalty\upxi
.
Let
r_{n}=|{p}-\penalty{x_{n}}|
, and let

\upsigma_{n}\colon[r_{n},\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2})\to\penalty\mathcal{L}
be the
(p,\upkappa)
-radial curve that starts at
x_{n}
.

By the radial comparison (
16I
),
the curves
\upsigma_{n}\colon[r_{n},\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2})\to\penalty\mathcal{L}

converge to a curve
\upsigma_{\upxi}\colon(0,\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2})\to\penalty\mathcal{L}
,
and this limit is independent of the choice of the sequence
x_{n}
.
Let
\upsigma_{\upxi}(0)=p
, and if
\upkappa>0
define

\upsigma_{\upxi}(\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2})=\lim_{t\to\penalty\frac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}}\upsigma_{\upxi}(t).

The resulting curve
\upsigma_{\upxi}
will be called the
(p,\upkappa)
-radial curve
in direction
\upxi
.

The
gradient exponential map

\mathop{\rm gexp}\nolimits^{\upkappa}_{p}\colon\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[0,\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}]_{\text{\rm T}_{p}}\to\penalty\mathcal{L}

is defined by

\mathop{\rm gexp}\nolimits^{\upkappa}_{p}\colon r{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upxi\mapsto\penalty\upsigma_{\upxi}(r).

Here are properties of radial curves reformulated in terms of the gradient exponential map:

16.36. Theorem.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space.
Then:

(a)

If
p,q\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
are points such that
|{p}-\penalty{q}|\leqslant\penalty\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}
, then for any geodesic
[pq]
in
\mathcal{L}
we have

\mathop{\rm gexp}\nolimits^{\upkappa}_{p}(\mathrm{log}[{p}{q}])=q.

(b)

For any
v,w\in\penalty\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[0,\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}]_{\text{\rm T}_{p}}
,

|{\mathop{\rm gexp}\nolimits^{\upkappa}_{p}v}-\penalty{\mathop{\rm gexp}\nolimits^{\upkappa}_{p}w}|\leqslant\penalty\mathop{\tilde{\curlyvee}}\nolimits^{\upkappa}[0\,{}^{v}_{w}].

In other words, if we denote by
\mathcal{T}_{p}^{\upkappa}
the set
\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[0,\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}]_{\text{\rm T}_{p}}

equipped with the metric
|{v}-\penalty{w}|_{\mathcal{T}^{\upkappa}_{p}}=\mathop{\tilde{\curlyvee}}\nolimits^{\upkappa}[0\,{}^{v}_{w}]
,
then

\mathop{\rm gexp}\nolimits^{\upkappa}_{p}:\mathcal{T}^{\upkappa}_{p}\to\penalty\mathcal{L}

is a short map.

(c)

Suppose

p,q\in\penalty\mathcal{L}

and
|{p}-\penalty{q}|\leqslant\penalty\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}
.
If
v\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}
,
|v|\leqslant\penalty 1
, and

\upsigma(t)=\mathop{\rm gexp}\nolimits^{\upkappa}_{p}(t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}v),

then the function

s\mapsto\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(\upsigma|_{0}^{s},q):=\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}\{|{q}-\penalty{\upsigma(s)}|;|{q}-\penalty{\upsigma(0)}|,s\}

is nonincreasing in its domain of definition.

Proof.

Follows directly from the construction of
\mathop{\rm gexp}\nolimits^{\upkappa}_{p}
and the radial comparison (
16I
).
∎

Applying the theorem above together with
15E
,
we obtain the following.

16.37. Corollary.

Let
p
be a point in an
m
-dimensional complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space
\mathcal{L}
,

m<\infty
, and
0<R\leqslant\penalty\tfrac{\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}}{2}
.
Then there is a short map

f\colon\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[R]_{\mathbb{M}^{m}({\upkappa})}\to\penalty\mathcal{L}

such that
\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits f=\overline{\mathrm{B}}{}[p,R]\subset\penalty\mathcal{L}
.

16.38. Exercise.

Let
\mathcal{L}\subset\penalty\mathbb{E}^{2}
be the Euclidean halfplane.
Clearly
\mathcal{L}
is a two-dimensional complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
space.
Given a point
x\in\penalty\mathbb{E}^{2}
, denote by
\mathop{\rm proj}\nolimits(x)
the closest point to
x
on
\mathcal{L}
.

Apply the radial comparison (
16I
) to show that for any interior point
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
and any
v\in\penalty\mathbb{R}^{2}
we have

\mathop{\rm gexp}\nolimits_{p}v=\mathop{\rm proj}\nolimits(p+v).

Suppose
x,p,
and
q
are points in a complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})

.

K
Remarks

Gradient flow on Riemannian manifolds

The gradient flow for general semiconcave functions
on smooth Riemannian manifolds can be introduced with much less effort.
To do this note that the distance estimates proved in the Section
16C

can be proved in the same way for gradient curves of smooth semiconcave subfunctions.
By the Greene–Wu lemma
[
69
]
,
given
a
\uplambda
-concave function
f
,
a compact set
K\subset\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
,
and
\upvarepsilon>0

there is a smooth
(\uplambda-\upvarepsilon)
-concave function that is

\upvarepsilon
-close to
f
on
K
.
Hence one can apply smoothing and pass to the limit as
\upvarepsilon\to\penalty 0
.
Note that by the second distance estimate (
16C
), the limit curve obtained does not depend on the smoothing.

Gradient curves of a family of functions

Gradient flow can be extended to a family of functions.
This type of flow was studied by Chanyoung Jun
[
89
,
90
]
, by Lucas Ferreira and Julio Valencia-Guevara
[
64
]
, and by Alexander Mielke, Riccarda Rossi, and Giuseppe Savaré
[
115
]
.
We will follow the simplified and generalized approach given by Alexander Lytchak and the third author
[
105
]
, where an application related to this type of flow is given.
The original motivation of Chanyoung Jun came from the study of pursuit-evasion problems.
Another application of this type of flow comes from the fact that
the optimal transport plan, or equivalently geodesics in the Wasserstein metric, can be described as gradient flow for a family of semiconcave functions.
This observation was used by the third author to prove that Alexandrov spaces with nonnegative curvature have nonnegative Ricci curvature in the sense of Lott–Villani–Sturm
[
127
]
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.
Let
f_{t}
be a family of functions defined on open subsets
\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f_{t}
of
\mathcal{Z}
.
More precisely, we assume that the parameter
t
lies in a real interval
\mathbb{I}
and

\Omega=\left\{\,{(x,t)\in\penalty\mathcal{Z}\times\penalty\mathbb{I}}\ {:}\ {x\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f_{t}}\,\right\}

is an open subset in
\mathcal{Z}\times\penalty\mathbb{I}
.

A family of functions
f_{t}
is called
Lipschitz
if
the function
(x,t)\mapsto\penalty f_{t}(x)
is

L
-Lipschitz for some constant
L
.

A family of functions
f_{t}
will be called
semiconcave
if
the function
x\mapsto\penalty f_{t}(x)
is
\uplambda
-concave for each
t
.
A family
f_{t}
is called
locally semiconcave
if for each
(p_{0},t_{0})\in\penalty\Omega
there is a neighborhood
\Omega^{\prime}
and
\uplambda\in\penalty\mathbb{R}
such that the restriction of
f_{t}
to
\Omega^{\prime}
is semiconcave.

One cannot expect that a direct generalization of Definition
16B
holds for every family of functions
f_{t}
; that is, gradient curves of a family
f_{t}
cannot be defined as curves satisfying the equation
\upalpha^{+}=\nabla_{\upalpha}f
.

For example, consider a
1
-Lipschitz curve
\upalpha
in the real line.
It is reasonable to assume that
\upalpha
is an
f_{t}
-gradient curve for the family
f_{t}(x)=\penalty-|x-\upalpha(t)|
.
(Indeed
\upalpha
can be realized as a limit of gradient curves for a family of functions obtained by smoothing
f_{t}
.)
On the other hand,
\upalpha^{+}(t)
might be undefined,
and even if it is defined, in general
\upalpha^{+}(t)\neq 0
, while
\nabla_{\upalpha(t)}f_{t}\equiv 0
.

Instead we define an
f_{t}
-gradient curve
as a Lipschitz curve
\upalpha
that satisfies the following inequality
for any point
p
, time
t
, and
small
\upvarepsilon>0
:

\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}{}{}\circ\penalty\upalpha(t+\upvarepsilon)\leqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}{}{}\circ\penalty\upalpha(t)-\upvarepsilon{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\bm{d}_{\upalpha(t)}f_{t}({\uparrow}_{[{\upalpha(t)}{p}]})+o(\upvarepsilon).

➊

If there is no geodesic
[\upalpha(t)\,p]
then we impose no condition.

If
\upalpha^{+}(t)=\nabla_{\upalpha(t)}f_{t}
for all
t
, then
➊
holds by the definition of gradient (
13E
).
On the other hand, the example above shows that the converse does not hold;
that is,
➊
generalizes Definition
16B
.
The defining inequality
➊
is closely related to the so-called
evolution variational inequality

[
19
, Theorem 4.0.4(iii)]
.

16.40. Distance estimate.

Let
f_{t}
and
h_{t}
be two Lipschitz families of
\uplambda
-concave functions on a complete length space
\mathcal{Z}
, and
s\geqslant\penalty 0
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.
Assume
f_{t}
and
h_{t}
have common domain
\Omega\subset\penalty{\mathcal{Z}}\times\penalty\mathbb{R}
, and
|f_{t}(x)-h_{t}(x)|\leqslant\penalty s
for any
(x,t)\in\penalty\Omega
.
Assume
t\mapsto\penalty\upalpha(t)
and
t\mapsto\penalty\upbeta(t)
are
f_{t}
- and
h_{t}
-gradient curves respectively defined on a common interval
t\in\penalty[a,b)
, and let
\ell(t)=\penalty|{\upalpha(t)}-\penalty{\upbeta(t)}|_{\mathcal{Z}}
.
If for all
t
, a minimizing geodesic
[\upalpha(t)\,\upbeta(t)]
lies in
\left\{\,{x\in\penalty{\mathcal{Z}}}\ {:}\ {(x,t)\in\penalty\Omega}\,\right\}
, then

\ell^{\prime}(t)\leqslant\penalty\uplambda{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell(t)+2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s/\ell(t),

whenever the left-hand side is defined.
Moreover,

\ell(t)^{2}+\tfrac{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s}{\uplambda}\leqslant\penalty(\ell(a)^{2}+\tfrac{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s}{\uplambda}){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}e^{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uplambda{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(t-a)}.

In particular, these inequalities hold for any
t\in\penalty\mathbb{I}
if
\Omega\supset\penalty B(p,2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}r)\times\penalty\mathbb{I}
and
\upalpha(t),\upbeta(t)\in\penalty\penalty B(p,r)
for any
t\in\penalty\mathbb{I}
.

Note that if
f_{t}=h_{t}
then
s=0
;
in this case the second inequality can be written as

\ell(t)\leqslant\penalty\ell(a){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}e^{\uplambda{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(t-a)}.

➋

In particular, it implies uniqueness of the future of gradient curves with given initial data.
This inequality also makes it possible to estimate the distance between two gradient curves for close functions.
In particular, it implies convergence for
f_{t}^{n}
-gradient curves if a sequence of
{\ell}
-Lipschitz and
\uplambda
-concave families
f^{n}_{t}
converges uniformly as
n\to\penalty\infty
.

Proof of
16K
.

Fix a time moment
t
and set
f=f_{t}
and
h=h_{t}
.
Let
m
be the midpoint of the geodesic
[\upalpha(t)\upbeta(t)]
.
Let
\upgamma\colon[0,\ell(t)]\to\penalty\mathcal{Z}
be an arclength parametrization of
[\upalpha(t)\upbeta(t)]
.
Note that
\bm{d}_{\upalpha(t)}f({\uparrow}_{[{\upalpha(t)}{m}]})
is the right derivative of
f\circ\penalty\upgamma
at
0

and
-\bm{d}_{\upalpha(t)}h({\uparrow}_{[{\upbeta(t)}{m}]})
is the left derivative of
h\circ\penalty\upgamma
at
\ell(t)
.
Since
f
and
h
are
\uplambda
-concave,

\displaystyle f\circ\penalty\upbeta(t)

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty f\circ\penalty\upalpha(t)+\ell(t){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\bm{d}_{\upalpha(t)}f({\uparrow}_{[{\upalpha(t)}{m}]})+\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uplambda{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell(t)^{2},

\displaystyle h\circ\penalty\upalpha(t)

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty h\circ\penalty\upbeta(t)+\ell(t){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\bm{d}_{\upalpha(t)}h({\uparrow}_{[{\upbeta(t)}{m}]})+\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uplambda{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell(t)^{2}.

Adding these inequalities and taking into account
|f(x)-h(x)|<s
for any
x
, we conclude that

\bm{d}_{\upalpha(t)}f({\uparrow}_{[{\upalpha(t)}{m}]})+\bm{d}_{\upalpha(t)}h({\uparrow}_{[{\upbeta(t)}{m}]})\geqslant\penalty\uplambda{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell(t)+2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s/\ell(t).

Applying the triangle inequality and
➊
at
m
, we obtain

\displaystyle\ell(t+\upvarepsilon)

\displaystyle=|{\upalpha(t+\upvarepsilon)}-\penalty{\upbeta(t+\upvarepsilon)}|\leqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty|{\upalpha(t+\upvarepsilon)}-\penalty{m}|+|{\upbeta(t+\upvarepsilon)}-\penalty{m}|\leqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty|{\upalpha(t)}-\penalty{m}|-\upvarepsilon{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\bm{d}_{\upalpha(t)}f({\uparrow}_{[{\upalpha(t)}{m}]})+

\displaystyle\quad+|{\upbeta(t+\upvarepsilon)}-\penalty{m}|-\upvarepsilon{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\bm{d}_{\upbeta(t)}h({\uparrow}_{[{\upbeta(t)}{m}]})+o(\upvarepsilon)=

\displaystyle=\ell(t)-\upvarepsilon{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(\uplambda{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell(t)+2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s/\ell(t))+o(\upvarepsilon)

for
\upvarepsilon>0
. The first inequality follows.

Since
\upalpha
and
\upbeta
are Lipschitz,
t\mapsto\penalty\ell(t)
is a Lipschitz function.
By Rademacher’s theorem, its derivative
\ell^{\prime}
is defined almost everywhere and satisfies the fundamental theorem of calculus.
Therefore the first inequality implies the second.
∎

16.41. Proposition.

Suppose
\mathcal{Z}
is a complete length space that is either
CBB
or
CAT
.
Let
f_{t}
be a family of
\uplambda
-concave functions for
t\in\penalty[a,b)
, where
\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f_{t}\supset\penalty B(z,2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}r)
for fixed
z\in\penalty\mathcal{Z}
,
r>0
and any
t
.

Let
\upalpha\colon[a,b)\to\penalty B(z,r)
be Lipschitz.
Then
\upalpha
is an
f_{t}
-gradient curve if and only if

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}{}{}\circ\penalty\upalpha(t+\upvarepsilon)\leqslant\penalty

➌

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}{}{}\circ\penalty\upalpha(t)-\upvarepsilon{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\left[\frac{f_{t}(p)-f_{t}\circ\penalty\upalpha(t)}{|{p}-\penalty{\upalpha(t)}|}-\tfrac{\uplambda}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{p}-\penalty{\upalpha(t)}|\right]+o(\upvarepsilon)

for any
t\in\penalty[a,b)
and
p\in\penalty B(z,r)\setminus\{\upalpha(t)\}
.

Proof.

Note that the geodesics
[\upalpha(t)p]
lie in
\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f_{t}
for any
t
.

Since
f_{t}
is
\uplambda
-concave, we have

\bm{d}_{\upalpha(t)}f_{t}({\uparrow}_{[{\upalpha(t)}{p}]})\geqslant\penalty\frac{f(p)-f\circ\penalty\upalpha(t)}{|{p}-\penalty{\upalpha(t)}|}-\tfrac{\uplambda}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{p}-\penalty{\upalpha(t)}|.

Hence the only-if part follows.

Given
p\in\penalty\mathcal{Z}
and
t
,
consider a point
\bar{p}\in\penalty[\upalpha(t)p]
.
Applying
➌
for
\bar{p}
, and the triangle inequality, we have

\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}{}{}\circ\penalty\upalpha(t+\upvarepsilon)\leqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}{}{}\circ\penalty\upalpha(t)-\upvarepsilon{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\left[\frac{f(\bar{p})-f\circ\penalty\upalpha(t)}{|{\bar{p}}-\penalty{\upalpha(t)}|}-\tfrac{\uplambda}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{\bar{p}}-\penalty{\upalpha(t)}|\right]+o(\upvarepsilon).

By taking
\bar{p}
close to
\upalpha(t)
,
the value
\tfrac{f(\bar{p})-f\circ\penalty\upalpha(t)}{|{\bar{p}}-\penalty{\upalpha(t)}|}-\tfrac{\uplambda}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{\bar{p}}-\penalty{\upalpha(t)}|
can be made arbitrarily close to
\bm{d}_{\upalpha(t)}f_{t}({\uparrow}_{[{\upalpha(t)}{p}]})
.
Therefore, given
\updelta>0
, the inequality

\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}{}{}\circ\penalty\upalpha(t+\upvarepsilon)\leqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}{}{}\circ\penalty\upalpha(t)-\upvarepsilon{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\bm{d}_{\upalpha(t)}f_{t}({\uparrow}_{[{\upalpha(t)}{p}]})+\upvarepsilon{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\updelta

holds for all sufficiently small
\upvarepsilon>0
.
Therefore
➊
holds.
∎

Now we are ready to formulate and prove global existence of gradient curves for time-dependent families — an analog of
16E
.

16.42. Theorem.

Suppose
\mathcal{Z}
is a complete length space that is either
CBB
or
CAT
.
Let

\{f_{t}\}
be a family of functions defined on an open set

\Omega=\left\{\,{(x,t)\in\penalty\mathcal{Z}\times\penalty\mathbb{R}}\ {:}\ {x\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f_{t}}\,\right\}.

Suppose that
f_{t}
is Lipschitz and locally semiconcave.
Then for any time
a
and initial point
p\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f_{a}
, there is a unique
f_{t}
-gradient curve
t\mapsto\penalty\upalpha(t)
defined on a maximal semiopen interval
[a,b)
.
Moreover, if
b<\infty
then
(\upalpha(t),t)
escapes from any closed set
K\subset\penalty\Omega
.

Proof.

Let
L
be a Lipschitz constant of
f_{t}
.
Fix
b>a
sufficiently small that
\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f_{t}\supset\penalty B(p,\upvarepsilon{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}L)
for any
t\in\penalty[a,b)
.
Consider a sequence
a=t_{0}<t_{1}\dots\penalty<t_{n}=\penalty b
, and a piecewise constant family of functions on
B(p,\upvarepsilon{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}L)
defined by
\hat{f}_{t}=f_{t_{i}}
if
t_{i}\leqslant\penalty t<t_{i+1}
.

Note that
\hat{f}_{t}
is time-independent on each interval
[t_{i},t_{i+1})
.
By
16E
applied recursively on each interval
[t_{i},t_{i+1})
, the proposition holds for
\hat{f}_{t}
.
That is, there is a unique
\hat{f}_{t}
-gradient curve
\hat{\upalpha}
that starts at
p
and is defined on the interval
[a,b)
.

The distance estimates (
16K
) show that as the partition gets finer, the gradient curves
\hat{\upalpha}
form a Cauchy sequence; denote its limit by
\upalpha
.
Then

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}{}{}\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}(t+\upvarepsilon)

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}{}{}\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}(t)-

\displaystyle\quad-\upvarepsilon{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\left[\frac{\hat{f}_{t}(p)-\hat{f}_{t}\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}(t)}{|{p}-\penalty{\upalpha(t)}|}-\tfrac{\uplambda}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{p}-\penalty{\hat{\upalpha}(t)}|\right]+o(\upvarepsilon)\leqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}{}{}\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}(t)-

\displaystyle\quad-\upvarepsilon{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\left[\frac{f_{t}(p)-f_{t}\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}(t)-2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s}{|{p}-\penalty{\upalpha(t)}|}-\tfrac{\uplambda}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{p}-\penalty{\hat{\upalpha}(t)}|\right]+o(\upvarepsilon),

where

s=\sup_{t,x}\{|f_{t}(x)-\hat{f}_{t}(x)|\}.

Since
s\to\penalty 0
as
\hat{\upalpha}\to\penalty\upalpha
, then
➌
holds for
\upalpha
;
that is,
\upalpha
is an
f_{t}
-gradient curve.

This proves short time existence.
Applying this argument recursively, we can find a gradient curve defined on a maximal interval
[a,b)
.
Uniqueness of this curve follows from the distance estimate
➋
.

Note that
\upalpha
is
L
-Lipschitz.
In particular, if
b<\infty
then
\upalpha(t)\to\penalty p^{\prime}
as
t\to\penalty b
.
If
(p^{\prime},b)\in\penalty\Omega
then we can repeat the procedure; otherwise
\upalpha
escapes from any closed set in
\Omega
.
∎

Gradient curves for non-Lipschitz functions

In this book, we only consider gradient curves for locally Lipschitz semiconcave subfunctions;
this turns out to be sufficient for all our needs.
However,
instead of Lipschitz semiconcave subfunctions,
it is more natural to consider upper semicontinuous semiconcave functions
with target in
[-\infty,\infty)
,
and to assume in addition that
the functions take finite values at a dense set in the domain of definition.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is a complete length space that is either
CBB
or
CAT
.
The set of such subfunctions on
\mathcal{Z}
will be denoted by

\mathrm{LCC}(\mathcal{Z})
(for
l
ower semi-
c
ontinous and semi-
c
oncave).

In this section we describe the adjustments needed
to construct gradient curves for the subfunctions in
\mathrm{LCC}(\mathcal{Z})
.

When
\mathcal{Z}=H
is a Hilbert space the theory we develop is equivalent to the classical theory of gradient flows on Hilbert space mentioned earlier
[
33
]
.

Further examples of such functions include the entropy and other closely related functionals on the Wasserstein space over a
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
space.
Another important example is given by the Cheeger energy on metric measure spaces, its gradient flow leads to the notion of the heat flow on such spaces.
The gradient flow for these functions plays an important role in the theory of optimal transport, see
[
155
]
and references there in.

Differential.

First we need to extend the definition of differential (
6G
) to
\mathrm{LCC}
subfunctions.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a complete length space and
f\in\penalty\mathrm{LCC}(\mathcal{Z})
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.
Given a point
p\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
and a geodesic direction
\upxi={\uparrow}_{[{p}{q}]}
,
let

\hat{\bm{d}}_{p}f(\upxi)=(f\circ\penalty\mathrm{geod}_{[pq]})^{+}(0)
.
Since
f
is semiconcave, the value
\hat{\bm{d}}_{p}f(\upxi)
is defined if
f\circ\penalty\mathrm{geod}_{[pq]}(t)
is finite at all sufficiently small values
t>0
,
but
\hat{\bm{d}}_{p}f(\upxi)
may take value
\infty
.
Note that
\hat{\bm{d}}_{p}f
is defined on a dense subset of
\Sigma_{p}
.

Let

\bm{d}_{p}f(\upzeta)=\varlimsup_{\upxi\to\penalty\upzeta}\hat{\bm{d}}_{p}f(\upxi),

and
\bm{d}_{p}f(v)=|v|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\bm{d}_{p}f(\upxi)
if
v=|v|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upxi
for some
\upxi\in\penalty\Sigma_{p}
.

In other words, we define differential as the smallest
upper semi-continuous positive-homogeneous function
\bm{d}_{p}f\colon\text{\rm T}_{p}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}

such that if
\hat{\bm{d}}_{p}f(\upxi)
is defined, then
\bm{d}_{p}f(\upxi)\geqslant\penalty\hat{\bm{d}}_{p}f(\upxi)
.

Existence and uniqueness of the gradient.

Note that in the proof of
13E
,
we used the Lipschitz condition just once,
to show that

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle=\sup\left\{\,{(\bm{d}_{p}f)(\upxi)}\ {:}\ {\upxi\in\penalty\Sigma_{p}}\,\right\}=

\displaystyle=\varlimsup_{x\to\penalty p}\frac{f(x)-f(p)}{|{x}-\penalty{p}|}<

\displaystyle<\infty.

The value
s
above will be denoted by
|\nabla|_{p}f
.
Note that
if the gradient
\nabla_{p}f
is defined then
|\nabla|_{p}f=|\nabla_{p}f|
,
and otherwise
|\nabla|_{p}f=\infty
.

Summarizing the discussion above,
we have the following.

13E
′
Existence and uniqueness of the gradient.

Assume
\mathcal{Z}
is a complete space and
f\in\penalty\mathrm{LCC}(\mathcal{Z})
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.
Then for any point
p\in\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
, either there is a unique gradient
\nabla_{p}f\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{p}

or
|\nabla|_{p}f=\infty
.

Further, in all the results of Section
13F

we may assume only that both
f
and the gradient of
f
are defined at the points under consideration. The proofs are the same.

Sections
13G
–
16D

require almost no changes.
Mainly, where appropriate
one needs to change
|\nabla_{p}f|

to
|\nabla|_{p}f

and/or assume that the gradient is defined at the points of interest.
Also,
➊
in Theorem
16A

is taken as the definition of gradient-like curve.
Then the theorem states that any gradient-like curve
\upalpha\colon\mathbb{I}\to\penalty\mathcal{Z}
satisfies Definition
16A
at
t\in\penalty\mathbb{I}
if
\nabla_{\hat{\upalpha}(s)}f
is defined.
Further, Definition
16B
, should be changed to the following:

16B
′
. Definition.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a complete length space
and
f\in\penalty\mathrm{LCC}(\mathcal{Z})
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{Z}
is either
CBB
or
CAT
.

A curve

\upalpha\colon[t_{\min},t_{\max})\to\penalty\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits f
will be called an
f
-gradient curve
if

\upalpha^{+}(t)=\nabla_{\upalpha(t)}f

when
\nabla_{\upalpha(t)}f
is defined and

(f\circ\penalty\upalpha)^{+}(t)=\infty

otherwise.

In the proof of local existence (
16D
), condition (
ii
)
should be changed to the following condition:

(
ii
)
′

f\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}_{n}(\bar{\upsigma}_{i})-f\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha}_{n}(\upsigma_{i})>(\bar{\upsigma}_{i}-\upsigma_{i}){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\max\{n,|\nabla|_{\hat{\upalpha}_{n}(\upsigma_{i})}f-\tfrac{1}{n})\}.

Any gradient curve
\upalpha[0,\ell)\to\penalty\mathcal{Z}

for a subfunction

f\in\penalty\mathrm{LCC}(\mathcal{Z})

satisfies the equation

\upalpha^{+}(t)=\nabla_{\upalpha(t)}f

at all values
t
, with the possible exception of
t=0
.
In particular, the gradient of
f
is defined at all points of any

f
-gradient curve, with the possible exception of the initial point.

16.43. Example.

Let
\mathcal{X}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Let
F\colon\thinspace\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
be given by
F(f)=-\int|\nabla f|^{2}d\mathcal{L}
.
Then
F
is a concave but not locally Lipschitz functional on
\mathcal{X}
and its finite precisely at functions
f\in\penalty W^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\subset\penalty L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
Integration by parts shows that for any smooth
f\in\penalty W^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
it holds that
\nabla_{f}F=\Delta f
.
The gradient flow of F is given by the heat flow
f_{t}
starting at
f
and
f_{t}
is smooth for all positive
t
.

Slower radial curves

Let
\upkappa\geqslant\penalty 0
.
Assume that for some function
\uppsi
, the curves defined by the equation

\upsigma^{+}(s)=\uppsi(s,|{p}-\penalty{\upsigma(s)}|){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nabla_{\upsigma(s)}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}{}{}

satisfy radial comparison
16I
.
Then in fact the
\upsigma(s)
are radial curves;
that is,

\uppsi(s,|{p}-\penalty{\upsigma(s)}|)=\frac{\mathrm{tg}^{\upkappa}|{p}-\penalty{\upsigma(s)}|)}{\mathrm{tg}^{\upkappa}s},

see exercise
16J
.

In case
\upkappa<0
, such a function
\uppsi
is not unique.
In particular, one can take curves defined by the simpler equation

\upsigma^{+}(s)=\frac{\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}|{p}-\penalty{\upsigma(s)}|}{\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}s}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nabla_{\upsigma(s)}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}{}{}=\frac{1}{\mathrm{sn}^{\upkappa}s}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nabla_{\upsigma(s)}(\mathrm{md}^{\upkappa}\circ\penalty\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}{}{}).

Among all curves of that type, the radial curves for curvature
\upkappa

as defined in
16H
maximize the growth of
|{p}-\penalty{\upsigma(s)}|
.

Appendix A
Semisolutions

1B
.

Suppose
\upalpha
is a closed spherical curve.
By Crofton’s formula, the length of
\upalpha
is
\uppi{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}n_{\upalpha}
, where
n_{\upalpha}
denotes the average number of crossings of
\upalpha
with equators.

Since
\upalpha
is closed, almost all equators cross it at an even number of points (we assume that
\infty
is an even number).
If
\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upalpha<2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi
then
n_{\upalpha}<2
.
Therefore there is an equator that does not cross
\upalpha
— hence the result.

2C
;
2C
.
Note that any Cauchy sequence
x_{n}
in
(\mathcal{X},\Arrowvert{*}-\penalty{*}\Arrowvert)
is also Cauchy in
\mathcal{X}
.
Since
\mathcal{X}
is complete,
x_{n}
converges; denote its limit by
x_{\infty}
.

Passing to a subsequence, we may assume that
\Arrowvert{x_{n-1}}-\penalty{x_{n}}\Arrowvert<\tfrac{1}{2^{n}}
.
It follows that there is a 1-Lipschitz curve
\upalpha\colon[0,1]\to\penalty(\mathcal{X},\Arrowvert{*}-\penalty{*}\Arrowvert)
such that
x_{n}=\upalpha(\tfrac{1}{2^{n}})
and
x_{\infty}=\upalpha(0)
.
In particular,
\Arrowvert{x_{n}}-\penalty{x_{\infty}}\Arrowvert\to\penalty 0
and
n\to\penalty\infty
.

2C
.

Fix two points
x,y\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
such that
\ell=\Arrowvert{x}-\penalty{y}\Arrowvert<\infty
.
Let
\upalpha_{n}
be a sequence of paths from
x
to
y
such that
\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits(\upalpha_{n})\to\penalty\ell
as
n\to\penalty\infty
.
Without loss of generality, we may assume that each
\upalpha_{n}
is
(\ell+1)
-Lipschitz.

Since
\mathcal{X}
is compact, there is a partial limit
\upalpha_{\infty}
of
\upalpha_{n}
as
n\to\penalty\infty
.
By semicontinuity of length,
\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upalpha_{\infty}\leqslant\penalty\ell
;
that is;
\upalpha
is a shortest path in
\mathcal{X}
.

Source:
Part
2C
appears as a Corollary in
[
83
]
; see also
[

, Lemma 2.3]
.

2C
.

The following example was suggested by Fedor Nazarov
[
119
]
.

Consider the unit ball
(B,\uprho_{0})

in the space
c_{0}
of all sequences converging to zero equipped with the sup-norm.

Consider another metric
\uprho_{1}
which is different from
\uprho_{0}
by the conformal factor

\upvarphi(\bm{x})=2+\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x_{1}+\tfrac{1}{4}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x_{2}+\tfrac{1}{8}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x_{3}+\dots\penalty,

where
\bm{x}=(x_{1},x_{2}\,\dots\penalty)\in\penalty B
.
That is, if
\bm{x}(t)
,
t\in\penalty[0,\ell]
, is a curve parametrized by
\uprho_{0}
-length
then its
\uprho_{1}
-length is

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits_{\uprho_{1}}\bm{x}=\int\limits_{0}^{\ell}\upvarphi\circ\penalty\bm{x}.

Note that the metric
\uprho_{1}
is bi-Lipschitz equivalent to
\uprho_{0}
.

Assume
\bm{x}(t)
and
\bm{x}^{\prime}(t)
are two curves parametrized by
\uprho_{0}
-length that differ only in the
m
-th coordinate; denote them by
x_{m}(t)
and
x_{m}^{\prime}(t)
respectively.
Note that if
x^{\prime}_{m}(t)\leqslant\penalty x_{m}(t)
for any
t
and
the function
x^{\prime}_{m}(t)
is locally
1
-Lipschitz at all
t
such that
x^{\prime}_{m}(t)<x_{m}(t)
, then

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits_{\uprho_{1}}\bm{x}^{\prime}\leqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits_{\uprho_{1}}\bm{x}.

Moreover this inequality is strict if
x^{\prime}_{m}(t)<x_{m}(t)
for some
t
.

Fix a curve
\bm{x}(t)
,
t\in\penalty[0,\ell]
, parametrized by
\uprho_{0}
-length.
We can choose
m
large so that
x_{m}(t)
is sufficiently close to
0
for any
t
.
In particular, for some values
t
, we have
y_{m}(t)<x_{m}(t)
, where

y_{m}(t)=(1-\tfrac{t}{\ell}){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x_{m}(0)+\tfrac{t}{\ell}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x_{m}(\ell)-\tfrac{1}{100}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\min\{t,\ell-t\}.

Consider the curve
\bm{x}^{\prime}(t)
as above with

x^{\prime}_{m}(t)=\min\{x_{m}(t),y_{m}(t)\}.

Note that
\bm{x}^{\prime}(t)
and
\bm{x}(t)
have the same endpoints, and by the above

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits_{\uprho_{1}}\bm{x}^{\prime}<\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits_{\uprho_{1}}\bm{x}.

That is, for any curve
\bm{x}(t)
in
(B,\uprho_{1})
, we can find a shorter curve
\bm{x}^{\prime}(t)
with the same endpoints.
In particular,
(B,\uprho_{1})
has no geodesics.

.

Choose a sequence of positive numbers
\upvarepsilon_{n}\to\penalty 0
and an

-net
N_{n}
of

for each

Consider the following subset of

equipped with the induced length metric

Note that
\mathcal{X}
is locally compact and geodesic.

Its completion

is isometric to the closure of
\mathcal{X}
equipped with the induced length metric;

\bar{\mathcal{X}}

by adding two points

and

.

The point

admits no compact neighborhood in
\bar{\mathcal{X}}

and there is no geodesic connecting

to

in

.

2G
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a compact metric space.
Let us identify
\mathcal{X}
with its image in
\mathrm{Bnd}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R})
under the Kuratowsky embedding (Section
2G
).
Denote by
\mathcal{K}
the
linear
convex hull of
\mathcal{X}
in the space of bounded functions on
\mathcal{X}
;
that is,
x\in\penalty\mathcal{K}
if and only if
x
cannot be separated from
\mathcal{X}
by a hyperplane.

Since
\mathcal{X}
is compact, so is
\mathcal{K}
.
It remains to observe that
\mathcal{K}
is a length space since it is convex.

Remark.

Alternatively, one can use the embedding of
\mathcal{X}
into its injective hull; see
[
85
]
.

4A
.

Let
F=\left\{\,{n\in\penalty\mathbb{N}}\ {:}\ {f(n)=n}\,\right\}
; we need to show that
\text{\rm}(F)=1
.

Consider an oriented graph
\Gamma
with vertex set
\mathbb{N}\setminus F
such that
m
is connected to
n
if
f(m)=n
.
Show that each connected component of
\Gamma
has at most one cycle.
Use it to subdivide vertices of
\Gamma
into three sets

,
S_{2}
, and
S_{3}
such that
f(S_{i})\cap\penalty S_{i}=\varnothing
for each

.

Conclude that
\text{\rm}(S_{1})=\text{\rm}(S_{2})=\text{\rm}(S_{3})=0
and hence

\text{\rm}(F)=\text{\rm}(\mathbb{N}\setminus(S_{1}\cup\penalty S_{2}\cup\penalty S_{3}))=1.

4C
.

Choose

, denote by
p_{n}
the corresponding point in
\mathbb{M}^{2}({n})=\penalty\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathbb{M}^{2}({1})

Suppose
p_{n}\to\penalty p_{\text{\rm}}
as
n\to\penalty\text{\rm}
; we can assume that
p_{\text{\rm}}\in\penalty\mathcal{T}
.
By
4C
, it is sufficient to show that

has a continuum degree.

Choose distinct geodesics
\upgamma_{1},\upgamma_{2}\colon[0,\infty)\to\penalty\mathbb{M}^{2}({1})
that start at a point
p_{1}
.
Show that the limits of
\upgamma_{1}
and
\upgamma_{2}
run in the different connected components of
\mathcal{T}\setminus\{p_{\text{\rm}}\}
.
Since there is a continuum of distinct geodesics starting at

,
we get that the degree of

is at least continuum.

On the other hand, the set of sequences of points

has cardinality continuum.
In particular, the set of points in
\mathcal{T}
has cardinality at most continuum.
It follows that the degree of any vertex is at most continuum.

5D
;
5D
.
Suppose
\mathcal{X}_{n}\mathbin{\begin{picture}\put(-1.0,1.0){$\longrightarrow$}\put(11.0,-1.5){\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\text{\tiny\rm GH}}}\end{picture}}\penalty\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{X}_{n}
are simply connected length metric spaces.
It is sufficient to show that any nontrivial covering map
f\colon\tilde{\mathcal{X}}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}
corresponds to a nontrivial covering map
f_{n}\colon\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{n}\to\penalty\mathcal{X}_{n}
for large
n
.

The latter can be constructed by covering
\mathcal{X}_{n}
by small balls that lie close to sets in
\mathcal{X}
evenly covered by
f
, preparing a few copies of these sets and gluing them in the same way as the inverse images of the evenly covered sets in
\mathcal{X}
are glued to obtain
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}
.

5D
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a cone over Hawaiian earrings.
Consider the
doubled cone

\mathcal{W}
— two copies of
\mathcal{V}
with their base points glued (see the diagram).

The space
\mathcal{W}
can be equipped with a length metric
(for example, the induced length metric from the shown embedding).

Show that
\mathcal{V}
is simply connected, but
\mathcal{W}
is not; the latter is a good exercise in topology.

If we delete from the earrings all small circles, then the obtained double cone becomes simply connected and it remains close to
\mathcal{W}
.
That is,
\mathcal{W}
is a Gromov–Hausdorff limit of simply connected spaces.

Remark.

Note that from part
5D
, the limit does not admit a nontrivial covering.
So, if we define the fundamental group as the inverse image of groups of deck transformations for all the coverings of the given space, then one may say that a Gromov–Hausdorff limit of simply connected length spaces is simply connected.

,

5D
.

Suppose that a metric on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
is close to the disk
\mathbb{D}^{2}
.
Note that
\mathbb{S}^{2}
contains a circle
\upgamma
that is close to the boundary curve of
\mathbb{D}^{2}
.
By the Jordan curve theorem,
\upgamma
divides
\mathbb{S}^{2}
into two disks, say
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
.

By
5D
, the Gromov–Hausdorff limits of
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
have to contain the whole
\mathbb{D}^{2}
, otherwise the limit would admit a nontrivial covering.

Consider points
p_{1}\in\penalty D_{1}
and
p_{2}\in\penalty D_{2}
that are close to the center of
\mathbb{D}^{2}
.
If
n
is large, the distance
|{p_{1}}-\penalty{p_{2}}|_{n}
has to be very small.
On the other hand, any curve from
p_{1}
to
p_{2}
must cross
\upgamma
, so it has length about 2 — a contradiction.

6C

6F
.

By the definition of a right derivative, there is a geodesic
\upgamma
such that both limits

\varlimsup_{\upvarepsilon\to\penalty 0+}\frac{|{\upalpha(\upvarepsilon)}-\penalty{\upgamma(\upvarepsilon)}|_{\mathcal{X}}}{\upvarepsilon}\quad\text{and}\quad\varlimsup_{\upvarepsilon\to\penalty 0+}\frac{|{\upbeta(\upvarepsilon)}-\penalty{\upgamma(\upvarepsilon)}|_{\mathcal{X}}}{\upvarepsilon}

are arbitrarily small.
By the triangle inequality, we get

\varlimsup_{\upvarepsilon\to\penalty 0+}\frac{|{\upalpha(\upvarepsilon)}-\penalty{\upbeta(\upvarepsilon)}|_{\mathcal{X}}}{\upvarepsilon}=0.

6F
.

Follows directly from the definition.

8B
.

On the plane, any nonnegatively curved metric having an everywhere dense set of singular points will do the job, where
by singular point we mean a point having total angle around it strictly smaller than
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi
.

Indeed, if
x_{i}
is a singular point, then there is
0<\upvarepsilon_{i}<1/20
such that no geodesic with ends outside of
\mathrm{B}{}(x_{i},r)
can meet the ball
\mathrm{B}{}(x_{i},\upvarepsilon_{i}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}r)
.
The set

\Omega_{n}=\bigcup_{i}\mathrm{B}{}(x_{i},\tfrac{\upvarepsilon_{i}}{n})

is open and everywhere dense.
Note that
\Omega_{n}
may intersect a geodesic of length
1/n
only within
\frac{1}{10n}
of its endpoints.
The intersection of the
\Omega_{n}
is a G-delta dense set that does not intersect the interior of any geodesic.

8C
.

Note that rescaling does not change the space.
Therefore if the space is
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
then it is
\text{\rm CBB}({\uplambda{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\upkappa})
for any
\uplambda>0
.
Passing to the limit as
\uplambda\to\penalty 0
, we may assume that the space is
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
.

The point-on-side comparison (
8C
) for
p=v
,
x=w
,
y=-w
and
z=0
implies that

\Arrowvert v+w\Arrowvert^{2}+\Arrowvert v-w\Arrowvert^{2}\leqslant\penalty 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\Arrowvert v\Arrowvert^{2}+2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\Arrowvert w\Arrowvert^{2}.

Applying the comparison for

p=v+w
,
x=w-v
,
y=v-w
and
z=0
gives the opposite inequality.
That is, the parallelogram identity

\Arrowvert v+w\Arrowvert^{2}+\Arrowvert v-w\Arrowvert^{2}=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\Arrowvert v\Arrowvert^{2}+2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\Arrowvert w\Arrowvert^{2}

holds for any vectors
v
and
w
.
Whence the statement follows.

).

8C
.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that the points
x,v,w,y
appear on the geodesic
[xy]
in that order.
By the point-on-side comparison (
8C
) we have

\displaystyle\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{y}_{p}\hskip-0.86108pt)\leqslant\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{w}_{p}\hskip-0.86108pt)

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{v}_{p}\hskip-0.86108pt),

\displaystyle\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(y\,{}^{w}_{p}\hskip-0.86108pt)

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(y\,{}^{v}_{p}\hskip-0.86108pt)\geqslant\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(y\,{}^{x}_{p}\hskip-0.86108pt).

Therefore

\displaystyle\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{y}_{p}\hskip-0.86108pt)<\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{w}_{p}\hskip-0.86108pt)\quad

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\quad\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{y}_{p}\hskip-0.86108pt)<\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{v}_{p}\hskip-0.86108pt),

\displaystyle\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(y\,{}^{x}_{p}\hskip-0.86108pt)<\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(y\,{}^{w}_{p}\hskip-0.86108pt)\quad

\displaystyle\Longleftarrow\quad\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(y\,{}^{x}_{p}\hskip-0.86108pt)<\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(y\,{}^{v}_{p}\hskip-0.86108pt).

By Alexandrov’s lemma (
6B
), we have

\displaystyle\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{y}_{p}\hskip-0.86108pt)<\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{v}_{p}\hskip-0.86108pt)\quad

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\quad\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(y\,{}^{x}_{p}\hskip-0.86108pt)<\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(y\,{}^{v}_{p}\hskip-0.86108pt),

\displaystyle\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{y}_{p}\hskip-0.86108pt)<\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{w}_{p}\hskip-0.86108pt)\quad

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\quad\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(y\,{}^{x}_{p}\hskip-0.86108pt)<\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(y\,{}^{w}_{p}\hskip-0.86108pt).

Hence the statement follows.

9E
) and use
\upvarepsilon
-convexity.

8D
.

By the definition of Busemann function (see
6A
),

\displaystyle\exp(\mathop{\rm bus}\nolimits_{\upgamma})

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to\penalty\infty}\exp(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{{\upgamma(t)}}{}{}-t)=

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to\penalty\infty}\left[\exp(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\upgamma(t)}{}{}-t)+\exp(-\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\upgamma(t)}-t)\right]=

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to\penalty\infty}\left(2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\exp(-t){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cosh\circ\penalty\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{\upgamma(t)}{}{}\right).

By the function comparison definitions of
\text{\rm CAT}({\upkappa})
space (
9E
) or
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
space (
8D
), for any
p\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
the function
f=\cosh\circ\penalty\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}{}{}
satisfies
f^{\prime\prime}+\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f\geqslant\penalty 1
(respectively
f^{\prime\prime}+\upkappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f\leqslant\penalty 1
).
The result follows.

8H
.
If

8H
.

Assume there are 4 such points
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}
.
Since the space
\mathcal{L}
is
\text{\rm CBB}({1})
it is also
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
.
By the angle comparison, the sum of the angles in any geodesic triangle in an
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
space is
\geqslant\penalty\uppi
.
Therefore the average of the
\measuredangle[x_{i}\,{}^{x_{j}}_{x_{k}}]
is larger than
\tfrac{\uppi}{3}
.
On the other hand, since each
x_{i}
has space of directions
\leqslant\penalty\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathbb{S}^{n}
and the perimeter of any triangle in
\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathbb{S}^{n}
is at most
\uppi
, the average of
\measuredangle[x_{i}\,{}^{x_{j}}_{x_{k}}]
is at most
\tfrac{\uppi}{3}
— a contradiction.

Source:
Based on the main idea in
[

]
.

9B
.

Suppose that a geodesic
[px]
is not extendable beyond
x
.
We may assume that
|{p}-\penalty{x}|<\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
;
otherwise move
p
along the geodesic toward
x
.

By the uniqueness of geodesics (
9B
), any point
y
in a neighborhood
\Omega\ni x
is connected to
p
by a unique geodesic path; denote it by
\upgamma_{y}
.
Note that
h_{t}(y)=\upgamma_{y}(t)
defines a homotopy, called the
geodesic homotopy
, between the identity map of
\Omega
and the constant map with value
p
.

Since
[px]
is not extendable,
x\notin h_{t}(\Omega)
for any
t<1
.
In particular, the local homology groups vanish at
x
— a contradiction.

at

It is sufficient to show that if

Similarly we have

9L
.

(Easier way.)

Let

(t,s)\mapsto\penalty\upgamma_{t}(s)
be the line-of-sight map
for
\upalpha
with respect to
\upalpha(0)
,
and

(t,s)\mapsto\penalty\tilde{\upgamma}_{t}(s)
be the line-of-sight map
for
\tilde{\upalpha}
with respect to
\tilde{\upalpha}(0)
.
Consider the map
F\colon\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits\tilde{\upalpha}\to\penalty\mathcal{U}
such that

F\colon\tilde{\upgamma}_{t}(s)\mapsto\penalty\upgamma_{t}(s)

Show that

majorizes
\upalpha

and conclude that
