is distance-preserving.

(Harder way.)

Prove and apply the following statement together with the Majorization theorem.

\diamond

Let
\upalpha
and
\upbeta
be two convex curves in
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\upkappa})
.
Assume

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upalpha=\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\upbeta<2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}

and there is a short bijecction
f\colon\upalpha\to\penalty\upbeta
.
Then
f
is an isometry.

9L
.

Suppose that points
p,x,q,y
appear on the curve in that cyclic order.
Assume that the geodesics
[pq]
and
[xy]
do not intersect.
Use the argument in the proof of the majorization theorem (
9L
) to show that in this case there are nonequivalent majorization maps.

Now we can assume that pairs of geodesics
[pq]
and
[xy]
intersect for all choices of points
p,x,q,y
on the curve in that cyclic order.
Show that in this case the convex hull
K
of the curve is isometric to a convex figure.

Note that the composition of a majorization map and closest point projection to
K
is a majorization.
Show and use that the boundary of a convex figure in the plane admits a unique majorization up to equivalence.

Remark.

A typical rectifiable closed curve in a
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
space can be majorized by more than one convex figure.
There are two exceptions: (1) the majorization map is distance-preserving, and (2) the curve is geodesic triangle.
It is expected that there are no other exceptions;
this question was asked by Richard Bishop in a private conversation.

9L
.

Show that quadrangle
[x^{1}x^{2}x^{3}x^{4}]
is majorized by the solid quadrangle
[\tilde{x}^{1}\tilde{x}^{2}\tilde{x}^{3}\tilde{x}^{4}]
.
Further show that the majorization is isometric;
argue as in
8C
.

9M
.

If
\ell
and
m
do not intersect, then the double cover
\mathcal{X}
is not simply connected.
In particular, by the Hadamard–Cartan theorem,
\mathcal{X}
is not
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
.

If
\ell
and
m
intersect then
\mathcal{X}
is a cone over a double cover
\Sigma
of
\mathbb{S}^{2}
branching at two pairs
(x,y)
and
(v,w)
of antipodal points.
Suppose
|{x}-\penalty{v}|_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}=\ell<\tfrac{\uppi}{2}
.
Note that the inverse image of
[xv]_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}
is a closed geodesic of length
4{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell<2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi
.
Therefore, by the generalized Hadamard–Cartan theorem,
\Sigma
is not
\text{\rm CAT}({1})
. Hence
\mathcal{X}
is not
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
by Theorem
11D
on curvature of cones.

9M
.

Let us do the second part first.
Assume
A
has nonempty interior.
Note that the space
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}
is simply connected and locally isometric to the doubling
\mathcal{W}
of
\mathcal{U}
in
A
;
that is, any point in
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}
has a neighborhood
that is isometric to a neighborhood of a point in
\mathcal{W}
.

By the Reshetnyak gluing theorem (
9I
),
\mathcal{W}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
.
Therefore
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}
is locally
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
;
it remains to apply the Hadamard–Cartan theorem (
9M
).

Let us come back to the general case.
The above argument can be applied to a closed
\upvarepsilon
-neighborhood of
A
.
After that we need to pass to a limit as
\upvarepsilon\to\penalty 0
.

The first part of the problem follows since a geodesic is a convex set.

9N
.

Let
p\mapsto\penalty\bar{p}
denote the closest-point projection to
K
.
We need to show that
|{\bar{p}}-\penalty{\bar{q}}|\leqslant\penalty|{p}-\penalty{q}|
for any
p,q\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
.

Assume
p\neq\bar{p}\neq\bar{q}\neq q
.
Note that in this case
\measuredangle[\bar{p}\,{}^{p}_{\bar{q}}]\geqslant\penalty\tfrac{\uppi}{2}
and
\measuredangle[\bar{q}\,{}^{q}_{\bar{p}}]\geqslant\penalty\tfrac{\uppi}{2}
.
Otherwise a point on the geodesic
[\bar{p}\bar{q}]
would be closer to
p
or to
q
than
\bar{p}
or
\bar{q}
respectively.
The latter is impossible since
K
is convex and therefore
[\bar{p}\bar{q}]\subset\penalty K
.

Applying the arm lemma (
9L
), we get the statement.

The cases
p=\bar{p}\neq\bar{q}\neq q
and
p\neq\bar{p}\neq\bar{q}=q
can be done similarly.
The rest of the cases are trivial.

9N
.

A more transparent, but less elementary solution via gradient flow is given by Alexander Lytchak and the third author
[
105
]
.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
p\in\penalty K
.

If
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{K}{x}{}\geqslant\penalty\uppi
, then set
\Psi(x)=p
.

Otherwise, if
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{K}{x}{}<\uppi
, by the closest-point projection lemma (
9N
),
there is a unique point
x^{*}\in\penalty K
that minimizes distance to
x
;
that is,
|{x^{*}}-\penalty{x}|=\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{K}{x}{}
.
Let us define
\ell_{x}
,
\upvarphi_{x}
and
\uppsi_{x}
using the following identities:

\displaystyle\ell_{x}

\displaystyle=|{p}-\penalty{x^{*}}|,

\displaystyle\upvarphi_{x}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\uppi}{2}-|{x^{*}}-\penalty{x}|,

\displaystyle\sin\uppsi_{x}

\displaystyle=\sin\upvarphi_{x}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sin\ell_{x},\quad 0\leqslant\penalty\uppsi_{x}\leqslant\penalty\tfrac{\uppi}{2}.

Let

\displaystyle\Psi(x)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{geod}_{[px^{*}]}(\uppsi_{x}).

Note that
\Psi
is a retraction to
K
;
that is,

\Psi(x)\in\penalty K
for any
x\in\penalty\mathcal{U}

and

\Psi(a)=a
for any
a\in\penalty K
.

Let us show that
\Psi
is short.
Given
x,y\in\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(K,\tfrac{\uppi}{2})
, let

\displaystyle x^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\Psi(x)

\displaystyle y^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\Psi(y)

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle=|{x}-\penalty{y}|

\displaystyle r^{\prime}

\displaystyle=|{x^{\prime}}-\penalty{y^{\prime}}|

\displaystyle d

\displaystyle=|{x^{*}}-\penalty{y^{*}}|

\displaystyle\upalpha

\displaystyle=\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{1}(p\,{}^{x^{*}}_{y^{*}}\hskip-0.86108pt).

Note that

\cos r\leqslant\penalty\cos\upvarphi_{x}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\upvarphi_{y}-\cos d{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sin\upvarphi_{x}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sin\upvarphi_{y}.

➊

Indeed, if
x,y\notin K
,
then

\measuredangle[x^{*}\,{}^{x}_{y*}],\measuredangle[y^{*}\,{}^{y}_{x*}]\geqslant\penalty\tfrac{\uppi}{2}

and
the inequality
➊
follows from the arm lemma (
9L
).
If
x\in\penalty K
and
y\notin K
, we obtain
➊
by the angle comparison (
9C
)
since
\measuredangle[y^{*}\,{}^{y}_{x*}]\geqslant\penalty\tfrac{\uppi}{2}
.
In the same way,
➊
is proved
if
x\notin K
and
y\in\penalty K
.
Finally, if
x,y\in\penalty K
, then
\upvarphi_{x}=\upvarphi_{y}=\tfrac{\uppi}{2}
and
r=d
;
that is, the inequality trivially holds.

Further note that

\cos\upalpha=\frac{\cos d-\cos\ell_{x}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\ell_{y}}{\sin\ell_{x}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sin\ell_{y}}.

Applying the angle-sidelength monotonicity (
9C
), we have

\displaystyle\cos r^{\prime}

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty\cos\uppsi_{x}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\uppsi_{y}-\cos\upalpha{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sin\uppsi_{x}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sin\uppsi_{y}=

\displaystyle=\cos\uppsi_{x}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\uppsi_{y}-(\cos d-\cos\ell_{x}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\ell_{y}){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sin\upvarphi_{x}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sin\upvarphi_{y}\geqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty\cos\uppsi_{x}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\uppsi_{y}-\cos d{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sin\upvarphi_{x}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sin\upvarphi_{y}.

Note that

\uppsi_{x}\leqslant\penalty\upvarphi_{x}

and

\uppsi_{y}\leqslant\penalty\upvarphi_{y}
;
in particular,

\cos\upvarphi_{x}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\upvarphi_{y}\leqslant\penalty\cos\uppsi_{x}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\uppsi_{y}.

Hence

\cos r^{\prime}\geqslant\penalty\cos r;

that is, the restriction
\Psi|_{\mathrm{B}{}(K,\tfrac{\uppi}{2})}
is short.
Clearly
\Psi
is continuous.
Since the complement of
\mathrm{B}{}(K,\tfrac{\uppi}{2})
is mapped to
p
,

\Psi
is short; that is,

r^{\prime}\leqslant\penalty r

➋

for any
x,y\in\penalty\mathcal{U}
.

If we have equality in
➋

then

\cos\ell_{x}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\ell_{y}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sin\upvarphi_{x}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sin\upvarphi_{y}=0.

If
K\subset\penalty\mathrm{B}{}(p,\tfrac{\uppi}{2})
, then
\ell_{x},\ell_{y}<\tfrac{\uppi}{2}
,
which implies that
x\in\penalty K
or
y\in\penalty K
.
Without loss of generality, we may assume that
x\in\penalty K
.

It remains to show that if
y\notin K

then the inequality
➋

is strict.
If
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{K}{y}{}\geqslant\penalty\tfrac{\uppi}{2}
, then
➋
holds since
the left-hand side is
<\tfrac{\uppi}{2}

while the right-hand side is
\geqslant\penalty\tfrac{\uppi}{2}
.
If
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{K}{y}{}<\tfrac{\uppi}{2}
, then
\upvarphi_{y}>0
. Clearly
\uppsi_{y}<\upvarphi_{y}
,
hence the inequality
➋
is strict.
∎

Below you will find a geometric way to think about the given construction;
it is close to the construction in the proof of Kirszbraun’s theorem (
10D
).

Geometric interpretation of the map
\Psi
.

Let
\mathring{\mathcal{U}}=\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits\mathcal{U}
, and
denote by
\mathring{K}
the subcone of
\mathring{\mathcal{U}}
spanned by
K
.
The space
\mathcal{U}
can be naturally identified with the unit sphere in
\mathring{\mathcal{U}}
,
that is, the set

\left\{\,{z\in\penalty\mathring{\mathcal{U}}}\ {:}\ {|z|=1}\,\right\}.

According to
11D
,
\mathring{\mathcal{U}}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
.
Note that
\mathring{K}
forms a convex closed subset of
\mathring{\mathcal{U}}
.
According to
9N
, for any point
x
there is a unique point
\hat{x}\in\penalty\mathring{K}

that minimizes the distance to
x
,
that is,
|{\hat{x}}-\penalty{x}|=\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{K}{x}{}
.
(If
|\hat{x}|\neq 0
, then in the notation above we have

x^{*}=\tfrac{1}{|\hat{x}|}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\hat{x}
.)

Consider the half-line
t\mapsto\penalty t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}p
in
\mathring{\mathcal{U}}
.
By comparison,
for given
s\in\penalty\mathring{\mathcal{U}}

the geodesics
\mathrm{geod}_{[s\ t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}p]}
converge as
t\to\penalty\infty
to a half-line,
say
\upalpha_{s}\colon[0,\infty)\to\penalty\mathring{\mathcal{U}}
.

Note that if
|x|=1
, then
|\hat{x}|\leqslant\penalty 1
.
By assumption, for any

the function

is monotonically increasing.
Therefore there is a unique value

such that

|\upalpha_{\hat{x}}(t_{x})|=1

Define

\Psi(x)=\upalpha_{\hat{x}}(t_{x}).

10A
.

Note that it is sufficient to show that any finite set of points
x^{1},\dots\penalty,x^{n}\in\penalty\mathcal{X}
lies in an isometric copy of a Euclidean polyhedron.

Observe that
\mathcal{X}
is
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
and
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
at the same time.
Show that there is a unique point
p
that minimizes the sum
|{p}-\penalty{x^{1}}|+\dots\penalty+|{p}-\penalty{x^{n}}|
.
Note that the vectors
v^{i}=\mathrm{log}[{p}{x^{i}}]
lie in a linear subspace of
\text{\rm T}_{p}
.
Moreover if
K
is the convex hull of
v_{i}
, then the origin of
\text{\rm T}_{p}
lies in the interior of
K
relative to its affine hull.
Finally observe that the exponential map is defined on all of
K
and is distance-preserving.
The statement follows since the exponential map sends
v^{i}\mapsto\penalty x^{i}
for each
i
.

10A
.

The answers are
s\leqslant\penalty\sqrt{3}
and
s\leqslant\penalty 2
respectively.

Let us start with the
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
case.
The upper bound
s\leqslant\penalty\sqrt{3}
follows from (2+2)-point comparison.
The Euclidean space works as an example if
s
is smaller than the large diagonal of the double pyramid with unit side (that is, if
s\leqslant\penalty 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sqrt{2/3}
).
Otherwise it can be embedded into a product of the real line with a two-dimensional cone.

For the
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
case, the needed space can be constructed by doubling a polyhedron
K\subset\penalty\mathbb{E}^{3}
in its boundary.
The obtained space is
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
by
12B
;
the same follows from Perelman’s doubling theorem
[
124
]
.
We assume that the points correspond to vertices of a regular tetrahedron with 3 vertices on the boundary of
K
and one in its interior; this point corresponds to a pair of points in the doubling at distance
s
from each other.

Remark.

The
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
case also follows from
[
154
,
103
]
.

10A
.

Choose a quadruple of points
p,q,r,s
.
Suppose that it admits a distance-preserving embedding into some
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\mathrm{K}})
for some
\mathrm{K}\geqslant\penalty\upkappa
.
Then

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\mathrm{K}}(p\,{}^{q}_{r}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\mathrm{K}}(p\,{}^{r}_{s}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\mathrm{K}}(p\,{}^{a}_{q}\hskip-0.86108pt)\leqslant\penalty 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi.

Applying monotonicity of the function
\upkappa\mapsto\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{q}_{r}\hskip-0.86108pt)
(
1A
) shows that

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{q}_{r}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{r}_{s}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{s}_{q}\hskip-0.86108pt)\leqslant\penalty 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi.

Since the quadruple
p,q,r,s
is arbitrary, the if part follows.

Now let us prove the only-if part.
Denote by
\upsigma
the exact upper bound on values
\mathrm{K}\geqslant\penalty\upkappa
such that all model triangles with the vertices
p,q,r,s
are defined.

Recall that
\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\mathrm{K}+}(p\,{}^{q}_{r}\hskip-0.86108pt)
denotes extended angle (
8H
).
Observe that if

\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\mathrm{K}+}(p\,{}^{q}_{r}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\mathrm{K}+}(p\,{}^{r}_{s}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\mathrm{K}+}(p\,{}^{s}_{q}\hskip-0.86108pt)=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi

➌

for some
\upsigma\geqslant\penalty\mathrm{K}\geqslant\penalty\upkappa
, then the quadruple admits a distance-preserving embedding into
\mathbb{M}^{2}({\mathrm{K}})
.

Observe that the left-hand side of
➌
is continuous in
\mathrm{K}
.
Since
\mathcal{L}
is
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
, for
\mathrm{K}=\upkappa
the left-hand side cannot exceed
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi
.
Therefore it remains smaller than
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi
for all
\upsigma\geqslant\penalty\mathrm{K}\geqslant\penalty\upkappa
;
moreover the same holds for all permutations of the labels
p,q,r,s
.

Note that we can assume the perimeter of the triple
q,r,s
is
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upsigma}}
, and use this and the overlap lemma (
10A
) to arrive at a contradiction.

According to our definition, the real line is
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
for any
\upkappa\in\penalty\mathbb{R}
,
but it does not satisfy the property for
\upkappa>0
.
The condition
\upkappa\leqslant\penalty 0
was used just once to ensure that the
\upkappa
-model triangles with the vertices
p,q,r,s
are defined.
One can assume instead that perimeters of all triangles in
\mathcal{L}
are at most
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varpi{}^{{\upkappa}}
.
This condition holds for all complete length
\text{\rm CBB}({\upkappa})
spaces of dimension at least 2; see
8H
.

10B
.

Let
\tilde{p},\tilde{x}_{1},\dots\penalty,\tilde{x}_{n}
be the array in
\mathbb{E}^{n}
provided by the (1+
n
)-point comparison (

We may assume that

Consider an

\tilde{m}_{i,j}=\langle\tilde{x}_{i},\tilde{x}_{j}\rangle

By the (1+

10D
.

Set
\tilde{Q}=\mathop{\rm Conv}\nolimits\{\tilde{x}^{0},\tilde{x}^{1},\dots\penalty,\tilde{x}^{k}\}
.
By Kirszbraun’s theorem, the map
\tilde{x}^{i}\mapsto\penalty x^{i}
can be extended to a short map
F\colon\tilde{Q}\to\penalty\mathcal{L}
;
it remains to show that the map
F
is distance-preserving.

Consider the logarithm map
G\colon x\mapsto\penalty\mathrm{log}[{x_{0}}{x}]
; note that
G
is short.
Observe that the composition
G\circ\penalty F
is distance-preserving.
Therefore
F
is distance-preserving;
in particular we can take
Q=F(\tilde{Q})
.

10D
.

Consider vectors
v^{i}=\mathrm{log}[{x^{0}}{x^{i}}]\in\penalty\text{\rm T}_{x^{0}}
.
Show that all the
v^{i}
lie in a linear subspace of
\text{\rm T}_{x^{0}}
and that
x^{i}\mapsto\penalty v^{i}
is distance-preserving.
It follows that we can identify the convex hull
K
of the
v^{i}
with the convex hull of the
\tilde{x}^{i}
.

Note that the gradient exponential map
\mathop{\rm gexp}\nolimits_{x_{0}}
maps
v^{i}
to
x^{i}
.
By assumption,

|{v^{i}}-\penalty{v^{j}}|=|{x^{i}}-\penalty{x^{j}}|

➍

for all
i
and
j
.
By
16J
,
\mathop{\rm gexp}\nolimits_{x_{0}}
is a short map.
By
➍
,
\mathop{\rm gexp}\nolimits_{x_{0}}
cannot be strictly short at a pair of points in
K
.
That is,
\mathop{\rm gexp}\nolimits_{x_{0}}
is distance-preserving on

.

10E
.
Consider the space
\mathcal{Y}^{\mathcal{X}}
of all maps
\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathcal{Y}
equipped with the product topology.

Denote by
\mathfrak{S}_{F}
the set of maps
h\in\penalty\mathcal{Y}^{\mathcal{X}}
such that the restriction
h|_{F}
is short and agrees with
f
in
F\cap\penalty A
.
Note that the sets
\mathfrak{S}_{F}\subset\penalty\mathcal{Y}^{\mathcal{X}}
are closed and any finite intersection of these sets is nonempty.

According to Tikhonov’s theorem,
\mathcal{Y}^{\mathcal{X}}
is compact.
By the finite intersection property, the intersection
\bigcap_{F}\mathfrak{S}_{F}
for all finite sets
F\subset\penalty X
is nonempty.
Hence the statement follows.

Source:
This statement appears in
[
125
]
; it is an analogous of the finite+one lemma (
10D
).

10E
.

The Kuratowsky embedding is a distance-preserving map of
\mathcal{X}
into the space of bounded functions
\mathcal{X}
equipped with the metric induced by the sup-norm (Section
2G
).
It remains to show that the latter space is injective.

The second part of the exercise is a classical result of John Isbell
[
85
]
which was rediscovered several times after him; for more on the subject see lecture notes of the third author
[
131
]
.

11D
.

By
11D
, the space
\mathcal{U}
,
\mathcal{V}
, or
\mathcal{U}\star\mathcal{V}
is
\text{\rm CBB}({1})
if and only if
\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits\mathcal{U}
,
\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits\mathcal{V}
, or
\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits(\mathcal{U}\star\mathcal{V})=\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits\mathcal{U}\times\penalty\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits\mathcal{V}
is
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
respectively.

By
11D
, the space
\mathcal{U}
,
\mathcal{V}
, or
\mathcal{U}\star\mathcal{V}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({1})
if and only if
\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits\mathcal{U}
,
\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits\mathcal{V}
, or
\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits(\mathcal{U}\star\mathcal{V})=\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits\mathcal{U}\times\penalty\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits\mathcal{V}
is
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
respectively.

It remains to show that the product of two spaces is
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
or
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
if and only if each space is
\text{\rm CBB}({0})
or
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
respectively.

12B
.

Assume
\mathcal{P}
is not
\text{\rm CAT}({0})
.
Then by
12B
, a link
\Sigma
of some simplex contains a closed local geodesic
\upalpha
with length
4{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell<2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi
.
We can assume that
\Sigma
has minimal possible dimension;
then by
12B
,
\Sigma
is locally
\text{\rm CAT}({1})
.

Divide
\upalpha
into two equal arcs
\upalpha_{1}
and
\upalpha_{2}

Assume
\upalpha_{1}
and
\upalpha_{2}
are length-minimizing, and

[-\ell,\ell]

Fix a small
\updelta>0
and

\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits\Sigma
given in polar coordinates by

\upgamma_{i}(t)=(\upalpha_{i}(\arctan\tfrac{t}{\updelta}),\sqrt{\updelta^{2}+t^{2}}).

Show that the curves
\upgamma_{1}

\upgamma_{2}
are geodesics in

Observe that a small neighborhood of the tip of
\mathop{\rm Cone}\nolimits\Sigma

\mathcal{P}

Hence we can construct two geodesics
\upgamma_{1}
and
\upgamma_{2}
in
\mathcal{P}

It remains to consider the case where
\upalpha_{1}
(and therefore
\upalpha_{2}
) is not length-minimizing.

Pass to a maximal length-minimizing arc
\bar{\upalpha}_{1}
of
\upalpha_{1}

Since

there is another geodesic

\bar{\upalpha}_{1}

It remains to repeat the above construction for the pair
\bar{\upalpha}_{1}
,
\bar{\upalpha}_{2}
.

Remark.

By
9B
the converse holds as well.
This problem was suggested by Dmitri Burago.

12C
.

Observe and use that (1) in the barycentric subdivision every vertex corresponds to a simplex of the original triangulation,
and (2) a simplex of the subdivision corresponds to a decreasing sequence of simplexes in the original triangulation.

Remark.

The second statement,
any finite simplicial complex is homeomorphic to a compact length
\text{\rm CAT}({1})
space
, is due to Valerii Berestovskii
[

]
.

12C
.

Use induction on the dimension to prove that if in a spherical simplex
\triangle
every edge is at least
\tfrac{\uppi}{2}
, then
all dihedral angles of
\triangle
are at least
\tfrac{\uppi}{2}
.

The rest of the proof goes along the same lines as the proof of the flag condition (
12C
).
The only difference is that a geodesic may spend time
at least

\uppi
on each visit to
\mathop{\rm Star}\nolimits_{v}
.

Remark.

It is not sufficient to assume only that all the dihedral angles of the simplexes are at least
\tfrac{\uppi}{2}
.
Indeed, the two-dimensional sphere with the interior of a small rhombus removed is a spherical polyhedral space glued from four triangles with angles at least
\tfrac{\uppi}{2}
.
On the other hand, the boundary of the rhombus is a closed local geodesic in this space and has length less than
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\uppi
.
Therefore the space cannot be
\text{\rm CAT}({1})
.

12C
.

Observe that if we glue two copies of spaces along
A_{i}
, then the copies of
A_{j}
for some
j\neq i
form a convex subset in the glued space.
Use this and the Reshetnyak gluing theorem (
9I
)
n
times, once for each label of the edges.

12C
.

The space
\mathcal{T}_{n}
has a natural cone structure whose vertex is the completely degenerate tree — all its edges have zero length.

Note that the space
\Sigma

over which the cone is taken comes naturally with a triangulation
by right-angled spherical simplexes.
Each simplex corresponds to the combinatorics of a possibly degenerate tree.

Note that the link of any simplex of this triangulation satisfies the no-triangle condition.
Indeed, fix a simplex
\triangle
of the complex;
suppose it is described by a possibly degenerate topological tree
t
.
A triangle in the link of
\triangle
can be described by three ways to resolve a degeneracy of
t
by adding one edge, where
(1) any pair of these resolutions can be done simultaneously, but (2) all three cannot be done simultaneously.
Direct inspection shows that this is impossible.

By Proposition
12C
, our complex is flag.
It remains to apply the flag condition (
12C
) and
11D
.

12C
.

Apply the flag condition (
12C
) and Theorem
11D
.

-space defined by

and

13F
.

Since
\upalpha
is Lipschitz, so is
f\circ\penalty\upalpha
.
By the standard Rademacher theorem, the derivative
(f\circ\penalty\upalpha)^{\prime}
is defined almost everywhere.
In particular,

(\bm{d}_{\upalpha(t)}f)(\upalpha^{+}(t))+(\bm{d}_{\upalpha(t)}f)(\upalpha^{-}(t))\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it a.e.}}}0.

Further, by the extended Rademacher theorem (more precisely its 1-dimensional case; see Proposition
13C
),
we have

\upalpha^{+}(t)+\upalpha^{-}(t)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it a.e.}}}0.

In particular,

\langle\nabla_{\upalpha(t)}f,\upalpha^{+}(t)\rangle+\langle\nabla_{\upalpha(t)}f,\upalpha^{-}(t)\rangle\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=\joinrel=}\limits^{\hbox{\tiny\it a.e.}}}0.

Finally, by the definition of gradient, we have

\langle\nabla_{\upalpha(t)}f,\upalpha^{\pm}(t)\rangle\geqslant\penalty(\bm{d}_{\upalpha(t)}f)(\upalpha^{\pm}(t)).

Hence the result follows.

13H
.

Let us pass to the ultrapower
\mathcal{L}^{\text{\rm}}\supset\penalty\mathcal{L}
.
Argue as in
13D
to show that there is a geodesic
[pb]_{\mathcal{L}^{\text{\rm}}}
such that

\bm{d}_{p}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{b}{}({\uparrow}_{[{p}{a}]})=-\langle{\uparrow}_{[{p}{b}]},{\uparrow}_{[{p}{a}]}\rangle

It follows that

\displaystyle(\bm{d}_{p}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{a}{}{})(\nabla_{p}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{b}{}{})

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty-\langle{\uparrow}_{[{p}{a}]},\nabla_{p}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{b}{}{}\rangle\leqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty-\bm{d}_{p}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{b}{}{}({\uparrow}_{[{p}{a}]})=

\displaystyle=\langle{\uparrow}_{[{p}{b}]},{\uparrow}_{[{p}{a}]}\rangle=

\displaystyle=\cos\measuredangle[p\,{}^{a}_{b}]_{\mathcal{L}^{\text{\rm}}}\leqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\leqslant\penalty\cos\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(p\,{}^{a}_{b}\hskip-0.86108pt).

15E
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{L}
is infinite-dimensional.
Denote by
\Omega_{m}\subset\penalty\mathcal{L}
the set of all points
p
with
\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits_{p}\geqslant\penalty m
.
Evidently
\Omega_{1}\supset\penalty\Omega_{2}\supset\penalty\dots\penalty

is open for each

.

By
15C
, each
\Omega_{m}
is dense in
\mathcal{L}
.
Hence there is a G-delta dense set of points
p\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
such that
\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits_{p}=\infty
.
It follows that
\Sigma_{p}
is not compact.

Source:
It was suggested by Alexander Lytchak.

16
.

Choose a finite sequence
t_{0}<\dots\penalty<t_{n}
.
Denote by
\Phi_{t_{i}}
the composition of the closest-point projections to
K_{t_{0}},\dots\penalty,K_{t_{i}}
.
Pass to a limit of the
\Phi_{t_{i}}
as the sequence becomes denser in the parameter interval.
Show and use that the limit
\upvarphi_{t}
does not depend on the choice of the sequences.

16B
.

Let
\ell(t)=|{\upalpha(t)}-\penalty{\upalpha(t_{3})}|
.
Note that

\ell^{\prime}(t)\leqslant\penalty-\langle\nabla_{\upalpha(t)}f,{\uparrow}_{[{\upalpha(t)}{\upalpha(t_{3})}]}\rangle.

Observe that the function
t\mapsto\penalty f\circ\penalty\upalpha(t)
is nondecreasing;
in particular,
f(\upalpha(t_{1}))\leqslant\penalty f(\upalpha(t_{2}))\leqslant\penalty f(\upalpha(t_{3}))
.
Therefore

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{\upalpha(t)}f,{\uparrow}_{[{\upalpha(t)}{\upalpha(t_{3})}]}\rangle

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty\bm{d}_{\upalpha(t)}f({\uparrow}_{[{\upalpha(t)}{\upalpha(t_{3})}]})\geqslant\penalty 0

for any
t\in\penalty[t_{1},t_{2}]
.
Therefore
\ell^{\prime}\leqslant\penalty 0
for any
t\in\penalty[t_{1},t_{2}]
.
Hence the statement.

16B
.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
(f\circ\penalty\upalpha)^{\prime}(t)>0
for any
t
.
Let
\hat{\upalpha}
be the arclength reparametrization of
\upalpha
.
Note that

(f\circ\penalty\hat{\upalpha})^{\prime}(s)\geqslant\penalty|\nabla_{\hat{\upalpha}(s)}f|

almost everywhere.
Therefore, by Theorem
16A
,
\hat{\upalpha}
is a gradient-like curve.
It remains to apply Lemma
16B
.

16B
.

Use
16B
to prove the only-if part.

To prove the if part, set
h(z)=\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{x}-\penalty{z}|^{2}
.
If
\upalpha
is an
f
-gradient curve, then

\displaystyle(h\circ\penalty\upalpha)^{+}

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty|{\upalpha(t)}-\penalty{x}|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle{\uparrow}_{[{\upalpha(t)}{x}]},\nabla_{\upalpha(t)}f\rangle\geqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty|{\upalpha(t)}-\penalty{x}|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\bm{d}_{\upalpha(t)}f({\uparrow}_{[{\upalpha(t)}{x}]})\geqslant\penalty

\displaystyle\geqslant\penalty f(x)-f\circ\penalty\upalpha(t).

It remains to integrate the inequality and observe that
f\circ\penalty\upalpha
is nondecreasing.

16I
.

Consider
(x,\upkappa)
- and
(z,\upkappa)
-radial curves that start at
y

and observe that they form a geodesic from
x
to
z
.
(Compare to Exercise
10D
.)

16J
.
Set
q=p+v
and
q^{\prime}=\mathop{\rm gexp}\nolimits_{p}v
.
By radial comparison,
|{q^{\prime}}-\penalty{x}|\leqslant\penalty|{q}-\penalty{x}|
for any
x\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
.
If
q\in\penalty\mathcal{L}
, this implies that
q=q^{\prime}
.
Otherwise note that
q^{\prime}
lies on the boundary line of
\mathcal{L}
, and
\mathop{\rm proj}\nolimits(q)
is the only point on this line that satisfies the inequality.

16J
.

By the angle comparison,

|\nabla_{x}\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}|\geqslant\penalty-\cos\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(x\,{}^{p}_{q}\hskip-0.86108pt)
.

Choose a
(p,\upkappa)
-radial curve
\upalpha
that starts at
p
.
Observe that

(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{p}\circ\penalty\upalpha)^{+}(t)\geqslant\penalty-|\upalpha^{+}(t)|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(\upalpha(t)\,{}^{p}_{q}\hskip-0.86108pt)

and

(\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits_{q}\circ\penalty\upalpha)^{+}(t)\geqslant\penalty-|\upalpha^{+}(t)|.

Therefore
t\mapsto\penalty\text{\hbox to0.0pt{$\tilde{\text{\phantom{n}}}$\hss}{$\measuredangle$}}^{\upkappa}(q\,{}^{\upalpha(t)}_{p}\hskip-0.86108pt)
is nondecreasing, hence the result.
